    HOW EVERY TOPIC TEACHES — THE 5-RAIL METHOD
    ────────────────────────────────────────────
    Every numbered topic (N.M) in this guide is built on the same five rails,
    in the same order. The HTML build must render all five — no rail skipped.

      🎯 GOAL      One sentence: what you will be able to DO after this topic.
      🧠 CONCEPT   Dual explanation, always as a pair:
                   [TECH]    — the technically precise version: exact terms,
                               real component names, correct mechanics.
                   [ANALOGY] — the human version: a picture from everyday life.
                   BOTH versions must point at the SAME illustration. The
                   illustration is the bridge between them — never decoration.
      🔬 EXAMPLE   The concept applied to easypj/jobpost (our Django job board:
                   models Skills, Author, Location, JobPost, User). Real
                   commands, real files, real output — never pseudo-code.
      🧪 PRACTICE  A hands-on drill the reader performs, ending with a
                   verification step: "how do you KNOW it worked?"
      📊 SUMMARY   2-4 lines compressing the topic + one ⚠️ gotcha to remember.

    [DIAGRAM:flow — The 5-Rail Method]
    ┌─────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌───────────┐  ┌──────────┐
    │ 🎯 GOAL │─▶│ 🧠 CONCEPT       │─▶│🔬 EXAMPLE│─▶│🧪 PRACTICE│─▶│📊 SUMMARY│
    │ why     │  │ [TECH]+[ANALOGY] │  │ easypj/  │  │ do it +   │  │ compress │
    │ bother? │  │ one illustration │  │ jobpost  │  │ verify it │  │ + gotcha │
    └─────────┘  └──────────────────┘  └──────────┘  └───────────┘  └──────────┘

    Chapter discipline:
      • Every chapter opens with WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS — the problem it
        solves and what breaks if you skip it.
      • Every part opens with a THINK box: What runs where? What talks to
        what? What breaks first?
      • Kubernetes-deep topics are never skipped silently — they are marked
        [KUBERNETES GUIDE] and handed off to the Kubernetes guide.
      • The 80/20 rule governs depth: the 20% of features that solve 80% of
        real problems get diagrams, labs and drills; the rest gets a table.

    🔄 The Container Revolution — Why Docker Changed Everything
       - Before Docker: "Works on my machine" syndrome
       - After Docker: Consistent environments everywhere
       - The shipping container analogy: standardized, stackable, portable

         [TIMELINE: Evolution of Software Deployment]
         ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
         │  🖥️ 2000s     │  │  💻 2010s     │  │  🐳 2013      │  │  ☸️ 2017+     │
         │  Bare Metal   │  │  Virtual      │  │  Docker       │  │  Kubernetes   │
         │              │  │  Machines     │  │  Containers   │  │  Orchestration│
         │ 1 app = 1    │  │ Multiple VMs  │  │ Lightweight,  │  │ Multi-host,   │
         │ server       │  │ per server    │  │ portable,     │  │ auto-scaling, │
         │ ❌ Wasteful  │  │ ⚠️ Heavy OS   │  │ fast startup  │  │ self-healing  │
         │ ❌ No isolation│ │ ⚠️ GBs of RAM │  │ ✅ MBs of RAM │  │ ✅ Cloud Native│
         └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘

    ☸️ Docker → Kubernetes Bridge Overview
       - Docker packages your app → Kubernetes runs it at scale
       - Dockerfile defines the blueprint → K8s Deployment defines the fleet
       - docker run tests locally → kubectl apply deploys globally

       [DOCKER TO KUBERNETES BRIDGE ARCHITECTURE]
       ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
       │                    YOUR APPLICATION JOURNEY                           │
       │                                                                      │
       │  💻 LOCAL DEV                   ☁️ PRODUCTION                         │

       │  │    +        │   push image   │  ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐       │       │
       │  │  Source     │──────────────▶│  │ Pod 1 │ │ Pod 2 │ ...   │       │
       │  │   Code      │               │  │easypj │ │easypj │       │       │
       │  └─────┬──────┘                │  └───┬───┘ └───┬───┘       │       │
       │        │ docker build          │      └───┬───┘             │       │
       │        ▼                       │       Service              │       │
       │  ┌────────────┐                │          │                 │       │

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 1: DOCKER CONCEPTS — THE BIG PICTURE                              🟢
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 1:
      What runs where?    → everything on YOUR laptop — one host, one daemon.
      What talks to what? → docker CLI → REST API → dockerd → containerd → runc.
      What breaks first?  → your mental model: a container is NOT a small VM.

Chapter 1: What is Docker? (The Container Revolution)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: You cannot debug, secure, or optimize what you
    cannot picture. This chapter builds the mental model — the problem Docker
    kills, the architecture that does the killing, and the vocabulary every
    later chapter assumes. Problem solved here: "I can type docker commands,
    but I have no idea what is actually happening."

    1.1  The Problem Docker Solves
         - "Works on my machine" — the universal developer frustration
         - Dependency hell: Python 3.8 vs 3.11, Django 4.2 vs 5.2
         - Inconsistent environments: dev ≠ staging ≠ production
         - Real-world: easypj runs on your laptop but crashes on the server

         [THE "WORKS ON MY MACHINE" PROBLEM — Illustrated]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                  🖥️  THE DEPENDENCY NIGHTMARE                              │
         │                                                                          │
         │   Developer Laptop              Staging Server            Production      │
         │   ┌────────────────┐          ┌────────────────┐       ┌──────────────┐  │
         │   │ Python 3.11 ✅ │          │ Python 3.8  ❌  │       │ Python 3.9   │  │
         │   │ Django 5.2  ✅ │          │ Django 4.2  ❌  │       │ Django 4.2   │  │
         │   │ psycopg2  ✅  │          │ psycopg2  ✅   │       │ psycopg2 ❌  │  │
         │   │ Node.js  ✅   │          │ Node.js  ❌    │       │ Node.js  ✅  │  │
         │   │ ───────────── │          │ ─────────────  │       │ ───────────  │  │
         │   │ App WORKS! 😊 │          │ App BROKEN! 😡 │       │ App BROKEN!  │  │
         │   └────────────────┘          └────────────────┘       └──────────────┘  │
         │                                                                          │
         │   "BUT IT WORKS ON MY MACHINE!!!" — Every Developer Ever                 │
         │                                                                          │
         │   SOLUTION: 🐳 Docker packages Python 3.11 + Django 5.2 + ALL deps      │
         │   into ONE portable container that runs IDENTICALLY everywhere!          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [PAIN POINT CARDS — 3 Scenarios Without Docker]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  😰 Scenario 1: New Developer Onboarding                                 │
         │  Without Docker: Install Python, Django, PostgreSQL, Redis, configure    │
         │  virtualenv, set env vars... (2+ hours)                                   │
         │  WITH Docker: docker compose up (30 seconds)                             │
         ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  🔥 Scenario 2: Production Emergency at 3 AM                             │
         │  Without Docker: SSH into server, find the right config, restart         │
         │  process manually, pray dependency versions match...                     │
         │  WITH Docker: docker pull easypj:v2 && docker compose up -d              │
         ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  📦 Scenario 3: "It worked yesterday, what changed?"                     │
         │  Without Docker: System update changed Python version, pip packages      │
         │  drifting over time. Impossible to reproduce exact environment.          │
         │  WITH Docker: Image is immutable snapshot. Yesterday's image = today's.  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Name the three environment problems Docker eliminates and
            point at exactly where each one bites easypj.
         [TECH] The root cause is environment drift: unpinned interpreter
            versions, diverging system libraries, and OS packages that mutate
            over time. Docker removes drift by shipping the entire userspace
            (interpreter + libraries + app code + config) as one immutable,
            versioned artifact — the image.
         [ANALOGY] Moving house with loose boxes vs one sealed shipping crate:
            loose boxes (pip installs, apt packages, env vars) get lost or
            crushed in transit; the sealed crate arrives exactly as packed.
            The DEPENDENCY NIGHTMARE diagram above IS the loose-boxes move —
            three machines, three different piles.
         🔬 easypj: laptop has Python 3.11 + Django 5.2; staging has 3.8 →
            `python manage.py runserver` dies on syntax it has never seen.
            One easypj image ends that entire class of bug permanently.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: Write down every single thing easypj needs to run
            (interpreter, pip packages, OS libraries, env vars, ports). That
            list is exactly what a container freezes. Verify: could a teammate
            rebuild your machine from the list alone? If not — that gap is
            where "works on my machine" lives.
         📊 SUMMARY: Docker packages the app + its full userspace into one
            immutable unit that runs identically everywhere.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Docker does NOT package the kernel — that still comes
            from the host (see 1.3). "Identical everywhere" means userspace.

    1.2  What is Docker? (The Simple Answer)
         - Docker = a platform for building, shipping, and running containers
         - Container = lightweight, standalone, executable package of software
         - The shipping container analogy in detail
         - Docker Inc. (company) vs Docker (technology) vs docker (CLI tool)

         [THE SHIPPING CONTAINER ANALOGY]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │             🚢 PHYSICAL SHIPPING  =  🐳 SOFTWARE CONTAINERS                │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐          ┌─────────────────────┐               │
         │  │  📦 Standard Container│          │  🐳 Docker Image     │               │
         │  │  Same size, shape,   │          │  Same format, runs   │               │
         │  │  fits on any ship,   │          │  on any OS, any      │               │
         │  │  truck, or train     │          │  cloud, any server   │               │
         │  └─────────┬───────────┘          └─────────┬───────────┘               │
         │            │                                │                            │
         │            ▼                                ▼                            │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐                │
         │  │  🏗️ Standardized, Stackable, Portable, Isolated      │                │
         │  │  What's INSIDE doesn't matter to the transport!      │                │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘                │
         │                                                                          │
         │  Analogy: Just like a shipping container can carry cars, TVs, or        │
         │  furniture — and any ship/truck/train can carry any container —          │
         │  Docker containers can run Python, Node.js, Go, or Java apps            │
         │  on ANY machine that has Docker installed. 🌍                           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Give the one-sentence definition of Docker and of a
            container, and separate the three things people call "Docker".
         [TECH] Docker is a container platform: a daemon (dockerd) exposing a
            REST API that builds OCI images and runs them as isolated Linux
            processes using kernel namespaces and cgroups. A container is a
            process with a private view of the OS — not a machine.
         [ANALOGY] The shipping container diagram above: transport companies
            stopped caring what is INSIDE the box once every box got the same
            corners and the same locks. Ships/trucks/trains = laptop/server/
            cloud; the standardized corners = the OCI image format.
         💡 Definitions to keep (exam-grade):
            • Image     = read-only, layered filesystem snapshot + metadata.
            • Container = a running (or stopped) instance of an image with
                          one writable layer on top.
            • Docker    = the platform that builds, ships and runs them.
         🔬 easypj: `docker run easypj:v1` starts gunicorn inside an isolated
            process — same laptop, but easypj believes it owns the machine.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: Say out loud, without notes: "an image is ___, a
            container is ___". Verify against 2.1's class↔object diagram.
         📊 SUMMARY: Build once → ship anywhere → run identically; container
            = isolated process, image = its frozen blueprint.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Docker Inc. (company) ≠ Docker (technology) ≠ docker
            (the CLI binary) — job interviews test exactly this sloppiness.

    1.3  Containers vs Virtual Machines — The Critical Difference
         - VMs: Each has its own OS kernel (heavy, GBs, minutes to boot)
         - Containers: Share host OS kernel (lightweight, MBs, seconds to start)
         - Side-by-side architecture diagram
         - Resource comparison table (CPU, RAM, Disk, Boot time, Density)

         [SIDE-BY-SIDE ARCHITECTURE: VM vs CONTAINER]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────┐  ┌───────────────────────────────────┐
         │        VIRTUAL MACHINES           │  │           CONTAINERS               │
         │                                   │  │                                   │
         │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ │  │  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ │
         │  │App A│ │App B│ │App C│ │App D│ │  │  │App A│ │App B│ │App C│ │App D│ │
         │  └──┬──┘ └──┬──┘ └──┬──┘ └──┬──┘ │  │  └──┬──┘ └──┬──┘ └──┬──┘ └──┬──┘ │
         │     │       │       │       │    │  │     │       │       │       │    │
         │  ┌──┴───────┴───────┴───────┴──┐ │  │  ┌──┴───────┴───────┴───────┴──┐ │
         │  │  Guest OS (Full Kernel)     │ │  │  │     Docker Engine           │ │
         │  │  ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐       │ │  │  └─────────────────────────────┘ │
         │  │  │Bin/Lib│ │Bin/Lib│       │ │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │  └───────┘ └───────┘       │ │  │  │     Host OS (Shared Kernel)  │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────┘ │  │  └─────────────────────────────┘ │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────┐ │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │      Hypervisor            │ │  │  │     Hardware / Cloud         │ │
         │  └────────────────────────────┘ │  │  └─────────────────────────────┘ │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────┐ │  │                                   │
         │  │    Host OS                 │ │  │  ❌ NO hypervisor needed          │
         │  └────────────────────────────┘ │  │  ❌ NO guest OS per app            │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────┐ │  │  ✅ Shared kernel = lightweight   │
         │  │    Hardware / Cloud        │ │  │                                   │
         │  └────────────────────────────┘ │  │                                   │
         └───────────────────────────────────┘  └───────────────────────────────────┘

         [STAT COMPARISON TABLE — VMs vs Containers]
         | Metric        | Virtual Machine      | Docker Container      | Winner     |
         | Startup Time  | 30-90 seconds        | 1-3 seconds           | 🐳 x30     |
         | Memory Usage  | 1-2 GB per VM        | ~50 MB per container  | 🐳 x20-40  |
         | Disk Space    | 10-40 GB per VM      | ~100 MB per image     | 🐳 x100+   |
         | CPU Overhead  | Significant (hyperv.)| Near-native           | 🐳         |
         | Density       | ~10 VMs per host     | 100s per host         | 🐳 x10+    |
         | Isolation     | Full OS isolation    | Process-level (weaker)| VM         |
         | Portability   | Hypervisor-dependent | Runs anywhere Docker  | 🐳         |

         🎯 GOAL: Draw the VM vs container stack from memory and defend the
            one case where a VM still wins.
         [TECH] A VM virtualizes hardware — a hypervisor boots a full guest
            kernel per VM. A container virtualizes the OS — all containers
            are ordinary processes scheduled by the ONE host kernel, isolated
            by namespaces (what you can SEE) and cgroups (what you can USE).
         [ANALOGY] Houses vs apartments: every house (VM) pours its own
            foundation and plumbing (guest OS); apartments (containers) share
            the building's foundation (host kernel) — cheaper, faster to move
            into, but a foundation problem touches every apartment at once.
            Both readings map onto the side-by-side diagram above.
         [DIAGRAM:decision — VM or Container?]
            Need a different KERNEL/OS (Windows app on Linux host)? ──▶ VM
            Hard multi-tenant isolation for untrusted code?          ──▶ VM
            Same-kernel app, fast start, high density?               ──▶ 🐳
            CI jobs, microservices, dev environments?                ──▶ 🐳
         🔬 easypj: one 4 GB laptop cannot run easypj + PostgreSQL + Redis +
            Nginx as four VMs (needs 16 GB+). As four containers: ~300 MB.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: Using the stat table above, compute how many
            easypj containers fit inside the RAM overhead of ONE idle VM.
            Verify: ~1.5 GB VM overhead ÷ ~50 MB per container ≈ 30 free
            containers before the VM has run any app code at all.
         📊 SUMMARY: VMs isolate with hardware + guest kernels; containers
            isolate with one shared kernel — lighter, faster, denser.
            ⚠️ Gotcha (exam favorite): you cannot run a Windows container on
            a Linux host — kernels must match. Docker Desktop only "does it"
            by hiding a Linux VM under the hood.

    1.4  Docker Architecture — How It All Fits Together
         - Docker Client (docker CLI) — what you type commands into
         - Docker Daemon (dockerd) — the background service that does the work
         - Docker Registry — where images are stored (Docker Hub, ECR, etc.)
         - Docker Objects — Images, Containers, Networks, Volumes

         [DOCKER ARCHITECTURE — CLIENT-DAEMON-REGISTRY]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                      🐳 DOCKER ARCHITECTURE                               │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │    Docker CLI        │          │        DOCKER HOST                │ │
         │  │    (Client)          │  REST    │                                  │ │
         │  │                      │  API     │  ┌────────────────────────────┐  │ │
         │  │  $ docker run       │─────────▶│  │      dockerd (Daemon)      │  │ │
         │  │  $ docker build     │ over     │  │                            │  │ │
         │  │  $ docker push      │ Unix     │  │  ┌──────────────────────┐  │  │ │
         │  │  $ docker compose   │ Socket   │  │  │    containerd         │  │  │ │
         │  │       ▲             │          │  │  │    (container runtime)│  │  │ │
         │  │       │             │          │  │  └──────────┬───────────┘  │  │ │
         │  │       │             │          │  │             │              │  │ │
         │  └───────┼─────────────┘          │  │      ┌──────┴──────┐       │  │ │
         │          │                        │  │      │    runc     │       │  │ │
         │          │                        │  │      │ (OCI runtime)│       │  │ │
         │     Docker Desktop                │  │      └──────┬──────┘       │  │ │
         │     (GUI + CLI +                   │  │             │              │  │ │
         │      Daemon bundle)               │  │      ┌──────┴──────┐       │  │ │
         │                                   │  │      │  Containers │       │  │ │
         │  ┌──────────────────────┐          │  │      │  ┌──┐ ┌──┐ │       │  │ │
         │  │   🗄️ Registry        │          │  │      │  │C1│ │C2│ │       │  │ │
         │  │   Docker Hub         │◀─────────│──┼──────│  └──┘ └──┘ │       │  │ │
         │  │   ECR / GCR / ACR   │ pull/push│  │      └────────────┘       │  │ │
         │  │   ghcr.io            │          │  │                          │  │ │
         │  └──────────────────────┘          │  │  Images: easypj:v1       │  │ │
         │                                   │  │  Networks: bridge, host   │  │ │
         │                                   │  │  Volumes: /var/lib/docker │  │ │
         │                                   │  └────────────────────────────┘  │ │
         │                                   └──────────────────────────────────┘ │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [DAEMON INTERNALS — containerd → runc → OCI Runtime]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                     🔧 DOCKER DAEMON INTERNALS                            │
         │                                                                          │
         │  dockerd ───▶ containerd ───▶ containerd-shim ───▶ runc ───▶ Container  │
         │  (API,       (Image mgmt,     (Handles stdio,     (OCI      (Your app)   │
         │   Build,      Pull/Push,      exit signals,       runtime,              │
         │   Network)    Lifecycle)      keeps container     creates               │
         │                               alive after         namespaces)            │
         │                               dockerd restart)                           │
         │                                                                          │
         │  Key: dockerd manages HIGH-LEVEL stuff (API, build, networking)          │
         │        containerd manages MID-LEVEL (image pull/push, container lifecycle)│
         │        runc does the LOW-LEVEL (creates namespaces, cgroups, starts proc)│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Trace one `docker run` end-to-end through every box in the
            architecture diagram above — no box skipped.
         [TECH] Request path: docker CLI → REST API over the Unix socket
            (/var/run/docker.sock) → dockerd (API, build, networking) →
            containerd (image + container lifecycle, over gRPC) →
            containerd-shim (per-container babysitter) → runc (creates
            namespaces + cgroups, execs your process, then exits).
         [ANALOGY] A restaurant: you (CLI) give the waiter (dockerd) an
            order; the waiter passes it to the kitchen manager (containerd),
            who assigns a line cook (runc) to actually make the dish; a food
            runner (shim) keeps your table served even when the waiter
            changes shift (daemon restart). Same boxes, same arrows, in the
            DAEMON INTERNALS diagram above.
         [DIAGRAM:flow — one docker run, numbered ①-⑦]
            ① $ docker run easypj:v1        ② POST /containers/create
            ③ image present? pull if not    ④ containerd creates task
            ⑤ shim spawns                   ⑥ runc builds namespaces/cgroups
            ⑦ gunicorn becomes PID 1 inside the container
         [TABLE: The 4 Docker objects — object | what it is | easypj example]
            Image     | read-only template          | easypj:v1
            Container | running instance            | easypj-web
            Network   | virtual LAN for containers  | easypj_default
            Volume    | persistent named storage    | pgdata
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `docker version` and split the output into
            Client vs Server halves; then `ls -l /var/run/docker.sock`.
            Verify: you can name which architecture box answered each half.
         📊 SUMMARY: the CLI is just an API client; dockerd delegates to
            containerd → shim → runc; the registry stores what they run.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: because the SHIM parents the container, restarting
            dockerd does not kill running containers (with live-restore on —
            see 2.7).

    1.5  The Docker Ecosystem
         - Docker Engine (core)
         - Docker Compose (multi-container)
         - Docker Swarm (native orchestration)
         - Docker Hub (public registry)
         - Docker Desktop (GUI for Mac/Windows)
         - Docker Scout (vulnerability scanning)
         - Docker Buildx (advanced builds)
         - Docker Build Cloud (remote builds)

         [DOCKER ECOSYSTEM ORBIT DIAGRAM]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                     🌍 THE DOCKER ECOSYSTEM                               │
         │                                                                          │
         │                         ┌──────────────┐                                 │
         │                         │  🖥️ Docker   │                                 │
         │                    ┌────│   Desktop    │────┐                            │
         │                    │    │ (GUI + CLI)  │    │                            │
         │                    │    └──────────────┘    │                            │
         │                    ▼                        ▼                            │
         │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────────┐  ┌──────────┐           │
         │  │ 🎼Compose │  │                            │  │ 🐝 Swarm │           │
         │  │ Multi-    │  │      🐳 DOCKER ENGINE       │  │ Native   │           │
         │  │ Container │  │      (dockerd + containerd) │  │ Cluster  │           │
         │  │ Local Dev │  │                            │  │ Orch.    │           │
         │  └──────────┘  └────────────┬───────────────┬─┘  └──────────┘           │
         │                             │               │                            │
         │                    ┌────────┴──────┐  ┌─────┴────────┐                  │
         │                    ▼               ▼  ▼              ▼                  │
         │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────┐            │
         │  │ 🏗️Buildx│  │ 🗄️Hub    │  │ 🔍Scout  │  │ ☁️Build Cloud│            │

         │                                                                          │
         │  TOOL BELT: docker init | docker extension | docker context | docker sbom │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: For any task, pick the right ecosystem tool in one step.
         [TABLE: Which tool, when — task | tool | one-liner]
            Run my app locally            | Engine + CLI 🐳 | docker run
            Multi-container dev stack     | Compose 🎼      | docker compose up
            Build faster / multi-arch     | Buildx 🏗️       | docker buildx build
            Store + share images          | Hub/registry 🗄️ | docker push
            Find CVEs before production   | Scout 🔍        | docker scout quickview
            Cluster without Kubernetes    | Swarm 🐝        | docker swarm init
            GUI + batteries on a laptop   | Desktop 🖥️      | (install once)
         [ANALOGY] The orbit diagram above: Docker Engine is the phone;
            Compose, Scout, Buildx, Swarm are apps installed around it. You
            never need every app — but you must know the phone.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: match blind: "team needs the same 5-service
            stack" / "image has 120 CVEs" / "build for ARM Mac AND AMD64
            server". Verify: Compose, Scout, Buildx — straight off the table.
         📊 SUMMARY: one engine, satellite tools — pick per job, not per hype.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Swarm is built INTO the engine (nothing to install);
            Compose v2 is a CLI plugin — `docker compose`, not the old
            `docker-compose` binary.

    1.6  Docker Editions & Installation Overview
         - Docker Desktop (Mac, Windows, Linux — recommended for beginners)
         - Docker Engine (Linux server — production)
         - Docker CE vs EE (Community vs Enterprise)
         - System requirements
         - Quick install commands per OS

         [TABLE: Docker Editions Compared]
         | Feature              | Docker Desktop    | Docker Engine (CE) | Docker EE/Mirantis |
         | Target               | Dev machines      | Servers            | Enterprise         |
         | GUI Dashboard        | ✅ Yes            | ❌ No              | ❌ No              |
         | Windows/Mac support  | ✅ Yes            | ❌ Linux only      | ❌ Linux only      |
         | Kubernetes built-in  | ✅ Yes            | ❌ No              | ❌ No              |
         | WSL2 integration     | ✅ Yes            | N/A                | N/A                |
         | Production-grade     | ⚠️ Not recommended| ✅ Yes            | ✅ Yes             |
         | License              | Free (personal)   | Free (Apache 2.0)  | Paid               |
         | Security scanning    | ✅ Scout included | Optional           | Advanced           |

         🎯 GOAL: Choose the right edition for laptop vs server and verify a
            fresh install in under a minute.
         [TECH] Desktop wraps Engine inside a lightweight Linux VM (WSL2 on
            Windows, Virtualization.framework on macOS) — that hidden VM is
            how Linux containers run on non-Linux machines. Servers run bare
            Engine (CE) as a systemd service; EE/Mirantis adds support/FIPS.
         [TERMINAL: 60-second install verification]
            $ docker version              # Client AND Server = daemon is up
            $ docker info                 # storage driver, cgroups, runtimes
            $ docker run --rm hello-world # full pull→create→run→exit path
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the three commands above on your machine.
            Verify: hello-world prints its message; `docker ps -a` stays
            empty because --rm cleaned up (drop --rm and look again).
         📊 SUMMARY: Desktop = dev convenience with a VM inside; Engine (CE)
            = what production actually runs.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Docker Desktop is NOT for production — and beyond a
            company size, Desktop needs a paid license; Engine never does.

    1.7  Real-World Docker Use Cases
         - Microservices: Each service in its own container
         - CI/CD: Build, test, deploy in identical environments
         - Development: Spin up databases, caches, queues instantly
         - Legacy app modernization: Containerize old apps without rewriting
         - Data science: Reproducible Jupyter/Python environments
         - easypj example: All 5 use cases applied

         [CASE STUDY CARDS — 5 Docker Use Cases]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

         │  │ easypj  │ │ Service  │ │ Service  │ │ Worker  │  its own container   │
         │  └─────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └─────────┘  → Independent scale │
         ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤

         🎯 GOAL: Map each use-case card above onto easypj's own lifecycle so
            none of them stays abstract.
         [TABLE: use case → the easypj moment that proves it]
            Microservices        → easypj web + celery worker split (Part 4)
            CI/CD                → every push builds & tests easypj (Part 8)
            Dev environments     → new hire runs the full stack in 30 s (Part 4)
            Legacy modernization → freeze an old PHP job board untouched
            Data science         → reproducible fixtures & notebooks
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: pick the ONE card your team needs most this

    1.8  Docker Terminology Cheat Sheet
         [TABLE: 25+ Docker terms defined — Image, Container, Dockerfile, Layer,
          Tag, Registry, Volume, Network, Compose, Swarm, etc.]


         - Terms are grouped, not alphabetical: BUILD terms (Dockerfile, layer,
           build context, .dockerignore, multi-stage) | RUN terms (container,
           ENTRYPOINT/CMD, restart policy) | SHIP terms (registry, tag, digest,
           manifest) | PLUMBING terms (daemon, containerd, runc, OCI, overlay2,
           namespace, cgroup)
         💡 Study trick: every term must attach to ONE diagram you have seen
            in this chapter — a term you cannot place on a picture is a term
            you do not own yet.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: cover the definition column and score yourself
            /25; anything below 20, reread the section printed next to it.
         📊 SUMMARY: this vocabulary is the API of the rest of the guide.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: image ≠ container is the #1 beginner confusion — when
            in doubt, return to the class↔object analogy (2.1).

    1.9  Containers Beyond Docker — OCI Standards & the Runtime Landscape
         - OCI (Open Container Initiative): image-spec + runtime-spec + distribution-spec
         - Docker images ARE OCI images — they run on any OCI-compliant runtime
         - The landscape: Docker | Podman (daemonless) | containerd (K8s) | CRI-O
         - Why it matters: Kubernetes removed the Docker SHIM, not Docker images
         🎯 GOAL: Explain why "Kubernetes dropped Docker" scared everyone and
            changed almost nothing.
         [TECH] OCI defines three specs: how an image is packaged
            (image-spec), how a container is started from it (runtime-spec),
            and how images move between registries (distribution-spec).
            `docker build` produces OCI artifacts; containerd + runc consume
            them — with or without dockerd in the picture. Kubernetes talks
            to containerd directly through CRI.
         [ANALOGY] OCI is the DVD standard; Docker is one brand of DVD
            player. Kubernetes swapped players (dockershim → containerd) —
            every movie (image) still plays exactly the same.
         [DIAGRAM:architecture — who implements which OCI spec]
            image-spec:    docker build │ buildah │ kaniko
            runtime-spec:  runc │ crun │ gVisor │ Kata Containers
            consumers:     dockerd │ Podman │ containerd ◀── Kubernetes (CRI)
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker info | grep -i runtime` — name the OCI
            runtime your engine uses right now. Verify: it says runc.
         📊 SUMMARY: learn Docker, inherit the whole OCI world — images you
            build here run under Kubernetes untouched. [KUBERNETES GUIDE]
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "K8s removed Docker support" = removed dockershim;
            Docker-BUILT images were never affected, only the runtime path.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 1 — minimum 10, covering: dependency hell, image
     vs container, VM vs container (incl. kernel sharing + density math),
     the dockerd→containerd→shim→runc chain, registry roles, ecosystem tool
     choice, data loss on container delete, OCI specs & runtime landscape]


Chapter 2: Docker Core Concepts — Images, Containers & Registries
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapter 1 told you WHY Docker exists; this one
    hands you the five nouns everything else conjugates: image, container,
    registry, layer, tag. Problem solved here: commands stop being
    incantations — you always know which object a command touches.

    2.1  Docker Images — The Blueprint
         - What is a Docker image? (read-only template)
         - Image = class (blueprint) | Container = object (running instance)
         - Image naming: repository:tag (e.g., easypj:v1.0, python:3.11-slim)
         - Official images vs community images
         - Image digests (SHA256) for immutability

         [IMAGE vs CONTAINER — CLASS vs OBJECT ANALOGY]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │            📦 IMAGE (Blueprint)  →  ▶️  CONTAINER (Running Instance)       │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────────┐        │
         │  │  Class in Python:       │         │  Object in Python:       │        │
         │  │  class Car:             │         │  my_car = Car()          │        │
         │  │      wheels = 4         │ create  │  my_car.wheels → 4       │        │
         │  │      color = "red"      │────────▶│  my_car.start()          │        │
         │  └─────────────────────────┘         └─────────────────────────┘        │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────────┐        │
         │  │  Docker Image:          │         │  Docker Container:       │        │
         │  │  easypj:v1.0            │ docker  │  docker run easypj:v1.0  │        │
         │  │  (read-only layers)     │  run    │  (read-write layer on    │        │
         │  │  stored on disk         │────────▶│   top, running process)  │        │
         │  └─────────────────────────┘         └─────────────────────────┘        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [DOCKER IMAGE LAYER CAKE — easypj Layers Visualized]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Layer 6 (R/W): Container Writable Layer      │  ← Container-only
         │  (logs, temp files, runtime changes)          │     (ephemeral!)
         ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Layer 5: CMD ["gunicorn", "easypj.wsgi:app"] │  ← Startup command
         │  (≈ 0 bytes — just metadata)                 │     (read-only)
         ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Layer 4: COPY . /app/                        │  ← Your code!
         │  (models.py, views.py, urls.py, settings.py)  │     (~500 KB)
         ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Layer 3: RUN pip install --no-cache-dir      │  ← Dependencies
         │  -r requirements.txt                          │     (~80 MB)
         │  (Django 5.2, gunicorn, psycopg2, etc.)      │
         ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Layer 2: COPY requirements.txt .             │  ← Deps list
         │  (≈ 200 bytes)                    ▲ CACHED!   │     (tiny)
         ├───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Layer 1: FROM python:3.11-slim               │  ← Base image
         │  (Debian 12 + Python 3.11, ~150 MB)           │     (never changes)
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         
         💡 KEY INSIGHT: Layers 1-5 are READ-ONLY and SHARED across containers.
         Layer 6 (writable) is unique per container and DELETED when container removed.

         🎯 GOAL: Read any image reference (registry/repo:tag@digest) and say
            exactly what each part pins down.
         [TECH] Full form: REGISTRY[:PORT]/NAMESPACE/REPOSITORY:TAG@DIGEST.
            Every omission has a default: registry→docker.io, namespace→
            library, tag→latest. A tag is a movable pointer; a digest
            (sha256:…) is content-addressed and immutable — same digest ⇒
            byte-identical image, forever.
         [ANALOGY] Class vs object (the diagram above): the image is the
            class definition frozen on disk; every `docker run` news-up one
            more object. Tag = a sticky note on the class; digest = its DNA.
         🔬 easypj: docker.io/you/easypj:v1.0 is convenient;
            ghcr.io/you/easypj@sha256:… is what production should pin.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker pull python:3.11-slim`, then
            `docker images --digests`. Verify: you can point at repository,
            tag, digest and IMAGE ID and state how they differ.
         📊 SUMMARY: image = read-only layered template; a full reference is
            registry/repo:tag@digest, with defaults filling the gaps.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `:latest` is only a tag NAME — nothing guarantees it
            is newest, and it silently changes underneath you.

    2.2  Docker Containers — The Running Instance
         - What is a container? (runnable instance of an image)
         - Container lifecycle: created → running → paused → stopped → deleted
         - Container isolation: Process, Network, Filesystem, IPC
         - Container is ephemeral by design (cattle, not pets)

         [CONTAINER LIFECYCLE STATE MACHINE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                     🔄 CONTAINER LIFECYCLE                                │
         │                                                                          │
         │                              docker create                               │
         │                          ┌───────────────────┐                          │
         │                          ▼                   │                          │
         │  ┌─────────┐     ┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐                    │
         │  │  📦      │     │  ▶️ CREATED  │     │  ▶️ RUNNING  │                    │
         │  │ Image   │────▶│             │────▶│             │                    │
         │  │         │     │ (on disk,   │     │ (process     │                    │
         │  │(blueprint)│    │  not started)│     │  active)    │                    │
         │  └─────────┘     └──────┬──────┘     └──┬──┬──┬───┘                    │
         │                        │               │  │  │                          │
         │                        │ docker start  │  │  │ docker stop (SIGTERM)    │
         │                        └───────────────┘  │  └──────────────┐           │
         │                                           │                 │           │
         │                                    docker │ pause    docker  │ kill      │
         │                                           │          │      │           │
         │                                           ▼          ▼      ▼           │
         │                                     ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌─────────┐  │
         │                                     │ ⏸️     │ │ ⏹️      │ │ 💀      │  │
         │                                     │PAUSED  │ │STOPPED │ │ DELETED │  │
         │                                     │(frozen)│ │(exited)│ │ (gone)  │  │
         │                                     └────┬───┘ └───┬────┘ └─────────┘  │
         │                                          │        │                     │
         │                               docker unpause  docker start              │
         │                                          │        │                     │
         │                                          ▼        ▼                     │
         │                                     ┌────────────────┐                 │
         │                                     │  ▶️ RUNNING     │                 │
         │                                     └────────────────┘                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Walk the lifecycle state machine above and name the command
            AND the signal behind every arrow.
         [TECH] stop = SIGTERM, then SIGKILL after --time (default 10 s) —
            graceful. kill = SIGKILL immediately. pause = cgroup freezer; the
            process never even sees a signal. The exit code stays on the
            stopped container (`docker ps -a`); 137 = 128+9 = SIGKILLed.
         [ANALOGY] stop is "please save your work and leave the building";
            kill is cutting the power; pause is stopping time in the room.
         🔬 easypj: `docker stop easypj-web` lets gunicorn finish in-flight
            job-post requests; `docker kill` drops them mid-response.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run nginx, then stop / start / pause / unpause /
            kill it, watching `docker ps -a` STATUS after each move.
            Verify: Exited (0) after stop, Exited (137) after kill.
         📊 SUMMARY: created→running→{paused|stopped|deleted}; stop is
            polite (SIGTERM), kill is not (SIGKILL).
            ⚠️ Gotcha: deleting a container deletes its writable layer —
            anything not on a volume is gone forever (Part 6 exists for this).

    2.3  Docker Registries — The Storage
         - What is a registry? (image storage and distribution)
         - Docker Hub: The default public registry
         - Private registries: ECR, GCR, ACR, GitHub Container Registry
         - Self-hosted: Docker Registry, Harbor, GitLab Container Registry
         - Registry authentication (docker login)

         [REGISTRY PUSH/PULL FLOW]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                     🗄️ DOCKER REGISTRY WORKFLOW                           │
         │                                                                          │
         │   DEVELOPER LAPTOP                      🗄️ REGISTRY                      │
         │   ┌────────────────┐                ┌────────────────────┐              │
         │   │ docker build   │   docker push  │  Docker Hub / ECR  │              │
         │   │ -t easypj:v1 . │──────────────▶│  ┌──────────────┐  │              │
         │   │                │                │  │ easypj       │  │              │
         │   │ ┌────────────┐ │                │  │  ├─ v1.0     │  │              │
         │   │ │ easypj:v1  │ │                │  │  ├─ v1.1     │  │              │
         │   │ │ (local)    │ │   docker pull  │  │  ├─ v2.0     │  │              │
         │   │ └────────────┘ │◀───────────────│  │  └─ latest   │  │              │
         │   └────────────────┘                │  └──────────────┘  │              │
         │                                     │  ┌──────────────┐  │              │
         │   CI/CD SERVER                       │  │ python       │  │              │
         │   ┌────────────────┐    docker pull  │  │  ├─ 3.11-slim│  │              │
         │   │ GitHub Actions │◀───────────────│  │  ├─ 3.12-slim│  │              │
         │   │ pulls + tests  │                │  │  └─ alpine    │  │              │
         │   └────────────────┘                │  └──────────────┘  │              │
         │                                     └────────────────────┘              │
         │                                                                          │
         │   REGISTRY AUTH: docker login -u username -p password registry.io        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [REGISTRY COMPARISON TABLE]
         | Registry              | Type    | Pricing         | Best For              |
         | Docker Hub            | Public  | Free (1 private)| Public images, OSS    |
         | Amazon ECR            | Cloud   | Pay per GB      | AWS workloads          |
         | Google GCR/Artifact   | Cloud   | Pay per GB      | GCP workloads          |
         | Azure ACR             | Cloud   | Pay per SKU     | Azure workloads        |
         | GitHub Container Reg. | Public  | Free (public)   | GitHub CI/CD           |
         | Harbor                | Self-Host| Free OSS       | Enterprise, air-gapped |
         | GitLab Container Reg. | Built-in| Free (per repo) | GitLab CI/CD           |

         🎯 GOAL: Push an image to a private registry and explain what
            actually moved over the wire.
         [TECH] A push uploads only the layers the registry does not already
            hold (content-addressed dedup), then the manifest. `docker login`
            stores a token in ~/.docker/config.json (or a credential helper).
         [ANALOGY] Registry = GitHub for images: Hub is public GitHub;
            ECR/GCR/ACR are the company's private server; push/pull =
            git push/pull — and unchanged layers, like unchanged files, are
            never re-uploaded. That is the dedup shown in the flow above.
         🔬 easypj: docker tag easypj:v1 ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1 &&
            docker push ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1 — the python:3.11-slim base
            layers are almost certainly on the registry already.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: push easypj:v1 twice in a row. Verify: the second
            push prints "Layer already exists" for every single layer.
         📊 SUMMARY: registries store layers + manifests; auth via docker
            login; only missing layers travel.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the image NAME decides the destination — you cannot
            push easypj:v1 to ghcr.io without re-tagging it
            ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1 first.

    2.4  Image Layers — The Secret to Docker's Speed
         - How layers work: each instruction in Dockerfile = one layer
         - Copy-on-Write (CoW) filesystem
         - Layer caching: why order matters in Dockerfile
         - Union filesystem (Overlay2, aufs, etc.)
         - Practical: easypj image layers visualized

         [LAYER CACHING STRATEGY VISUALIZED]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │               🏎️ LAYER CACHING — WHY ORDER MATTERS                        │
         │                                                                          │
         │  BAD ORDER (cache useless after code change):                            │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐         │
         │  │ FROM python:3.11-slim    │ ✅ CACHED (never changes)        │         │
         │  │ COPY . /app/             │ ❌ REBUILT (code changed!)       │         │
         │  │ COPY requirements.txt .  │ ❌ REBUILT (previous layer       │         │
         │  │ RUN pip install -r req.. │ ❌ REBUILT  changed!)            │         │
         │  └──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘         │
         │                                                                          │
         │  GOOD ORDER (only changed layers rebuilt):                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┐         │
         │  │ FROM python:3.11-slim    │ ✅ CACHED (never changes)        │         │
         │  │ COPY requirements.txt .  │ ✅ CACHED (same requirements)    │         │
         │  │ RUN pip install -r req.. │ ✅ CACHED (same install)         │         │
         │  │ COPY . /app/             │ ❌ REBUILT (only code changed!) │         │
         │  └──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┘         │
         │                                                                          │
         │  RESULT: Good order = 3 layers cached (fast!), Bad order = 3 rebuilt     │
         │  For easypj: Good build = ~3 seconds (only code changed)                 │
         │              Bad build = ~45 seconds (pip install reruns)                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CACHING STRATEGY — What Changes → What Gets Rebuilt]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  📋 CACHE INVALIDATION RULE: If layer N changes → layers N, N+1, N+2... │
         │  ALL rebuild! (Docker doesn't know if later layers depend on earlier.)   │
         │                                                                          │
         │  OUR STRATEGY FOR easypj:                                                │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │ Layer 1: FROM python:3.11-slim       ← Only rebuild if upgrading│    │
         │  │ Layer 2: COPY requirements.txt .      ← Only rebuild if deps change│  │
         │  │ Layer 3: RUN pip install              ← Only rebuild if deps change│  │
         │  │ Layer 4: COPY . /app/                 ← Rebuilds on ANY code change│ │
         │  │ Layer 5: CMD ["gunicorn", ...]       ← Almost never rebuilds    │    │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │  💡 99% of your changes are in Layer 4 (code).                            │
         │     Only 3 layers get cache checked, 1 gets rebuilt. 🚀                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Predict which layers rebuild when you change one line of
            code — BEFORE running the build.
         [TECH] Every layer is a content-addressed diff. Cache rule: a layer
            is reused iff its parent chain is identical AND its instruction +
            inputs (file checksums for COPY/ADD) are unchanged. The first
            miss invalidates every layer after it — no exceptions.
         [ANALOGY] The layer cake above: you cannot swap a middle layer
            without re-baking every layer above it — frosting included.
         🔬 easypj: editing models.py touches only the COPY . . layer →
            ~3-second rebuild. Editing requirements.txt melts the pip layer →
            ~45-second rebuild. That asymmetry IS the caching strategy.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build easypj twice, then `touch` one .py file and
            rebuild. Verify: early steps print CACHED; only COPY . . and
            later steps re-run.
         📊 SUMMARY: layers = cached diffs; order the Dockerfile from
            rarely-changing to always-changing.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a lone `RUN apt-get update` layer caches a stale
            package index forever — always chain `update && install && clean`
            in ONE layer.

    2.5  Tags and Versioning
         - What are tags? (human-readable labels for image versions)
         - Semantic versioning: easypj:v1.2.3
         - latest tag: convenience and danger
         - Multi-tagging: same image, multiple tags
         - Tagging strategies: git SHA, semantic, date-based
         [TABLE: Tagging strategy comparison]

         [TABLE: Tagging strategies — drawn]
         | Strategy | Example              | Pro                | Con                     |
         | Semantic | easypj:1.4.2         | human meaning      | manual discipline       |
         | Git SHA  | easypj:a1b2c3d       | exact provenance   | unreadable to humans    |
         | Date     | easypj:2026-07-02    | sortable           | says nothing of content |
         | Env alias| easypj:staging       | simple promotion   | mutable — it MOVES      |
         | Combined | easypj:1.4.2-a1b2c3d | best of both       | long                    |
         🎯 GOAL: Design easypj's tag scheme so any running container can be
            traced back to one exact commit.
         [TECH] Tags are mutable pointers in the registry database; nothing
            stops :v1 from being force-pushed onto different bytes tomorrow.
            Only the digest is immutable — CI should always record it.
         [ANALOGY] Tag = the name plate on an office door; digest = the
            occupant's fingerprint. People swap offices; fingerprints don't.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: tag one build as 1.0.0 AND latest AND its git
            SHA, then `docker images | grep easypj`. Verify: one IMAGE ID,
            three tags, zero extra disk used.
         📊 SUMMARY: tag for humans, digest for truth; never deploy prod from
            :latest.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: pulling a newer :latest does NOT update running
            containers — they keep the image they were created from.

    2.6  Docker Manifest and Multi-Architecture Images
         - What is a manifest? (image metadata)
         - Multi-arch images: one tag, multiple platforms (linux/amd64, linux/arm64)
         - docker manifest inspect
         - Buildx for multi-platform builds
         [DIAGRAM: One image tag → multiple platform-specific images]


         [DIAGRAM:architecture — one tag, many platforms]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │             $ docker pull easypj:v1                       │
         │                        │                                  │
         │              ┌─────────▼─────────┐                        │
         │              │   MANIFEST LIST   │  ("fat manifest")      │
         │              │   easypj:v1       │                        │
         │              └─────┬───────┬─────┘                        │
         │        linux/amd64 │       │ linux/arm64                  │
         │            ┌───────▼──┐ ┌──▼───────┐                      │
         │            │ manifest │ │ manifest │                      │
         │            │ + layers │ │ + layers │                      │
         │            └──────────┘ └──────────┘                      │
         │   Your CPU architecture decides which one downloads.      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         🎯 GOAL: Explain why the same `docker pull` works on an ARM MacBook
            and on an AMD64 server.
         [TECH] A manifest list maps platform → per-architecture manifest;
            the client sends its os/arch pair and receives the matching
            manifest, then that manifest's layers. `docker manifest inspect`
            shows the whole map.
         [ANALOGY] One book title, many translations: you ask the shop for
            "easypj v1" and are handed the edition in YOUR language — the
            title on the cover never changes.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker manifest inspect python:3.11-slim` and
            count the platforms. Verify: amd64 and arm64 are both listed.
         📊 SUMMARY: one tag → manifest list → per-arch image; Buildx (5.5)
            is how you PRODUCE these yourself.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: build on an ARM Mac without --platform and the AMD64
            server greets you with "exec format error".

    2.7  Docker Daemon Configuration — daemon.json Deep Dive
         - Location: /etc/docker/daemon.json (Linux) | Desktop → Settings → Docker Engine
         - Apply: sudo systemctl restart docker — invalid JSON = daemon won't start
         - Keys that matter: log-driver/log-opts, storage-driver, registry-mirrors,
           insecure-registries, default-address-pools, live-restore, userns-remap, icc
         🎯 GOAL: Add log rotation + live-restore to a fresh engine without
            taking running containers down — and know why both belong on
            every production host.
         [TECH] dockerd parses daemon.json once at startup; a key set BOTH as
            a CLI flag and in the file is a fatal conflict, not a merge.
            live-restore:true lets containers survive daemon restarts because
            the containerd-shim, not dockerd, parents them (see 1.4).
         [ANALOGY] daemon.json is the building's house rules: every tenant
            (container) inherits them, changing the rules requires a building
            meeting (daemon restart) — and live-restore is the clause letting
            tenants stay home during the meeting.
         [CODE: complete production daemon.json]
            {
              "log-driver": "json-file",
              "log-opts": { "max-size": "10m", "max-file": "3" },
              "live-restore": true,
              "storage-driver": "overlay2",
              "default-address-pools": [ { "base": "172.80.0.0/16", "size": 24 } ]
            }
         [TABLE: 10 daemon.json keys — key | what it does | when you need it;
            include userns-remap (container root ≠ host root), icc:false
            (inter-container comms off, Part 7), registry-mirrors (CI and
            air-gapped pulls), data-root (relocate /var/lib/docker),
            insecure-registries (lab-only HTTP registries)]
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: apply the file above, start a long-running nginx,
            restart the daemon, then `docker info | grep -A2 "Logging
            Driver"`. Verify: json-file with your limits AND nginx never
            stopped running — that is live-restore, proven.
         📊 SUMMARY: one JSON file sets every container's defaults on this
            host; log rotation + live-restore are non-negotiable in prod.
            ⚠️ Gotcha (exam + real life): unbounded json-file logs are the #1
            silent disk-filler on real Docker hosts.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 2 — minimum 10, covering: image vs container,
     reference anatomy (registry/repo:tag@digest + defaults), lifecycle
     signals (SIGTERM vs SIGKILL vs freezer), push mechanics & re-tagging,
     layer-cache prediction, tag strategy design, manifest lists,
     daemon.json keys incl. live-restore and log rotation]


Chapter 3: Installing Docker & Your First Container
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Concepts become muscle memory only through a
    terminal. This chapter installs the engine, runs your first containers,
    and teaches the CLI's grammar so every future command is guessable, not
    memorized. Problem solved here: "Docker is installed… now what do I type?"

    3.1  Installing Docker Desktop (Windows/Mac)
         - Download and install Docker Desktop
         - System requirements and prerequisites
         - First launch and onboarding wizard
         - Docker Dashboard overview (GUI)
         - WSL2 integration (Windows)
         [STEP-BY-STEP SCREENSHOTS: Installation walkthrough]

         🎯 GOAL: Get a healthy Docker Desktop and prove it with one command.
         [TECH] Desktop = a managed Linux VM (WSL2 backend on Windows,
            Virtualization.framework on macOS) + the engine inside it + GUI +
            bundled Compose/Buildx/Scout. Containers never run "on Windows" —
            they run inside that VM's Linux kernel.
         [ANALOGY] Desktop is an aquarium starter kit: tank (the VM), pump
            (the engine), instruction booklet (the GUI). Engine-on-Linux is
            buying just the pump.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: install, then `docker run --rm hello-world`.
            Verify: the message prints, and Settings shows the WSL2 backend
            enabled (Windows).
         📊 SUMMARY: Desktop = dev bundle wrapped around a hidden Linux VM.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "Docker is eating my RAM" is usually the WSL2 VM's
            appetite — tune it in .wslconfig, not in Docker.

    3.2  Installing Docker Engine on Linux
         - Ubuntu/Debian installation
         - CentOS/RHEL installation
         - Post-installation steps (docker group, start on boot)
         - Verifying installation: docker version, docker info
         [CODE BLOCKS: Exact terminal commands for each OS]

         🎯 GOAL: Install the engine on a fresh Ubuntu box and run docker as
            a non-root user.
         [TECH] The apt repo installs docker-ce + docker-ce-cli + containerd
            + the buildx/compose plugins; systemd starts dockerd; the docker
            group owns the socket — membership is root-equivalent power
            (Part 7 explains why that matters).
         [TERMINAL: the 4 lines that matter]
            $ curl -fsSL https://get.docker.com | sh      # convenience script
            $ sudo usermod -aG docker $USER && newgrp docker
            $ docker version
            $ docker run --rm hello-world
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: do it on any free-tier cloud VM. Verify:
            hello-world runs WITHOUT sudo.
         📊 SUMMARY: repo → service → group → verify; four lines, done.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: skipping the group re-login is the #1 cause of
            "permission denied on /var/run/docker.sock".

    3.3  Your First Docker Command — Hello World
         - docker run hello-world (the "Hello World" of Docker)
         - Step-by-step breakdown of what happens:
           1. Docker Client sends command to Daemon
           2. Daemon checks local images (not found)
           3. Daemon pulls hello-world from Docker Hub
           4. Daemon creates a new container from the image
           5. Container runs, prints message, exits

         [FLOWCHART: docker run hello-world — 5 Steps Visualized]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │              ⚡ docker run hello-world — WHAT ACTUALLY HAPPENS              │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌─────────┐      ┌──────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐                │
         │  │ You type │      │              │      │              │                │
         │  │ $ docker │──1──▶│  dockerd     │──2──▶│ Local Image  │                │
         │  │ run hello│      │  (Daemon)    │      │ Cache        │                │
         │  │ -world   │      │              │◀─────│ "hello-world"│                │
         │  └─────────┘      └──────┬───────┘      │ NOT FOUND!   │                │
         │                         │              └──────────────┘                │
         │                         │ 3. Pull image                                 │
         │                         ▼                                               │
         │                  ┌──────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐                │
         │                  │  🗄️ Docker   │─────▶│ Local Image  │                │
         │                  │  Hub         │      │ Cache        │                │
         │                  │  hello-world │      │ hello-world  │                │
         │                  │  (tiny: ~13KB)│      │ (cached now!)│                │
         │                  └──────────────┘      └──────┬───────┘                │
         │                                               │ 4. Create container     │
         │                                               ▼                         │
         │                  ┌──────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐                │
         │                  │ 5. Container │◀─────│  dockerd     │                │
         │                  │ Runs +       │      │  Creates     │                │
         │                  │ Prints:      │      │  Container   │                │
         │                  │ "Hello from  │      │  from Image  │                │
         │                  │  Docker!"    │      │              │                │
         │                  │ Then EXITS   │      └──────────────┘                │
         │                  └──────────────┘                                       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Narrate all 5 steps of the flowchart above from memory the
            next time any image "just works".
         [TECH] Not found locally → pull from registry → create container
            (new writable layer + namespaces) → start process → process exits
            → container remains on disk, STATUS Exited (0).
         [ANALOGY] Ordering a pizza you don't have in the freezer: delivery
            (pull) happens once; every later run is just reheating (local
            image cache).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker run hello-world` twice. Verify: the
            second run shows NO "Pulling from library/hello-world" lines —
            you just proved the image cache exists.
         📊 SUMMARY: run = (pull if absent) + create + start.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: hello-world exits instantly BY DESIGN — a stopped
            container is not an error; it is a finished process.

    3.4  Running easypj in Docker — First Contact
         - Pull Python base image: docker pull python:3.11-slim
         - Run interactively: docker run -it python:3.11-slim bash
         - Inside the container: ls, python --version, exit
         - Understanding -it flags (-i interactive, -t TTY)
         - docker ps vs docker ps -a (running vs all containers)
         [TERMINAL: Full session capture with annotations]

         [TERMINAL: first contact, annotated — drawn]
            $ docker pull python:3.11-slim           # fetch the base image
            $ docker run -it python:3.11-slim bash   # -i keep stdin, -t give TTY
            root@9f2c1a7b3e4d:/# python --version    #   Python 3.11.x — inside!
            root@9f2c1a7b3e4d:/# ls /                #   a whole Debian userland
            root@9f2c1a7b3e4d:/# exit                # PID 1 ends → container stops
            $ docker ps                              # empty — nothing running
            $ docker ps -a                           # the exited shell is HERE
         🎯 GOAL: Enter a container, look around, leave — and know where the
            container went afterwards.
         [ANALOGY] -it is scuba gear: -i is the air hose (stdin stays
            connected), -t is the mask (a terminal you can see through).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: repeat the session with --rm added. Verify:
            after exit, `docker ps -a` shows nothing — cleanup on exit.
         📊 SUMMARY: -it = interactive TTY; exit kills PID 1; the corpse
            stays visible in ps -a unless --rm.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: easypj's code is NOT in this container — a base image
            is an empty stage; COPYing the app in is Chapter 4's job.

    3.5  Essential docker run Flags
         - --name: Give your container a name
         - -d: Detached mode (run in background)
         - -p: Port mapping (host:container)
         - -e: Environment variables
         - -v: Volume mounting
         - --rm: Auto-remove on exit
         - --restart: Restart policy
         [TABLE: 15+ docker run flags with examples]

         [TABLE: the 12 flags you will actually use — flag | meaning | easypj example]
            --name     | stable handle          | --name easypj-web
            -d         | detach (background)    | -d easypj:v1
            -p         | host:container port    | -p 8000:8000
            -e         | one env var            | -e DEBUG=False
            --env-file | many env vars          | --env-file .env
            -v         | volume / bind mount    | -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
            --rm       | auto-delete on exit    | --rm easypj:v1 python manage.py test
            --restart  | restart policy         | --restart unless-stopped
            --network  | attach to a network    | --network easypj-backend
            --memory   | RAM cap                | --memory 512m
            --cpus     | CPU cap                | --cpus 1.5
            -w         | working directory      | -w /app
         🎯 GOAL: Compose a correct docker run for easypj from memory —
            named, detached, port-mapped, env-fed, restartable.
         ⚡ docker run -d --name easypj-web -p 8000:8000 --env-file .env
            --restart unless-stopped easypj:v1
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: type the ⚡ line from memory, then break it on
            purpose (-p 8000 alone; image name before the flags). Verify:
            you can read both error messages and explain each.
         📊 SUMMARY: 12 flags cover 95% of daily docker run usage.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: everything AFTER the image name is the container's
            COMMAND, not a docker flag — position is grammar, not style.

    3.6  Docker CLI Structure & Help System
         - docker <command> <subcommand> [options] [arguments]
         - docker --help, docker <command> --help
         - Tab completion setup
         - Common command patterns

         [DOCKER CLI — COMMAND ANATOMY]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                        🔤 DOCKER CLI STRUCTURE                             │
         │                                                                          │
         │   docker [GLOBAL_OPTIONS] COMMAND [SUBCOMMAND] [OPTIONS] [ARGUMENTS]     │
         │   ────── ──────────────── ─────── ──────────── ──────── ──────────        │
         │     │          │             │         │           │          │           │
         │     │          │             │         │           │          └─ Targets │
         │     │          │             │         │           │             (images, │
         │     │          │             │         │           │            containers│
         │     │          │             │         │           └─ Flags      names...)│
         │     │          │             │         │              -p, -d, -e,         │
         │     │          │             │         │              --name, -v,         │
         │     │          │             │         │              --rm, --restart     │
         │     │          │             │         └─ Subcommand                     │
         │     │          │             │            (run, ls, rm, start,           │
         │     │          │             │             stop, build, push...)         │
         │     │          │             └─ Management Group                          │
         │     │          │                container, image, volume,                │
         │     │          │                network, system, compose,                │
         │     │          │                swarm, stack, secret, config...          │
         │     │          └─ Global Flags                                           │
         │     │             --help, --version, --config, --context,                │
         │     │             --log-level, --tls, --host (rarely used)               │
         │     └─ The Docker CLI binary itself                                      │
         │                                                                          │
         │  EXAMPLES:                                                                │
         │  docker container run -d -p 8000:8000 --name easypj easypj:v1            │
         │  docker image ls --filter "reference=easypj*"                            │
         │  docker compose -f docker-compose.yml up -d                              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Predict what any docker command does from its shape alone —
            management group + subcommand + flags + target.
         [TECH] Modern CLI grammar: docker <group> <verb> [flags] [target].
            Legacy shortcuts (docker ps, docker images) are aliases for
            docker container ls / docker image ls — same code path.
         [ANALOGY] The anatomy diagram above is sentence grammar: group =
            subject, verb = verb, flags = adverbs, target = object. Commands
            stop being memorization the day you parse them as grammar.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: without running them, translate: docker network
            ls / docker volume rm pgdata / docker image prune -a. Verify
            with --help: every guess matched.
         📊 SUMMARY: groups make the CLI guessable — learn the grammar, not
            300 commands.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: --help works at EVERY level (docker --help, docker
            image --help, docker image ls --help) — the fastest docs you
            own, exam included.

    3.7  When docker Commands Fail — Reading Error Messages
         - The five errors every beginner hits in week one, decoded
         - permission denied on docker.sock → not in docker group (see 3.2)
         - Cannot connect to the Docker daemon → daemon not running
         - port is already allocated → host port taken: change -p or free it
         - No such container → name typo, or it was --rm'd / never started
         - exec format error → wrong CPU architecture image (see 2.6)
         [TABLE: error string | real meaning | first command to run]
         🎯 GOAL: Turn the five most common error strings into 10-second
            fixes instead of 30-minute searches.
         [TECH] docker errors have two sources: the CLIENT (bad grammar,
            unknown flag — fails before any API call) and the DAEMON (API
            errors: conflicts, missing objects, kernel refusals). Knowing
            which side failed halves your search space instantly.
         [ANALOGY] Client errors = the waiter rejects your order (you
            misread the menu); daemon errors = the kitchen reports a problem
            (out of stock, oven busy). You complain to different people.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: cause all five errors on purpose (stop the
            daemon, double-book a port, typo a name…). Verify: for each, you
            can say client-side or daemon-side BEFORE reading the fix.
         📊 SUMMARY: read the error's SOURCE first, the string second —
            docker errors are short, precise, and never random.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "port is already allocated" usually means a FORGOTTEN
            CONTAINER owns it — docker ps first, lsof second.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 3 — minimum 10, covering: Desktop vs Engine
     internals (the hidden VM), Linux install + docker group, hello-world
     5-step flow, -it flags, docker ps vs ps -a, the 12 run flags, restart
     policies, CLI grammar, error-message triage]

PART 2: DOCKER HANDS-ON — BUILDING & RUNNING                                🟢
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 2:
      What runs where?    → your code, inside an image YOU built, one host.
      What talks to what? → Dockerfile → context → dockerd → image → container.
      What breaks first?  → the cache: one misordered COPY = 45 s rebuilds.

Chapter 4: The Dockerfile — Your App's Recipe
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Until now you ran OTHER people's images. This
    chapter teaches the file that turns easypj's source tree into an image
    of its own: every instruction, the CMD/ENTRYPOINT trap, and multi-stage
    builds. Problem solved here: "I can run python:3.11-slim, but how does
    MY Django code get inside a container?"

    4.1  What is a Dockerfile?
         - Dockerfile = text file with instructions to build an image
         - Analogy: Recipe (Dockerfile) → Baked cake (Image) → Served slice (Container)
         - Dockerfile is declarative: you describe the RESULT, not the steps
