         [ILLUSTRATION: Recipe card → Cake → Plated slice]

         🎯 GOAL: Define what a Dockerfile is and place it correctly in the
            recipe → cake → slice chain without mixing the three up.
         [TECH] A Dockerfile is a plain-text build script: each instruction
            (FROM, RUN, COPY…) is executed top-to-bottom by BuildKit, and
            most produce one image layer. The output is an immutable image;
            the Dockerfile itself never ships inside it. Declarative means
            you commit the RECIPE to git — anyone rebuilds the same cake.
         [ANALOGY] The recipe-card illustration above IS the model: recipe
            (Dockerfile) → baked cake (image) → plated slice (container).
            You never serve the recipe, and editing a slice on the plate
            never changes the card — to change the cake, rewrite the card
            and bake again.
         🔬 easypj: a 10-line Dockerfile next to manage.py replaces the
            entire "install Python 3.11, pip install, set env vars" wiki
            page — `git clone` + `docker build` is the whole onboarding.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create a Dockerfile containing only
            `FROM python:3.11-slim` and run `docker build -t probe .`.
            Verify: `docker images probe` shows an image — one instruction
            was already a buildable recipe.
         📊 SUMMARY: Dockerfile = versioned recipe; image = the bake;
            container = the serving. Edit the recipe, never the slice.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: changes made INSIDE a running container die with it —
            they never flow back into the Dockerfile or the image.

    4.2  Dockerfile Instruction Reference
         - FROM: Base image (MANDATORY, always first)
         - LABEL: Metadata (maintainer, version, description)
         - RUN: Execute commands during build (apt-get, pip install)
         - COPY: Copy files from host to image
         - ADD: Like COPY but with URL/tar extraction (use COPY when possible)
         - WORKDIR: Set working directory
         - ENV: Set environment variables
         - ARG: Build-time variables
         - EXPOSE: Document which port the container listens on
         - CMD: Default command (overridable at runtime)
         - ENTRYPOINT: Fixed command (harder to override)
         - USER: Switch user for security
         - VOLUME: Create mount point
         - HEALTHCHECK: Container health monitoring
         - STOPSIGNAL: Signal to stop container
         - SHELL: Change default shell
         [TABLE: All instructions with syntax, description, and easypj examples]

         [TABLE: core instructions — instr | phase | easypj example]
            FROM     | build      | FROM python:3.11-slim
            WORKDIR  | build+run  | WORKDIR /app
            COPY     | build      | COPY requirements.txt .
            RUN      | build      | RUN pip install -r requirements.txt
            ENV      | build+run  | ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
            ARG      | build only | ARG APP_VERSION=dev
            EXPOSE   | metadata   | EXPOSE 8000
            USER     | run        | USER appuser
            CMD      | run        | CMD ["gunicorn", "easypj.wsgi:application"]
         🎯 GOAL: For any instruction, say whether it acts at BUILD time, at
            RUN time, or is pure metadata — that one axis explains them all.
         [TECH] RUN executes during build and bakes its result into a layer;
            CMD/ENTRYPOINT only record what to execute later, at run time.
            EXPOSE documents a port but publishes nothing (-p does that).
            COPY beats ADD unless you need tar auto-extraction — ADD's URL
            fetching is a classic audit red flag.
         [ANALOGY] A recipe has prep steps ("chop onions" = RUN, done once,
            result frozen in) and serving notes ("serve warm" = CMD, applied
            every time a slice is plated). EXPOSE is a note in the margin.
         🔬 easypj: RUN pip install bakes Django+gunicorn+psycopg2 into a
            layer once; CMD ["gunicorn", ...] fires at every docker run.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build with EXPOSE 8000 but run without -p, then
            curl localhost:8000. Verify: connection refused — EXPOSE alone
            published nothing; rerun with -p 8000:8000 and it answers.
         📊 SUMMARY: build-time instructions create layers; run-time ones
            write metadata the container consults at start.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: ENV values persist into the running container (and
            docker inspect!) — never put secrets in ENV; 5.7 shows the fix.

    4.3  CMD vs ENTRYPOINT — The Most Confusing Part
         - CMD: Default arguments, easily overridden
         - ENTRYPOINT: Fixed executable, arguments appended
         - Shell form vs Exec form
         - Combined usage: ENTRYPOINT ["gunicorn"] + CMD ["easypj.wsgi:application"]

         [CMD vs ENTRYPOINT DECISION MATRIX]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                🎯 CMD vs ENTRYPOINT — WHICH TO USE?                       │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │              │      CMD             │       ENTRYPOINT             │  │
         │  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤  │
         │  │ Purpose      │ Default command      │ Fixed executable             │  │
         │  │ Overridable? │ ✅ Easily            │ ❌ Needs --entrypoint flag   │  │
         │  │ Use When...  │ App has one purpose  │ App is a wrapper/tool        │  │
         │  │              │ but user may override │ that should always run       │  │
         │  │ Analogy      │ Default radio station│ Car ENGINE (not optional!)   │  │
         │  └──────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  [6 COMBINATIONS — What Happens?]                                        │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │ # | Dockerfile                    | docker run img | Result        │  │
         │  │ 1 | CMD ["echo", "hello"]        | (none)          | echo hello    │  │
         │  │ 2 | CMD ["echo", "hello"]        | world            | error(no cmd)│  │
         │  │ 3 | ENTRYPOINT ["echo"]           | (none)          | echo(no arg) │  │
         │  │ 4 | ENTRYPOINT ["echo"]           | hello           | echo hello    │  │
         │  │ 5 | ENTRYPOINT ["echo"]+CMD["Hi"]| (none)          | echo Hi       │  │
         │  │ 6 | ENTRYPOINT ["echo"]+CMD["Hi"]| world←OVERRIDES!| echo world    │  │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  🏆 For easypj: ENTRYPOINT ["gunicorn"] +                                │
         │     CMD ["easypj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"]            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Predict what runs for all six ENTRYPOINT/CMD combinations
            in the matrix above — before Docker shows you.
         [TECH] Both settle the same question: what is PID 1? Rule: final
            command = ENTRYPOINT + CMD concatenated; anything typed after
            the image name replaces CMD; --entrypoint replaces ENTRYPOINT.
            Always prefer exec form ["cmd","arg"] — shell form wraps your
            app in /bin/sh -c, and then SIGTERM hits the shell, not the app.
         [ANALOGY] The "Analogy" row in the matrix above says it: ENTRYPOINT
            is the car's engine (fixed — the car always drives), CMD is the
            radio preset (a default the driver overrides freely).
         🔬 easypj: ENTRYPOINT ["gunicorn"] + CMD ["easypj.wsgi:application",
            "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"] — so `docker run easypj:v1
            easypj.wsgi:application --workers 4` swaps args, never gunicorn.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: rebuild combination #6 from the matrix with
            echo, then run it with and without a trailing word.
            Verify: output flips exactly as rows 5 and 6 predict.
         📊 SUMMARY: ENTRYPOINT = fixed verb, CMD = default arguments; the
            run command line only ever replaces CMD.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: shell-form CMD means gunicorn never receives docker
            stop's SIGTERM — 10 s of silence, then SIGKILL mid-request.

    4.4  Building an easypj Dockerfile — Step by Step
         - Step 1: FROM python:3.11-slim (why slim, not alpine for Django)
         - Step 2: WORKDIR /app
         - Step 3: COPY requirements.txt . (caching strategy)
         - Step 4: RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
         - Step 5: COPY . .
         - Step 6: ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
         - Step 7: EXPOSE 8000
         - Step 8: CMD ["gunicorn", "easypj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"]
         [COMPLETE DOCKERFILE CODE with annotated comments on every line]
         [LAYER VISUALIZATION: Which lines create which layers]

         [CODE: easypj/Dockerfile — the 8 steps as one real file]
            FROM python:3.11-slim                # 1 base: Debian + Py 3.11
            WORKDIR /app                         # 2 all paths now /app
            COPY requirements.txt .              # 3 deps list FIRST (cache)
            RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt        # 4
            COPY . .                             # 5 code LAST (changes most)
            ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1          # 6
            EXPOSE 8000                          # 7 documentation only
            CMD ["gunicorn", "easypj.wsgi:application", \
                 "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"]       # 8 PID 1 at run time
         🎯 GOAL: Write easypj's first Dockerfile from memory and justify
            the ORDER of all eight lines, not just their content.
         [TECH] Steps 3-4 before step 5 is the caching strategy from 2.4:
            requirements.txt changes monthly, code changes hourly, so the
            45-second pip layer stays cached across code edits.
            python:3.11-slim over alpine: psycopg2 needs glibc wheels —
            musl forces slow, fragile source compiles (8.2 has the duel).
         [ANALOGY] Packing a suitcase: heavy rarely-touched items at the
            bottom (base, deps), things you swap daily on top (your code) —
            so repacking never means emptying the whole case.
         🔬 easypj: edit jobpost/models.py, rebuild → steps 1-4 all print
            CACHED; only COPY . . and later metadata steps re-run: ~3 s.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build the file above as easypj:v1, touch
            jobpost/views.py, rebuild. Verify: build log shows CACHED for
            the pip layer and a rebuild only from COPY . . onward.
         📊 SUMMARY: 8 lines, ordered stable→volatile; slim base because
            Django + psycopg2 want glibc.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: this image still runs as root and has no
            migrations/static story — 4.6, 4.7 and 9.7 finish the job.

    4.5  Multi-Stage Dockerfiles — Smaller, Safer Images
         - Problem: Build tools bloating the final image (gcc, headers)
         - Solution: Build in one stage, copy artifacts to slim final stage
         - Multi-stage for Python: Build stage → Final stage
         - Multi-stage for Django: Collect static in build, serve in final
         - Size comparison: Single-stage vs Multi-stage

         [MULTI-STAGE BUILD — ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                   🏗️ MULTI-STAGE BUILD ARCHITECTURE                        │
         │                                                                          │
         │  STAGE 1: BUILDER (python:3.11)              STAGE 2: FINAL (slim)        │
         │  ┌───────────────────────────┐              ┌───────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ FROM python:3.11 AS build │              │ FROM python:3.11-slim     │ │
         │  │                           │              │                           │ │
         │  │ COPY requirements.txt .   │              │ COPY --from=build         │ │
         │  │ RUN pip install --user    │   COPY       │      /root/.local          │ │
         │  │   -r requirements.txt     │─────────────▶│      /root/.local          │ │
         │  │                           │  artifacts   │                           │ │
         │  │ RUN python manage.py      │              │ COPY --from=build         │ │
         │  │   collectstatic           │─────────────▶│      /app/static           │ │
         │  │                           │  static      │                           │ │
         │  │ Includes: gcc, make,      │              │ NO gcc, NO build tools!   │ │
         │  │   git, headers, dev libs  │              │ Only runtime deps.        │ │
         │  │                           │              │                           │ │
         │  │ SIZE: ~850 MB             │              │ SIZE: ~180 MB (5x smaller!)│ │
         │  └───────────────────────────┘              └───────────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                                          │
         │  [SIZE COMPARISON TABLE]                                                  │
         │  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────┐         │
         │  │ Metric               │ Single-Stage      │ Multi-Stage      │         │
         │  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────┤         │
         │  │ Image Size           │ ~850 MB           │ ~180 MB ✅       │         │
         │  │ Build Time           │ 45s               │ 42s (similar)    │         │
         │  │ Attack Surface       │ Large (gcc, git)  │ Small ✅         │         │
         │  │ CVEs                 │ 120+              │ 30-40 ✅         │         │
         │  │ Pull Time (10Mbps)   │ ~11 min           │ ~2.4 min ✅      │         │
         │  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────┘         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Split easypj's build into builder + final stages and cut
            the image from ~850 MB to ~180 MB, as the diagram above shows.
         [TECH] Every FROM opens a new stage; only the LAST stage becomes
            the image. COPY --from=build lifts chosen artifacts (installed
            packages, collected static) across; gcc, headers and git stay
            behind in the discarded builder stage. Name stages (AS build)
            so COPY --from survives reordering.
         [ANALOGY] A workshop and a showroom: you saw, sand and paint in
            the workshop (builder stage, sawdust everywhere), then carry
            only the finished chair into the showroom (final stage). The
            architecture diagram above draws exactly that left-to-right
            move — and its size table prices it.
         🔬 easypj: builder installs with gcc available and runs
            collectstatic; the final slim stage COPYs /root/.local and
            /app/static — the CVE count drops with the toolchain (see the
            table above: 120+ CVEs → 30-40).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build single-stage and multi-stage variants,
            then `docker images easypj`. Verify: the multi-stage tag is
            several times smaller, and `docker run --rm easypj:multi
            which gcc` finds nothing.
         📊 SUMMARY: build where the tools live, ship only artifacts —
            smaller, faster to pull, less to exploit.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: pip install --user in the builder means the final
            stage must copy /root/.local AND keep PATH pointing at it.

    4.6  Dockerfile Best Practices
         - Minimize layers (chain RUN commands)
         - Order from least to most frequently changing
         - Use .dockerignore
         - Use specific base image tags (not :latest)
         - Run as non-root user
         - Scan for vulnerabilities
         - Use multi-stage builds
         - Remove unnecessary packages
         [BEST PRACTICE CHECKLIST — 15 items]
         [DO/DON'T TABLE — Common mistakes with corrections]


         [CHECKLIST: the 8 checks that catch 80% of bad Dockerfiles]
            [ ] Base image pinned (python:3.11-slim, never :latest)
            [ ] requirements.txt COPYed before the code
            [ ] apt-get update && install && rm lists in ONE RUN
            [ ] .dockerignore excludes .git, env/, __pycache__
            [ ] USER appuser set — container is not root
            [ ] CMD/ENTRYPOINT in exec (JSON) form
            [ ] One process per container (no PostgreSQL inside easypj!)
            [ ] docker scout quickview run before every release
         🎯 GOAL: Review any Dockerfile against the checklist above in two
            minutes and name WHY each violation hurts, not just that it does.
         [TECH] Each rule maps to a failure class: unpinned tags → silent
            drift (2.5); wrong COPY order → dead cache (2.4); split RUN
            update/install → stale apt index (2.4's gotcha); root user →
            container escape = host root (Part 7); shell-form CMD → lost
            SIGTERM (4.3); two processes → tangled logs and restarts.
         [ANALOGY] A pre-flight checklist: pilots don't debate each item
            per flight — every line exists because something once crashed.
         🔬 easypj: adding `RUN adduser --disabled-password appuser` +
            `USER appuser` costs two lines; skipping them means a Django
            RCE hands the attacker root in the container.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: score the 4.4 Dockerfile against all 8 boxes.
            Verify: it fails USER and scanning — exactly what 9.7 fixes.
         📊 SUMMARY: best practices are compressed postmortems — pin,
            order, ignore, drop root, exec form, one process.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "minimize layers" is NOT "one giant RUN" — collapse
            only related commands; readability and cache still matter.

    4.7  The Entrypoint Script Pattern — Migrations at Startup
         - The problem: easypj needs migrate + collectstatic BEFORE gunicorn
         - docker-entrypoint.sh: wait for PostgreSQL → migrate → exec "$@"
         - exec "$@" hands PID 1 to gunicorn — signals keep working
         - Waiting for PostgreSQL: container up ≠ accepting connections
         - ENTRYPOINT ["/app/docker-entrypoint.sh"] + CMD ["gunicorn", ...]

         [CODE: docker-entrypoint.sh — the pattern in full]
            #!/bin/sh
            set -e
            until pg_isready -h "$DB_HOST" -p 5432 -q; do
              echo "waiting for PostgreSQL..."; sleep 1
            done
            python manage.py migrate --noinput
            python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
            exec "$@"        # replace shell with CMD → gunicorn = PID 1
         🎯 GOAL: Make every easypj container self-preparing: it waits for
            PostgreSQL, migrates, then becomes gunicorn — no manual steps.
         [TECH] CMD alone runs ONE command; Django needs a sequence. The
            script runs it, and `exec "$@"` is the load-bearing line: exec
            REPLACES the shell with CMD's process, so gunicorn inherits
            PID 1 and docker stop's SIGTERM reaches it directly. Without
            exec, the shell stays PID 1 and swallows every signal (4.3).
         [ANALOGY] A theatre stage manager: checks the orchestra is seated
            (pg_isready), sets the props (migrate), then steps OFF the
            stage and hands the spotlight to the actor (exec "$@") — the
            manager never stands in front of the performance.
         🔬 easypj: compose starts postgres and easypj together; without
            the wait loop, migrate races the DB and dies with "connection
            refused" — the loop turns a crash-loop into a 2-second wait.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: add the script, chmod +x it, wire ENTRYPOINT +
            CMD, then start easypj BEFORE the database. Verify: logs show
            "waiting for PostgreSQL..." lines, then migrations, then
            gunicorn — and `docker exec easypj-web ps -o pid,comm` shows
            gunicorn as PID 1, not sh.
         📊 SUMMARY: ENTRYPOINT script = ordered startup (wait → migrate →
            serve); exec "$@" keeps signals and PID 1 correct.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: forgetting exec is invisible until docker stop
            takes 10 s and kills gunicorn mid-request — test PID 1, not
            vibes.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 4 — minimum 10, covering: Dockerfile as recipe
     (declarative vs imperative), build-time vs run-time instructions,
     COPY vs ADD, EXPOSE vs -p, the six ENTRYPOINT/CMD combinations, shell
     vs exec form + SIGTERM, easypj Dockerfile line order & cache effects,
     slim vs alpine for psycopg2, multi-stage size/CVE wins, best-practice
     checklist violations, entrypoint script + exec "$@" + PID 1]

Chapter 5: Building Images — docker build Deep Dive
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapter 4 wrote the recipe; this chapter
    masters the oven. docker build looks like one command but hides the
    build context, the cache engine, ARG/ENV plumbing and multi-platform
    output — the difference between 3-second and 5-minute builds lives
    here. Problem solved here: "my build is slow, my context is huge, and
    my ARM Mac image dies on the AMD64 server."

    5.1  docker build Fundamentals
         - docker build -t easypj:v1 .
         - The build context (the "." at the end)
         - How Docker sends build context to daemon
         - Build output and progress indicators
         - BuildKit: next-generation builder (DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1)
         [TERMINAL: Complete annotated build output for easypj]

         [TERMINAL: one build, annotated — drawn]
            $ docker build -t easypj:v1 .
            [+] Building 42.3s (10/10) FINISHED
             => [internal] load build definition from Dockerfile    0.0s
             => [internal] load .dockerignore                       0.0s
             => [internal] load build context                       0.4s
             => => transferring context: 1.2MB                      0.4s
             => CACHED [2/5] WORKDIR /app                           0.0s
             => [4/5] RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requi...   38.1s
             => exporting to image                                  1.1s
             => => naming to docker.io/library/easypj:v1            0.0s
         🎯 GOAL: Read every line of the build output above and name which
            part produced it — client, BuildKit step, or cache.
         [TECH] `docker build -t easypj:v1 .` — the trailing dot is the
            build CONTEXT: that directory is packaged and sent to the
            daemon, and only files inside it are COPYable. BuildKit (the
            default builder) solves steps as a dependency graph: it skips
            unused stages, runs independent steps in parallel, and prints
            the => lines with per-step timing.
         [ANALOGY] The context is the shopping bag you hand the baker: the
            recipe may use only the flour, but you still carried the whole
            bag to the bakery — which is why 5.2 teaches you to pack light.
         🔬 easypj: -t easypj:v1 names the result; forget -t and you get a
            dangling <none>:<none> image findable only by IMAGE ID (7.1).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the build twice; on the second run count
            the CACHED lines. Verify: total time collapses from ~42 s to
            ~1 s — nothing but cache lookups happened.
         📊 SUMMARY: build = context upload + graph solve + layer export;
            the dot is a directory, not punctuation.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker build .` from your home directory ships
            your ENTIRE home dir to the daemon — watch the "transferring
            context" line like a hawk.

    5.2  Build Context & .dockerignore
         - What is build context? (files sent to daemon for build)
         - Why .dockerignore matters (smaller context = faster builds)
         - What to exclude: __pycache__, .git, env/, db.sqlite3, *.pyc
         - Example .dockerignore for Django/easypj
         [COMPLETE .dockerignore FILE with explanations]

         [CODE: easypj/.dockerignore — drawn]
            .git/              # history: MBs of noise, real secrets risk
            __pycache__/       # host bytecode — rebuilt inside anyway
            *.pyc
            env/               # host virtualenv: WRONG platform binaries
            .env               # runtime secrets — inject at run, not build
            media/             # user uploads live in volumes (Part 6)
            Dockerfile         # the recipe isn't an ingredient
            .dockerignore
         🎯 GOAL: Cut easypj's build context from tens of MB to ~1 MB and
            say what each excluded entry would have broken.
         [TECH] The context is tarred and streamed to the daemon BEFORE
            any instruction runs; .dockerignore filters that tar. Less
            context = faster upload AND fewer files whose checksums can
            invalidate COPY layers. Syntax is .gitignore-like (globs,
            ! negation, directory/ suffixes).
         [ANALOGY] An airport packing list in reverse — the no-fly list.
            The virtualenv is a hair dryer with the wrong plug: it flies,
            weighs a ton, and cannot work at the destination.
         🔬 easypj: a stray env/ directory adds ~200 MB to every build
            upload; a committed .env inside the image is a leaked
            SECRET_KEY the moment anyone pulls the image.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build with and without .dockerignore and watch
            the "transferring context" line. Verify: size drops by orders
            of magnitude; `docker run --rm easypj:v1 ls -a /app` shows no
            .env, no env/, no .git.
         📊 SUMMARY: .dockerignore trims what travels to the daemon —
            speed, cache stability and secret hygiene in one file.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: COPY . . happily copies your .env into a shareable
            image layer — .dockerignore is a SECURITY control, not a
            nicety.

    5.3  Layer Caching Deep Dive
         - How Docker decides to use cache vs rebuild
         - Cache invalidation: any change = that layer + all after rebuilt
         - The "COPY requirements first" trick
         - --no-cache flag: force full rebuild
         - --cache-from: use external cache (CI/CD)
         [DIAGRAM: Cache hit vs miss visualization for easypj build]

         [DIAGRAM:flow — how BuildKit judges one step]
            parent layer unchanged? ──no──▶ REBUILD this + all later steps
                     │yes
            instruction text same?  ──no──▶ REBUILD this + all later steps
                     │yes
            COPY/ADD checksums same? ──no──▶ REBUILD this + all later steps
                     │yes
                     ▼
                  CACHED ✅
         🎯 GOAL: Given any easypj change, state which layers rebuild —
            then prove your prediction with the build log.
         [TECH] The rules in the flow above are absolute: cache keys are
            parent chain + instruction string + input checksums. RUN lines
            are NOT re-executed to check — same text = same layer, which
            is why a lone `RUN apt-get update` serves stale indexes
            forever. --no-cache ignores all keys for one build;
            --cache-from imports a pushed image's layers as cache donors
            on cold CI runners (Part 8).
         [ANALOGY] A meticulous accountant: identical invoice number and
            amounts → reuse last year's filing without reopening the box.
            Change one digit and every later filing must be redone.
         🔬 easypj: an ENV APP_VERSION line near the top of the Dockerfile
            rebuilds pip install on every bump — volatile metadata goes
            LAST, below COPY . .
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: rebuild after (a) touching README.md excluded
            by .dockerignore, (b) editing requirements.txt. Verify: (a) is
            all CACHED; (b) rebuilds from the COPY requirements.txt step.
         📊 SUMMARY: cache = parent + text + content checksums; the first
            miss kills everything after it; --no-cache is the reset button.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: cache never expires by TIME — a year-old cached
            apt-get update still "succeeds" with a year-old package list.

    5.4  Build Arguments (ARG) & Environment Variables (ENV)
         - ARG: Build-time only, not in final image (unless persisted)
         - ENV: Available at build AND runtime
         - --build-arg flag: pass values during build
         - Use case: Django SECRET_KEY at build vs runtime
         [CODE EXAMPLES: ARG vs ENV with comments]

         [TABLE: ARG vs ENV — drawn]
            | Lives at build?     | ARG ✅ (until stage ends) | ENV ✅    |
            | Lives in container? | ARG ❌                    | ENV ✅    |
            | Set from CLI?       | --build-arg VER=1.4       | -e at run |
            | Visible later?      | ⚠️ in history if consumed | ⚠️ inspect|
         🎯 GOAL: Choose ARG vs ENV correctly for version stamps, Python
            flags, and secrets — three different answers.
         [TECH] ARG exists only while the build runs (and only in the
            stage that declares it); ENV is baked into image config and
            inherited by every container. ARG values consumed by a RUN
            line land in that layer's history; ENV values sit in plain
            sight in docker inspect. Neither is a secret store — 5.7
            exists for that.
         [ANALOGY] ARG is a note taped to the oven during baking — gone
            when the cake leaves the kitchen. ENV is icing text: written
            once, read by everyone who ever gets a slice.
         🔬 easypj: ARG APP_VERSION=dev + LABEL version=$APP_VERSION
            stamps CI builds; ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 must be ENV —
            gunicorn needs it at RUNTIME. Django's SECRET_KEY must be
            NEITHER: pass it at docker run with --env-file (3.5).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build with --build-arg APP_VERSION=9.9, then
            `docker run --rm easypj:v1 printenv APP_VERSION`.
            Verify: empty — the ARG evaporated; the LABEL kept the value
            (`docker inspect --format '{{.Config.Labels.version}}'`).
         📊 SUMMARY: ARG = build-time dial, ENV = runtime default; secrets
            belong to neither.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: ENV SECRET_KEY=... at build time ships the secret
            to every registry the image ever touches — forever.

    5.5  Multi-Platform Builds with Buildx
         - Why multi-platform? (Intel Mac + Apple Silicon + Linux server)
         - docker buildx create, docker buildx build --platform
         - Building for linux/amd64, linux/arm64 simultaneously
         - QEMU emulation
         [TERMINAL: Full buildx workflow]

         [TERMINAL: multi-platform build, annotated — drawn]
            $ docker buildx create --name xbuilder --use
            $ docker buildx build \
                --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 \
                -t ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1 --push .
            [+] Building 96.2s ... => [linux/arm64 4/5] RUN pip install
            $ docker manifest inspect ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1  # both archs
         🎯 GOAL: Ship ONE easypj tag that runs natively on an ARM MacBook
            and an AMD64 server — no "exec format error" ever again.
         [TECH] Buildx drives BuildKit once per platform; the foreign
            architecture runs through QEMU user-mode emulation (slow but
            automatic), then a manifest list (2.6) is pushed tying both
            images to one tag. --push is required: the local image store
            cannot hold a multi-arch result directly.
         [ANALOGY] Printing a book in two languages at once: one
            manuscript (Dockerfile), two presses (amd64/arm64 builders),
            one ISBN (the tag) that hands each reader their edition — the
            same manifest-list machinery you inspected in 2.6.
         🔬 easypj: an M-series Mac builds arm64 natively in ~40 s and
            emulates the amd64 pip install in ~4 min — CI on amd64 flips
            which half is slow.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the session above, then manifest-inspect
            the tag. Verify: two entries, linux/amd64 and linux/arm64 —
            and pulls on both machines just work.
         📊 SUMMARY: buildx + --platform + --push = one tag, every CPU;
            QEMU pays the emulation tax at build time, not run time.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: without --push (or --load, single-arch only) the
            result vanishes — buildx builds into thin air by default.

    5.6  docker buildx bake — Advanced Builds
         - Building multiple images in parallel
         - HCL/JSON build definition files
         - Matrix builds for multiple platforms
         [CODE: bake.hcl for easypj multi-variant builds]


         [CODE: docker-bake.hcl — easypj dev + prod in one file]
            group "default" { targets = ["dev", "prod"] }
            target "dev" {
              dockerfile = "Dockerfile"
              tags       = ["easypj:dev"]
              args       = { APP_VERSION = "dev" }
            }
            target "prod" {
              inherits   = ["dev"]
              tags       = ["ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1"]
              platforms  = ["linux/amd64", "linux/arm64"]
            }
         🎯 GOAL: Replace a wall of docker build commands with one
            `docker buildx bake` that builds every easypj variant in
            parallel.
         [TECH] bake reads targets from HCL/JSON; each target is a full
            build definition (dockerfile, tags, args, platforms), groups
            batch them, inherits deduplicates config, and BuildKit shares
            cached layers ACROSS targets — dev and prod pip layers build
            once.
         [ANALOGY] A bakery order sheet instead of shouting one cake at a
            time: the kitchen sees all orders, preps shared dough once,
            and bakes everything in parallel ovens.
         🔬 easypj: `docker buildx bake` = the dev image for your laptop
            AND the multi-arch prod image — one command, shared cache.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: write the file above, run `docker buildx bake
            dev`. Verify: easypj:dev exists; then bake with no args and
            watch both targets build concurrently in one output tree.
         📊 SUMMARY: bake = declarative builds; targets, groups and
            inheritance turn build commands into reviewable config.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: bake is BUILD orchestration only — it never starts
            containers; compose (Part 4) owns runtime.

    5.7  BuildKit Cache Mounts & Build Secrets
         - --mount=type=cache: persistent pip cache ACROSS builds
         - Layer cache vs cache mount: reuse layers vs reuse downloads
         - --mount=type=secret: use a credential during RUN, ship nothing
         - Why ARG/ENV secrets always leak: docker history shows all
         - Proof workflow: docker history --no-trunc after both builds

         [CODE: Dockerfile fragments — cache mount + secret]
            RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/pip \
                pip install -r requirements.txt
            # build with: docker build --secret id=pipconf,src=pip.conf .
            RUN --mount=type=secret,id=pipconf,target=/etc/pip.conf \
                pip install -r requirements.txt  # private index, no trace
         🎯 GOAL: Make dependency reinstalls fast even when the layer
            cache misses, and use private credentials in a build that
            leaves ZERO trace in the image.
         [TECH] A cache mount is a persistent directory BuildKit
            re-attaches to that RUN step on every build — when
            requirements.txt changes, the layer rebuilds but pip finds
            its wheel cache warm (note: no --no-cache-dir here; the cache
            IS the point). A secret mount appears as a file only DURING
            that one RUN and is never written to any layer; ARG/ENV
            values, by contrast, are recoverable by anyone with the image.
         [ANALOGY] Cache mount = the baker's pantry: every cake is baked
            fresh, but flour is not re-bought per cake. Secret mount = a
            door code whispered to the builder — used, never written on
            the blueprints (ARG writes it on the blueprint in pen).
         🔬 easypj: requirements.txt edits drop from ~45 s to ~8 s because
            Django/psycopg2 wheels come from the mounted cache, not PyPI.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build once with ARG TOKEN=hunter2 consumed by
            a RUN line, once with --mount=type=secret. Then run
            `docker history --no-trunc easypj:v1 | grep hunter2`.
            Verify: the ARG build leaks the token in history; the secret
            build shows nothing anywhere — history, inspect, or layers.
         📊 SUMMARY: cache mounts keep downloads warm across layer
            misses; secret mounts use credentials without storing them.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: docker history --no-trunc is the auditor's first
            command — if a secret ever met ARG or ENV, it is in there.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 5 — minimum 10, covering: build context & the
     trailing dot, reading BuildKit output, .dockerignore entries & why
     (secrets, virtualenvs), cache-decision rules + stale apt-get update,
     --no-cache vs --cache-from, ARG vs ENV lifetimes & leak surfaces,
     buildx multi-platform + QEMU + manifest lists, --push vs --load,
     bake targets/groups/inheritance, cache mounts vs layer cache,
     secret mounts vs ARG proven via docker history]

Chapter 6: Running Containers — docker run & Container Management
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: An image on disk earns nothing — value starts
    when containers RUN and someone keeps them alive. This chapter is the
    operator's manual: starting, stopping, inspecting, entering, feeding
    and limiting easypj containers. Problem solved here: "the container
    started yesterday… how do I see inside it, and why did it die at 3 AM?"

    6.1  docker run — The Complete Reference
         - Modes: Foreground (-it), Detached (-d), Ephemeral (--rm)
         - Port mapping: -p host:container, -P (all exposed ports)
         - Environment: -e VAR=value, --env-file
         - Container naming: --name
         - Restart policies: no, on-failure, always, unless-stopped
         [TABLE: Every docker run option with example using easypj]

         [TABLE: restart policies — policy | restarts when | easypj use]
            no             | never                  | one-off scripts
            on-failure[:N] | exit code != 0, N max  | migrations, cron jobs
            always         | any stop, even manual  | rarely what you want
            unless-stopped | crashes and reboots,   | easypj-web in
                           | but respects your stop | single-host prod
         🎯 GOAL: Extend the 12-flag core from 3.5 with the run-mode and
            restart decisions that keep easypj alive across reboots.
         [TECH] Three modes: -it (foreground, terminal attached), -d
            (detached, daemon babysits), --rm (delete on exit — mutually
            exclusive with restart policies). -p 8000:8000 binds exactly;
            -P auto-publishes every EXPOSEd port onto RANDOM host ports.
            Restart policies are enforced by dockerd itself, so they
            survive host reboots (when the daemon service is enabled).
         [ANALOGY] Restart policies are pet-care instructions taped to the
            container: "if it falls over, stand it back up (always)…
            unless I laid it down myself (unless-stopped)."
         ⚡ docker run -d --name easypj-web -p 8000:8000 --env-file .env
            --restart unless-stopped easypj:v1
         🔬 easypj: the ⚡ line is the canonical single-host production
            start — every flag earned its place in a previous section.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: start easypj with --restart unless-stopped,
            then `docker kill` it. Verify: docker ps shows it back within
            seconds; now docker stop it — it STAYS down. That asymmetry
            is the whole policy.
         📊 SUMMARY: -d for services, --rm for chores, unless-stopped for
            anything a reboot must bring back.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: --restart always resurrects containers you stopped
            manually once the daemon restarts — use unless-stopped and
            keep control.

    6.2  Container Lifecycle Management
         - docker create (create but don't start)
         - docker start, docker stop, docker restart
         - docker pause / docker unpause
         - docker kill (SIGKILL vs SIGTERM)
         - docker rm, docker rm -f
         [LIFECYCLE STATE DIAGRAM: Created → Running → Paused → Stopped → Deleted]

         🎯 GOAL: Drive every arrow of the 2.2 state machine with the
            right command — and know which data each transition destroys.
         [TECH] create = allocate container + writable layer, don't start;
            start = launch PID 1; stop = SIGTERM, grace period (-t,
            default 10 s), then SIGKILL; kill = SIGKILL now; pause =
            cgroup freezer (no signal at all); rm = delete container AND
            its writable layer; rm -f = kill + rm in one stroke.
            restart = stop + start with the SAME writable layer — files
            survive; rm is the only eraser.
         [ANALOGY] A stage play: create = actors in the wings, start =
            curtain up, pause = freeze mid-scene, stop = bow and exit,
            kill = fire alarm, rm = strike the set — props (writable
            layer) gone for good. Same stage as 2.2's diagram.
         🔬 easypj: docker stop easypj-web gives gunicorn 10 s to finish
            in-flight JobPost requests; a deploy script using rm -f drops
            user requests on the floor at every single release.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: docker exec easypj-web touch /tmp/marker, then
            restart the container and look for the file; then rm it,
            rerun, and look again. Verify: marker survives restart, dies
            with rm — the writable layer's exact lifespan.
         📊 SUMMARY: stop/start recycle the same container; rm destroys
            its writable layer; only volumes (Part 6) outlive rm.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: docker stop timing out every time means PID 1
            ignores SIGTERM — the shell-form CMD trap from 4.3 again.

    6.3  Viewing & Inspecting Containers
         - docker ps (list running), docker ps -a (all)
         - docker logs (view stdout/stderr)
         - docker logs -f (follow, like tail -f)
         - docker logs --tail 100 (last 100 lines)
         - docker inspect (JSON metadata dump)
         - docker stats (live resource usage — CPU, RAM, Network)
         - docker top (processes inside container)
         [OUTPUT EXAMPLES: Each command with annotated output]

         [TERMINAL: the observation toolkit on easypj-web — drawn]
            $ docker ps --format "table {{.Names}}\t{{.Status}}\t{{.Ports}}"
            NAMES        STATUS                  PORTS
            easypj-web   Up 2 hours (healthy)    0.0.0.0:8000->8000/tcp
            $ docker logs --tail 5 -f easypj-web    # last 5, then follow
            [2026-07-02 09:14:02] "GET /jobs/ HTTP/1.1" 200
            $ docker stats --no-stream easypj-web   # CPU / MEM snapshot
            $ docker top easypj-web                 # processes, host view
            $ docker inspect -f '{{.State.ExitCode}}' easypj-web
         🎯 GOAL: Answer "what is this container doing RIGHT NOW?" in
            under thirty seconds using four read-only commands.
         [TECH] logs reads the json-file driver's capture of PID 1's
            stdout/stderr (rotation rules live in 2.7) — gunicorn must log
            to stdout, never to files inside the container. inspect dumps
            the full state/config JSON; -f with a Go template extracts one
            field script-ably. stats streams live cgroup counters; top
            maps container processes to host PIDs.
         [ANALOGY] A hospital ward round: ps is the bed chart, logs the
            patient's own account, stats the live vitals monitor, inspect
            the complete medical file — read them in that order.
         🔬 easypj: after a crash, `docker logs --tail 50 easypj-web`
            shows the Django traceback; the ExitCode template says HOW it
            died — 137 = OOM-killed or docker kill (2.2, 6.6).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run all five commands above on a running
            easypj-web. Verify: you can state uptime, last request, RAM
            use, worker count and exit-code-if-any without guessing once.
         📊 SUMMARY: ps → logs → stats/top → inspect: cheap looks first,
            full JSON last.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: docker logs shows ONLY stdout/stderr of PID 1 — an
            app writing /var/log/app.log inside the container is
            invisible to it.

    6.4  Executing Commands Inside Containers
         - docker exec -it <container> bash (get a shell)
         - docker exec <container> <command> (run one command)
         - Debugging Django: docker exec easypj python manage.py check
         - Running migrations: docker exec easypj python manage.py migrate
         - Running management commands inside container
         [PRACTICAL SCENARIO TABLE: 10 common docker exec use cases]

         [TABLE: exec scenarios — need | command (docker exec easypj-web …)]
            open a shell        | -it … bash
            check Django config | … python manage.py check
            run migrations      | … python manage.py migrate
            create admin user   | -it … python manage.py createsuperuser
            Django shell (ORM)  | -it … python manage.py shell
            inspect env vars    | … printenv DB_HOST
            list processes      | … ps aux
         🎯 GOAL: Operate a LIVE easypj container — shell, migrations,
            ORM queries — without ever restarting it.
         [TECH] exec starts an ADDITIONAL process inside the container's
            existing namespaces; PID 1 (gunicorn) is untouched, and when
            exec's process exits nothing else stops. -it applies exactly
            as in 3.4. Exit codes pass through — `docker exec … manage.py
            check` in CI fails the pipeline honestly.
         [ANALOGY] exec is stepping through the kitchen door to taste the
            sauce while the chef keeps cooking; docker run would build a
            second kitchen. run = new container; exec = visit an existing
            one.
         🔬 easypj: docker exec -it easypj-web python manage.py shell →
            `JobPost.objects.filter(location__name="Remote").count()` —
            live ORM queries against the real PostgreSQL, zero downtime.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: exec a bash shell, run `ps aux`, exit, then
            `docker ps`. Verify: gunicorn was PID 1 while your bash was a
            high-numbered PID, and exiting bash left the container Up.
         📊 SUMMARY: exec = extra process in an existing container; the
            daily driver for shells, migrations and management commands.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: exec needs a RUNNING container — for a crashed one
            read docker logs; you cannot exec into a corpse.

    6.5  Copying Files To/From Containers
         - docker cp host_path container:path
         - docker cp container:path host_path
         - Use case: extract db.sqlite3 backup from container
         [EXAMPLES: 5 copy scenarios for easypj]

         [TERMINAL: cp in both directions — drawn]
            $ docker exec easypj-db pg_dump -U easypj -f /tmp/easypj.sql easypj
            $ docker cp easypj-db:/tmp/easypj.sql ./backups/  # out
            $ docker cp ./fixtures/jobs.json easypj-web:/app/ # in
            $ docker cp easypj-web:/app/staticfiles ./static_audit
         🎯 GOAL: Move files across the container boundary in both
            directions — and know when cp is the wrong tool.
         [TECH] docker cp streams a tar through the API — no network, no
            SSH, and it works even on STOPPED containers. Paths are
            literal (container:/absolute/path); ownership lands as root
            on the host side. It copies bytes, not state: for anything
            recurring, volumes (Part 6) replace cp entirely.
         [ANALOGY] A prison visitation slot: you can pass a document in
            or out through the tray, but you would not run the mailroom
            through it — regular traffic deserves a proper door (volume).
         🔬 easypj: the session above takes a real PostgreSQL dump with
            pg_dump, lands it in ./backups/, and pushes a fixture in for
            `docker exec easypj-web python manage.py loaddata jobs.json`.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the dump + cp lines above.
            Verify: `head -3 backups/easypj.sql` shows SQL DDL on the
            HOST — your data now survives anything Docker does.
         📊 SUMMARY: cp = ad-hoc tar over the API, both directions, even
            stopped containers; recurring data belongs on volumes.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: cp INTO a running container changes files behind
            the app's back — copied code is not deployed code; rebuild
            the image instead (that discipline is 9.4's whole point).

    6.6  Container Resource Limits
         - --memory (RAM limit, e.g., --memory=512m)
         - --cpus (CPU limit, e.g., --cpus=1.5)
         - Why limit resources? (noisy neighbor problem)
         - OOM (Out of Memory) behavior
         [TABLE: Resource limit flags with descriptions]

         [TABLE: resource flags — flag | caps | on breach]
            --memory 512m      | RAM incl. page cache | OOM-kill (137)
            --memory-swap 512m | RAM+swap combined    | no swap escape
            --cpus 1.5         | CPU time (CFS quota) | throttled, alive
            --pids-limit 200   | process count        | fork fails
         🎯 GOAL: Cap easypj so one runaway worker cannot starve
            PostgreSQL on the same host — and read the evidence when a
            cap fires.
         [TECH] Limits are cgroup settings (1.3): a --memory breach
            invokes the kernel OOM killer — the container dies with exit
            code 137 and OOMKilled:true in docker inspect. A --cpus
            breach merely THROTTLES: the app gets slow, never killed.
            Unlimited is the default — the noisy-neighbor problem is
            opt-out, not opt-in.
         [ANALOGY] A hotel: the room's electricity has a breaker (memory
            — trips, lights out) while the shared gym has a booking cap
            (CPU — you wait your turn, nobody is thrown out).
         🔬 easypj: gunicorn with 3 workers × ~90 MB fits --memory 512m
            with headroom; a memory-leaking view now kills ONE container
            (which --restart resurrects) instead of the whole host.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: docker run -d --name probe --memory 64m
            easypj:v1 — deliberately too small. Verify: it exits fast and
            `docker inspect -f '{{.State.OOMKilled}} {{.State.ExitCode}}'
            probe` prints "true 137". Clean up with docker rm probe.
         📊 SUMMARY: memory kills, CPU throttles, defaults are unlimited;
            every prod container ships with both flags set.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: exit 137 has TWO faces — OOM kill or docker kill;
            only inspect's OOMKilled field tells you which one happened.

    6.7  Health Checks & Logs in Daily Operations
         - HEALTHCHECK: the container's own liveness probe, in the image
         - docker ps STATUS: Up (healthy) | (unhealthy) | (health: starting)
         - "Up" alone only means PID 1 is alive — up can still be broken
         - Log discipline: stdout/stderr only; rotation configured in 2.7
         - docker events: the daemon's audit feed (die, oom, health_status)

         [CODE: HEALTHCHECK for easypj — Dockerfile lines]
            HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --retries=3 \
                        --start-period=15s \
              CMD curl -fsS http://localhost:8000/healthz/ || exit 1
         🎯 GOAL: Make docker ps tell the truth: not "the process exists"
            but "easypj is actually answering requests".
         [TECH] The daemon runs the HEALTHCHECK command INSIDE the
            container on the interval; 3 straight failures flip STATUS to
            (unhealthy) and emit a health_status event — Docker alone
            does NOT restart unhealthy containers (Compose/Swarm act on
            it in Parts 4/9; probes go deeper in [KUBERNETES GUIDE]).
            start-period shields slow Django startup (4.7's migrations!)
            from false alarms.
         [ANALOGY] A pulse vs a conversation: docker ps without
            HEALTHCHECK checks the pulse (PID 1 exists); the health
            command asks the patient a question every 30 seconds and
            writes the answer on the chart — the (healthy) tag in ps.
         🔬 easypj: a /healthz/ Django view that runs SELECT 1 against
            PostgreSQL means "healthy" covers app AND database
            connectivity — deadlocked gunicorn workers surface in
            minutes, not from user complaints.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: rebuild with the HEALTHCHECK above, run, and
            watch `docker ps` move from (health: starting) to (healthy).
            Then break the view on purpose and run
            `docker events --filter event=health_status`.
            Verify: the unhealthy transition appears in BOTH ps and
            events — your first real-time daemon feed.
         📊 SUMMARY: HEALTHCHECK upgrades "Up" from a pulse to an answer;
            logs go to stdout with rotation per 2.7; docker events is
            where the daemon confesses everything it does.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: curl must EXIST in the image (slim has none by
            default) — a missing binary fails every probe and marks a
            perfectly good easypj (unhealthy).

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 6 — minimum 10, covering: -it vs -d vs --rm,
     -p vs -P, restart policies (kill vs stop asymmetry), lifecycle
     commands + what each destroys, exit code 137's two meanings,
     logs/stats/top/inspect triage order, exec vs run, live ORM shell,
     docker cp directions + pg_dump extraction, memory vs CPU breach
     behavior, HEALTHCHECK states + docker events]


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 3: DOCKER IMAGES — CREATING, MANAGING & OPTIMIZING                  🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 3:
      What runs where?    → images at rest — local store, registry, tarballs.
      What talks to what? → tag → push → registry → pull; history reads layers.
      What breaks first?  → disk (dangling ghosts) and trust (CVE-fat images).

Chapter 7: Image Management — docker image Commands
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Builds multiply: within a week you own fifty
    images, twelve <none> ghosts and a full disk. This chapter is image
    custody — listing, tagging, moving (push/pull/save/load), pruning and
    auditing what you hold. Problem solved here: "which of these easypj
    images is production, why is /var/lib/docker 40 GB, and how do I ship
    an image to a server with no internet?"

    7.1  Listing & Filtering Images
         - docker images (list all)
         - docker image ls (same, newer syntax)
         - Filtering: docker images --filter "dangling=true"
         - Filtering: docker images --filter "reference=python*"
         - Formatting: docker images --format "table {{.Repository}}\t{{.Tag}}\t{{.Size}}"
         [OUTPUT EXAMPLES: Annotated image listings]

         [TERMINAL: listing with intent — drawn]
            $ docker images --filter "reference=easypj*"
            REPOSITORY   TAG     IMAGE ID       CREATED       SIZE
            easypj       v1.1    3f9a12bc77de   2 hours ago   181MB
            easypj       v1.0    9c31e0a4f552   3 days ago    184MB
            $ docker images --filter "dangling=true" -q    # just the IDs
            77af4d6b9913
            $ docker images --format "{{.Repository}}:{{.Tag}} {{.Size}}"
         🎯 GOAL: Find any image by name, hunt every <none> ghost, and
            feed image lists into scripts — no eyeballing walls of text.
         [TECH] dangling=true matches layers whose tag was STOLEN by a
            newer build of the same name — untagged but still on disk.
            --format uses Go templates over fields (.Repository, .Tag,
            .ID, .Size); -q prints IDs only, which is what makes
            `docker rmi $(docker images -f dangling=true -q)` possible.
         [ANALOGY] A warehouse manifest: filters are clipboard queries
            ("aisle easypj*", "unlabeled boxes"), --format picks the
            printout's columns, -q prints barcode stickers only.
         🔬 easypj: every rebuild of easypj:v1 demotes the previous build
            to <none>:<none> — after a day of iterating you hold a dozen
            dangling ghosts. 7.5 deletes them; here you LIST them first.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: rebuild easypj:v1 after touching a file, then
            run the dangling filter above. Verify: yesterday's IMAGE ID
            now shows as <none>:<none> — the tag moved, the bytes stayed.
         📊 SUMMARY: images lists the local store; filters + Go-template
            formats turn it from wallpaper into a queryable database.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: SIZE double-counts shared layers — ten easypj tags
            do not cost 10 × 180 MB; docker system df tells disk truth.

    7.2  Tagging Images Properly
         - docker tag source target
         - docker tag easypj:v1 easypj:latest
         - docker tag easypj:v1 myregistry.com/easypj:v1
         - Multiple tags for same image (same IMAGE ID)
         - Untagging: docker rmi easypj:v1 (removes tag, not image if others exist)
         [DIAGRAM: One IMAGE ID → multiple tags]

         [DIAGRAM:architecture — one IMAGE ID, many name tags]
                              ┌─ easypj:v1.0
            3f9a12bc77de ─────┼─ easypj:latest
            (the bytes)       └─ ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.0
            one image on disk — three sticky notes, zero extra megabytes
         🎯 GOAL: Re-name images freely — versions, latest, registry
            prefixes — knowing tags are free and the ID is the identity.
         [TECH] docker tag SRC DST creates another name → same IMAGE ID;
            nothing is copied. The name's PREFIX is routing: tagging as
            ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.0 is what makes push go to ghcr (2.3).
            docker rmi easypj:v1.0 removes ONE name; the bytes die only
            when the LAST name (and last container) lets go.
         [ANALOGY] The diagram above: one person (ID), three name badges —
            work alias, nickname, passport name for border crossings
            (registry prefix). Shredding a badge never harms the person.
         🔬 easypj: release ritual — docker tag easypj:v1.1 easypj:latest
            && docker tag easypj:v1.1 ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.1 — one
            build, three names, each with a different job.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: apply both tags above, run docker images,
            then rmi the latest tag. Verify: all three rows shared one
            IMAGE ID; after rmi, v1.1 still exists — only the badge died.
         📊 SUMMARY: tag = alias, ID = identity; the registry prefix in
            the name IS the push destination.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker rmi 3f9a12bc77de` by ID with several tags
            present refuses (or with -f, kills EVERY name) — untag by
            NAME; delete by ID only when one name remains.

    7.3  Pushing & Pulling Images
         - docker push registry/repo:tag
         - docker pull registry/repo:tag
         - Authentication: docker login
         - Docker Hub namespace: username/repo:tag
         - Private registry push workflow
         [TERMINAL: Complete push/pull workflow for easypj]

         [TERMINAL: the full round trip — drawn]
            $ docker login ghcr.io -u you           # token → config.json
            $ docker tag easypj:v1.1 ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.1
            $ docker push ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.1
            9d3a: Pushed   f2c1: Layer already exists  # dedup, see 2.3
            v1.1: digest: sha256:4be2... size: 1786
            $ ssh server 'docker pull ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.1'
         🎯 GOAL: Move easypj laptop → registry → server and pin what the
            server got to a digest, not a hope.
         [TECH] push uploads missing layers + the manifest, then prints
            the manifest DIGEST — record it: deploying by digest
            (ghcr.io/you/easypj@sha256:4be2...) is immune to tag moves
            (2.5). pull on the server reverses the trip; Docker Hub names
            omit the registry (you/easypj:v1.1 = docker.io implied).
         [ANALOGY] Registered mail: layers are parcels the post office
            already partly holds (dedup), the manifest is the delivery
            slip, and the digest is the tracking number — quote THAT in
            disputes, not "the brown box" (:latest).
         🔬 easypj: CI pushes ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.1, records the
            digest, and the deploy step pulls BY DIGEST — what was tested
            is byte-for-byte what runs.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: push v1.1, note the printed digest, then
            `docker pull ghcr.io/you/easypj@sha256:<it>`. Verify: the
            pull succeeds with no tag at all — content-addressing live.
         📊 SUMMARY: login → tag with registry prefix → push → pull;
            digests, not tags, are the deployable truth.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "denied: requested access to the resource" is
            almost always a missing/wrong NAMESPACE in the name, not a
            password problem — check the prefix before the credentials.

    7.4  Saving & Loading Images (Offline Transfer)
         - docker save -o easypj.tar easypj:v1 (export as tar)
         - docker load -i easypj.tar (import from tar)
         - docker export (container filesystem, NOT image)
         - docker import (create image from filesystem)
         - save vs export: critical difference
         [TABLE: save/load vs export/import comparison]

         [TABLE: save/load vs export/import — drawn]
            | pair           | moves          | layers+tags kept? | CMD? |
            | save → load    | IMAGE          | ✅ history intact | ✅   |
            | export→ import | container's FS | ❌ flattened      | ❌   |
         🎯 GOAL: Carry easypj onto an air-gapped server on a USB stick —
            and never grab export when you meant save.
         [TECH] save serializes the IMAGE: all layers, tags, config —
            load restores it identically, cache-compatible. export tars a
            CONTAINER's merged filesystem: one flat layer, no CMD/ENV/
            EXPOSE — importing it yields an image that does not even know
            how to start. gzip the save (~40% smaller) for transport.
         [ANALOGY] save ships the printing plates (reprint anytime,
            edition data intact); export photocopies one printed book —
            readable, but the plates, metadata and cover are gone.
         🔬 easypj: docker save easypj:v1.1 | gzip > easypj-v1.1.tar.gz →
            USB → `gunzip -c easypj-v1.1.tar.gz | docker load` on the
            offline server: tag, layers and CMD arrive untouched.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: save + load easypj:v1.1, then export a
            container and import it; docker inspect both results.
            Verify: the loaded image kept CMD ["gunicorn", ...]; the
            imported one has Cmd: null — run it and watch it exit
            instantly, which is the whole lesson.
         📊 SUMMARY: save/load = image, layered, metadata kept;
            export/import = flattened container FS, metadata lost.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker save easypj` without a tag saves EVERY
            easypj tag into one (huge) tarball — pipes hide the mistake.

    7.5  Removing Images & Cleanup
         - docker rmi <image> (remove image)
         - docker image prune (remove dangling)
         - docker image prune -a (remove all unused)
         - docker system df (disk usage)
         - docker system prune -a (nuclear: remove ALL unused)
         [BEFORE/AFTER: Disk space freed after cleanup]

         [TERMINAL: cleanup ladder, least → most destructive — drawn]
            $ docker system df               # who eats the disk?
            TYPE          TOTAL  ACTIVE  SIZE    RECLAIMABLE
            Images        23     4       9.8GB   7.1GB (72%)
            Build Cache   118    0       4.2GB   4.2GB
            $ docker image prune             # dangling <none> only: safe
            $ docker image prune -a          # ALL images no container uses
            $ docker builder prune           # build cache
            $ docker system prune -a --volumes   # ☢️ everything unused
         🎯 GOAL: Reclaim gigabytes on a build machine deliberately —
            climbing the ladder above one rung at a time, never jumping.
         [TECH] rmi refuses while ANY container (even Exited) references
            the image — rm the container first. prune deletes dangling
            images (the 7.1 ghosts); prune -a widens to every image with
            zero containers, meaning the next run re-PULLS them. df's
            RECLAIMABLE column is the honest number — shared layers
            counted once.
         [ANALOGY] Cleaning a garage: df is the inventory walk, prune
            tosses unlabeled boxes, prune -a tosses everything not bolted
            to a car, system prune -a --volumes sells the garage — each
            rung is cheaper to regret than the next.
         🔬 easypj: a week of iteration leaves ~15 dangling builds;
            `docker image prune` frees GBs and cannot touch easypj:v1.1
            while easypj-web runs — that reference protects it.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: df → image prune → df again. Verify: the
            Images RECLAIMABLE number fell by roughly the pruned total,
            and every named easypj tag survived.
         📊 SUMMARY: df to diagnose, prune to trim, -a to purge, system
            prune -a --volumes only when you can re-pull and rebuild ALL.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: on CI runners, prune -a also evicts your
            --cache-from sources — the next build goes cold; prune
            surgically there.

    7.6  Image History & Inspection
         - docker image history easypj:v1 (show layers)
         - docker image inspect easypj:v1 (JSON metadata)
         - Understanding image IDs and digests
         - Image signing and verification (Docker Content Trust)
         [OUTPUT EXAMPLES: history and inspect with annotations]


         [TERMINAL: reading an image's autopsy — drawn]
            $ docker history easypj:v1.1
            IMAGE     CREATED BY                                SIZE
            3f9a12bc  CMD ["gunicorn" "easypj.wsgi:applicat…    0B
            <missing> COPY . . # buildkit                      612kB
            <missing> RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r req…   142MB
            <missing> FROM python:3.11-slim                    130MB
            $ docker inspect -f '{{.Config.Env}}' easypj:v1.1
            $ docker inspect -f '{{index .RepoDigests 0}}' easypj:v1.1
         🎯 GOAL: Audit any image you did NOT build: where its size comes
            from, what env it injects, and which digest it really is.
         [TECH] history walks layers newest-first: CREATED BY shows each
            instruction, SIZE its cost — the 142 MB pip layer is where
            Chapter 8 aims. <missing> is cosmetic (parent layer IDs not
            stored locally). inspect exposes Config (Env, Cmd, User,
            ExposedPorts) and RepoDigests — the sha256 identity from 2.1.
            Docker Content Trust (DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1) adds signature
            verification on pull for publishers that sign.
         [ANALOGY] history is a geological core sample — every layer's
            thickness on display; inspect is the lab report on the same
            rock: composition, provenance, fingerprint.
         🔬 easypj: before basing on any community image, history it — a
            surprise `curl … | sh` layer or a bloated 400 MB RUN is
            visible in seconds, BEFORE it ever runs on your machine.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: history easypj:v1.1 and rank its three
            fattest layers. Verify: your ranking names pip install and
            the base image first — exactly the targets Chapter 8 attacks.
         📊 SUMMARY: history = per-layer cost and origin; inspect = full
            metadata + digest; audit before you trust.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: history's CREATED BY proves 5.7's warning — any
            ARG/ENV secret consumed by a RUN is readable here forever.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 7 — minimum 10, covering: filters (dangling,
     reference) + Go-template formats, why rebuilds create <none> images,
     tag vs IMAGE ID identity, retagging for registries, push/pull with
     digests, denied-access diagnosis, save/load vs export/import (CMD
     survival), the cleanup ladder + system df, prune -a risks on CI,
     history-based size audit, inspecting Env/User/RepoDigests]

Chapter 8: Optimizing Docker Images — Smaller, Faster, Safer
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: easypj works — as an 850 MB image with 120
    CVEs. Every megabyte is pulled by every deploy, cached on every node
    and scanned by every audit; every package is attack surface. This
    chapter turns a working image into a professional one: right base,
    tight layers, scanned, and — where it fits — distroless. Problem
    solved here: "it runs, but it is fat, slow to ship, and indefensible."

    8.1  Why Image Size Matters
         - Smaller = faster push/pull (critical in CI/CD)
         - Smaller = less attack surface (fewer packages to exploit)
         - Smaller = less disk usage on nodes
         - Target: keep images under 200MB for production
         [SIZE COMPARISON GRAPH: easypj before vs after optimization]

         🎯 GOAL: Put numbers on "smaller is better" — pull time, disk,
            CVE count — so optimization is a budget, not an aesthetic.
         [TECH] Size taxes every stage: registry storage, network on each
            pull (CI pulls per job!), node disk, scanner runtime. Attack
            surface scales with PACKAGE COUNT more than bytes — every
            binary is a potential CVE. The 200 MB target is the point
            where a Django+gunicorn+psycopg2 image stops carrying
            anything it never executes.
         [ANALOGY] Airline baggage: every kilo flies on EVERY flight, and
            every extra item is one more thing security must screen. You
            pay per deploy, forever, for weight you packed once.
         [TABLE: what 850 MB vs 180 MB actually costs — drawn]
            | cost            | 850 MB single-stage | 180 MB optimized |
            | pull @ 10 Mbps  | ~11 min             | ~2.4 min         |
            | 20 CI runs/day  | ~17 GB pulled       | ~3.6 GB          |
            | CVE findings    | 120+                | 30-40            |
         🔬 easypj: a 3-line hotfix to jobpost/views.py should deploy in
            seconds; with an 850 MB image the fix waits on an 11-minute
            pull at the worst possible moment.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: docker images easypj + docker history (7.6)
            to find the two heaviest layers. Verify: you can name the
            exact instruction behind each — those are 8.2-8.4's targets.
         📊 SUMMARY: size = a recurring tax on ship speed, disk and
            security; under 200 MB is the professional bar for easypj.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: optimizing the APP layer is pointless if the BASE
            is 700 MB — always attack the biggest layer first (8.2).

    8.2  Choosing the Right Base Image
         - Alpine (5MB): smallest but musl libc compatibility issues
         - Slim (40-80MB): Debian-based, good balance for Python
         - Full Debian/Ubuntu (100MB+): most compatible, largest
         - Distroless (20MB): Google's minimal, no shell, ultra-secure
         - Scratch (0MB): completely empty, for static binaries
         [TABLE: Base image comparison — size, compatibility, security, use case]

         [TABLE: base images for a Django app — drawn]
            | base         | size   | verdict for easypj                |
            | python:3.11  | ~1.0GB | dev toolbox — never ship          |
            | 3.11-slim    | ~150MB | ✅ THE choice: glibc + apt        |
            | 3.11-alpine  | ~50MB  | ⚠️ musl: psycopg2/wheel pain      |
            | distroless   | ~50MB  | 🔴 no shell — 8.6's endgame       |
            | scratch      | 0MB    | static binaries (Go) only — not   |
            |              |        | an option for CPython             |
         🎯 GOAL: Defend "python:3.11-slim" for easypj in one paragraph
            against both the alpine fans and the full-image comfort camp.
         [TECH] The axis is the C library: slim = Debian glibc, so PyPI's
            prebuilt manylinux wheels (psycopg2-binary, Pillow) install
            in seconds. Alpine = musl: many wheels don't apply, pip
            compiles from source — slower builds, subtle libc bugs, and
            the 100 MB you "saved" comes back as gcc in the build stage.
         [ANALOGY] Choosing a venue: full Debian is a furnished mansion
            (pay for rooms you never enter), slim a fitted studio, alpine
            a tiny cabin with NON-STANDARD sockets — half your appliances
            (wheels) need adapters (compilers).
         🔬 easypj: FROM python:3.11-slim + psycopg2-binary → a clean
            40 s install; the same on alpine wants gcc, musl-dev and
            postgresql-dev plus minutes of compiling — for a BIGGER
            effective image.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: time `pip install psycopg2-binary` inside
            python:3.11-slim vs python:3.11-alpine containers. Verify:
            slim installs a wheel in seconds; alpine attempts a source
            build (or fails wanting pg_config) — the table, proven.
         📊 SUMMARY: slim is the Django default: glibc wheels, small
            enough; alpine optimizes bytes at the cost of correctness.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the tag matters twice — 3.11-slim pins the PYTHON
            too; plain python:latest can silently jump interpreter
            versions between builds (2.5's lesson, again).

    8.3  Layer Optimization Techniques
         - Chain RUN commands: RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
         - Clean up in same layer: pip cache, apt cache, temp files
         - COPY specific files, not entire directory
         - Use .dockerignore aggressively
         [BEFORE/AFTER DOCKERFILE: Optimized layer structure]

         [CODE: one RUN — install and clean in the same layer]
            RUN apt-get update \
             && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends libpq5 \
             && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
         🎯 GOAL: Make every layer pay rent: nothing installed in layer N
            and deleted in layer N+1, nothing copied that isn't needed.
         [TECH] Layers are additive: a file deleted in a LATER layer
            still ships inside the earlier one (a whiteout entry merely
            hides it), so cleanup only works in the SAME RUN.
            --no-install-recommends halves apt payloads; COPY of specific
            paths beats COPY . . where practical; .dockerignore (5.2) is
            layer optimization too — it shrinks what COPY can even see.
         [ANALOGY] Sedimentary rock: each layer fossilizes forever.
            Burying garbage under a new layer of soil does not remove
            it — the truck must leave DURING the same geological event.
         🔬 easypj: `RUN pip install -r requirements.txt` followed by a
            separate `RUN rm -rf /root/.cache` shrinks NOTHING — the
            cache is fossilized in the pip layer; --no-cache-dir (or
            5.7's cache mount) is the correct move.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build one image installing a package and
            rm-ing it in a second RUN, and one doing both in a single
            RUN; compare docker images sizes. Verify: only the single-RUN
            variant got smaller — deletion across layers is a lie.
         📊 SUMMARY: clean in the layer that dirtied; chain related RUNs;
            copy precisely. Layers only ever grow.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: docker history shows a "0 B" layer for the late
            rm — the bytes did not leave, they just went invisible.

    8.4  Python/Django Specific Optimizations
         - Use --no-cache-dir with pip
         - Use slim image instead of full Debian
         - Multi-stage: collect static separately
         - Remove dev dependencies for production
         - Use .dockerignore for __pycache__, .git, env/
         [CODE: Optimized easypj Dockerfile with all techniques applied]

         [CHECKLIST: the Django shrink list — applied to easypj]
            [ ] FROM python:3.11-slim (8.2 — glibc wheels)
            [ ] pip install --no-cache-dir (or 5.7 cache mount)
            [ ] PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 — no .pyc in the image
            [ ] multi-stage: gcc & friends stay in the builder (4.5)
            [ ] collectstatic in builder; final stage gets /app/static
            [ ] prod requirements.txt: no pytest, no debug-toolbar
         🎯 GOAL: Apply every generic technique from 8.2-8.3 in its
            Django-specific form and land easypj under the 200 MB bar.
         [TECH] Split requirements into base/prod/dev files and install
            ONLY prod in the image — pytest, coverage and debug toolbars
            are dev-machine software. collectstatic belongs in the
            builder stage: the final image serves /app/static as plain
            files and never needs the toolchain that produced them.
            PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 is for logs (6.3), not size — but it
            rides the same ENV line.
         [ANALOGY] Touring vs rehearsal gear: the band rehearses with
            spare amps, cables and a sound engineer (dev deps, gcc); the
            tour bus (image) carries only what is played ON STAGE.
         🔬 easypj: dropping dev-only packages plus the multi-stage split
            is the 850 MB → ~180 MB story the 4.5 table promised —
            measured, not vibes.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build easypj with dev requirements, then with
            prod-only; compare docker images, then try
            `docker run --rm easypj:prod python -c "import pytest"`.
            Verify: tens of MB gone AND the import fails — proof the
            ballast (and its CVEs) left the ship.
         📊 SUMMARY: slim base, no pip cache, no bytecode, no dev deps,
