            static collected in the builder — Django's 20% that cuts 80%.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: deleting psycopg2's system library goes too far —
            keep libpq5 in the FINAL stage or Django dies at first query.

    8.5  Image Scanning — Docker Scout
         - What is Docker Scout? (vulnerability scanning)
         - docker scout quickview easypj:v1
         - Understanding CVE reports
         - Fixing vulnerabilities: update base, update packages
         - docker scout recommendations
         [TERMINAL: Annotated scout output walkthrough]

         [TERMINAL: scout on easypj — drawn]
            $ docker scout quickview easypj:v1.1
              Target    easypj:v1.1       1C 3H 12M 41L
              Base      python:3.11-slim  0C 1H  9M 38L
            $ docker scout cves --only-severity critical,high easypj:v1.1
            $ docker scout recommendations easypj:v1.1
              ↳ update base image to 3.11-slim (newer point release)
         🎯 GOAL: Read a CVE report like an engineer: what is CRITICAL,
            what lives in YOUR layers vs the base, and what to fix first.
         [TECH] Scout matches the image's SBOM (every package + version)
            against CVE databases; severities run C/H/M/L. The quickview
            split above is the key diagnostic: findings on the BASE row
            are fixed by REBUILDING on a newer base (slim receives
            patches continuously); only the delta above it belongs to
            your requirements.txt. Zero CVEs is not the goal — zero
            UNREVIEWED criticals is.
         [ANALOGY] A building inspection: the report separates faults in
            the foundation you bought (base image) from the walls you
            built (pip layer). You don't re-pour the foundation yourself
            — you take the builder's newer revision.
         🔬 easypj: a Critical in Django lands in YOUR layer — bump the
            pin in requirements.txt and rebuild; a Critical in zlib is
            the base's — pull the fresh 3.11-slim and rebuild. Same
            command, different line changed.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the three commands above on easypj.
            Verify: you can state (a) the critical/high count, (b) the
            base vs your-layer split, (c) the single next action — in
            one minute flat.
         📊 SUMMARY: scan every release; read base vs own-layer findings
            differently; rebuild-on-newer-base is the cheapest patch.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: images do not rot visibly — an UNCHANGED easypj
            image gains CVEs as databases learn; rescan on a schedule,
            not only on rebuild.

    8.6  Distroless Images — The Ultimate Security
         - What is "distroless"? (no shell, no package manager, no utilities)
         - Why distroless? (no shell = attacker can't exec)
         - How to debug distroless containers (kubectl debug, ephemeral containers)
         - Distroless for Python/Go/Java
         [CODE: Distroless Dockerfile for easypj (multi-stage with distroless final)]


         🎯 GOAL: Say precisely what distroless removes, what that buys,
            and what it costs you at 3 AM — then decide for easypj.
         [TECH] Distroless bases (gcr.io/distroless/python3 family) ship
            ONLY a runtime: no shell, no package manager, no coreutils.
            docker exec -it … bash cannot work — there is no bash; most
            of an attacker's post-exploitation toolkit (sh, curl, apt)
            simply does not exist. Debugging shifts to docker cp, logs,
            or ephemeral debug containers ([KUBERNETES GUIDE] covers
            kubectl debug).
         [ANALOGY] A bank vault with no doorway: builders assembled it
            around the contents (multi-stage COPY in), so there is no
            door for burglars — or for the locksmith. You interact only
            through slots designed in advance (port 8000, stdout logs).
         🔬 easypj: multi-stage — the builder on python:3.11-slim does
            pip install + collectstatic; the final distroless stage
            copies site-packages and /app. The 4.7 entrypoint SCRIPT
            needs /bin/sh — distroless forces migrations OUT of startup
            (run them as a separate exec step or one-off container).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build the distroless variant, then try
            docker exec -it easypj-dl sh. Verify: "no such file or
            directory" — the missing shell IS the security feature, and
            docker logs still shows everything gunicorn says (6.3).
         📊 SUMMARY: distroless = runtime-only final stage: minimal CVE
            surface, no shell for attackers or for you; pay with harder
            debugging and no shell-based entrypoint scripts.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: HEALTHCHECK CMD curl … (6.7) breaks too — probe
            with `python -c "import urllib.request; …"` or move health
            probing to the orchestrator [KUBERNETES GUIDE].

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 8 — minimum 10, covering: the size tax (pull
     math, CI volume, CVE counts), slim vs alpine (glibc/musl, psycopg2
     wheels), same-layer cleanup + whiteout files, --no-install-recommends,
     dev vs prod requirements split, collectstatic placement, scout
     quickview base-vs-own-layer triage, scheduled rescans, distroless
     trade-offs (no shell, no entrypoint script, health probes)]

Chapter 9: Containerizing easypj/jobpost — The Complete Walkthrough
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapters 4-8 taught pieces; production ships
    wholes. This chapter containerizes easypj end-to-end with zero gaps:
    real tree, real Dockerfile, real build, a running container, migrations
    against PostgreSQL, and the debugging session for when step five fails.
    Problem solved here: "each chapter made sense alone — now walk me
    through MY project, first file to first request, nothing skipped."

    9.1  Project Structure Review
         - easypj/ (Django project root)
         - jobpost/ (Django app — models, views, urls, admin)
         - Models: Skills, Author, Location, JobPost (with User FK)
         - Dependencies: Django 5.2, gunicorn, psycopg2
         [DIRECTORY TREE DIAGRAM: Full easypj project structure]

         [DIAGRAM:architecture — the easypj tree, container's-eye view]
            easypj/
            ├── manage.py           ← exec target for migrations (6.4)
            ├── requirements.txt    ← COPYed FIRST (cache layer, 4.4)
            ├── Dockerfile          ← this chapter's artifact
            ├── .dockerignore       ← gatekeeper of the context (5.2)
            ├── easypj/             ← settings.py, wsgi.py (gunicorn's
            │                         entry: easypj.wsgi:application)
            └── jobpost/            ← models.py: Skills, Author,
                                      Location, JobPost (FK → User)
         🎯 GOAL: Map every file the container story touches to the
            chapter that consumes it — before writing a single line.
         [TECH] What matters to Docker: requirements.txt pins Django 5.2,
            gunicorn and psycopg2-binary (PostgreSQL is the only database
            in this guide — settings.py reads DB_HOST/DB_NAME/DB_USER/
            DB_PASSWORD from the environment, 12-factor style). wsgi.py
            exposes `application` — the exact dotted path gunicorn's CMD
            names. The database itself is NOT in this tree: it runs as a
            separate container (Part 4 formalizes that).
         [ANALOGY] An architect's site survey: before pouring concrete
            (building images) you mark what exists — load-bearing walls
            (wsgi.py, requirements.txt) vs furniture (everything
            .dockerignore will keep out of the truck).
         🔬 easypj: jobpost/models.py holds Skills, Author, Location and
            JobPost — migrate (via 4.7's entrypoint or 6.4's exec) is
            what turns those classes into PostgreSQL tables at startup.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: in your checkout run `python -c "import
            easypj.wsgi"` and open requirements.txt. Verify: the import
            succeeds and the file pins Django, gunicorn, psycopg2-binary
            — the three names the Dockerfile is about to depend on.
         📊 SUMMARY: know the tree, the wsgi path and the env-driven DB
            settings before Docker enters; the database lives in its own
            container, always.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: hardcoded DB credentials in settings.py sink the
            whole chapter — the container gets its database address from
            the ENVIRONMENT or it is not portable.

    9.2  Writing the Dockerfile
         - Complete Dockerfile for easypj (production-ready)
         - Line-by-line explanation of each instruction
         - Why each choice was made (python:3.11-slim, gunicorn, etc.)
         [DOCKERFILE CODE with extensive annotations]

         🎯 GOAL: Assemble the production Dockerfile from decisions
            already won — and cite the chapter behind every single line.
         [TECH] The file is 4.4's skeleton hardened by 4.6: slim base
            pinned (8.2), requirements-first COPY (5.3), --no-cache-dir
            (8.4), a created appuser + USER before CMD, exec-form
            gunicorn CMD (4.3), EXPOSE 8000 as documentation (4.2).
            Nothing here is new — that is the point: production files
            are arguments you can replay, not folklore you copy.
         [ANALOGY] A chess game's annotated score sheet: every move
            written WITH the reason it was played; a reviewer replays
            the reasoning, not just the moves.
         [CODE: the two lines beginners miss — drawn]
            RUN adduser --disabled-password --gecos "" appuser \
             && chown -R appuser:appuser /app
            USER appuser       # everything after this runs unprivileged
         🔬 easypj: with USER appuser, a hijacked Django process cannot
            apt-get, cannot write outside /app, cannot bind port 80 —
            three attack chapters closed by one line.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build, then `docker run --rm easypj:v1.0
            whoami` and `docker run --rm easypj:v1.0 id -u`.
            Verify: appuser and a non-zero UID — root is gone from
            runtime.
         📊 SUMMARY: every line is a chapter's conclusion: base (8.2),
            order (5.3), cleanup (8.3), USER (4.6), exec CMD (4.3).
            ⚠️ Gotcha: set USER BEFORE CMD but AFTER installs — flip the
            order and pip fails on permissions, or worse, you ship root.

    9.3  Writing .dockerignore
         - Complete .dockerignore for Django projects
         - Why each entry is excluded
         [.dockerignore CODE with annotations]

         🎯 GOAL: Ship the 5.2 ignore file with easypj and prove it does
            its three jobs: speed, cache stability, secret hygiene.
         [TECH] Same entries as 5.2 (.git, __pycache__, env/, .env,
            media/, the Dockerfile itself); the Django-specific stakes:
            env/ would ship a wrong-platform interpreter, .env would
            fossilize SECRET_KEY and DB_PASSWORD into a pullable layer,
            media/ belongs in a volume (Part 6), and host __pycache__ is
            stale bytecode that can shadow fresh code.
         [ANALOGY] The bouncer's list at the build's door — written once,
            enforced identically on every build, no judgment calls at
            2 AM.
         🔬 easypj: the .env next to manage.py holds DB_PASSWORD for
            local runs — precisely why it must NEVER enter the image;
            the container receives it at RUN time (--env-file, 3.5).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker build . 2>&1 | grep "transferring
            context"` before and after adding the file, then
            `docker run --rm easypj:v1.0 ls -a /app`.
            Verify: context shrinks to ~1 MB; no .env, env/ or .git
            inside the image's /app.
         📊 SUMMARY: one dotfile, three protections; copy it into every
            Django repo you ever containerize.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: .dockerignore lives in the CONTEXT ROOT (next to
            the dot in docker build .) — one directory off and it is
            silently ignored, with zero warnings.

    9.4  Building the Image
         - docker build -t easypj:v1.0 .
         - Verifying the build: docker images | grep easypj
         - Checking image size: docker image ls easypj
         [TERMINAL SESSION: Full build with annotations]

         [TERMINAL: build + verify — drawn]
            $ docker build -t easypj:v1.0 .
            [+] Building 41.7s (12/12) FINISHED
            $ docker images easypj
            REPOSITORY   TAG    IMAGE ID       CREATED         SIZE
            easypj       v1.0   8a4be2c91f03   10 seconds ago  183MB
            $ docker history easypj:v1.0 | head -4   # sanity: layer sizes
         🎯 GOAL: Build easypj:v1.0 and VERIFY the artifact — tag, size,
            layers — before ever trying to run it.
         [TECH] Verification order: exit code 0 (build finished), docker
            images (tag exists, size ≈ 180 MB not 900 MB — a fat result
            means .dockerignore or the slim base did not apply), docker
            history (pip layer ~140 MB, code layer sub-MB, as 7.6
            taught). A second identical build should be near-instant:
            all CACHED.
         [ANALOGY] A pre-delivery inspection at the factory gate: check
            the paperwork (tag), weigh the crate (size), open the packing
            list (history) BEFORE the truck (docker run) leaves the yard.
         🔬 easypj: 183 MB confirms slim base + no dev deps + clean
            layers — one glance at SIZE audits four chapters of choices.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the session above, then rebuild without
            changes. Verify: second build all-CACHED in ~1 s, same IMAGE
            ID — determinism, proven.
         📊 SUMMARY: build, then read tag/size/history before running; a
            wrong SIZE is the cheapest bug report you will ever get.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker build` succeeding does NOT mean Django
            starts — imports, settings and DB wiring are RUNTIME facts;
            that is exactly what 9.5 and 9.6 exist for.

    9.5  Running the Container Locally
         - docker run -d -p 8000:8000 --name easypj-container easypj:v1.0
         - docker ps (verify it's running)
         - docker logs easypj-container (check startup logs)
         - Open browser: http://localhost:8000
         - docker exec easypj-container python manage.py migrate
         [TERMINAL SESSION: Full run with verification]

         [TERMINAL: first run, full circuit — drawn]
            $ docker network create easypj-net
            $ docker run -d --name easypj-db --network easypj-net \
                -e POSTGRES_DB=easypj -e POSTGRES_USER=easypj \
                -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=devpass postgres:16
            $ docker run -d --name easypj-container --network easypj-net \
                -p 8000:8000 -e DB_HOST=easypj-db --env-file .env \
                easypj:v1.0
            $ docker logs -f easypj-container    # migrations, then boot
            [INFO] Listening at: http://0.0.0.0:8000
            $ curl -I http://localhost:8000/jobs/   # HTTP/1.1 200 OK
         🎯 GOAL: Take easypj:v1.0 to a real answered HTTP request, with
            PostgreSQL running beside it — the full local circuit.
         [TECH] Two containers, one user-defined network: easypj reaches
            the database as host "easypj-db" (container DNS — Part 5 does
            the deep dive), the browser reaches easypj via -p 8000:8000.
            gunicorn must bind 0.0.0.0 — 127.0.0.1 inside a container is
            unreachable from outside it. Migrations run via 4.7's
            entrypoint (or manually: docker exec easypj-container
            python manage.py migrate).
         [ANALOGY] Opening night with the full cast: the database is the
            orchestra in its own pit (container), DNS the conductor's eye
            contact, and the curl is the first paying customer.
         🔬 easypj: after migrate, /admin/ lists Skills, Authors,
            Locations and Job Posts — the model tables now live in the
            PostgreSQL CONTAINER, not on your laptop's system install.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the session above, create a superuser
            (6.4), open http://localhost:8000/admin/. Verify: login
            works, and a JobPost saved in admin survives
            `docker restart easypj-container` — state lives in the DB
            container (and Part 6 will make THAT survive too).
         📊 SUMMARY: network + db container + web container + port map +
            migrate = running easypj; every piece is a prior chapter.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: localhost inside a container is THAT container —
            DB_HOST=localhost is the #1 first-run failure; it must be
            the DB container's NAME on a shared network.

    9.6  Debugging When Things Go Wrong
         - Container exits immediately → docker logs
         - Port already in use → change -p mapping
         - Module not found → check requirements.txt
         - Django settings error → check DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE env
         - Database connection refused → check DB_HOST network
         [TROUBLESHOOTING TABLE: 10 common errors + solutions]

         [TABLE: symptom → first move — drawn]
            exits instantly          | docker logs — read the dying words
            :8000 connection refused | docker ps — is -p there? gunicorn
                                     | on 0.0.0.0, not 127.0.0.1?
            OperationalError (DB)    | docker network inspect — same net?
                                     | DB_HOST = db container's NAME?
            ModuleNotFoundError      | docker exec … pip list — is it in
                                     | requirements.txt at all?
            port is already allocated| docker ps — old container owns it
            exit code 137            | docker inspect — OOMKilled? (6.6)
         🎯 GOAL: Debug a dead easypj container by protocol, not panic:
            observe (6.3) → hypothesize → verify inside (6.4).
         [TECH] The invariant: docker logs FIRST, always — a container
            that "won't start" is a process that started and printed its
            dying words. Then docker inspect for the exit code and the
            config actually received (Env, Networks), then exec to test
            the hypothesis from inside. Every error in the table is one
            of three classes: image content, runtime config, or wiring
            between containers.
         [ANALOGY] An aircraft accident board, not a séance: flight
            recorder (logs), maintenance file (inspect), then walking
            the wreck (exec). Boards publish causes BECAUSE they read
            instruments in a fixed order.
         🔬 easypj: "OperationalError: could not translate host name
            easypj-db" reads as: code fine, image fine, WIRING broken —
            check both containers' networks before touching Python.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: sabotage on purpose: run without -e DB_HOST,
            then on the wrong network, then with a typo'd module in CMD.
            Verify: for each, the table's first command names the culprit
            in under a minute — you just rehearsed the 3 AM drill.
         📊 SUMMARY: logs → inspect → exec, and classify: content,
            config, or wiring. The table covers 90% of first-week pain.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: if the container dies too fast to exec into, hold
            the door open with `docker run -it --entrypoint bash
            easypj:v1.0` — an autopsy room where the body can't vanish.

    9.7  The Complete easypj Dockerfile (Final Version)
         - Multi-stage production Dockerfile
         - Development vs Production variants
         [COMPLETE CODE: Final optimized Dockerfile]

         [CODE: easypj/Dockerfile — final, multi-stage, production]
            # ---- stage 1: builder ------------------------------------
            FROM python:3.11-slim AS build
            WORKDIR /app
            COPY requirements.txt .
            RUN pip install --no-cache-dir --prefix=/install \
                -r requirements.txt
            COPY . .
            RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
            # ---- stage 2: runtime ------------------------------------
            FROM python:3.11-slim
            WORKDIR /app
            RUN adduser --disabled-password --gecos "" appuser
            COPY --from=build /install /usr/local
            COPY --from=build --chown=appuser:appuser /app /app
            ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
            USER appuser
            EXPOSE 8000
            ENTRYPOINT ["/app/docker-entrypoint.sh"]
            CMD ["gunicorn", "easypj.wsgi:application", \
                 "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "3"]
         🎯 GOAL: Read the final Dockerfile above as the Parts 2-3
            midterm: every line traceable, dev-vs-prod split explained.
         [TECH] The builder installs into --prefix=/install and collects
            static; the runtime stage copies /install into /usr/local and
            /app with --chown, drops to appuser, and hands 4.7's
            entrypoint the wait-migrate-exec sequence in front of a
            3-worker gunicorn. Dev variant: same image, overridden at run
            time — `docker run -p 8000:8000 -v "$PWD":/app easypj:v1.0
            python manage.py runserver 0.0.0.0:8000` (bind mounts:
            Part 6).
         [ANALOGY] The difference between a home recipe and a printed
            restaurant menu card: same dish as 4.4, but now portioned
            (workers), allergen-labeled (USER, ENV) and reproducible by
            any cook on any shift.
         🔬 easypj: this exact file is what Parts 4-8 build on — compose
            wraps it, networks connect it to PostgreSQL, volumes keep its
            media, CI builds and ships it.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build it, rerun the 9.5 circuit, then
            `docker scout quickview easypj:v1.0` and `docker run --rm
            easypj:v1.0 whoami`. Verify: ~180 MB, sub-50 CVE count,
            appuser — Chapters 4 through 8 signed one artifact.
         📊 SUMMARY: multi-stage + non-root + entrypoint + pinned slim
            base: the production baseline every later part assumes.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the entrypoint script must be chmod +x BEFORE the
            COPY (git preserves the bit) — "permission denied" at start
            is that missing bit, not a Docker mystery.

    9.8  docker init — Scaffolding Dockerfiles Automatically
         - docker init: interactive generator run in the project directory
         - Detects Python; asks version, port, and the run command
         - Generates Dockerfile, .dockerignore, compose.yaml, README.Docker.md
         - A starting point, not a shipped artifact — audit before trust
         - Diff its output against 9.7 line by line: the diff is the lesson

         [TERMINAL: docker init in the easypj checkout — drawn]
            $ cd easypj && docker init
            ? What application platform does your project use?  Python
            ? What version of Python do you want to use?        3.11
            ? What port do you want your app to listen on?      8000
            ? What is the command you use to run your app?
              gunicorn easypj.wsgi:application --bind 0.0.0.0:8000
            ✔ Created → Dockerfile  .dockerignore  compose.yaml
         🎯 GOAL: Scaffold Docker files for any new Python project in one
            minute — and know exactly which generated lines to fix.
         [TECH] docker init inspects the directory (requirements.txt →
            Python template) and writes four files. The generated
            Dockerfile is genuinely decent: slim base, cache-mounted pip
            (5.7!), a non-root user, requirements-first ordering. What
            it CANNOT know: your entrypoint needs (4.7's wait-and-
            migrate), collectstatic, the dev/prod requirements split
            (8.4), or that compose.yaml needs a postgres service with a
            volume — it scaffolds a generic app; you re-apply the Django
            decisions.
         [ANALOGY] A tailor's standard pattern: right fabric, straight
            seams, average measurements. It fits the mannequin perfectly
            — YOUR shoulders (migrations, static, PostgreSQL) still need
            fitting by hand.
         🔬 easypj: after init, the edit list is short and principled:
            add the 4.7 entrypoint, add collectstatic (multi-stage for
            9.7 parity), point compose at postgres:16 with env vars —
            every edit cites a section of this guide.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run docker init in a scratch copy of easypj
            and diff its Dockerfile against 9.7's.
            Verify: you can defend every difference — and you spot that
            init already used the pip cache mount you learned in 5.7.
         📊 SUMMARY: init writes the 80% boilerplate correctly; your 20%
            — entrypoint, static, PostgreSQL wiring — is the judgment
            this guide trained.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: rerunning docker init OVERWRITES existing files
            after one confirm — never say yes in a repo holding
            hand-tuned, uncommitted Docker files.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 9 — minimum 10, covering: project tree →
     Docker mapping (wsgi path, env-driven PostgreSQL settings), citing
     the chapter behind each Dockerfile line, USER/adduser placement,
     .dockerignore's three protections, build verification (size,
     history, cache determinism), the two-container run circuit
     (network, DB_HOST, 0.0.0.0 bind), admin + migrate proof, the
     logs→inspect→exec debugging protocol + sabotage drills, the final
     multi-stage file (--chown, --prefix, entrypoint), docker init
     output audit vs 9.7]

PART 4: DOCKER COMPOSE — MULTI-CONTAINER APPLICATIONS                    🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 4:
      What runs where?    → four services, one host, one compose project.
      What talks to what? → nginx → django:8000 → postgres:5432 & redis:6379.
      What breaks first?  → ordering — Django starts before Postgres is READY.

Chapter 10: Docker Compose Fundamentals
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapter 3 taught you to run ONE container;
    easypj is FOUR (Django, PostgreSQL, Redis, Nginx) that must find each
    other, start in order, and die together. This chapter replaces a page
    of docker run flags with one declarative file and one command. Problem
    solved here: "my app needs five terminals and a wiki page to start."

    10.1 What is Docker Compose?
         - Tool for defining and running multi-container Docker applications
         - Compose file (docker-compose.yml or compose.yaml)
         - One command: docker compose up (starts everything)
         - Use case: easypj needs Django + PostgreSQL + Redis + Nginx

         [DOCKER COMPOSE — ONE COMMAND, 4 CONTAINERS]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                  🎼 docker compose up — THE MAGIC                          │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────┐                                         │
         │  │  docker-compose.yml         │     ONE COMMAND:                        │
         │  │                             │     $ docker compose up -d              │
         │  │  services:                  │                                         │
         │  │    django:   build: .       │         │                               │
         │  │    postgres: image: pg:15   │         │                               │
         │  │    redis:    image: redis:7 │         ▼                               │
         │  │    nginx:    image: nginx   │                                         │
         │  └─────────────┬───────────────┘                                         │
         │                │                                                         │
         │                ▼                                                         │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │                    🐳 DOCKER HOST                                │    │
         │  │                                                                  │    │
         │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐        │    │
         │  │  │ 🐍 Django │  │ 🐘 Postgres│ │ 🔴 Redis  │  │ 🟢 Nginx  │        │    │
         │  │  │  :8000   │  │  :5432   │  │  :6379   │  │  :80     │        │    │
         │  │  │          │  │          │  │          │  │          │        │    │
         │  │  │ easypj   │  │ easypj_db│  │ cache    │  │ reverse  │        │    │
         │  │  │ app      │  │ data 📂  │  │ backend  │  │ proxy    │        │    │
         │  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘        │    │
         │  │       └─────────────┴──────────────┴─────────────┘              │    │
         │  │                        │                                        │    │
         │  │           easypj_default (auto-created network)                 │    │
         │  │           DNS: "django", "postgres", "redis", "nginx"           │    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                                          │
         │  🌐 Browser → http://localhost:80 → nginx → django:8000 → postgres:5432 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Say what Compose is, what a compose file declares, and
            why easypj's 4-service stack needs it.
         [TECH] Compose is a declarative orchestrator for ONE host: you
            describe desired state (services, networks, volumes) in
            compose.yaml; `docker compose up` diffs desired vs actual and
            creates/starts whatever is missing. The MAGIC diagram above is
            that diff applied: one file → four containers + one DNS network.
         [ANALOGY] docker run is cooking each dish by shouting instructions;
            compose.yaml is the printed menu the kitchen executes as one
            order. In the diagram, the YAML box is the menu; the four
            containers on the host are the dishes arriving together.
         🔬 easypj: Django + PostgreSQL + Redis + Nginx = four docker run
            commands with a dozen flags each, or ONE `docker compose up -d`.
            The easypj_default network in the diagram comes free.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: count the docker run flags you would need for
            the diagram's four services (names, ports, env, volumes,
            network). Verify: 20+ flags collapse into one readable file.
         📊 SUMMARY: Compose = declared desired state for a multi-container
            app on one host; up/down manage the whole stack as a unit.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Compose is single-host by design — multi-host
            orchestration is Swarm/Kubernetes territory (Part 9).

    10.2 Docker Compose vs docker run
         - docker run: one container at a time, manual networking
         - docker compose: multiple containers, automatic networking
         - Compose = infrastructure as code for local dev

         [COMPOSE vs DOCKER RUN — 10 DIMENSIONS COMPARED]
         | Aspect              | docker run                     | docker compose              |
         | Containers/command  | 1 container per command        | Multiple in one file         |
         | Networking          | Manual (docker network create) | Auto-created DNS network     |
         | Startup Order       | Manual (wait + run sequence)   | depends_on + health checks   |
         | Configuration       | CLI flags only                 | Declarative YAML file        |
         | Reproducibility     | Shell script dependent         | compose.yaml = single source |
         | Tear Down           | docker rm for each container   | docker compose down (all)    |
         | Environment Vars    | -e flag per container          | .env file + env_file key     |
         | Volumes             | -v flag per mount              | volumes: section declarative |
         | Team Sharing        | Share shell scripts            | Share compose.yaml (git)     |
         | Production Ready    | ❌ No                          | ⚠️ Dev only (use K8s/Swarm) |

         🎯 GOAL: Defend "put it in compose.yaml" with three concrete wins
            straight from the 10-dimension table above.
         [TECH] docker run state lives in your shell history; Compose state
            lives in a versioned file. Compose also NAMESPACES everything:
            the project name prefixes containers (easypj-django-1),
            networks (easypj_default) and volumes — stacks never collide.
         [ANALOGY] docker run is texting directions turn by turn; Compose
            is sharing the pinned route. The table's Reproducibility and
            Team Sharing rows are exactly "screenshots of texts" vs "the
            pin everyone opens".
         🔬 easypj: a teammate clones the repo, runs `docker compose up`,
            and gets THE SAME stack — no README with 12 docker run lines.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: write the docker run equivalent of just the
            postgres service (image, env, volume, network, restart).
            Verify: your one-liner is already longer than the YAML block.
         📊 SUMMARY: same engine underneath — Compose replaces YOUR hands,
            turning imperative flags into declarative state.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: mixing manual docker run containers INTO a compose
            project confuses `compose down` — it only removes what the
            file declares.

    10.3 Installing Docker Compose
         - Docker Compose v2 (docker compose, built into Docker Desktop)
         - Linux standalone install
         - docker compose version (verify)
         [INSTALL COMMANDS per OS]

         🎯 GOAL: Get `docker compose version` answering on any machine —
            and know exactly which binary answered.
         [TECH] Compose v2 is a Go CLI plugin living in
            ~/.docker/cli-plugins/ (or /usr/libexec/docker/cli-plugins).
            Docker Desktop bundles it; Linux servers install the
            docker-compose-plugin package. v1 (python docker-compose) is
            end-of-life and should be retired on sight.
         [TERMINAL: install + verify — drawn]
            $ sudo apt-get install docker-compose-plugin   # Debian/Ubuntu
            $ sudo dnf install docker-compose-plugin       # RHEL/Fedora
            $ docker compose version                # v2.x — plugin found
            $ docker-compose version                # v1 relic — retire it
         [ANALOGY] v2 is a new attachment for the drill you already own
            (the docker CLI); v1 was a separate battery-powered tool —
            fits the same screws, different engine, no longer maintained.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `docker compose version`, then
            `ls ~/.docker/cli-plugins/`. Verify: version prints v2.x and
            you can point at the plugin file that produced it.
         📊 SUMMARY: Compose v2 = `docker compose` (space), a CLI plugin
            shipped with Desktop and the Linux plugin package.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: tutorials writing `docker-compose` (hyphen) are v1
            era — the commands mostly translate, the binary may not exist.

    10.4 The compose.yaml File Structure
         - Version (optional in v2)
         - services: containers to run
         - networks: custom networks
         - volumes: persistent storage
         - configs: configuration files
         - secrets: sensitive data

         [COMPOSE.YAML ANATOMY — EVERY KEY EXPLAINED]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                    📋 compose.yaml ANATOMY                                │
         │                                                                          │
         │  # compose.yaml                                                          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │ services:              ← CONTAINERS: your app stack               │   │
         │  │   django:              ← Service name (becomes DNS hostname!)     │   │
         │  │     build: .           ← Build from Dockerfile in current dir     │   │
         │  │     image: easypj:v1   ← OR use pre-built image                   │   │
         │  │     ports:             ← Port mapping (HOST:CONTAINER)            │   │
         │  │       - "8000:8000"    ← Host 8000 → Container 8000               │   │
         │  │     environment:       ← Environment variables                    │   │
         │  │       - DEBUG=False    ← (use .env file for secrets!)             │   │
         │  │     volumes:           ← Mount storage                            │   │
         │  │       - ./src:/app     ← Bind mount (dev)                         │   │
         │  │       - static:/static ← Named volume (prod)                      │   │
         │  │     depends_on:        ← Startup order (NOT readiness!)           │   │
         │  │       postgres:        ← Wait for postgres container to START     │   │
         │  │         condition:     ← v2.1+: can wait for health check         │   │
         │  │           service_healthy                                        │   │
         │  │     healthcheck:       ← Container health monitoring              │   │
         │  │       test: ["CMD",   │   │
         │  │         "curl","-f",   │   │
         │  │         "http://local.."]│ │
         │  │       interval: 30s    │   │
         │  │       retries: 3       │   │
         │  │     restart: always    ← Restart policy (no/always/on-failure/etc)│   │
         │  │     networks:          ← Which networks to join                   │   │
         │  │       - backend        ← Django on backend network                │   │
         │  │       - frontend       ← Django also on frontend (for nginx)      │   │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
         │  │ networks:              ← CUSTOM NETWORKS                           │   │
         │  │   backend:             ← Internal network (DB, Redis, Django)      │   │
         │  │     driver: bridge     ← Network driver                            │   │
         │  │     internal: true     ← No external access! 🔒                    │   │
         │  │   frontend:            ← External network (Nginx, Django)          │   │
         │  │     driver: bridge     ← (or overlay for Swarm)                    │   │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
         │  │ volumes:               ← NAMED VOLUMES                             │   │
         │  │   postgres_data:       ← Persists DB data                          │   │
         │  │   static:              ← Persists collected static files           │   │
         │  │   media:               ← Persists user uploads                     │   │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
         │  │ secrets:               ← SWARM SECRETS (encrypted at rest)         │   │
         │  │   db_password:         ← Sensitive! Never in plain text.           │   │
         │  │     external: true     ← Pre-created: docker secret create        │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


         🎯 GOAL: Read any compose.yaml top-down and name what every
            top-level key owns, using the anatomy diagram above as the map.
         [TECH] Four top-level keys matter: services (containers),
            networks, volumes, secrets/configs. Every service key maps 1:1
            onto a docker run flag: ports→-p, environment→-e, volumes→-v,
            restart→--restart. The service NAME becomes the DNS hostname —
            the single most load-bearing line in the anatomy diagram.
         [ANALOGY] The file is a building plan: services = apartments,
            networks = corridors connecting them, volumes = the storage
            basement that survives renovations, secrets = the safe. The
            anatomy diagram is that plan with every room labeled.
         🔬 easypj: `django:` under services is why nginx.conf can say
            proxy_pass http://django:8000 — name = hostname, no IPs ever.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: cover the right column of the anatomy diagram
            and recite what each key does; then run `docker compose
            config` on any file. Verify: it prints the resolved model —
            or names the exact line that is wrong.
         📊 SUMMARY: services/networks/volumes/secrets — four keys, one
            mental model: each service block is a docker run in YAML.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: depends_on orders STARTUP only — it never waits for
            readiness unless you add condition: service_healthy (12.4).

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 10 — minimum 10, covering: what Compose is and
     its single-host scope, compose.yaml keys ↔ docker run flags mapping,
     project namespacing (easypj-django-1, easypj_default), v1 vs v2
     install + verification, the four top-level keys, service name = DNS
     hostname, depends_on vs readiness, up/down as stack-level verbs,
     when Compose is the wrong tool]

Chapter 11: Compose for easypj — The Complete Full Stack
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapter 10 gave you the grammar; this chapter
    writes the actual sentence — the complete, runnable compose.yaml that
    boots easypj end to end: gunicorn serving jobpost, PostgreSQL holding
    the five models, Redis caching, Nginx fronting. Problem solved here:
    "I understand Compose examples, but I still can't compose MY app."

    11.1 Planning the easypj Stack
         - Service 1: Django app (easypj)
         - Service 2: PostgreSQL database (PostGIS if needed)
         - Service 3: Redis (cache + Celery broker)
         - Service 4: Nginx (reverse proxy + static files)

         [easypj COMPOSE STACK — 4 SERVICES ARCHITECTURE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │               🏗️ easypj DOCKER COMPOSE STACK                              │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │                    🌐 EXTERNAL TRAFFIC                             │   │
         │  │                    http://localhost:80                             │   │
         │  └────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                                   │                                      │
         │                                   ▼                                      │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │  🟢 NGINX (port 80 → django:8000)                               │    │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │    │
         │  │  │ Serves: /static/* (from static volume)                   │   │    │
         │  │  │ Proxies: /api/* → django:8000                            │   │    │
         │  │  │ Proxies: /admin/* → django:8000                          │   │    │
         │  │  │ Proxies: /jobs/* → django:8000 (jobpost app!)            │   │    │
         │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                     │                                    │
         │                  ┌──────────────────┴──────────────────┐                │
         │                  ▼                                      ▼                │
         │  ┌───────────────────────────────┐  ┌───────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │  🐍 DJANGO (port 8000)        │  │  🔴 REDIS (port 6379)         │  │
         │  │                               │  │                               │  │
         │  │  • easypj project             │  │  • Django cache backend       │  │
         │  │  • jobpost app                │  │  • Celery message broker      │  │
         │  │  • 5 models (Skills, Author,  │  │  • Session storage            │  │
         │  │    Location, JobPost, User)   │  │  • No persistence needed      │  │
         │  │  • Gunicorn WSGI server       │  │                               │  │
         │  └──────────────┬────────────────┘  └───────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                 │                                                       │
         │                 ▼                                                       │
         │  ┌───────────────────────────────┐                                      │
         │  │  🐘 POSTGRESQL (port 5432)    │                                      │
         │  │                               │                                      │
         │  │  • Database: easypj_db        │  💾 Volume: postgres_data            │
         │  │  • All Django model data      │     /var/lib/postgresql/data         │
         │  │  • User accounts & auth       │     PERSISTENT across restarts!      │
         │  │  • Job posts, skills, etc.    │                                      │
         │  └───────────────────────────────┘                                      │
         │                                                                          │
         │  NETWORKS:                                                               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  frontend (nginx + django)    backend (django + postgres + redis)│   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Justify every service and both networks in the stack
            diagram above BEFORE writing a single line of YAML.
         [TECH] Planning = three decisions per service: image source
            (build: . for Django vs pull postgres:15 / redis:7-alpine /
            nginx:alpine), state (postgres_data volume vs stateless), and
            network placement. The diagram encodes all three: only nginx
            touches port 80; postgres and redis sit backend-only,
            unreachable from the host.
         [ANALOGY] A restaurant floor plan: nginx is the front door,
            Django the dining room, PostgreSQL the locked pantry, Redis
            the pass-through counter. The diagram's frontend/backend split
            is the STAFF ONLY door — guests never enter the pantry.
         🔬 easypj: Skills, Author, Location, JobPost and User rows all
            live in postgres → it gets the volume; Redis holds cache +
            Celery jobs it can afford to lose → no volume; Django stays
            stateless so it can be rebuilt and scaled freely.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: for each of the 4 services write one line:
            image? state? networks?. Verify: your table reproduces the
            diagram — postgres/redis backend-only, nginx the only :80.
         📊 SUMMARY: decide images, state and network placement first —
            the YAML in 11.2 then writes itself.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: exposing postgres with ports: "5432:5432" "for
            convenience" puts your database on the hotel corridor —
            backend-internal is the default posture, not paranoia.

    11.2 compose.yaml — Django + PostgreSQL Service
         - Django service: build context, ports, env vars, volumes
         - PostgreSQL service: image, env vars (POSTGRES_DB, POSTGRES_USER, POSTGRES_PASSWORD)
         - depends_on: db (wait for database)
         - Healthcheck for PostgreSQL
         [COMPLETE compose.yaml CODE with annotations]

         🎯 GOAL: Write the two-service core of easypj — Django +
            PostgreSQL — with a healthcheck-gated startup, from memory.
         [CODE: compose.yaml — the easypj core, drawn]
            services:
              django:
                build: .
                command: >
                  gunicorn easypj.wsgi:application
                  --bind 0.0.0.0:8000 --workers 3
                ports: ["8000:8000"]
                env_file: .env
                depends_on:
                  postgres: { condition: service_healthy }
                networks: [backend, frontend]
              postgres:
                image: postgres:15
                environment:
                  POSTGRES_DB: easypj_db
                  POSTGRES_USER: easypj
                  POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}
                volumes: ["postgres_data:/var/lib/postgresql/data"]
                healthcheck:
                  test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U easypj -d easypj_db"]
                  interval: 5s
                  timeout: 3s
                  retries: 5
                networks: [backend]
            volumes: { postgres_data: {} }
            networks: { backend: {}, frontend: {} }
         [TECH] The POSTGRES_* variables are read ONCE by the postgres
            image's init script — only when the data volume is EMPTY. The
            healthcheck runs pg_isready inside the container; django's
            depends_on gate waits for "healthy", not merely "started".
         [ANALOGY] pg_isready is knocking on the pantry door and hearing
            "come in" — depends_on without a condition only checks the
            pantry LIGHT is on, not that anyone can serve you.
         🔬 easypj: settings.py reads HOST="postgres", NAME="easypj_db",
            USER="easypj" — the service name IS the DB host, never an IP.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker compose up -d`, then
            `docker compose exec django python manage.py migrate`.
            Verify: jobpost migrations apply, and \dt inside
            `docker compose exec postgres psql -U easypj easypj_db`
            lists the jobpost tables.
         📊 SUMMARY: two services, one gated startup — postgres declares
            healthy via pg_isready, THEN gunicorn boots.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: changing POSTGRES_PASSWORD after first boot does
            NOTHING — the credential lives inside postgres_data now;
            recreate the volume or ALTER ROLE inside psql.

    11.3 Adding Redis & Nginx Services
         - Redis service: image redis:7-alpine, no persistence
         - Nginx service: custom nginx.conf, port 80:80
         - Django settings: CACHES with Redis backend
         - Static files via Nginx
         [COMPLETE compose.yaml with all 4 services]
         [nginx.conf CODE]

         🎯 GOAL: Complete the stack: Redis for cache/broker, Nginx as the
            only door, static files served without waking Django.
         [CODE: the two added services + nginx.conf core — drawn]
            # compose.yaml (added to 11.2's file)
              redis:
                image: redis:7-alpine
                networks: [backend]
              nginx:
                image: nginx:alpine
                ports: ["80:80"]
                volumes:
                  - ./nginx.conf:/etc/nginx/conf.d/default.conf:ro
                  - static:/static:ro
                depends_on: [django]
                networks: [frontend]
            # nginx.conf — the lines that matter
            upstream easypj { server django:8000; }
            server {
              listen 80;
              location /static/ { alias /static/; }
              location / { proxy_pass http://easypj;
                           proxy_set_header Host $host; }
            }
         [TECH] `server django:8000` works because nginx and django share
            the frontend network — Compose DNS resolves the service name.
            Redis needs no ports: and no volume: cache loss is a non-event.
         [ANALOGY] Nginx is the maître d': greets everyone at :80, hands
            out static files from its own tray, and walks dynamic requests
            to the dining room (django:8000) — never into the pantry.
         🔬 easypj: settings.py CACHES → redis://redis:6379/1; the Celery
            broker → redis://redis:6379/0. Two databases, one container.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: up the stack, then curl http://localhost/jobs/
            and http://localhost/static/admin/css/base.css. Verify: both
            return 200 — the first appears in django's logs, the second
            ONLY in nginx's (compare docker compose logs nginx vs django).
         📊 SUMMARY: 4 services, one entry point; DNS names glue nginx →
            django → postgres/redis.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: editing nginx.conf on the host does not reload
            nginx — `docker compose exec nginx nginx -s reload` or
            restart the service.

    11.4 Environment Variables & Secrets in Compose
         - .env file for environment variables
         - env_file vs environment in compose
         - secrets: Docker secrets (Swarm) vs env vars
         - Django SECRET_KEY: never in compose.yaml, use .env
         [CODE: .env and compose.yaml environment configuration]

         🎯 GOAL: Keep SECRET_KEY and POSTGRES_PASSWORD out of
            compose.yaml and out of git — while every service still
            receives them.
         [CODE: .env — drawn (git-ignored!)]
            DEBUG=False
            SECRET_KEY=django-insecure-CHANGE-ME-51-chars
            POSTGRES_DB=easypj_db
            POSTGRES_USER=easypj
            POSTGRES_PASSWORD=pg-pass
            DATABASE_URL=postgres://easypj:pg-pass@postgres:5432/easypj_db
         [TECH] Two distinct mechanisms share the .env file: (1) compose
            interpolation — ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD} substituted INTO the yaml
            at parse time; (2) env_file: — variables injected into the
            CONTAINER at run time. environment: beats env_file on conflict.
         [ANALOGY] compose.yaml is the postcard anyone may read; .env is
            the sealed envelope the postcard refers to. Committing .env is
            mailing the envelope open.
         🔬 easypj: settings.py does os.environ["SECRET_KEY"] — the value
            travels .env → env_file → container env → Django, and never
            appears in compose.yaml, git history, or the image itself.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: add .env to .gitignore, then run
            `docker compose config`. Verify: the rendered output shows the
            real password — proof interpolation worked, and proof you must
            never paste config output into a ticket.
         📊 SUMMARY: .env feeds both ${interpolation} and env_file;
            secrets live in the one file git never sees.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: real Docker SECRETS (files under /run/secrets)
            need Swarm (Part 9); in plain Compose, "secrets" are still
            env vars and mounted files — treat .env as the crown jewels.

    11.5 Running the Full easypj Stack
         - docker compose up -d (start everything)
         - docker compose ps (see all services)
         - docker compose logs -f (follow all logs)
         - docker compose logs django (specific service)
         - docker compose exec django python manage.py migrate
         [TERMINAL: Complete startup and verification sequence]

         🎯 GOAL: Boot the whole stack cold and prove every layer works —
            DB, migrations, admin, jobs list — in six commands.
         [TERMINAL: cold start → verified, drawn]
            $ docker compose up -d           # build + create + start all 4
            $ docker compose ps              # postgres (healthy), 4 running
            $ docker compose exec django python manage.py migrate
            $ docker compose exec django python manage.py createsuperuser
            $ docker compose logs -f django  # gunicorn: Listening at :8000
            $ curl -I http://localhost/jobs/         # HTTP/1.1 200 OK
         [TECH] up -d = create networks + volumes + containers, started in
            dependency order (postgres → healthy → django → nginx). ps
            reads compose labels — it shows only THIS project's containers.
         [ANALOGY] Opening night checklist: power on (up), walk the rooms
            (ps), stock the pantry (migrate), hand the manager keys
            (createsuperuser), listen at the kitchen door (logs -f).
         🔬 easypj: after migrate, the five model tables exist; /admin/
            can create a JobPost linking Author, Location and Skills —
            that row is now bytes inside the postgres_data volume.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the six lines, then `docker compose down`
            and `up -d` again. Verify: your superuser and JobPost survive
            — the volume outlives the containers (down keeps volumes).
         📊 SUMMARY: up -d / ps / exec manage.py / logs -f — the four
            verbs of daily Compose life.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `down -v` ALSO deletes postgres_data — one flag
            turns "restart the stack" into "drop the database".

    11.6 Development Workflow with Docker Compose
         - Hot reload with volume mounts (.:/app)
         - docker compose watch (auto-sync + rebuild on changes)
         - Debugging inside compose: docker compose exec
         - Running tests: docker compose run django python manage.py test
         [WORKFLOW DIAGRAM: Edit code → Compose watches → Auto-reload → Test]


         🎯 GOAL: Edit models.py on the host and see the change live in
            the container without rebuilding anything.
         [DIAGRAM:flow — the dev loop, drawn]
            edit jobpost/views.py ─▶ bind mount .:/app ─▶ dev server sees
            the change ─▶ auto-restart ─▶ refresh browser ─▶ repeat
         [TECH] Two hot-reload routes: (a) volumes: [".:/app"] plus a
            reloading server — file changes cross the mount instantly;
            (b) docker compose watch, whose develop.watch actions are
            sync (copy files in), rebuild (Dockerfile/deps changed) and
            sync+restart (config changed). Details in 12.5.
         [ANALOGY] A bind mount is a serving hatch between your editor and
            the container's kitchen: dishes pass through instantly; watch
            is a waiter who also rebuilds the kitchen when the OVEN
            (Dockerfile) itself changes.
         🔬 easypj: the dev override runs command: python manage.py
            runserver 0.0.0.0:8000 with .:/app mounted; tests run one-shot
            in a clean container:
            docker compose run --rm django python manage.py test jobpost
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: change a string in a jobpost view, save,
            refresh the page. Verify: visible with NO rebuild; then check
            the test command above exits 0 and leaves nothing behind
            (--rm) in docker ps -a.
         📊 SUMMARY: bind mount + reloading server for code; compose watch
            when you also want rebuild-on-change; run --rm for tests.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: gunicorn does NOT reload by default — dev uses
            runserver or gunicorn --reload; forgetting this makes people
            "fix" it by rebuilding images all day.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 11 — minimum 10, covering: service/state/
     network planning, POSTGRES_* init-only semantics, pg_isready
     healthcheck + service_healthy gating, the gunicorn bind/workers
     command, nginx upstream django:8000 & static alias, redis cache vs
     broker DBs, .env's two mechanisms (interpolation vs env_file),
     the cold-start sequence, down vs down -v, hot-reload routes]

Chapter 12: Docker Compose Advanced Features
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: One compose.yaml now has three masters — your
    laptop, CI, and something production-like — and they disagree about
    ports, mounts and debug flags. This chapter adds the machinery that
    keeps ONE source of truth serving all three: profiles, override files,
    health-gated ordering, watch, and scaling. Problem solved here: "I keep
    editing compose.yaml back and forth between dev and prod."

    12.1 Compose Profiles — Environment-Specific Services
         - What are profiles? (conditional service inclusion)
         - dev profile, prod profile, debug profile
         - docker compose --profile dev up
         [CODE: compose.yaml with profiles]

         🎯 GOAL: Keep optional services (pgadmin, mailhog, celery-beat)
            declared in the file but OUT of the default `up`.
         [TECH] profiles: ["debug"] tags a service; untagged services
            always run, tagged ones only with --profile debug (or the
            COMPOSE_PROFILES env var). One file, N shapes — no copy-paste
            forks of the yaml.
         [CODE: a profile-tagged service — drawn]
              pgadmin:
                image: dpage/pgadmin4
                ports: ["127.0.0.1:5050:80"]
                profiles: ["debug"]
                networks: [backend]
         [ANALOGY] Theatre lighting presets: every lamp is wired
            (declared), but the "rehearsal" preset powers only some.
            --profile picks the preset; the wiring never changes.
         🔬 easypj: pgadmin under profile debug — daily `up` boots 4
            services; `docker compose --profile debug up -d` adds the DB
            GUI on :5050 when you need to eyeball jobpost rows.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: tag pgadmin as above, run plain `up -d`, then
            again with --profile debug. Verify: compose ps shows 4 then 5
            services — and plain `down` needs the profile to remove it.
         📊 SUMMARY: profiles = conditional membership in the stack; one
            file serves dev/debug/prod shapes.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: depends_on a profiled service from an unprofiled
            one and default `up` fails — dependencies must share profiles.

    12.2 Compose Overrides — Multiple Compose Files
         - docker-compose.yml (base)
         - docker-compose.override.yml (dev overrides)
         - docker-compose.prod.yml (production settings)
         - Merging: docker compose -f base.yml -f prod.yml up
         [CODE: 3 compose files with merge explanation]

         🎯 GOAL: Run dev and prod from the SAME base file with two small
            deltas — and predict the merged result before running it.
         [TECH] `docker compose up` auto-loads compose.yaml PLUS
            compose.override.yaml. Explicit -f lists load left→right,
            later files winning per key: scalars replace, maps merge,
            lists (ports, volumes) merge/replace per spec — always confirm
            with `docker compose config`.
         [CODE: the three-file layout — drawn]
            compose.yaml           # base: services, networks, volumes
            compose.override.yaml  # dev: mount .:/app, runserver,
                                   #      DEBUG=True, ports 8000:8000
            compose.prod.yaml      # prod: gunicorn, restart: always,
                                   #      no source mount, no 8000 exposed
            $ docker compose -f compose.yaml -f compose.prod.yaml up -d
         [ANALOGY] The base file is the house blueprint; overrides are
            transparent sheets laid on top — dev's sheet draws a workbench
            in the living room, prod's sheet bolts the doors. The bottom
            blueprint never changes.
         🔬 easypj: dev override swaps command to runserver and mounts the
            source; the prod file swaps in the gunicorn command and drops
            the mount so the image's COPY'd code is what actually runs.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `docker compose config` twice — once bare,
            once with -f base -f prod. Verify: command, volumes and ports
            differ exactly where the sheets said they would.
         📊 SUMMARY: base + override(s), later file wins; the merged truth
            is whatever compose config prints.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the dev override loads AUTOMATICALLY — on a server,
            forgetting -f can deploy runserver with DEBUG=True.

    12.3 Compose Networking Deep Dive
         - Default network: auto-created, services reachable by name
         - Custom networks: isolation between service groups
         - Network drivers: bridge, host, overlay, none
         - DNS resolution: service name → container IP

         [COMPOSE NETWORKING DIAGRAM — 4 Services Connected]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                 🌐 DOCKER COMPOSE NETWORKING                              │
         │                                                                          │
         │  Network: easypj_default (auto-created by compose)                       │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
         │  │                                                                     │  │
         │  │  ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐       │  │
         │  │  │   nginx      │────▶│   django     │────▶│  postgres    │       │  │
         │  │  │  (port 80)   │     │  (port 8000) │     │  (port 5432) │       │  │
         │  │  │              │     │              │     │              │       │  │
         │  │  │  DNS: nginx  │     │  DNS: django │     │ DNS: postgres│       │  │
         │  │  │  PortMap:    │     │  PortMap:    │     │  PortMap:    │       │  │
         │  │  │  80:80       │     │  (internal)  │     │  (internal)  │       │  │
         │  │  └──────┬───────┘     └──────┬───────┘     └──────┬───────┘       │  │
         │  │         │                   │                     │                │  │
         │  │         │    ┌──────────────┘                     │                │  │
         │  │         │    │                                    │                │  │
         │  │         │    │  django can: docker exec django    │                │  │
         │  │         │    │  ping postgres  → resolves! ✅     │                │  │
         │  │         │    │  (compose DNS = service name)      │                │  │
         │  │         │    │                                    │                │  │
         │  │         ▼    ▼                                    │                │  │
         │  │  ┌──────────────┐                                 │                │  │
         │  │  │    redis     │◀────────────────────────────────┘                │  │
         │  │  │  (port 6379) │   django can: docker exec django                  │  │
         │  │  │              │   redis-cli -h redis ping  → PONG ✅              │  │
         │  │  │  DNS: redis  │                                                  │  │
         │  │  └──────────────┘                                                  │  │
         │  │                                                                     │  │
         │  │  EXTERNAL: curl http://localhost:80 → nginx → django                │  │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  💡 Each service can reach any other service by its SERVICE NAME!        │
         │     Compose creates a DNS network automatically. No IPs to remember.     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Explain every arrow in the compose networking diagram
            above in terms of DNS names, never IPs.
         [TECH] Compose creates <project>_default and attaches every
            service unless you declare networks:. The embedded DNS server
            (127.0.0.11) maps service names → container IPs; a service on
            TWO networks answers on both. internal: true networks get no
            route out of the host — the DB-isolation pattern.
         [ANALOGY] The diagram above is an office floor with no walls
            yet built: one open-plan room where everyone answers to
            their NAME and can walk to anyone else's desk. Chapter 14's
            custom networks are the walls added later — postgres moves
            into a windowless back office that only django has a door
            to; `internal: true` (TECH, above) is what removes that
            office's street exit too.
         🔬 easypj: django reaches postgres:5432 and redis:6379 by name in
            the diagram; only nginx carries a PortMap (80:80) — everything
            else stays internal exactly as drawn.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker compose exec django ping -c1 postgres`
            then `docker network inspect easypj_default`. Verify: ping's
            reported IP matches postgres in the inspect output.
         📊 SUMMARY: service name = DNS record on the project network;
            custom networks are the isolation tool, port maps the only
            doors to the host.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: container-to-container traffic uses CONTAINER ports
            (django:8000), never host-mapped ones — and localhost inside a
            container is that container, not your laptop.

    12.4 depends_on with Health Checks
         - Problem: depends_on only waits for container START, not READINESS
         - Solution: depends_on with condition: service_healthy
         - Writing health checks for PostgreSQL, Redis, Django
         [CODE: Health check configurations + depends_on with condition]

         🎯 GOAL: Make "started" and "ready" two different words in your
            stack — and gate startup on the second one.
         [TECH] depends_on alone = wait until the container process
            EXISTS. condition: service_healthy = wait until the service's
            own healthcheck passes. Health states: starting → healthy /
            unhealthy — visible in compose ps and docker inspect.
         [CODE: health checks for all three tiers — drawn]
            postgres: test: ["CMD-SHELL","pg_isready -U easypj -d easypj_db"]
            redis:    test: ["CMD","redis-cli","ping"]           # → PONG
            django:   test: ["CMD-SHELL","curl -fs localhost:8000 || exit 1"]
            # each with: interval: 5s, timeout: 3s, retries: 5
         [ANALOGY] A container that started but isn't ready is a shop with
            the lights on and the till not counted. The healthcheck is the
            OPEN sign the shopkeeper flips — service_healthy means "queue
            outside until the sign flips".
         🔬 easypj: without the gate, gunicorn boots first and Django's
            first query hits a postgres still running initdb → connection
            refused → crash loop. With pg_isready gating, cold starts are
            boring — which is the goal.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: remove the condition, `docker compose down &&
            docker compose up -d`, read django's logs; restore it, repeat.
            Verify: crash + restart in run one, clean single boot in two.
         📊 SUMMARY: healthchecks define ready; service_healthy makes
            dependents wait for it.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the healthcheck command runs INSIDE the container —
            curl/pg_isready must exist in that image, or the check fails
            forever and the stack never starts.

    12.5 docker compose watch — Hot Reload
         - Sync: Copy changed files into container
         - Rebuild: Rebuild image when Dockerfile changes
         - Restart: Restart container when config changes
         [CODE: compose.yaml with watch configuration]

         🎯 GOAL: Choose the right watch action — sync, rebuild, or
            sync+restart — for each kind of file easypj changes.
         [CODE: develop.watch for the django service — drawn]
              django:
                develop:
                  watch:
                    - action: sync            # .py, templates → copy in
                      path: .
                      target: /app
                      ignore: [".git/", "*.pyc"]
                    - action: rebuild         # deps changed → new image
                      path: requirements.txt
                    - action: sync+restart    # config → restart process
                      path: easypj/settings.py
                      target: /app/easypj/settings.py
         [TECH] `docker compose watch` runs alongside up: sync copies
            changed files into the RUNNING container (no bind mount
            required); rebuild triggers build + container replacement;
            sync+restart bounces the service after copying.
         [ANALOGY] Three severities of edits to a play mid-run: sync =
            slip the actor a corrected line; sync+restart = redo the
            scene; rebuild = new script, restage the whole play.
         🔬 easypj: views.py edits sync in a blink; touching
            requirements.txt correctly forces the pip-layer rebuild from
            2.4 — watch knows the difference so you don't have to.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `docker compose watch`, edit a jobpost
            template, then add a package to requirements.txt. Verify: the
            first logs "Syncing", the second logs "Rebuilding".
         📊 SUMMARY: watch = a file-event router: sync for code, rebuild
            for deps, sync+restart for config.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: synced files land in the container's writable layer
            — a rebuild discards them; your disk copy (the source of the
            sync) is the only durable one, by design.

    12.6 Scaling Services with Compose
         - docker compose up --scale django=3
         - Load balancing with Nginx
         - Limitations: all on one host (for multi-host, use K8s/Swarm)
         [DIAGRAM: 3 Django containers behind Nginx on single host]

         🎯 GOAL: Run three gunicorn containers behind one nginx and know
            exactly what Compose scaling can and cannot do.
         [DIAGRAM:architecture — --scale django=3, drawn]
                          ┌────────────────────┐
               :80 ──────▶│  nginx (1)         │
                          │  upstream easypj   │
                          └──┬──────┬──────┬───┘
                 DNS "django"│      │      │  round-robin
                        ┌────▼─┐ ┌──▼───┐ ┌▼─────┐
                        │ dj-1 │ │ dj-2 │ │ dj-3 │  easypj-django-{1,2,3}
                        │:8000 │ │:8000 │ │:8000 │  no ports: published!
                        └──────┘ └──────┘ └──────┘
         [TECH] `docker compose up -d --scale django=3` creates N replicas
            of one service. Compose DNS returns all three IPs for
            "django"; nginx resolves and spreads requests. REQUIREMENT:
            a scaled service cannot publish a fixed host port — three
            containers cannot share 8000:8000.
         [ANALOGY] One reception desk (nginx), three clerks (replicas):
            callers dial one NAME and the switchboard (compose DNS) picks
            a clerk. Hiring clerk #4 is one flag, not new YAML.
         🔬 easypj: stateless Django (sessions in redis, uploads in
            volumes, rows in postgres) is WHY scaling works — replica 2
            can answer a request that replica 1 started.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: remove django's ports:, scale to 3, curl
            localhost/jobs/ six times, then read
            `docker compose logs django | grep GET`. Verify: the hits
            spread across django-1/2/3.
         📊 SUMMARY: --scale = N replicas on ONE host behind DNS
            round-robin; real multi-host scaling is Swarm (Part 9) or
            [KUBERNETES GUIDE].
            ⚠️ Gotcha: scaling with ports: "8000:8000" still set kills
            replica 2 with "port is already allocated" — 3.7's old friend.

    12.7 Validating & Debugging Compose Files
         - docker compose config: render the MERGED, interpolated truth
         - ${VAR:-default} and ${VAR:?error} interpolation syntax
         - Env precedence: shell > --env-file > .env > env_file > image ENV
         - Top YAML traps: tabs, unquoted ports, boolean strings
         - compose config --services / --volumes for quick sanity lists

         🎯 GOAL: Debug any "compose does something weird" in one move:
            render the merged truth and read what the engine actually saw.
         [TECH] `docker compose config` parses ALL -f files, applies
            override merging, resolves profiles and substitutes every
            ${VAR} — its output is the ONLY file the engine acts on.
            Interpolation grammar: ${VAR} (empty if unset), ${VAR:-8000}
            (default if unset/empty), ${VAR:?msg} (abort loudly if unset).
         [TABLE: env value precedence — highest wins]
            1 | shell env of the compose command  | export DEBUG=True
            2 | --env-file FILE flag              | --env-file .env.prod
            3 | .env beside compose.yaml          | POSTGRES_PASSWORD=…
            4 | env_file: inside the service      | runtime-only vars
            5 | ENV in the image's Dockerfile     | last-resort defaults
         [TABLE: top YAML traps — trap | what YAML does | fix]
            TAB indentation   | hard parse error       | spaces, always
            ports: [22:22]    | sexagesimal int 1342   | quote "22:22"
            DEBUG: false      | boolean, not string    | quote "false"
            COUNTRY: NO       | YAML 1.1 → false       | quote "NO"
         [ANALOGY] compose config is asking the pharmacist to print the
            FINAL prescription after every doctor's note was merged — you
            argue with that paper, not with the sticky notes (-f files).
         🔬 easypj: SECRET_KEY=${SECRET_KEY:?set it in .env} makes prod
            fail fast instead of running with an empty key, while
            PORT=${PORT:-8000} lets a fresh clone boot with zero setup.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: break the file on purpose — indent one line
            with a TAB, then unquote a port as 22:22. Run compose config
            after each. Verify: config names the tab's line number, and
            shows the port parsed into a number you never wrote.
         📊 SUMMARY: compose config = merged, interpolated, validated
            truth; ${VAR:-} for dev ergonomics, ${VAR:?} for prod safety.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: compose config prints RESOLVED secrets in plain
            text — pipe it to your eyes, never into a ticket or a commit.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 12 — minimum 10, covering: profiles & their
     dependency pitfall, override merge order + config verification, the
     auto-loading compose.override.yaml risk, project networks & embedded
     DNS, internal: true, started vs healthy + per-tier healthchecks,
     watch actions (sync/rebuild/sync+restart), --scale + fixed-port
     conflict, compose config as merged truth, ${VAR:-default} vs
     ${VAR:?}, env precedence order, YAML traps (tabs/ports/booleans)]


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 5: DOCKER NETWORKING — HOW CONTAINERS COMMUNICATE                   🟡
