━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 5:
      What runs where?    → virtual cables & switches inside the Linux kernel.
      What talks to what? → container → veth → bridge → NAT → outside world.
      What breaks first?  → DNS — names don't resolve on the DEFAULT bridge.

Chapter 13: Docker Networking Fundamentals
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Compose "just worked" — nginx found django,
    django found postgres — and you deserve to know why. This chapter opens
    the hood: the CNM model, the five drivers, the docker0 bridge and the
    embedded DNS server. Problem solved here: "containers talk to each
    other and I'm treating it as magic I can't debug."

    13.1 How Docker Networking Works
         - Container Network Model (CNM)
         - Sandbox, Endpoint, Network
         - Network namespaces and virtual Ethernet pairs
         - docker0 bridge (default)
         [ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM: CNM components]

         🎯 GOAL: Name the three CNM nouns — sandbox, endpoint, network —
            and point at each one inside a running container.
         [DIAGRAM:architecture — CNM on one host, drawn]
            ┌──────────────────── HOST ────────────────────┐
            │                                              │
            │  SANDBOX C1 (netns)      ┌────────────────┐  │
            │  [eth0]══ENDPOINT═══════▶│                │  │
            │                          │    NETWORK     │  │
            │  SANDBOX C2 (netns)      │    (docker0)   │  │
            │  [eth0]══ENDPOINT═══════▶│                │  │
            │                          └───────┬────────┘  │
            │                                  ▼ NAT       │
            └──────────────────────────────────┼───────────┘
                                               ▼ outside world
         [TECH] Sandbox = the container's private network namespace (own
            interfaces, routes, port table). Endpoint = one veth pair: one
            end becomes eth0 INSIDE the sandbox, its twin plugs into a
            network. Network = a virtual switch that a libnetwork driver
            implements — docker0 by default.
         [ANALOGY] Hotel wiring: sandbox = the room's own wall sockets,
            endpoint = the cable from room to patch panel, network = the
            patch panel itself. The diagram is that wiring closet.
         🔬 easypj: `docker exec easypj-web ip addr` shows eth0@ifXX — the
            veth's inside end; `ip link` on the host shows its twin
            attached to the bridge.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run both commands above and match the ifXX
            index across host and container. Verify: the numbers pair up —
            you just traced one endpoint end to end.
         📊 SUMMARY: sandbox (namespace) + endpoint (veth pair) + network
            (bridge/driver) — every Docker network is these three nouns.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a container can hold MANY endpoints (one per
            network) but exactly ONE sandbox — multi-homed, never
            multi-brained.

    13.2 Network Drivers Overview
         - bridge: Default, containers on same host (docker0)
         - host: Container uses host's network directly (no isolation)
         - none: No networking (loopback only)
         - overlay: Multi-host Swarm networking
         - ipvlan/macvlan: Assign physical IP to container

         [DOCKER NETWORK DRIVERS — ARCHITECTURE COMPARISON]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                     🌐 5 NETWORK DRIVERS VISUALIZED                        │
         │                                                                          │
         │  1. BRIDGE (default)          2. HOST                  3. NONE            │
         │  ┌──────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐  │
         │  │ Host OS          │        │ Host OS          │     │ Host OS      │  │
         │  │ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐  │        │ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐  │     │ ┌──┐         │  │
         │  │ │C1│ │C2│ │C3│  │        │ │C1│ │C2│ │C3│  │     │ │C1│         │  │
         │  │ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘  │        │ │  shares host   │     │ │lo only│    │  │
         │  │  docker0 bridge │        │ │  network!      │     │ │       │    │  │
         │  │  (NAT to host)  │        │ └──────────────────┘     │ │127.0.0│    │  │
         │  │  -p 8080:80     │        │  ⚠️ No isolation         │ └──────────────┘  │
         │  └──────────────────┘        │  ✅ Max performance      │  🔒 Max security  │
         │                              └──────────────────┘                      │
         │  4. OVERLAY (Swarm)                             5. MACVLAN/IPVLAN       │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐   │
         │  │         overlay network (VXLAN)       │       │ Physical Network │   │
         │  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐        │       │ 192.168.1.0/24   │   │
         │  │  │ 🐝 Node 1 │    │ 🐝 Node 2 │        │       │ ┌──┐  ┌──┐      │   │
         │  │  │ ┌──┐┌──┐ │    │ ┌──┐┌──┐ │        │       │ │C1│  │C2│      │   │
         │  │  │ │C1││C2│ │◀═══▶│ │C3││C4│ │        │       │ │.5│  │.6│      │   │
         │  │  │ └──┘└──┘ │    │ └──┘└──┘ │        │       │ └──┘  └──┘      │   │
         │  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘        │       │  Gets REAL IPs!  │   │
         │  │  Multi-host communication! ✅         │       └──────────────────┘   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘                               │
         │                                                                          │
         │  [DRIVER SELECTION GUIDE]                                                │
         │  | Driver    | Isolation | Multi-Host | Performance | Use Case          │
         │  | bridge    | ✅ Good  | ❌ No      | 🟡 Medium   | Default, most apps │
         │  | host      | ❌ None  | ❌ No      | 🟢 Max      | High-perf, no isol.│
         │  | none      | ✅ Max   | ❌ No      | 🟢 N/A      | Security-sensitive │
         │  | overlay   | ✅ Good  | ✅ Yes     | 🟡 Medium   | Swarm clusters     │
         │  | macvlan   | ✅ Good  | ❌ No      | 🟢 High     │ Legacy apps, own IP│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Pick the right driver from the 5-driver diagram above in
            one sentence per use case.
         [TECH] Drivers are libnetwork plugins answering one question:
            what does the endpoint plug INTO? bridge → virtual switch +
            NAT; host → nothing, share the host's stack; none → loopback
            only; overlay → a VXLAN tunnel spanning hosts; macvlan/ipvlan
            → the physical NIC itself, with real LAN addresses.
         [ANALOGY] Housing options for the same tenant: bridge = apartment
            with a shared front desk (NAT), host = living in the lobby,
            none = a bunker, overlay = sister buildings with a private
            skybridge, macvlan = your own street address. All five are
            drawn above — same containers, different walls.
         🔬 easypj: everything in Parts 4-5 runs on user-defined BRIDGE
            networks; overlay arrives when easypj meets Swarm (Part 9);
            host mode fits only a latency-obsessed metrics agent.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker network ls` on a fresh engine. Verify:
            exactly bridge, host and none exist — the other drivers appear
            only when YOU create networks with them.
         📊 SUMMARY: bridge (default, NAT), host (no isolation), none
            (absolute isolation), overlay (multi-host), macvlan/ipvlan
            (real LAN presence) — the selection table above is the recap.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: --network host silently IGNORES -p — there is
            nothing to map when there is no NAT layer.

    13.3 Bridge Networks — The Default
         - How bridge networking works
         - docker network create my-bridge
         - DNS resolution: container name → IP
         - Port mapping: -p 8080:80

         [BRIDGE NETWORK — DEEP DIVE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                     🌉 BRIDGE NETWORK ARCHITECTURE                         │
         │                                                                          │
         │  EXTERNAL WORLD (Internet / LAN)                                         │
         │       │                                                                  │
         │       │  curl http://host:8080                                           │
         │       ▼                                                                  │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │                         HOST (192.168.1.100)                       │   │
         │  │                                                                   │   │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────────┐   │   │
         │  │  │  eth0 (physical) │         │       docker0 bridge          │   │   │
         │  │  │  192.168.1.100   │  NAT    │       172.17.0.1              │   │   │
         │  │  └────────┬─────────┘◀────────│                               │   │   │
         │  │           │                   │  -p 8080:80 → routes to C2:80│   │   │
         │  │           │                   │                               │   │   │
         │  │           │     ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐                │   │   │
         │  │           │     │             │             │                │   │   │
         │  │           │     ▼             ▼             ▼                │   │   │
         │  │           │  ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐    ┌──────┐               │   │   │
         │  │           │  │  C1  │    │  C2  │    │  C3  │               │   │   │
         │  │           │  │.0.2  │    │.0.3  │    │.0.4  │               │   │   │
         │  │           │  │ easypj│   │nginx │    │redis │               │   │   │
         │  │           │  │:8000 │    │:80   │    │:6379 │               │   │   │
         │  │           │  └──────┘    └──────┘    └──────┘               │   │   │
         │  │           │                                                  │   │   │
         │  │           │  ❌ C1 pings "nginx" by NAME → fails (no DNS here) │  │   │
         │  │           │  ✅ C1 pings 172.17.0.3 by IP → works (docker0 has │  │   │
         │  │           │     NO embedded DNS — that's only on user-defined  │  │   │
         │  │           │     bridges, see 13.4/13.5)                        │  │   │
         │  │           └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │   │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Trace curl http://host:8080 through the bridge diagram
            above hop by hop — and explain both DNS lines at its bottom.
         [TECH] A user-defined bridge = a Linux bridge + a subnet + the
            embedded DNS at 127.0.0.11. The DEFAULT docker0 bridge LACKS
            that DNS: containers on it resolve nothing by name. Inbound:
            eth0:8080 → DNAT (the -p rule) → 172.17.0.3:80. Outbound:
            container → bridge → MASQUERADE → eth0 → world.
         [ANALOGY] The diagram is an office building: eth0 is the street
            entrance, docker0 the internal corridor, -p 8080:80 the sign
            "deliveries for suite 8080 go to room .0.3". DNS is the
            receptionist — and the DEFAULT corridor has no receptionist.
         🔬 easypj: `docker network create easypj-net`, then run postgres
            and easypj-web on it — settings.py can say HOST="postgres"
            precisely because a USER-DEFINED bridge has DNS.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run two alpines on the default bridge and ping
            by name (fails); repeat on a created network (works). Verify:
            both results — this is the single most-asked Docker
            networking interview question.
         📊 SUMMARY: bridge = switch + NAT; user-defined bridges add DNS
            and isolation — ALWAYS prefer them over docker0.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: containers on DIFFERENT bridges cannot talk at all
            — isolation is per-bridge, not per-host.

    13.4 docker network Commands
         - docker network ls (list networks)
         - docker network create (create network)
         - docker network inspect (view network details)
         - docker network connect (connect container)
         - docker network disconnect (disconnect)
         - docker network rm (remove network)
         - docker network prune (remove unused)
         [TERMINAL: All network commands with output examples]

         🎯 GOAL: Drive the whole network lifecycle — create, wire,
            inspect, unwire, destroy — with the commands below.
         [TERMINAL: full lifecycle, drawn]
            $ docker network create --driver bridge easypj-net
            $ docker network ls                  # bridge/host/none + yours
            $ docker run -d --name web --network easypj-net nginx
            $ docker network connect easypj-net some-running-container
            $ docker network inspect easypj-net  # subnet, gateway,
                                                 # "Containers": {…}
            $ docker network disconnect easypj-net web
            $ docker network rm easypj-net       # refuses if still in use
            $ docker network prune               # sweep unused networks
         [TECH] connect/disconnect operate on RUNNING containers — a new
            veth (eth1) appears inside, live. inspect is the map: subnet,
            gateway, and every attached container's IP and MAC.
         [ANALOGY] Patch-panel operations: create = mount a new panel,
            connect = plug a cable in, inspect = read the port labels,
            prune = rip out panels with no cables left in them.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create easypj-net, connect a running alpine,
            run `ip addr` inside before and after. Verify: eth1 appears on
            connect and vanishes on disconnect — live rewiring, no
            restart.
         📊 SUMMARY: ls/create/inspect/connect/disconnect/rm/prune — the
            complete verb set; inspect is your debugging home base.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: network rm refuses while ANY container is still
            attached ("active endpoints") — disconnect or remove the
            tenants first.

    13.5 Container DNS & Service Discovery
         - Built-in DNS server (127.0.0.11)
         - Container name resolution
         - --network-alias for multiple names
         - --link (legacy, avoid)
         [DIAGRAM: DNS resolution flow between containers]


         🎯 GOAL: Explain who answers when a container asks "who is
            postgres?" — and what --network-alias adds on top.
         [DIAGRAM:flow — one DNS lookup between containers, drawn]
            django asks "postgres" ─▶ /etc/resolv.conf → 127.0.0.11
              ─▶ embedded DNS (dockerd) ─▶ known name on THIS network?
                 ├─ yes ─▶ answer 172.20.0.3 (container IP)
                 └─ no  ─▶ forward to the host's upstream DNS
         [TECH] Every container on a user-defined network gets resolv.conf
            pointing at 127.0.0.11; dockerd answers for container names,
            compose service names and every --network-alias; unknown names
            are forwarded upstream. Aliases allow many names → one
            container, or one name → many containers (poor man's LB).
         [ANALOGY] The embedded DNS is the office receptionist: colleagues
            by first name (container names), departments by role
            (aliases), and unknown visitors get pointed to the city
            directory (upstream DNS).
         🔬 easypj: `--network-alias db` on postgres lets legacy config
            using HOST="db" and new config using HOST="postgres" both
            resolve to the same container.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker exec easypj-web cat /etc/resolv.conf`,
            then run nslookup postgres from inside. Verify: nameserver
            127.0.0.11, and an answer inside the network's subnet.
         📊 SUMMARY: 127.0.0.11 = dockerd's per-network phonebook; aliases
            add extra names; unknown names go upstream.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: --link is legacy and effectively dead — it predates
            user-defined networks; a tutorial using it is an OLD tutorial.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 13 — minimum 10, covering: CNM nouns
     (sandbox/endpoint/network), veth pair tracing host↔container, the 5
     drivers & selection table, default docker0 vs user-defined bridge
     DNS, -p DNAT inbound vs MASQUERADE outbound, the network command
     lifecycle, live connect/disconnect, 127.0.0.11 resolution path,
     --network-alias uses, why --link is legacy]

Chapter 14: Networking easypj Containers
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Theory becomes architecture here — Chapter
    13's bridges and DNS get applied to easypj on purpose: a backend
    network the internet can never reach, a frontend network nginx guards,
    and a database with NO published port. Problem solved here: "my stack
    works, but every service is one -p flag away from the public internet."

    14.1 Network Design for easypj Stack
         - Backend network: Django + PostgreSQL + Redis
         - Frontend network: Nginx + Django
         - Database isolation: PostgreSQL NOT exposed to host
         [ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM: Two networks with services placed]

         🎯 GOAL: Place easypj's four services on two networks so the
            blast radius of a compromised nginx is one hop — not the DB.
         [DIAGRAM:architecture — the two-network design, drawn]
            internet ──▶ host:80
                          │
            ┌─ frontend ──▼────────────────────────┐
            │  [nginx] ───────▶ [django]           │
            └───────────────────────┬──────────────┘
            ┌─ backend ─────────────┼──────────────┐
            │  [postgres] ◀── [django] ──▶ [redis] │
            │  (no ports:)             (no ports:) │
            └──────────────────────────────────────┘
         [TECH] django is dual-homed (frontend + backend); nginx sees ONLY
            django; postgres/redis sit backend-only with zero published
            ports. Add internal: true to backend and its members also lose
            the NAT route out — no ingress, no egress.
         [ANALOGY] A bank branch: frontend is the customer hall, django
            the teller window, backend the vault corridor. The teller
            works both zones; customers never enter the corridor; the
            vault has no street door AT ALL (no ports:).
         🔬 easypj: JobPost rows and User password hashes — everything
            valuable lives in postgres, and postgres is reachable ONLY as
            postgres:5432 from backend members. No -p, no exposure.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: from nginx, try `wget -qO- postgres:5432`;
            repeat from django. Verify: nginx cannot even RESOLVE the name
            (different network); django connects.
         📊 SUMMARY: two networks, one dual-homed app tier, zero published
            DB ports — this diagram is the reference posture for Part 7.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "it's just dev" is how 5432 ends up on 0.0.0.0 —
            design the isolation BEFORE the first up, not after the first
            breach.

    14.2 Creating Custom Networks for easypj
         - docker network create easypj-backend
         - docker network create easypj-frontend
         - docker network inspect easypj-backend
         [TERMINAL: Network creation and inspection]

         🎯 GOAL: Create both easypj networks with explicit subnets and
            read an inspect output like a map.
         [TERMINAL: create + inspect, drawn]
            $ docker network create easypj-frontend
            $ docker network create --subnet 172.28.0.0/24 \
                --gateway 172.28.0.1 easypj-backend
            $ docker network inspect easypj-backend
                "Driver": "bridge",
                "Subnet": "172.28.0.0/24",
                "Containers": {}        # empty — nothing attached yet
         [TECH] Without --subnet, dockerd allocates the next free pool
            from default-address-pools (2.7). Explicit subnets buy stable
            firewall rules and no collisions with the office VPN's ranges.
         [ANALOGY] Naming the corridors before moving furniture: create
            networks first, then attach containers — better than letting
            every docker run improvise its own hallway.
         🔬 easypj: two creates, ten seconds — the entire architecture of
            14.1 now EXISTS, waiting for tenants.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create both networks, then `docker network ls`
            and inspect each. Verify: driver bridge, distinct subnets,
            empty Containers — and you can say which subnet is backend.
         📊 SUMMARY: create (optionally --subnet), inspect to confirm;
            networks are cheap — name them like you mean it.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a subnet overlapping your VPN or LAN silently
            blackholes traffic — the #1 "Docker broke my VPN" cause; pick
            ranges like 172.28.x.x deliberately.

    14.3 Connecting Containers to Networks
         - docker run --network easypj-backend ...
         - docker network connect easypj-frontend django-container
         - Verify: docker exec django ping postgres
         [TERMINAL: Connection + verification workflow]

         🎯 GOAL: Attach each easypj container to its planned network(s) —
            at run time and live — and prove the wiring in both directions.
         [TERMINAL: wire the stack by hand, drawn]
            $ docker run -d --name postgres --network easypj-backend \
                -e POSTGRES_DB=easypj_db -e POSTGRES_USER=easypj \
                -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=pg-pass postgres:15
            $ docker run -d --name django --network easypj-backend \
                --env-file .env easypj:v1
            $ docker network connect easypj-frontend django  # 2nd network!
            $ docker run -d --name nginx --network easypj-frontend \
                -p 80:80 nginx:alpine
            $ docker exec django ping -c1 postgres    # backend    ✅
            $ docker exec nginx  ping -c1 django      # frontend   ✅
            $ docker exec nginx  ping -c1 postgres    # FAILS — by design
         [TECH] --network at run time attaches the FIRST network; further
            ones need docker network connect (live — adds eth1). The
            failing third ping is the design working: nginx and postgres
            share no network, so not even DNS crosses.
         [ANALOGY] Issuing keycards: django gets two (both corridors),
            nginx one, postgres one — and the ping test is walking each
            corridor rattling every door.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the block above. Verify: two pings answer;
            the third says "bad address" — name resolution itself refused.
         📊 SUMMARY: --network at birth, network connect for the second
            home; verify with exec + ping in the directions you expect AND
            the one you don't.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: docker run accepts only ONE --network flag — the
            "django on both" step MUST be a separate connect (Compose does
            this for you; the raw CLI makes you feel it).

    14.4 Port Publishing & Exposure
         - EXPOSE in Dockerfile (documentation only!)
         - -p (publish) in docker run (actual mapping)
         - Short syntax: -p 8000:8000
         - Long syntax in compose: protocol, mode
         [TABLE: Port mapping scenarios with examples]

         🎯 GOAL: Stop confusing EXPOSE with -p forever, and pick the
            right publish syntax per scenario.
         [TECH] EXPOSE in a Dockerfile is pure METADATA — documentation
            for humans and input for -P. Only -p/--publish (or compose
            ports:) creates the DNAT rule that makes a container reachable
            from outside (full mechanics: 15.6).
         [TABLE: publish scenarios — need | flag | result]
            laptop dev           | -p 8000:8000           | host:8000 → app
            loopback only        | -p 127.0.0.1:8000:8000 | not LAN-visible
            random host port     | -p 8000                | ephemeral high
            all EXPOSEd ports    | -P                     | quick demos
            container→container  | (no flag at all)       | DNS + port
            UDP service          | -p 514:514/udp         | proto explicit
         [ANALOGY] EXPOSE is the "doorbell here" sticker printed on the
            blueprint; -p actually wires the doorbell to the street. A
            sticker never rang anything.
         🔬 easypj: nginx gets -p 80:80 — the ONLY publish in the stack;
            django, postgres and redis rely on container-to-container DNS:
            row five of the table.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run easypj:v1 (EXPOSE 8000) without -p; curl
            localhost:8000 (fails), then docker exec nginx curl
            django:8000 (works). Verify: EXPOSE published exactly nothing.
         📊 SUMMARY: EXPOSE documents, -p publishes; container-to-
            container needs neither — DNS name + container port.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: -p 8000:8000 binds 0.0.0.0 — your "local dev" is
            LAN-visible at the coffee shop; the 127.0.0.1: prefix is one
            keystroke of paranoia that pays for itself.

    14.5 Service Discovery in Compose
         - Compose creates a default network automatically
         - Services reachable by service name (DNS)
         - Example: Django connects to postgres://postgres:5432/db
         - Custom networks in Compose
         [CODE: compose.yaml with network configuration]


         🎯 GOAL: Encode the ENTIRE 14.1 design — both networks, all
            placements — as ten lines of compose.yaml.
         [CODE: networks section of easypj's compose.yaml — drawn]
            services:
              nginx:    { networks: [frontend], ports: ["80:80"] }
              django:   { networks: [frontend, backend] }
              postgres: { networks: [backend] }
              redis:    { networks: [backend] }
            networks:
              frontend: {}
              backend:
                internal: true    # no NAT out — the vault corridor
         [TECH] Compose creates real networks named <project>_frontend /
            <project>_backend and registers every service in the embedded
            DNS of each network it joins. DATABASE_URL uses the service
            name: postgres://easypj:pg-pass@postgres:5432/easypj_db.
         [ANALOGY] 14.3 wired the stack by hand, cable by cable; this file
            is the same wiring as a printed patch-panel chart — applied
            with one `up`, reproducible forever.
         🔬 easypj: the manual demo (14.3) and this yaml produce the SAME
            three ping results — nginx→django yes, django→postgres yes,
            nginx→postgres refused.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker compose up -d`, then re-run 14.3's
            three pings via compose exec. Verify: identical outcomes with
            zero manual connects — the file did the wiring.
         📊 SUMMARY: service name = one DNS record per joined network;
            networks: is the placement column of your architecture diagram.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: internal: true also blocks OUTBOUND traffic — a
            backend-internal django could not pip install or call a
            payments API; that is why django, not postgres, is dual-homed.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 14 — minimum 10, covering: two-network design
     & the dual-homed app tier, internal: true (ingress AND egress),
     explicit --subnet planning vs VPN collisions, run --network vs live
     network connect, cross-network DNS refusal, EXPOSE vs -p, loopback
     binding 127.0.0.1:8000, -P behavior, compose networks: placement,
     verifying isolation with exec + ping]

Chapter 15: Advanced Docker Networking
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: The last 10% of Docker networking causes 90%
    of the 2 a.m. pages: host-mode surprises, containers that need real
    LAN IPs, multi-host overlays, and "the app is fine but the network
    isn't" mysteries. This chapter arms you with the exotic drivers, a
    proper toolbox (netshoot), and the iptables truth behind -p. Problem
    solved here: "I can wire containers, but I can't DEBUG the wiring."

    15.1 Host Networking
         - When to use --network host (maximum performance)
         - When NOT to use (port conflicts, reduced isolation)
         - Comparison: bridge vs host performance
         [TABLE: Bridge vs Host networking trade-offs]

         🎯 GOAL: Decide in one breath when --network host is the right
            tool and when it is a foot-gun.
         [TABLE: bridge vs host — dimension | bridge | host]
            isolation      | own netns, own IP     | NONE — host's stack
            port mapping   | -p required (NAT)     | -p IGNORED silently
            performance    | NAT + veth overhead   | native wire speed
            port conflicts | impossible (mapped)   | app fights the host
            multi-instance | many per host port    | one owner per port
            DNS names      | embedded 127.0.0.11   | host's resolv.conf
         [TECH] --network host deletes the sandbox boundary: the container
            shares the host's interfaces, routes and port table. gunicorn
            binding :8000 binds the HOST's :8000 — no veth, no bridge, no
            DNAT hop (which is exactly where the performance win is).
         [ANALOGY] Bridge = your own hotel room with mail forwarding; host
            = sleeping in the lobby. Fastest path to the front door, zero
            privacy, and only ONE guest can claim the sofa.
         🔬 easypj: never for django or postgres; plausible for a
            node-exporter-style metrics agent that must see the host's
            real interfaces to be useful at all.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker run --rm --network host alpine ip
            addr`, then compare with the host's own ip addr. Verify:
            IDENTICAL interface lists — the isolation is simply gone.
         📊 SUMMARY: host mode trades ALL network isolation for native
            speed and real-interface visibility; rare, deliberate,
            Linux-only.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: on Docker Desktop (Mac/Windows) host mode attaches
            to the hidden VM's network, not your laptop's — "works only on
            the Linux server" is the tell.

    15.2 Macvlan / IPvlan Networking
         - Macvlan: Container gets its own MAC address
         - IPvlan: Container shares MAC but has own IP
         - Use case: Legacy apps that need physical network presence
         [DIAGRAM: Macvlan vs IPvlan architecture]

         🎯 GOAL: Give a container a REAL address on the office LAN — and
            choose macvlan vs ipvlan by one criterion: MAC count.
         [DIAGRAM:architecture — macvlan vs ipvlan, drawn]
            physical LAN 192.168.1.0/24 ── switch ── router
                               │
                           host eth0
                  ┌────────────┴────────────┐
              MACVLAN sub-ifs          IPVLAN L2 sub-ifs
              C1 .50  MAC aa:aa        C1 .50 ┐ share the
              C2 .51  MAC bb:bb        C2 .51 ┘ host's MAC
              (a MAC per container:    (one MAC on the wire:
               switch sees them all)    port-security friendly)
         [TECH] Both drivers bond container sub-interfaces to the parent
            NIC (-o parent=eth0), skipping bridge and NAT entirely — DHCP
            servers, printers and legacy scanners see a first-class LAN
            citizen. macvlan mints one MAC per container; ipvlan reuses
            the host's MAC and differs only at L3.
         [ANALOGY] macvlan = each container gets its own house on the
            street (own mailbox, own nameplate); ipvlan = apartments in
            the host's building — one street door (MAC), many unit
            numbers (IPs).
         🔬 easypj: not for the web stack — but a legacy license server or
            an IoT integration that must ARP on the real LAN is the
            genuine use case.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create a macvlan network with your LAN's
            subnet and -o parent=eth0, run alpine with --ip, then ping it
            from ANOTHER machine. Verify: an outside device reaches a
            container with no -p anywhere in sight.
         📊 SUMMARY: real-LAN presence without NAT; macvlan = MAC per
            container, ipvlan = shared MAC — pick ipvlan when switches or
            wifi filter MACs.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the HOST cannot reach its own macvlan containers
            (kernel hairpin restriction) — plan a macvlan shim interface
            or accept host-blindness.

    15.3 Overlay Networking (Swarm)
         - Multi-host container communication
         - VXLAN tunneling
         - Encryption (--opt encrypted)
         [DIAGRAM: Overlay network across 3 Swarm nodes]

         🎯 GOAL: Explain how two containers on DIFFERENT machines share
            one subnet — and what --opt encrypted changes.
         [DIAGRAM:architecture — one overlay, three nodes, drawn]
            overlay easypj-net 10.0.9.0/24 (VXLAN)
            ┌── node1 ───┐   ┌── node2 ───┐   ┌── node3 ───┐
            │ [django]   │   │ [django]   │   │ [postgres] │
            │  10.0.9.3  │   │  10.0.9.4  │   │  10.0.9.5  │
            └────╥───────┘   └────╥───────┘   └────╥───────┘
                 ╚═══ UDP 4789 ═══╩═ VXLAN tunnels ╝
                 (containers' L2 frames ride inside UDP packets)
         [TECH] VXLAN wraps container Ethernet frames in UDP/4789 packets
            between nodes; each node's kernel unwraps and delivers as if
            one switch spanned the cluster. Needs Swarm mode (Part 9) for
            the control plane; --opt encrypted adds IPsec between nodes.
         [ANALOGY] Sister office buildings with a pneumatic tube system:
            interoffice envelopes (frames) ride inside courier capsules
            (UDP packets); staff just write room numbers (10.0.9.x) and
            never see the tubes.
         🔬 easypj: on a 3-node Swarm, django on node1 dials postgres:5432
            exactly as it did in Compose — the connection string never
            learns the database moved machines.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: docker swarm init, join a second node, create
            an attachable overlay, run alpine on each, ping across.
            Verify: tcpdump on eth0 shows UDP 4789 while the ping runs.
         📊 SUMMARY: overlay = one virtual switch across hosts via VXLAN;
            encrypt with --opt encrypted; deep dive in Part 9, CNI
            equivalents in [KUBERNETES GUIDE].
            ⚠️ Gotcha: overlays need 2377/tcp, 7946/tcp+udp and 4789/udp
            OPEN between nodes — a cloud security group eats one and
            "overlay is broken" tickets follow.

    15.4 Network Troubleshooting Tools
         - docker run --rm alpine ping <target>
         - docker run --rm nicolaka/netshoot (swiss army knife)
         - docker run --rm alpine nslookup <hostname>
         - docker run --rm curlimages/curl <url>
         - docker network inspect (see connected containers)
         [PRACTICAL: Debugging connectivity between easypj containers]

         🎯 GOAL: Debug "django can't reach postgres" with a fixed 4-rung
            ladder instead of random restarts.
         [TERMINAL: the debugging ladder, drawn]
            # 1. Is the target on the network I think it is?
            $ docker network inspect easypj-backend | grep -A3 Name
            # 2. Does DNS resolve from the source's viewpoint?
            $ docker run --rm --network easypj-backend alpine \
                nslookup postgres
            # 3. Is the PORT open (not just the host alive)?
            $ docker run --rm --network easypj-backend \
                nicolaka/netshoot nc -zv postgres 5432
            # 4. Full toolbox INSIDE the source's own netns:
            $ docker run --rm -it --network container:django \
                nicolaka/netshoot        # tcpdump, dig, ss, curl, iperf
         [TECH] Rung 4's --network container:django joins django's OWN
            network namespace — you see its eth0, its resolv.conf, its
            routes, without installing one tool into the app image.
         [ANALOGY] A plumber's ladder: check the house is on the street
            (inspect), the address resolves (nslookup), the tap opens
            (nc -zv), then put your ear on THIS house's pipes (netshoot
            inside the same netns).
         🔬 easypj: classic finding — rung 2 fails because django ran with
            --network easypj-frontend only; one `docker network connect
            easypj-backend django` later, rungs 2-4 go green.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: break the stack on purpose (disconnect django
            from backend), walk the ladder, fix, re-walk. Verify: you can
            name WHICH rung failed and why — that sentence is the skill.
         📊 SUMMARY: inspect → nslookup → nc -zv → netshoot-in-netns; four
            rungs diagnose 95% of container connectivity tickets.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a NEW container on the same network is NOT the same
            as the source's netns — wrong resolv.conf or per-container
            rules only show up via --network container:<name>.

    15.5 Network Security Basics
         - Principle of least privilege: only expose what's needed
         - Internal-only networks (no host port mapping)
         - Docker network encryption (Swarm)
         - Network policies (with K8s, not native Docker)
         [BEST PRACTICE CHECKLIST: 10 network security rules]

         🎯 GOAL: Ship a default-deny network posture for easypj — every
            open path a decision, never an accident.
         [CHECKLIST: 10 network security rules — drawn]
            □ 1  Publish NOTHING but the edge (nginx :80/:443)
            □ 2  DB/cache on internal: true networks — no NAT either way
            □ 3  Bind dev-only ports to 127.0.0.1, never 0.0.0.0
            □ 4  One network per trust zone; dual-home only the app tier
            □ 5  Never --network host for internet-facing services
            □ 6  Encrypt overlays that cross datacenters (--opt encrypted)
            □ 7  icc=false on the default bridge (2.7) — or don't use it
            □ 8  Explicit subnets — auditable firewall rules, no VPN wars
            □ 9  Re-scan `docker ps` for 0.0.0.0 leaks after every change
            □ 10 Know -p bypasses ufw (15.6) — audit the DOCKER chain too
         [TECH] Docker networking is default-ALLOW inside a network and
            default-DENY across networks — segmentation IS the firewall.
            Rules 1-4 are pure topology; 5-10 are the host-level escape
            hatches people forget they opened.
         [ANALOGY] Castle design: one gate (nginx), an inner keep with no
            outside door (internal: true), separate courtyards per trust
            zone — and rule 10 is checking the tunnel the drawbridge
            keeper (iptables) dug without telling the guards (ufw).
         🔬 easypj: the 14.1 design already scores rules 1, 2 and 4;
            adding 127.0.0.1 binds for dev tools and explicit subnets
            completes the posture in one afternoon.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run
            docker ps --format '{{.Names}} {{.Ports}}' on your stack.
            Verify: exactly ONE 0.0.0.0 entry (nginx) — any other line is
            a checklist violation with your name on it.
         📊 SUMMARY: segment first, publish least, bind narrow, audit the
            iptables reality; NetworkPolicies extend this — see
            [KUBERNETES GUIDE].
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Docker recreates its iptables rules on daemon
            restart — hand-edited firewall "fixes" silently vanish; encode
            policy in networks and daemon.json, not ad-hoc iptables.

    15.6 How -p Really Works — NAT, iptables & the Published-Port Path
         - Packet path: eth0 → PREROUTING → DOCKER chain → DNAT → container
         - docker-proxy: the userland fallback (localhost + hairpin cases)
         - Binding scopes: -p 127.0.0.1:5432:5432 vs -p 5432:5432 (0.0.0.0)
         - Why ufw/firewalld rules never see published-port traffic
         - Reading the truth: iptables -t nat -L DOCKER -n

         🎯 GOAL: Trace one inbound packet from the wire to gunicorn and
            explain why your ufw "deny 8000" rule never fired.
         [DIAGRAM:flow — the published-port path, drawn]
            client:54321 ─▶ host eth0 :8000
              ─▶ nat PREROUTING ─▶ DOCKER chain
                   match tcp dpt:8000 → DNAT to 172.18.0.4:8000
              ─▶ FORWARD (DOCKER-USER first, then DOCKER)
              ─▶ bridge ─▶ veth ─▶ container eth0 ─▶ gunicorn :8000
            reply: conntrack rewrites the source back — the client
            never sees 172.18.0.4
         [TECH] -p writes iptables rules, not proxies: a nat-table DNAT in
            the DOCKER chain rewrites the destination BEFORE routing.
            docker-proxy (one process per published port) survives only as
            a userland fallback for localhost and hairpin traffic. ufw's
            INPUT rules never match because DNATed traffic traverses
            FORWARD, not INPUT — the one hook Docker guarantees you is the
            DOCKER-USER chain.
         [ANALOGY] The mailroom rewrites the envelope's address (DNAT) at
            the loading dock BEFORE the receptionist (INPUT/ufw) ever sees
            it — complaining to the receptionist about deliveries she
            never handles is the whole "ufw doesn't block Docker" bug.
         🔬 easypj: -p 5432:5432 "just to debug" DNATs the whole internet
            to PostgreSQL even with ufw deny 5432 active. The fix is
            binding scope: -p 127.0.0.1:5432:5432 makes the DNAT rule
            itself match only loopback destinations.
         [TERMINAL: see it with your own eyes, drawn]
            $ sudo iptables -t nat -L DOCKER -n | grep 5432
              DNAT tcp -- 0.0.0.0/0 ... dpt:5432 to:172.18.0.3:5432
            $ ps aux | grep docker-proxy    # one per published port
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: publish nginx as -p 8080:80, add ufw deny
            8080, curl from another machine (still answers!); re-run with
            -p 127.0.0.1:8080:80. Verify: LAN curl now refused, localhost
            fine — and the iptables line shows the 127.0.0.1 match.
         📊 SUMMARY: -p = kernel DNAT in the DOCKER chain + conntrack
            replies; docker-proxy is a niche fallback; firewall Docker in
            DOCKER-USER (or bind narrow), never in INPUT.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: -p with no address means 0.0.0.0 AND [::] — the
            port answers on every interface the host owns, cloud-public
            IP included.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 15 — minimum 10, covering: host-mode
     trade-offs & the Desktop caveat, -p ignored under host mode, macvlan
     vs ipvlan MAC criterion, host-to-macvlan blindness, VXLAN mechanics +
     required ports, --opt encrypted, the 4-rung debugging ladder,
     netshoot inside another container's netns, the 10-rule checklist,
     the DNAT path & ufw bypass, 127.0.0.1 vs 0.0.0.0 binds, the
     DOCKER-USER chain]


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 6: DOCKER STORAGE — VOLUMES & PERSISTENT DATA                        🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 6:
      What runs where?    → the container; the DATA lives in a named volume.
      What talks to what? → container mount point → volume/bind/tmpfs → disk.
      What breaks first?  → `docker rm -v` — one flag deletes volumes too.

Chapter 16: Docker Storage Fundamentals
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Every container you have run so far dies with
    its data intact only by accident — kill it and whatever it wrote is
    gone. Chapter 16 replaces that accident with a deliberate choice:
    volumes, bind mounts, and tmpfs, each solving a different persistence
    problem. Skip this chapter and easypj's PostgreSQL data, and every
    user upload, evaporates on the next `docker compose down`.

    16.1 The Ephemeral Container Problem
         - Containers are ephemeral: data dies with the container
         - Problem: PostgreSQL data must survive container restarts
         - Problem: User uploads in easypj must persist

         [EPHEMERAL CONTAINER vs PERSISTENT VOLUME]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │              💾 THE EPHEMERAL CONTAINER PROBLEM                            │
         │                                                                          │
         │  WITHOUT VOLUMES (Data dies with container):                             │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐                     │
         │  │ Container: postgres │     │ Container: DELETED  │                     │
         │  │ ┌─────────────────┐ │     │                     │                     │
         │  │ │ /var/lib/       │ │     │  💀 DB GONE!        │                     │
         │  │ │   postgresql/   │ │ ──▶ │  💀 Users GONE!     │                     │
         │  │ │   data/         │ │ rm  │  💀 Orders GONE!    │                     │
         │  │ │   (20 GB DB)    │ │     │  💀 Everything!     │                     │
         │  │ └─────────────────┘ │     └─────────────────────┘                     │
         │  └─────────────────────┘                                                │
         │                                                                          │
         │  WITH VOLUMES (Data survives container):                                 │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────┐                │
         │  │ Container: postgres │         │ Container: DELETED  │                │
         │  │ ┌─────────────────┐ │         │                     │                │
         │  │ │ /var/lib/       │ │         │  Container gone,    │                │
         │  │ │   postgresql/   │ │    ──▶  │  but...             │                │
         │  │ │   data/         │ │   rm    │                     │                │
         │  │ └───────┬─────────┘ │         └─────────────────────┘                │
         │  └─────────┼───────────┘                                               │
         │            │ mount                                                      │
         │            ▼                                                            │
         │  ┌─────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────┐                │
         │  │ 📦 VOLUME: pg_data  │────────▶│ 📦 VOLUME: pg_data  │                │
         │  │ /var/lib/docker/    │  new    │ STILL HERE! ✅      │                │
         │  │ volumes/pg_data/    │  cont.  │ Mount to new        │                │
         │  │ (20 GB, persistent) │         │ container!          │                │
         │  └─────────────────────┘         └─────────────────────┘                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: State exactly what dies with a container and what a
            volume protects — before you ever lose real easypj data.
         [TECH] A container's writable layer lives inside the container's
            storage-driver directory; `docker rm` deletes that layer along
            with everything the process wrote. The WITHOUT VOLUMES half of
            the diagram above IS this deletion. A volume is a separate,
            named object Docker manages outside that layer — mounting one
            over /var/lib/postgresql/data routes writes there instead, as
            the WITH VOLUMES half of the diagram shows.
         [ANALOGY] A hotel room vs a safety-deposit box: whatever you leave
            in the room (writable layer) gets cleared at checkout (rm); the
            box (volume) sits in the vault regardless of which room you
            book next — same building, independent lifecycle.
         🔬 easypj: `docker run postgres` with no -v, then `docker rm -f`,
            then `docker volume ls` — nothing there; the 20 GB dataset the
            diagram warns about was never anything but container-local.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run postgres WITHOUT a volume, INSERT one row,
            `docker rm -f` it, start a fresh postgres container. Verify:
            the row is gone — you just reproduced the top half of the
            diagram with your own data.
         📊 SUMMARY: containers are disposable compute; volumes are where
            anything that must outlive a container has to live.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker compose down` (no -v) keeps named volumes,
            but `docker compose down -v` deletes them — one flag away from
            the WITHOUT VOLUMES scenario in production.

    16.2 Storage Types Overview
         - Volumes: Managed by Docker (/var/lib/docker/volumes/)
         - Bind mounts: Any host directory mounted into container
         - tmpfs mounts: In-memory only (Linux), never on disk
         - Named pipes (npipe): Windows only

         [4 STORAGE TYPES — VISUAL COMPARISON]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                    💾 DOCKER STORAGE MOUNT TYPES                           │
         │                                                                          │
         │  1. VOLUME (Recommended)           2. BIND MOUNT                         │
         │  ┌──────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────┐              │
         │  │ Docker manages:      │          │ You manage:          │              │
         │  │ ┌──────────────────┐ │          │ ┌──────────────────┐ │              │
         │  │ │ Container        │ │          │ │ Container        │ │              │
         │  │ │ /app/data ─────┐ │ │          │ │ /app/data ─────┐ │ │              │
         │  │ └────────────────┼─┘ │          │ └────────────────┼─┘ │              │
         │  │                  │   │          │                  │   │              │
         │  │  ┌───────────────▼─┐ │          │  ┌───────────────▼─┐ │              │
         │  │  │ 📦 my-volume    │ │          │  │ 📁 /home/me/    │ │              │
         │  │  │ /var/lib/docker │ │          │  │    data/        │ │              │
         │  │  │ /volumes/...    │ │          │  │ (any host path) │ │              │
         │  │  └────────────────┘ │          │  └────────────────┘ │              │
         │  │  ✅ Managed backup  │          │  ⚠️ Permissions issues│              │
         │  │  ✅ Driver plugins  │          │  ✅ Dev hot-reload   │              │
         │  └──────────────────────┘          └──────────────────────┘              │
         │                                                                          │
         │  3. tmpfs (RAM disk)               4. npipe (Windows only)               │
         │  ┌──────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────┐              │
         │  │ ┌──────────────────┐ │          │ Windows named pipes  │              │
         │  │ │ Container        │ │          │ for Docker API comm. │              │
         │  │ │ /tmp ───┐       │ │          │ (rarely used)        │              │
         │  │ └─────────┼───────┘ │          └──────────────────────┘              │
         │  │           │         │                                                 │
         │  │  ┌────────▼───────┐ │          [STORAGE TYPE DECISION MATRIX]         │
         │  │  │ 🧠 RAM (tmpfs) │ │          | Type   |Persists|Speed  |Use Case   │
         │  │  │ NEVER on disk! │ │          | Volume |✅ Yes |Fast   |DB data,    │
         │  │  │ Fast, volatile │ │          |        |       |       |uploads     │
         │  │  └────────────────┘ │          | Bind   |✅ Yes |Fast   |Dev reload, │
         │  │  ✅ Secrets/temp    │          |        |       |       |config files│
         │  └──────────────────────┘          | tmpfs  |❌ No  |🚀Fastest|Secrets,  │
         │                                   |        |       |       |temp files │
         │                                   | npipe  |N/A   |N/A    |Windows IPC│
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Pick the correct one of Docker's four mount types for a
            given easypj need, on sight.
         [TECH] The 4 STORAGE TYPES diagram above draws the real split:
            volumes (Docker-managed, under /var/lib/docker/volumes/), bind
            mounts (you pick the host path), tmpfs (RAM only, Linux),
            npipe (Windows IPC, not a data store). Only the first three
            ever hold application data; npipe never does.
         [ANALOGY] Renting a Docker-managed storage unit (volume) vs
            using your own garage (bind mount) vs a whiteboard wiped
            nightly (tmpfs) — each box in the diagram above is one of
            those three real-world containers for your stuff.
         [TABLE: pick the mount — need | type]
            Survive restart, don't care where on disk | volume
            Edit code live from your host editor        | bind mount
            Secret that must never touch disk           | tmpfs
         🔬 easypj: postgres data → volume; `./app:/app` while coding →
            bind mount; a decrypted API key for one request → tmpfs.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: for each easypj directory (media/, static/,
            your working copy, a decrypted secret), name its mount type
            using ONLY the decision table above. Verify against 17.1-17.5.
         📊 SUMMARY: four mount types, three real choices — volume, bind,
            tmpfs — decided by durability and disk-visibility needs.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: bind mounts inherit host file permissions/UID
            exactly — the #1 cause of "permission denied" in a container.

    16.3 Docker Volumes — The Recommended Way
         - What are volumes? (Docker-managed storage)
         - docker volume create my-volume
         - docker run -v my-volume:/data ...
         - Volumes survive container deletion
         - Volume drivers (local, nfs, cloud-specific)

         [VOLUME LIFECYCLE — INDEPENDENT OF CONTAINERS]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                     🔄 VOLUME LIFECYCLE                                   │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌─────────┐   create    ┌─────────┐   mount    ┌─────────┐            │
         │  │ Nothing │────────────▶│ 📦Volume│──────────▶│Container│            │
         │  │         │             │ (empty) │            │ uses it │            │
         │  └─────────┘             └────┬────┘            └────┬────┘            │
         │                               │                      │                  │
         │                               │   Container writes   │                  │
         │                               │◄─────────────────────┘                  │
         │                               │                                         │
         │                               │   Container deleted                     │
         │                               │   ┌─────────┐                          │
         │                               │   │Container│ 💀                       │
         │                               │   │ DELETED │                          │
         │                               │   └─────────┘                          │
         │                               │                                         │
         │                               ▼                                         │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐                          │
         │  │ 📦 Volume STILL EXISTS with all data!   │                          │
         │  │ docker volume ls → my-volume            │                          │
         │  │ docker run -v my-volume:/data new-img   │◀── Remount!             │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────┘                          │
         │                                                                          │
         │  💡 KEY INSIGHT: Volume lifecycle is COMPLETELY separate from container! │
         │     docker volume rm my-volume ← ONLY way to delete the volume.         │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Explain why a volume's lifecycle is independent of every
            container that ever mounts it.
         [TECH] The VOLUME LIFECYCLE diagram above shows the real sequence:
            `docker volume create` makes an empty object with no owning
            container; `-v name:/path` mounts it; deleting the container
            leaves the volume untouched; only `docker volume rm` removes
            it. Volumes can also be created implicitly by the first `-v`.
         [ANALOGY] A shared storage locker with its own key, not tied to
            any one tenant: tenants (containers) move in and out, the
            locker (volume) and its contents outlast every one of them —
            exactly the "STILL EXISTS" box the diagram ends on.
         🔬 easypj: `docker volume create pgdata`, mount it into postgres,
            `docker rm -f` the container, `docker run -v pgdata:/var/lib/
            postgresql/data postgres` again — same rows, new container.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker volume create test`, run alpine with
            `-v test:/data`, write a file, remove the container, run a
            SECOND alpine with the same volume. Verify: `cat /data/file`
            in container #2 prints what container #1 wrote.
         📊 SUMMARY: volumes are first-class Docker objects that outlive
            containers by design — remount, don't recreate, to keep data.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: an anonymous volume (`-v /data`, no name) is just as
            persistent but nearly impossible to find later — always name
            volumes you intend to keep.

    16.4 docker volume Commands
         - docker volume ls (list volumes)
         - docker volume create (create)
         - docker volume inspect (details + mountpoint)
         - docker volume rm (remove)
         - docker volume prune (remove unused)
         [TERMINAL: All volume commands with output examples]


         🎯 GOAL: Run the five `docker volume` subcommands from memory and
            read their output correctly.
         [TERMINAL: the volume command set, drawn]
            $ docker volume create pgdata
            pgdata
            $ docker volume ls
            DRIVER    VOLUME NAME
            local     pgdata
            $ docker volume inspect pgdata
            [{"Mountpoint": "/var/lib/docker/volumes/pgdata/_data", ...}]
            $ docker volume rm pgdata
            pgdata
            $ docker volume prune
            WARNING! This will remove all unused local volumes.
            Total reclaimed space: 0B
         [TECH] `inspect` is the command that matters most: it prints the
            real Mountpoint on the host — the ONE place you can `ls` a
            volume's contents without going through a container at all.
         🔬 easypj: `docker volume inspect pgdata` on the host, then
            `sudo ls` that Mountpoint to confirm PostgreSQL's real files
            exist even while every easypj container is stopped.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create, inspect, and remove a throwaway volume;
            note the Mountpoint path each time. Verify: `docker volume ls`
            returns empty after `rm` — no orphaned entry left behind.
         📊 SUMMARY: create/ls/inspect/rm/prune cover the full volume
            lifecycle from the CLI; inspect is your window into the host.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker volume prune` deletes EVERY unused volume,
            not just Docker's guesses — a stopped-but-not-removed backup
            container's volume is safe, but nothing else is.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 16 — minimum 10, covering: what survives
     `docker rm`, the WITHOUT/WITH VOLUMES diagram, volumes vs bind
     mounts vs tmpfs vs npipe, the mount-type decision table, bind-mount
     permission inheritance, why volume lifecycle is independent of
     containers, anonymous vs named volumes, `docker volume create` vs
     implicit creation via `-v`, reading `docker volume inspect`
     Mountpoint output, and the blast radius of `docker volume prune`]

Chapter 17: Persistent Data for easypj
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapter 16 gave you three storage tools;
    this chapter decides which easypj directory gets which one, and
    proves each choice under real failure. Skip it and the result stays
    vague instead of exact: postgres_data on the DB, scoped media and
    static volumes, dev-only bind mounts that never reach production,
    and a backup you have actually restored at least once.

    17.1 What Needs to Persist in easypj?
         - PostgreSQL data directory (/var/lib/postgresql/data)
         - Django media files (user uploads)
         - Django static files (collected by collectstatic)
         - Application logs
         [DATA MAP: Which directories need persistence]

         🎯 GOAL: Name the four easypj data categories and the ONE
            question that decides each one's mount type.
         [TABLE: easypj data map — data | must persist? | where]
            PostgreSQL data dir  | yes, always | named volume (17.2)
            Django media uploads | yes, always | named volume (17.3)
            Django static files  | rebuildable | named volume (17.3)
            Application logs     | usually     | volume or log driver
         [TECH] "Must persist" means: would losing it on the next
            `docker rm` be a data-loss incident (DB rows, user uploads)
            or just a rebuild step (collectstatic output, cached logs)?
            Only the first category is non-negotiable.
         [ANALOGY] Sorting moving boxes into "irreplaceable" (family
            photos → DB rows, uploads) vs "can be bought again"
            (furniture from a catalog → generated static files).
         🔬 easypj: delete django_static and one `collectstatic` rebuilds
            it; delete postgres_data and every JobPost, Skills, Author,
            Location, and User row is gone with no rebuild possible.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: list every path easypj writes to at runtime
            and mark each irreplaceable or rebuildable. Verify: your list
            matches the four rows in the table above.
         📊 SUMMARY: irreplaceable data gets a volume without debate;
            rebuildable output gets one anyway, but for convenience.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: application logs feel disposable until an incident
            review needs last week's — decide log retention on purpose,
            not by accident, in Chapter 17.6.

    17.2 Volume Setup for PostgreSQL
         - Named volume: postgres_data
         - Mount: /var/lib/postgresql/data
         - Backup strategy: docker run --rm -v postgres_data:/data alpine tar czf - /data > backup.tar.gz
         [CODE: compose.yaml with PostgreSQL volume]
         [BACKUP/RESTORE COMMANDS]

         🎯 GOAL: Mount a named volume onto PostgreSQL's data directory so
            `docker compose down` never touches jobpost's rows.
         [CODE: compose.yaml — postgres_data volume, drawn]
            services:
              db:
                image: postgres:16
                environment:
                  POSTGRES_DB: jobpost
                  POSTGRES_USER: jobpost_user
                volumes:
                  - postgres_data:/var/lib/postgresql/data
            volumes:
              postgres_data:
         [TECH] `postgres_data:/var/lib/postgresql/data` is the ONE mount
            that matters — that path is where postgres's own data files
            live; everything else in the container is disposable image
            content, rebuildable from `postgres:16` at any time.
         [ANALOGY] The volume is the filing cabinet; the postgres
            container is just this month's clerk operating it — replace
            the clerk (docker rm db), hire a new one, cabinet untouched.
         [TERMINAL: backup / restore commands, drawn]
            $ docker run --rm -v postgres_data:/data -v $(pwd):/backup \
                alpine tar czf /backup/pgdata.tar.gz -C /data .
            $ docker run --rm -v postgres_data:/data -v $(pwd):/backup \
                alpine tar xzf /backup/pgdata.tar.gz -C /data
         🔬 easypj: after this change, `docker compose down && docker
            compose up -d` restarts the whole stack — Skills, Author,
            Location, JobPost, and User rows survive untouched.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker compose down`, `docker compose up -d`,
            then `docker compose exec db psql -U jobpost_user -d jobpost
            -c "SELECT count(*) FROM jobpost_jobpost;"`. Verify: the count
            matches what it was before you brought the stack down.
         📊 SUMMARY: one named volume on one path turns PostgreSQL from
            disposable to durable — the container above it stays stateless.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: compose prefixes volume names with the PROJECT
            name (`<dir>_postgres_data`) unless you set `name:
            postgres_data` explicitly under the top-level volumes:.

    17.3 Volume Setup for Django Media & Static
         - Named volume: django_media, django_static
         - Mount: /app/media, /app/static
         - Nginx serving static files from volume
         [CODE: compose.yaml with all volumes]

         🎯 GOAL: Give Django media uploads and collected static files the
            same durability postgres just got — without conflating them.
         [CODE: compose.yaml — django_media & django_static, drawn]
            services:
              django:
                volumes:
                  - django_media:/app/media
                  - django_static:/app/static
              nginx:
                volumes:
                  - django_static:/app/static:ro
            volumes:
              postgres_data:
              django_media:
              django_static:
         [TECH] django_static is mounted :ro into nginx — nginx only ever
            READS what `collectstatic` wrote into the SAME volume from
            the django container; two services, one volume, one direction.
         [ANALOGY] A shared print tray: Django prints (writes) pages into
            it, nginx only ever picks pages up (reads) — the :ro flag is
            the label on nginx's side saying "no writing here".
         🔬 easypj: a user's uploaded resume PDF lands in django_media via
            a FileField; nginx never touches media — Django (or a signed
            URL) serves that path directly.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: upload a file through easypj's admin, `docker
            volume inspect django_media` for its Mountpoint, `sudo ls` it
            on the host. Verify: the uploaded filename appears there.
         📊 SUMMARY: three named volumes now cover DB rows, user uploads,
            and generated static assets — none of it lives in a container.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: skipping `:ro` on nginx's static mount doesn't break
            anything today, but it lets a compromised nginx write into a
            path Django trusts — least privilege, even on volumes.

    17.4 Bind Mounts for Development
         - Live code reload: -v .:/app (mount project root)
         - Why not in production
         - Permissions issues (especially on Linux)
         - :ro flag (read-only mount)
         [CODE: dev compose override with bind mounts]

         🎯 GOAL: Get live code reload in dev without ever baking that
            habit into the production image.
         [CODE: compose.override.yaml — dev bind mount, drawn]
            services:
              django:
                volumes:
                  - .:/app                  # live host code, read-write
                  - django_media:/app/media
                command: python manage.py runserver 0.0.0.0:8000
         [TECH] compose.override.yaml auto-merges over compose.yaml on
            plain `docker compose up` (11.6's file-merge rules) — the
            bind mount and dev command apply locally, never in a prod
            build where this file is intentionally absent.
         [ANALOGY] A whiteboard clipped over the framed production poster:
            erase and redraw all day (bind mount), then unclip it (deploy
            without the override) and the original poster is untouched.
         🔬 easypj: edit views.py in your editor, runserver inside the
            container sees the change immediately — no rebuild, no
            `docker cp`, because .:/app IS your working directory.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: with the override active, change a Django
            template's text, refresh the browser. Verify: the new text
            appears with no `docker compose build` in between.
         📊 SUMMARY: bind mounts trade isolation for iteration speed — the
            right trade in dev, the wrong one in prod (5.2's .dockerignore
            already blocks dev-only mounts from leaking into an image).
            ⚠️ Gotcha: on Linux, files the container writes appear owned
            by the CONTAINER's UID on your host — mismatched UIDs between
            image and host user are the #1 "why can't I edit this" bug.

    17.5 tmpfs Mounts — In-Memory Storage
         - What is tmpfs? (RAM-based, never written to disk)
         - Use case: temporary cache, secrets that must not hit disk
         - --tmpfs /tmp:exec (allow execution)
         - Performance comparison: tmpfs vs volume vs bind mount
         [TABLE: tmpfs use cases and limitations]

         🎯 GOAL: Decide, without hesitating, when tmpfs beats a volume
            for one specific easypj value.
         [TABLE: tmpfs — use case | limitation]
            Decrypted DB creds for one process | gone on container stop
            Session cache faster than a volume | capped by --tmpfs-size
            Scratch space for image processing  | counts against host RAM
         [TECH] tmpfs is a RAM-backed filesystem the KERNEL exposes — it
            never touches the storage driver or the host disk at all;
            `--tmpfs /tmp:size=64m` caps it so one runaway write can't
            exhaust host memory.
         [ANALOGY] A whiteboard, not a notebook: fast to write on, but
            wiped the instant the room (container) is vacated — nothing
            written there was ever meant to be found later.
         🔬 easypj: mounting `--tmpfs /run/secrets:size=1m` for a
            decrypted DATABASE_URL keeps it out of both the image layer
            and any volume backup that might leak it.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run
            `docker run --tmpfs /tmp:size=16m alpine df -h /tmp`.
            Verify: /tmp shows filesystem type tmpfs sized 16m, not the
            container's overlay root.
         📊 SUMMARY: tmpfs trades persistence for speed and secrecy —
            perfect for secrets and scratch space, wrong for anything
            easypj needs tomorrow.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: tmpfs on Docker Desktop for Windows isn't native
            Linux tmpfs — it's backed by the Desktop VM's RAM, not the
            host you think you have.

    17.6 Backup & Restore Strategies
         - Volume backup: docker run --rm -v vol:/data -v $(pwd):/backup alpine tar czf /backup/vol.tar.gz -C /data .
         - Volume restore: reverse the process
         - Database-specific: pg_dump inside container
         - Automated backup with CronJobs
         [TERMINAL: Complete backup/restore workflow]


         🎯 GOAL: Run one real pg_dump-based backup and restore cycle for
            jobpost end to end, not just tar the raw data files.
         [TERMINAL: pg_dump backup/restore workflow, drawn]
            $ docker compose exec db pg_dump -U jobpost_user jobpost \
                > jobpost_backup.sql
            $ ls -lh jobpost_backup.sql
            -rw-r--r-- 1 you you 42K jobpost_backup.sql
            $ docker compose exec -T db psql -U jobpost_user jobpost \
                < jobpost_backup.sql
         [TECH] `pg_dump` produces a logical, version-portable SQL dump —
            unlike 17.2's `tar` of the raw data directory, this restores
            cleanly onto a DIFFERENT postgres minor version and can target
            a brand-new, empty database.
         [ANALOGY] A recipe card vs a frozen dish: the tar backup (17.2)
            is the frozen meal — only reheats in an identical oven; the
            pg_dump SQL is the recipe — any working kitchen can cook it.
         🔬 easypj: `pg_dump -U jobpost_user jobpost` captures every row
            across Skills, Author, Location, JobPost, and User in one
            portable .sql file you can commit to encrypted backup storage.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: dump jobpost, drop and recreate the database,
            restore the dump. Verify: `SELECT count(*) FROM
            jobpost_jobpost;` returns the same number as before the drop.
         📊 SUMMARY: tar the volume for fast whole-disk snapshots; pg_dump
            for portable, restorable, version-safe database backups.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `psql < dump.sql` needs `-T` on `docker compose
            exec` (disable pseudo-TTY) or the piped stdin silently fails.

    17.7 Automated Backups — cron + pg_dump Pattern
         - Host cron vs a dedicated backup sidecar container that wakes
           on a schedule (a sidecar travels with the compose stack; host
           cron does not)
         - `pg_dump | gzip` streamed straight to a timestamped file on a
           mounted backup volume — never written inside the ephemeral
           database container itself
         - Retention policy: keep the last N days, prune older archives
           automatically so the backup volume never grows unbounded
         - The RESTORE drill: a backup nobody has restored is not a
           backup — schedule a periodic test restore into a throwaway
           postgres container and diff the row counts

         🎯 GOAL: Stand up an automated, retained, and PROVEN-restorable
            backup for easypj's PostgreSQL data.
         [TECH] Two valid schedulers: host cron calling `docker exec db
            pg_dump ...` from outside, or a sidecar service in
            compose.yaml running its own loop over the compose network —
            the sidecar deploys with the stack, host cron does not.
            Either way, `pg_dump` streams SQL to stdout; piping through
            gzip before it touches disk keeps a growing jobpost
            database's backups small.
         [ANALOGY] A fire drill, not a fire extinguisher on the wall: an
            extinguisher (backup file) never test-fired might be empty.
            The RESTORE drill is pulling the pin once a month on purpose,
            on a THROWAWAY building, to prove it still sprays.
         [CODE: sidecar backup service in compose.yaml, drawn]
            services:
              db-backup:
                image: postgres:16
                depends_on: [db]
                environment:
                  PGPASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}
                volumes:
                  - ./backups:/backups
                entrypoint: >
                  sh -c "while true; do
                  pg_dump -h db -U jobpost_user jobpost |
                  gzip > /backups/jobpost_$(date +%F_%H%M).sql.gz;
                  find /backups -name '*.sql.gz' -mtime +7 -delete;
                  sleep 86400; done"
         🔬 easypj: the service above dumps the jobpost database (Skills,
            Author, Location, JobPost, and User tables) daily, gzips it
            into ./backups on the host, and prunes anything past 7 days
            with `find -mtime +7` — a three-line retention policy.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run db-backup once by hand (`docker compose
            run --rm db-backup`), then RESTORE it into a fresh container:
            `gunzip -c backups/latest.sql.gz | docker exec -i newpg psql
            -U jobpost_user jobpost`. Verify: `SELECT count(*) FROM
            jobpost_jobpost;` matches the original database.
         📊 SUMMARY: schedule the dump, compress it, prune old copies,
            and — the step everyone skips — actually restore one on a
            recurring basis.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a backup volume on the SAME Docker host as the
            database is not disaster recovery — one disk failure takes
            both; ship backups off-host (S3, another server) for real DR.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 17 — minimum 10, covering: which easypj
     paths must persist, the postgres_data volume mount path, why
     django_static is :ro in nginx, bind mounts vs volumes for dev, UID
     mismatch permission errors, when to reach for tmpfs, pg_dump vs raw
     volume tar backups, restoring a dump into a fresh database, cron vs
     sidecar backup scheduling, retention pruning, and why an untested
     backup is not a backup]

Chapter 18: Advanced Storage Topics
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Volumes (Chapters 16-17) solved DATA
    persistence; this chapter opens the box underneath — how Docker
    itself stores image layers and a container's writable layer on
    disk. Skip it and two real incidents have no explanation: a host
    filesystem change wiping every image, and one file edit taking
    seconds instead of milliseconds — both trace to the mechanics here.

    18.1 Storage Drivers (Overlay2, aufs, etc.)
         - What is a storage driver? (manages image layers and container filesystem)
         - overlay2: Default, best performance, recommended
         - aufs, devicemapper, btrfs, zfs: legacy/niche
         - docker info | grep "Storage Driver"
         [TABLE: Storage driver comparison]

         🎯 GOAL: Name the default storage driver, say why it won, and
            read it back out of a running daemon.
         [TABLE: storage driver comparison — driver | status]
            overlay2      | default since Docker 18.09, best all-round
            aufs          | legacy, older kernels only, overlay2 replaced it
            devicemapper  | legacy CentOS/RHEL path, slower, more fragile
            btrfs / zfs   | niche, needs that host filesystem specifically
         [TECH] A storage driver manages how IMAGE LAYERS and a
            container's writable layer are stored and merged on disk —
            it is orthogonal to volumes (16.3), which bypass it entirely.
            overlay2 uses the kernel's native OverlayFS — no extra kernel
            module, which is why it displaced aufs/devicemapper.
         [ANALOGY] The storage driver is the warehouse's shelving system;
