            volumes are a separate locker room down the hall — reshelving
            the warehouse (changing storage drivers) never touches what's
            in the lockers.
         🔬 easypj: whichever driver is active, `postgres_data` (a named
            volume) is completely unaffected — only easypj's own IMAGE
            layers and its container's writable layer go through it.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `docker info | grep "Storage Driver"`.
            Verify: it prints `overlay2` on any current Docker install —
            anything else is worth investigating, not assuming.
         📊 SUMMARY: overlay2 is the correct default almost everywhere;
            legacy drivers exist for legacy kernels, not new decisions.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: changing storage drivers wipes ALL local images and
            containers (each keeps its own on-disk layout) — named
            volumes under /var/lib/docker/volumes/ are untouched by this.

    18.2 Volume Drivers & Plugins
         - Local driver (default)
         - NFS driver (network file system)
         - Cloud drivers: AWS EBS, Azure Disk, GCE Persistent Disk
         - Third-party: REX-Ray, Portworx, NetApp
         [TABLE: Volume driver comparison — persistence, performance, cloud]

         🎯 GOAL: Explain what a volume DRIVER changes vs a storage driver,
            and when easypj would ever need one besides `local`.
         [TABLE: volume driver comparison — driver | persists beyond host]
            local (default)      | no — lives on this one Docker host
            nfs                  | yes — shared network filesystem
            cloud (EBS/Azure/GCE)| yes — cloud block storage, one AZ
            REX-Ray / Portworx   | yes — multi-host, replicated, HA
         [TECH] `docker volume create --driver <name>` swaps WHERE and HOW
            volume data is stored; the container-side mount point and
            your application code never change — only the backing plugin.
         [ANALOGY] Choosing a moving company for the SAME storage unit:
            local is a truck to your own garage; nfs/cloud drivers are a
            truck to a shared warehouse other buildings can also reach.
         🔬 easypj: single-host deploys use `local` for postgres_data with
            zero config; a multi-host Swarm deploy of easypj (Part 9)
            needs `nfs` or a cloud driver so ANY node can mount the DB.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `docker info | grep -A3 "Plugins"` and
            find the Volume line. Verify: `local` is listed even if
            nothing else is installed — it ships with every Docker Engine.
         📊 SUMMARY: `local` covers single-host easypj entirely; only
            multi-host orchestration forces a networked volume driver.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a `local` volume is invisible to every OTHER host —
            moving a container to a new Swarm node does NOT move its
            local-driver data with it; that's exactly what 18.3 solves.

    18.3 NFS Volumes in Docker
         - docker volume create --driver local --opt type=nfs --opt o=addr=192.168.1.100,rw --opt device=:/path
         - Use case: shared storage across multiple Docker hosts
         [CODE: NFS volume creation and usage]

         🎯 GOAL: Create and mount an NFS-backed volume so postgres_data
            is reachable from more than one Docker host.
         [TERMINAL: NFS volume creation and usage, drawn]
            $ docker volume create --driver local \
                --opt type=nfs \
                --opt o=addr=192.168.1.100,rw \
                --opt device=:/exports/pgdata \
                nfs_pgdata
            $ docker run -d --name db \
                -v nfs_pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data postgres:16
         [TECH] Despite `--driver local`, `--opt type=nfs` tells the LOCAL
            driver to mount a remote NFS export as its backing store —
            no separate plugin install needed for basic NFSv3/v4 shares.
         [ANALOGY] A shelving unit (local driver) bolted to a wall that
            happens to be a shared warehouse's wall (the NFS server) — any
            other room (Docker host) with a door to that warehouse reaches
            the identical shelf.
         🔬 easypj: two easypj web nodes on separate hosts both mount
            nfs_pgdata; whichever node currently runs the db container,
            the SAME PostgreSQL files back it — the basis for HA failover.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create the NFS volume above (or a loopback NFS
            export for practice), mount it into alpine on TWO hosts, write
            a file from host A. Verify: host B's mount shows that file.
         📊 SUMMARY: NFS volumes turn Docker's single-host `local` driver
            into a shared, multi-host-reachable one — only the volume's
            creation flags differ, not your application code.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: NFS adds real network latency to every filesystem
            call — a chatty database on NFS is often SLOWER than expected;
            benchmark before trusting it under production write load.

    18.4 Container Filesystem Internals
         - Union filesystem (Overlay2)
         - Copy-on-Write explained
         - Image layers = LowerDir, Container layer = UpperDir
         - Merged view: what the container sees
         [DIAGRAM: Overlay2 layers — LowerDir, UpperDir, WorkDir, Merged]

         🎯 GOAL: Explain copy-on-write well enough to predict which
            layer a given file edit actually lands in.
         [DIAGRAM:layers — Overlay2 merged view, drawn]
            ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
            │ MergedDir = what the CONTAINER actually sees │
            ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
            │ UpperDir  = container's writable layer (CoW) │
            ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
            │ LowerDir N   = image layer, read-only        │
            │   ...        = older image layers, read-only │
            │ LowerDir 1   = base image layer, read-only   │
            ├─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
            │ WorkDir   = overlay's internal scratch space │
            └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         [TECH] Overlay2 unions every LowerDir (one per read-only image
            layer, bottom = base image) with one writable UpperDir into
            a single MergedDir. Editing an existing file triggers
            copy-up: the WHOLE file copies from its LowerDir into
            UpperDir first, THEN the write applies — never in place.
         [ANALOGY] Tracing paper over a printed map: the map (LowerDirs)
            never changes; every correction you draw lands on the sheet
            on top (UpperDir); lift the tracing paper (rm the container)
            and the original map is exactly as printed.
         🔬 easypj: editing one line of manage.py inside a RUNNING
            container copy-ups the entire file into UpperDir — that
            change is lost on `docker rm`, which is exactly why 17.4's
            bind mount, not live-editing the image, is the dev pattern.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker exec` into a running container, `echo
            x >> /app/manage.py`, then `docker diff <container>`.
            Verify: manage.py is listed with a `C` (changed) — that line
            lives only in UpperDir until the container is removed.
         📊 SUMMARY: LowerDirs are read-only and shared across every
            container from an image; UpperDir is one container's private,
            disposable delta — volumes (16.3) are the only escape from it.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: copy-up cost scales with FILE size, not edit size —
            appending one byte to a 2 GB log file copies all 2 GB into
            UpperDir first; that's a real, measurable latency spike.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 18 — minimum 10, covering: what a storage
     driver manages vs what a volume driver manages, why overlay2 is
     default, the blast radius of switching storage drivers, `local` vs
     nfs vs cloud volume drivers, when multi-host storage is needed, NFS
     volume creation flags, NFS latency trade-offs, LowerDir vs UpperDir
     vs WorkDir vs MergedDir, copy-on-write's copy-up cost, and reading
     `docker diff` output]


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 7: DOCKER SECURITY — PROTECTING YOUR CONTAINERS                     🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Chapter 19: Container Security Fundamentals
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    THINK BEFORE PART 7:
      What runs where?    → same shared kernel — the boundary is SOFTWARE now.
      What talks to what? → namespaces/caps/seccomp gate what a process can
                            touch; the daemon socket gates who is "root".
      What breaks first?  → one over-privileged flag (--privileged, a mounted
                            docker.sock) collapses every other control at once.

    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: A container is not a security boundary by default —
    it is a shared-kernel process wearing isolation. This chapter builds the
    defense-in-depth model: namespaces, capabilities, seccomp, AppArmor, non-
    root users, and a read-only root filesystem, each an independent layer.
    Problem solved: "my container runs as root with full capabilities, and one
    RCE in Django would own the whole host."

    19.1 Why Container Security Matters
         - Shared kernel = shared risk
         - Container breakout: escaping to host
         - Supply chain attacks: malicious base images
         - Real incidents: cryptojacking, data exfiltration
         [STATS: Container security breach statistics]

         🎯 GOAL: State the three container-specific attack classes and why a
            shared kernel raises the stakes.
         [TECH] Containers share the host kernel, so a kernel exploit or a
            misconfigured capability can escape to the host (breakout).
            Three classes: (1) breakout via over-privileged containers,
            (2) supply-chain via malicious/vulnerable base images, (3)
            runtime abuse — cryptojacking, data exfiltration from a popped app.
         [ANALOGY] Apartments sharing one foundation: a fire in one unit
            (kernel-level compromise) threatens the whole building, unlike
            detached houses (VMs) with separate foundations.
         [TABLE: attack class → easypj exposure]
            Breakout     | root container + host mount → host takeover
            Supply chain | unpinned base image ships a backdoored lib
            Runtime      | popped Django worker mines crypto / exfiltrates DB
         🔬 easypj: a single RCE in a JobPost upload handler, in a root
            container with the docker socket mounted, is not "one app hacked" —
            it is the whole host and every other container compromised.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `docker scout quickview` (or trivy) on your base
            image. Verify: it lists CVEs you inherited for free — the supply-
            chain surface you did not write but still ship.
         📊 SUMMARY: shared kernel means container security is HOST security;
            breakout, supply chain, and runtime abuse are the three fronts.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "it's just a container" is the mindset that gets hosts
            owned — treat every container as internet-facing and hostile.

    19.2 Docker Security Architecture
         - Namespaces (isolation): PID, NET, IPC, MNT, UTS, USER, CGROUP
         - Control Groups (cgroups): resource limiting
         - Capabilities: fine-grained permissions (CAP_NET_BIND, etc.)
         - Seccomp: system call filtering
         - AppArmor/SELinux: mandatory access control

         [DOCKER SECURITY — LAYERS OF DEFENSE AROUND CONTAINER]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                  🔐 DOCKER SECURITY — ONION OF DEFENSE                      │
         │                                                                          │
         │                           ┌───────────┐                                  │
         │                           │  🐳 Your   │                                 │
         │                           │ Container │                                 │
         │                           └─────┬─────┘                                 │
         │                                 │                                        │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │  LAYER 1: User Namespace     │  🔑 USER instruction in Dockerfile│    │
         │  │  (uid=1000, not root!)       │  docker run --user 1000:1000      │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 2: Capabilities       │  🎯 Drop all, add back only needed│    │
         │  │  (not full root!)            │  --cap-drop ALL                   │    │
         │  │                              │  --cap-add NET_BIND_SERVICE       │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 3: Seccomp Profile    │  🚫 Block dangerous syscalls      │    │
         │  │  (system call filter)        │  ~300 syscalls allowed of ~400    │    │
         │  │                              │  Blocked: mount, reboot, etc.     │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 4: AppArmor/SELinux   │  📋 Mandatory Access Control      │    │
         │  │  (per-process profiles)      │  Limit file access per container  │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 5: Cgroups            │  💪 Limit CPU, RAM, disk I/O     │    │
         │  │  (resource limits)           │  --memory=512m --cpus=1.5         │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 6: Read-Only Root FS  │  📖 --read-only (immutable!)      │    │
         │  │  (no writes to root)         │  + tmpfs for /tmp, /run           │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 7: Network Isolation  │  🌐 No port exposure unless -p    │    │
         │  │  (bridge/host/none)          │  Internal networks only            │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 8: Image Scanning     │  🔍 docker scout quickview         │    │
         │  │  (CVE detection)             │  Trivy, Snyk, Grype                │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 9: Content Trust      │  ✍️ Docker Content Trust (DCT)     │    │
         │  │  (image signing)             │  DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1            │    │
         │  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤    │
         │  │  LAYER 10: Host Security     │  🖥️ Docker Bench Security          │    │
         │  │  (daemon config, CIS)        │  Secure daemon.json config         │    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                                          │
         │  🏆 RESULT: Even if attacker escapes one layer, 9 more to go!           │
         │     Defense in depth = multiple independent security controls.           │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Name each layer of the security onion and the kernel feature
            behind it.
         [TECH] The onion diagram above is defense in depth: user namespace
            (drop root), capabilities (split root's powers), seccomp (filter
            syscalls), AppArmor/SELinux (mandatory access control), cgroups
            (resource caps), read-only rootfs, network isolation, image
            scanning, content trust, host/CIS hardening — 10 independent rings.
         [ANALOGY] The onion IS the analogy: an attacker peeling one layer
            still faces nine more, each a different lock needing a different
            key — no single failure is game over.
         [DIAGRAM:layers — the load-bearing four, from the onion]
            namespaces   → what a container can SEE (PID/NET/MNT/USER...)
            capabilities → which root powers it KEEPS (drop the rest)
            seccomp      → which SYSCALLS it may call (~44 blocked default)
            AppArmor     → which FILES/ops the profile permits
         🔬 easypj: web tier needs almost none of root's power — drop all caps,
            keep the default seccomp profile, run as uid 1000, mount rootfs
            read-only; that alone removes most of the breakout surface.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker inspect` a default container and find its
            CapAdd/CapDrop, SecurityOpt, and Privileged fields. Verify: by
            default caps are the standard set and Privileged is false.
         📊 SUMMARY: security is layered, not a single switch — namespaces +
            caps + seccomp + AppArmor + non-root + read-only compound.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `--privileged` collapses the ENTIRE onion at once
            (all caps, all devices, no seccomp) — it is the anti-pattern the
            whole diagram exists to prevent.

    19.3 Running as Non-Root User
         - Default: container runs as root (dangerous!)
         - USER instruction in Dockerfile
         - Creating a non-root user: RUN addgroup && adduser
         - --user flag: docker run --user 1000:1000
         [CODE: Dockerfile with non-root user for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Make easypj run as a non-root user and prove it.
         [TECH] Containers default to UID 0 (root). If the process is root and
            an attacker breaks out, they are root on the host. Fix: create a
            user in the Dockerfile and switch with USER, or override at runtime
            with `--user 1000:1000`. The app must own the paths it writes.
         [ANALOGY] Handing the new hire a visitor badge, not the master key:
            they can do their job, but they cannot open the server room even
            if they wander off.
         [CODE: Dockerfile — non-root easypj]
            FROM python:3.12-slim
            RUN addgroup --system app && adduser --system --ingroup app app
            WORKDIR /app
            COPY --chown=app:app . .
            RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
            USER app
            CMD ["gunicorn", "easypj.wsgi:application", "-b", "0.0.0.0:8000"]
         🔬 easypj: gunicorn now runs as uid "app"; a breakout lands as an
            unprivileged user with no host root, no ability to load kernel
            modules, no write to system paths.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker exec <ctr> id`. Verify: uid is NOT 0 (e.g.
            uid=100(app)); try to write /etc/passwd and get permission denied.
         📊 SUMMARY: USER app turns a root container into an unprivileged one —
            the single highest-value hardening step, one line long.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: switching to non-root can break writes to /app/static,
            /app/media, or a bind mount — chown those paths (or use a volume
            the user owns) or the app crashes on first write.

    19.4 Linux Capabilities
         - What are capabilities? (splitting root's power)
         - Default capabilities given to containers
         - --cap-drop ALL --cap-add NET_BIND_SERVICE
         - CAP_SYS_ADMIN danger (≈ root)
         [TABLE: Common capabilities — risk, when needed]

         🎯 GOAL: Drop all Linux capabilities and add back only what easypj
            actually needs.
         [TECH] Root's power is split into ~40 capabilities. Docker grants a
            default subset; best practice is `--cap-drop ALL` then
            `--cap-add` the few required. CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE lets a process
            bind ports <1024; CAP_SYS_ADMIN is a near-root grab-bag and should
            never be added to an app container.
         [ANALOGY] Instead of one master key (root), a ring of labeled keys;
            hand out only "bind low port" and take back the rest — especially
            the "does everything" master (SYS_ADMIN).
         [TABLE: capabilities for easypj]
            NET_BIND_SERVICE | bind :80/:443       | add only if needed
            CHOWN / SETUID   | entrypoint privs    | usually droppable
            SYS_ADMIN        | ~= root             | NEVER add to the app
            NET_RAW          | ping / raw sockets  | drop (blocks spoofing)
         🔬 easypj: gunicorn binds 8000 (>1024) behind nginx, so easypj needs
            ZERO added capabilities: `--cap-drop ALL` and it still runs fine.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run easypj with `--cap-drop ALL`. Verify: the app
            still serves; then `--cap-drop ALL` a container that pings and
            watch ping fail (NET_RAW gone) — proof caps are actually enforced.
         📊 SUMMARY: drop ALL, add back the minimum — most web apps need none,
            and SYS_ADMIN is the capability to fear.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `--privileged` is NOT "all capabilities" — it is caps
            PLUS device access PLUS no seccomp; even --cap-add ALL is safer.

    19.5 Read-Only Root Filesystem
         - --read-only flag
         - tmpfs for writable locations: --tmpfs /tmp, --tmpfs /run
         - Django: /tmp needs to be writable for WSGI sockets
         [CODE: Read-only container configuration for easypj]


         🎯 GOAL: Run easypj with a read-only root filesystem plus tmpfs for
            the few writable paths.
         [TECH] `--read-only` mounts the container's root filesystem immutable,
            so a compromised process cannot drop a binary, edit configs, or
            persist. Genuinely writable spots (/tmp, /run, WSGI sockets) get
            explicit `--tmpfs` mounts; real data goes on named volumes.
         [ANALOGY] A hotel room bolted shut except for one labeled scratchpad
            (tmpfs): guests can jot a note, but they cannot repaint the walls
            or hide anything for the next occupant.
         [CODE: read-only easypj container]
            docker run --read-only \
              --tmpfs /tmp \
              --tmpfs /run \
              -v media:/app/media \
              easypj:v1
         🔬 easypj: Django + gunicorn need /tmp for sockets and uploads-in-
            progress; those go on tmpfs, media persists on a volume, and the
            rest of the filesystem is frozen against tampering.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run easypj `--read-only` with no tmpfs and watch it
            fail writing /tmp; add `--tmpfs /tmp` and it starts. Verify: `touch
            /etc/x` inside is denied while `touch /tmp/x` works.
         📊 SUMMARY: read-only rootfs + narrow tmpfs + volumes = an immutable
            container where writes are a deliberate, listed exception.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: forgetting a required writable path (/tmp, /run, cache)
            makes the app crash on boot — enumerate them and add tmpfs, do not
            abandon --read-only.

    19.6 Seccomp & AppArmor in Practice
         - Default seccomp profile blocks ~44 dangerous syscalls of ~350+
         - Custom profile = a JSON allow/deny list passed with --security-opt
         - --security-opt seccomp=unconfined DISABLES the filter (danger!)
         - AppArmor docker-default confines file/capability/network per process
         - Denials surface as EPERM in the app and in the host audit log

         🎯 GOAL: Explain what the default seccomp and AppArmor profiles block,
            and where a denial shows up.
         [TECH] Seccomp filters syscalls: Docker's default profile allows the
            common ~300 and blocks ~44 dangerous ones (mount, reboot,
            kexec_load, ptrace of others, etc.). A custom profile is JSON
            (defaultAction + a syscalls allow-list) passed via
            `--security-opt seccomp=profile.json`. AppArmor's docker-default
            profile independently confines file access and capabilities per
            container process.
         [ANALOGY] Seccomp is the bouncer checking each SYSCALL against a
            guest list at the kernel door; AppArmor is building security
            deciding which ROOMS (files) that guest may enter once inside.
         [TERMINAL: inspect and apply]
            $ docker run --rm alpine grep Seccomp /proc/1/status  # 2 = filtered
            $ docker run --security-opt seccomp=custom.json easypj:v1
            $ docker run --security-opt apparmor=docker-default easypj:v1
            # denials appear as EPERM; on the host:
            $ dmesg | grep -i apparmor    # DENIED audit lines
         🔬 easypj: the default profiles are enough for Django + gunicorn —
            easypj never calls mount() or reboot(); keep them on and only write
            a custom profile if a library needs a specifically blocked syscall.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run a container with `--security-opt
            seccomp=unconfined` and one without; compare
            `grep Seccomp /proc/1/status`. Verify: 0 (unconfined) vs 2
            (filtered) — proof the profile is really attached.
         📊 SUMMARY: keep the default seccomp + AppArmor profiles on; they cost
            nothing and block whole syscall/file-access attack classes for free.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `seccomp=unconfined` and `--privileged` both strip the
            seccomp filter entirely — a container that "only works" unconfined
            is telling you it is doing something the default rightly blocks.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 19 — minimum 10, covering: shared-kernel risk & the
     three attack classes, the 10-ring security onion, why --privileged is the
     anti-pattern, non-root USER in the Dockerfile, cap-drop ALL + minimal add,
     CAP_SYS_ADMIN danger, read-only rootfs + tmpfs, default seccomp (~44
     syscalls) + custom JSON profiles, AppArmor docker-default, where denials
     (EPERM/audit) appear]

Chapter 20: Securing the easypj Stack
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapter 19 gave you the security primitives; this
    chapter assembles them into a hardened easypj stack — a concrete checklist,
    real secrets handling, a locked-down daemon, image signing, and the rootless
    option. Problem solved: "I know about capabilities and seccomp, but what is
    the actual, ordered list of things I must do to ship easypj safely?"

    20.1 Security Hardening Checklist
         - Use specific base image tags (not :latest)
         - Run as non-root user
         - Drop all capabilities, add back only needed
         - Use read-only root filesystem
         - Scan images for vulnerabilities (Docker Scout)
         - Use secrets, not environment variables for sensitive data
         - Limit resources (--memory, --cpus)
         - Use health checks
         - Enable Docker Content Trust (DCT)
         - Keep Docker Engine updated
         [CHECKLIST: 15 security items for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Apply an ordered hardening checklist to easypj and know why
            each item earns its place.
         [TECH] The high-value controls, in priority order: pin base image
            tags (not :latest), run non-root, `--cap-drop ALL`, read-only
            rootfs + tmpfs, scan images (Scout/Trivy), secrets NOT in ENV,
            resource limits (--memory/--cpus), health checks, Content Trust,
            keep the Engine patched. Each maps to a layer of the onion.
         [ANALOGY] A pre-flight checklist: pilots do not "remember" to lower
            the gear, they READ the list every time — hardening is a list you
            run on every deploy, not a thing you recall.
         [CHECKLIST: easypj hardening — top items]
            [ ] base image pinned (python:3.12-slim, not :latest)
            [ ] USER app (non-root)          [ ] --cap-drop ALL
            [ ] --read-only + tmpfs /tmp     [ ] --memory / --cpus set
            [ ] secrets via file, not ENV    [ ] HEALTHCHECK defined
            [ ] image scanned, 0 criticals   [ ] Engine + base patched
         🔬 easypj: a single `docker run` line for easypj web carries USER,
            cap-drop, read-only, tmpfs, memory limit and a health check — the
            checklist rendered as flags on one command.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: take a plain easypj run command and add every
            checklist flag one at a time, confirming the app still boots after
            each. Verify: the final container passes `docker scout quickview`
            and runs as non-root with limits set.
         📊 SUMMARY: hardening is a repeatable checklist, ordered by payoff —
            pin, de-root, de-cap, freeze, scan, limit, sign, patch.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: :latest silently changes under you between builds —
            an unpinned base is an unreviewed dependency upgrade every deploy.

    20.2 Secrets Management
         - Problem: ENV vars visible in docker inspect
         - Docker secrets (Swarm only): encrypted at rest, in transit
         - docker secret create, docker service --secret
         - For non-Swarm: .env file mounted as volume (limited)
         - Third-party: HashiCorp Vault, AWS Secrets Manager
         [CODE: Swarm secrets for Django SECRET_KEY, DB_PASSWORD]

         🎯 GOAL: Keep easypj's SECRET_KEY and DB password out of environment
            variables and image layers.
         [TECH] ENV vars leak: they show in `docker inspect`, `/proc/1/environ`,
            and child processes. Swarm secrets are encrypted at rest + in
            transit and appear only as files under /run/secrets. Non-Swarm:
            mount a file and read it, or use Vault/AWS Secrets Manager. NEVER
            bake secrets into a Dockerfile — they persist in image layers.
         [ANALOGY] ENV secrets are a password on a sticky note on the monitor
            (anyone glancing sees it); a mounted secret file is the password in
            a sealed envelope handed only to the one process that needs it.
         [CODE: file-based secret for easypj]
            services:
              web:
                environment:
                  DB_PASSWORD_FILE: /run/secrets/db_password
                secrets:
                  - db_password
            secrets:
              db_password:
                file: ./secrets/db_password.txt   # gitignored
         🔬 easypj: Django reads SECRET_KEY and DB_PASSWORD from files at
            /run/secrets/*; `docker inspect web` shows only the FILE PATH, never
            the secret value.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: set a secret via ENV, run `docker inspect | grep -i
            secret`, and see it in plain text; switch to a file secret and
            inspect again. Verify: the value is no longer visible in inspect.
         📊 SUMMARY: secrets belong in mounted files (or a vault), never in ENV
            or image layers — inspect and layer history are both readable.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a secret ARG/ENV in an early Dockerfile stage is baked
            into that layer forever — even if a later stage unsets it; use
            build secrets (`--mount=type=secret`) instead.

    20.3 Securing the Docker Daemon
         - Docker socket (/var/run/docker.sock) — root equivalent
         - Never expose Docker daemon to internet without TLS
         - Docker daemon configuration (daemon.json)
         - User namespace remapping (userns-remap)
         [CODE: daemon.json security settings]

         🎯 GOAL: Lock down the Docker daemon and understand why the socket is
            the crown jewel.
         [TECH] /var/run/docker.sock is the daemon's API endpoint, and the
            daemon runs as ROOT. Anyone who can talk to that socket can run
            `docker run -v /:/host --privileged` and own the entire host —
            mounting the socket into a container is handing that container
            root on the host. Harden the daemon in daemon.json: userns-remap,
            no-new-privileges, disable inter-container comms, never expose the
            API over TCP without mutual TLS.
         [ANALOGY] docker.sock is the master key to the whole building. A
            container with the socket mounted is a tenant holding that master
            key — polite until it is not, then every door is already open.
         [CODE: daemon.json hardening]
            {
              "userns-remap": "default",
              "no-new-privileges": true,
              "icc": false,
              "live-restore": true,
              "log-driver": "json-file",
              "log-opts": {"max-size": "10m", "max-file": "3"}
            }
         🔬 easypj: NO easypj container ever mounts docker.sock. A CI job that
            "needs Docker" is the classic trap — that mount makes the CI
            container root on the runner host (see 22.6 for safe options).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `docker run -v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/
            docker.sock alpine` and from inside, list host containers via the
            API. Verify: you can see and control the host's Docker — proof the
            socket = root. Then DON'T do this in production.
         📊 SUMMARY: the socket is root-equivalent; guard it like root, never
            mount it casually, and never expose the API without TLS.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `-v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock` grants
            full host root to whatever runs in that container — the single
            most dangerous flag in common use. Treat it as host --privileged.

    20.4 Image Signing & Content Trust
         - Docker Content Trust (DCT): sign and verify images
         - DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1 docker pull
         - Notary: the signing server
         - docker trust commands
         [TERMINAL: DCT setup and verification workflow]

         🎯 GOAL: Sign easypj images and make pulls verify signatures.
         [TECH] Docker Content Trust (DCT) signs image tags with Notary so a
            consumer can verify an image was published by you and not tampered
            with. `export DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1` makes push sign and pull
            REFUSE unsigned/altered tags. `docker trust` manages signer keys.
         [ANALOGY] A wax seal on a letter: the recipient (pull) checks the seal
            is intact and yours before trusting the contents; a broken or
            missing seal means reject, do not open.
         [TERMINAL: sign + enforce for easypj]
            $ export DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1
            $ docker push easypj/web:v1.2.3     # prompts for signing key
            $ docker trust inspect easypj/web:v1.2.3 --pretty
            $ docker pull easypj/web:v1.2.3     # verifies before pulling
            $ docker pull easypj/web:tampered   # ERROR: no valid trust data
         🔬 easypj: production hosts run with DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1, so a
            compromised registry serving a swapped easypj image is rejected at
            pull time instead of silently deployed.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: enable DCT, push a signed tag, then try to pull a
            tag you never signed. Verify: the signed pull works and the
            unsigned one is refused with a trust-data error.
         📊 SUMMARY: DCT = signed, verifiable image tags; turn it on in prod so
            "pull" also means "prove it is authentic and unmodified".
            ⚠️ Gotcha: DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1 only protects tags you actually
            SIGNED — an unsigned :latest simply fails to pull, and losing your
            root signing key means you can never sign that repo again.

    20.5 Podman vs Docker — Rootless Containers
         - What is Podman? (daemonless, rootless by default)
         - podman vs docker CLI compatibility (alias docker=podman)
         - Rootless Docker mode
         - Why rootless matters
         [COMPARISON TABLE: Docker vs Podman — 10 dimensions]


         🎯 GOAL: Explain rootless containers and when Podman beats Docker for
            security.
         [TECH] Podman is daemonless and rootless by default: containers run
            as your user via user namespaces, so "root" inside maps to your
            unprivileged UID outside — no root daemon, no socket to steal.
            Docker offers rootless mode too (dockerd as a non-root user). The
            CLIs are near-identical (`alias docker=podman` often just works).
         [ANALOGY] Docker's default is a valet (the root daemon) who parks
            everyone's car with the master key; rootless/Podman gives each
            driver their own limited key — no central master to hijack.
         [TABLE: Docker vs Podman — security dimensions]
            Daemon        | Docker: root daemon | Podman: none (fork/exec)
            Default user  | root                | rootless
            Socket risk   | docker.sock = root  | no daemon socket
            Systemd units | via daemon          | native `podman generate`
            Compose       | docker compose      | podman-compose / quadlet
         🔬 easypj: on a shared CI runner, running the easypj build under
            rootless Podman means a build-time compromise cannot escalate to
            host root — there is no root daemon to reach.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run easypj under `podman run` (or rootless docker)
            and check `id` on the host for the container process. Verify: it
            maps to your UID, not 0 — root inside ≠ root outside.
         📊 SUMMARY: rootless (Podman, or Docker rootless mode) removes the
            root daemon and the root-equivalent socket — a strictly smaller
            attack surface for multi-tenant and CI hosts.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: rootless cannot bind ports <1024 without extra config
            and some volume/network features differ — test easypj under it
            before assuming drop-in parity.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 20 — minimum 10, covering: the ordered hardening
     checklist, why :latest is unsafe, ENV vs file secrets & inspect leakage,
     build-secret layer baking, docker.sock = host root, daemon.json hardening
     (userns-remap/icc/no-new-privileges), Content Trust sign + verify, DCT
     enforcement on pull, rootless/Podman vs root daemon, socket mount danger]

Chapter 21: Docker Security Scanning & Compliance
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Hardening you cannot measure is faith, not
    security. This chapter is the instrumentation: scanning images for CVEs
    (Scout, Trivy), auditing the host against the CIS Benchmark (Docker Bench),
    generating an SBOM of everything you ship, and wiring all of it into CI.
    Problem solved: "how do I PROVE, on every build, that easypj has no known
    critical vulnerabilities and follows the benchmark?"

    21.1 Docker Scout Deep Dive
         - docker scout quickview <image>
         - docker scout cves <image>
         - docker scout recommendations <image>
         - docker scout compare <image1> <image2>
         - Scout in CI/CD pipelines
         [TERMINAL: Complete docker scout workflow for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Use Docker Scout to find, explain, and fix CVEs in the easypj
            image.
         [TECH] Scout analyzes an image's SBOM against CVE databases.
            `quickview` gives a severity summary; `cves` lists each finding;
            `recommendations` suggests base-image bumps; `compare` diffs two
            tags. It runs locally and in CI, failing a build over a policy.
         [ANALOGY] A home inspector for your image: quickview is the one-page
            summary, cves is the full defect list, recommendations is "replace
            this old water heater", compare is "before vs after the repairs".
         [TERMINAL: the Scout workflow on easypj]
            $ docker scout quickview easypj:v1
              Critical 0  High 3  Medium 12  Low 20
            $ docker scout cves easypj:v1 --only-severity critical,high
            $ docker scout recommendations easypj:v1   # e.g. slim → newer slim
            $ docker scout compare easypj:v1 --to easypj:v2
         🔬 easypj: quickview flags 3 highs from an old base; `recommendations`
            says bump python:3.12-slim to the latest patch, which
            `docker scout compare` then confirms cleared them.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: scan easypj:v1, bump the base image one patch
            version, rebuild, and `docker scout compare` old vs new. Verify:
            the high-severity count drops in the comparison.
         📊 SUMMARY: Scout turns "is my image safe?" into a measured, diffable
            number — quickview to triage, recommendations to fix, compare to prove.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a clean scan is a POINT-IN-TIME result; new CVEs land
            daily, so re-scan on every build and on a schedule, not once.

    21.2 Docker Bench Security
         - Automated checks against CIS Docker Benchmark
         - docker run --rm --pid host --net host --cap-add audit_control -v /var/lib:/var/lib -v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock -v /etc:/etc docker/docker-bench-security
         [OUTPUT: Annotated docker-bench-security report]

         🎯 GOAL: Audit the Docker host against the CIS Benchmark with Docker
            Bench Security.
         [TECH] docker-bench-security is a script (shipped as a container) that
            checks host config, daemon.json, image, and runtime settings
            against the CIS Docker Benchmark, emitting [PASS]/[WARN]/[INFO]
            per check. It reads host paths, so it needs several host mounts and
            pid/net host to inspect the real daemon.
         [ANALOGY] A code-compliance inspector walking your building with the
            official checklist, ticking each rule PASS or WARN — the CIS
            Benchmark is the building code.
         [TERMINAL: run the benchmark]
            $ docker run --rm --net host --pid host --cap-add audit_control \
              -v /var/lib:/var/lib -v /etc:/etc \
              -v /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock \
              docker/docker-bench-security
              [WARN] 2.1  - Restrict network traffic between containers (icc)
              [PASS] 4.1  - Ensure a user for the container is created
         🔬 easypj: the bench WARNs that icc is enabled — feeding the exact
            daemon.json fix (icc:false) already recommended in 20.3.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run docker-bench-security and pick the top 3 WARNs.
            Verify: each maps to a concrete daemon.json or run-flag change you
            can apply, then re-run to see it flip to PASS.
         📊 SUMMARY: Docker Bench converts the CIS Benchmark into an automated
            PASS/WARN report — the host-hardening counterpart to image scanning.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the bench itself needs docker.sock + host mounts to run;
            treat it as a trusted, one-off audit tool, not a long-lived
            container, and remove it after.

    21.3 Trivy — Open Source Vulnerability Scanner
         - What is Trivy? (Aqua Security's scanner)
         - trivy image easypj:v1
         - trivy fs /path/to/dockerfile
         - Trivy in CI/CD
         [TERMINAL: Trivy output for easypj image]

         🎯 GOAL: Scan easypj with Trivy as an open-source, CI-friendly
            alternative to Scout.
         [TECH] Trivy (Aqua Security) scans images, filesystems, and IaC for
            CVEs and misconfigurations. `trivy image easypj:v1` reports OS and
            language-package vulnerabilities; `--exit-code 1 --severity
            CRITICAL,HIGH` fails a CI job on serious findings; `trivy fs .`
            scans a source tree before it is ever built.
         [ANALOGY] A free, open-source metal detector you can run anywhere —
            on the finished image, on the raw source, or wired to the CI gate —
            no vendor account required.
         [TERMINAL: Trivy on easypj]
            $ trivy image easypj:v1
              Total: 15 (HIGH: 3, CRITICAL: 0)
            $ trivy image --severity CRITICAL,HIGH --exit-code 1 easypj:v1
            $ trivy fs --scanners vuln,misconfig .
         🔬 easypj: the same 3 highs Scout found appear in Trivy with fix
            versions; CI runs `trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL`
            so a critical CVE hard-fails the easypj pipeline.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `trivy image` on easypj, then again with
            `--exit-code 1 --severity CRITICAL,HIGH` and check `$?`. Verify:
            exit 0 when clean, 1 when a high/critical exists — a usable CI gate.
         📊 SUMMARY: Trivy is a free, scriptable scanner for images, filesystems
            and IaC — the open-source workhorse of a scanning gate.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Trivy caches its vuln DB; in air-gapped/CI runs update or
            mount the DB explicitly, or you scan against a stale database and
            miss fresh CVEs.

    21.4 SBOM (Software Bill of Materials)
         - What is an SBOM? (list of all components in an image)
         - docker sbom easypj:v1
         - Syft + Grype tools
         - SBOM formats: SPDX, CycloneDX
         [CODE: SBOM generation and interpretation]

         🎯 GOAL: Generate an SBOM for easypj and use it to answer "are we
            affected by CVE-X?" instantly.
         [TECH] An SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) is a machine-readable list
            of every component + version in an image. `docker sbom` (or Syft)
            emits SPDX/CycloneDX; feed it to Grype to match against CVEs. When
            the next Log4j-style bug drops, you grep the SBOM instead of
            rebuilding and rescanning everything.
         [ANALOGY] The ingredients label on the package: when a recall hits
            "batch of ingredient X", you read labels to find affected products
            — you do not re-cook every meal to check.
         [CODE: SBOM generate + match]
            $ docker sbom easypj:v1 -o spdx-json > easypj.sbom.json
            $ syft easypj:v1 -o cyclonedx-json > easypj.cdx.json
            $ grype sbom:easypj.sbom.json          # CVEs from the SBOM alone
            $ grep -i "django" easypj.sbom.json    # which version do we ship?
         🔬 easypj: the SBOM lists Django 5.x, psycopg, gunicorn and every OS
            lib with exact versions; a new psycopg CVE is answered by grepping
            the file, no rebuild required.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: generate easypj's SBOM, then find the exact Django
            and psycopg versions in it. Verify: the versions match
            `pip freeze` inside the container.
         📊 SUMMARY: an SBOM is the durable inventory of what you shipped — the
            fastest path from "is CVE-X in our stack?" to a yes/no answer.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: an SBOM is a SNAPSHOT of one build; regenerate it per
            image tag, and store it as a build artifact — a stale SBOM answers
            the wrong version's question.

    21.5 CIS Docker Benchmark Overview
         - What is CIS Benchmark? (security configuration standard)
         - Key checks: Host configuration, Docker daemon, Container runtime
         - Automated compliance with docker-bench-security
         [TABLE: CIS Benchmark categories and key checks]

         🎯 GOAL: Describe what the CIS Docker Benchmark covers and how to
            check compliance automatically.
         [TECH] The CIS Docker Benchmark is a consensus security-configuration
            standard grouped into sections: Host config, Docker daemon config,
            daemon files/permissions, container images, container runtime, and
            operations. docker-bench-security automates most checks; the
            benchmark is the WHAT, the bench script is the HOW-to-measure.
         [ANALOGY] CIS is the published building code; Docker Bench is the
            inspector who walks the site with that code in hand. You can read
            the code yourself, but the inspector makes it a checklist.
         [TABLE: CIS Docker sections → example check]
            Host config   | audit the docker daemon & files
            Daemon config | icc=false, no-new-privileges, live-restore
            Images        | non-root USER, no secrets in layers
            Runtime       | --cap-drop, --read-only, --memory limits
         🔬 easypj: nearly every easypj control so far — non-root, cap-drop,
            read-only, memory limits, hardened daemon.json — is a specific CIS
            line item; the benchmark is a rollup of Chapters 19-21.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: pick one check from each CIS section and confirm
            easypj meets it (e.g. Images → USER set, Runtime → cap-drop ALL).
            Verify: docker-bench PASSes those exact lines for your container.
         📊 SUMMARY: CIS is the standard, Docker Bench is the meter — together
            they turn "is easypj hardened?" into a section-by-section score.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: CIS is guidance, not law — some checks (e.g. auditd
            rules, AppArmor) are host-wide and cannot be satisfied from a
            Dockerfile alone; hardening spans the host, not just the image.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 21 — minimum 10, covering: Scout quickview/cves/
     recommendations/compare, point-in-time scans & re-scanning, Docker Bench
     vs CIS Benchmark, reading PASS/WARN output, Trivy image/fs + --exit-code
     CI gate, SBOM (SPDX/CycloneDX) + Grype matching, answering "are we
     affected by CVE-X", CIS sections, host-wide vs image-level checks]


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 8: CI/CD WITH DOCKER — BUILD, TEST, DEPLOY                           🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 8:
      What runs where?    → the build runs on a CI RUNNER, not your laptop; the
                            image is the artifact that moves to the registry.
      What talks to what? → runner → Docker build → registry → deploy target;
                            every stage is itself a container for consistency.
      What breaks first?  → the build strategy: dind vs a mounted docker.sock —
                            the socket mount makes the CI job root on the runner.

Chapter 22: Docker in CI/CD Pipelines
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: The "works on my machine" problem returns with a
    vengeance in CI — different runners, different tool versions, flaky tests.
    This chapter makes Docker the constant: build, test, scan and push easypj
    inside containers on GitHub Actions, GitLab CI and Jenkins, so every stage
    runs in an identical environment. Problem solved: "my tests pass locally but
    the CI build is a different machine I cannot reproduce."

    22.1 Why Docker in CI/CD?
         - Consistent build environment
         - No "works on my machine" in CI
         - Parallel testing in isolated containers
         - Easy artifact management (images = artifacts)

         [CI/CD PIPELINE — DOCKER AT EVERY STAGE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                    🚀 CI/CD PIPELINE WITH DOCKER                           │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌──────────┐ │
         │  │ 👨‍💻 Push  │   │ 🏗️ Build │   │ 🧪 Test  │   │ 🔍 Scan │   │ 📦 Push  │ │
         │  │  Code   │──▶│  Image  │──▶│  Image   │──▶│  Image  │──▶│  Image   │ │
         │  └─────────┘   └─────────┘   └──────────┘   └─────────┘   └──────────┘ │
         │       │             │              │              │             │         │
         │       ▼             ▼              ▼              ▼             ▼         │
         │  ┌─────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌─────────┐   ┌──────────┐ │
         │  │ GitHub  │   │ docker  │   │ docker   │   │ docker  │   │ docker   │ │
         │  │ /GitLab │   │ build   │   │ compose  │   │ scout   │   │ push     │ │
         │  │         │   │ -t      │   │ run test │   │ quick-  │   │ registry │ │
         │  │ Trigger │   │ easypj  │   │ django   │   │ view    │   │ easypj   │ │
         │  │ on push │   │ :ci-123 │   │ test     │   │ easypj  │   │ :v1.2.3  │ │
         │  └─────────┘   └─────────┘   └──────────┘   └─────────┘   └──────────┘ │
         │                                                                  │       │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
         │  │                                                                      │
         │  ▼                                                                      │
         │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐    │
         │  │ 🚢 Deploy│   │ 🏃 Smoke │   │ 📊 Monitor│   │ 🔄 Auto-Rollback │    │
         │  │  to K8s  │──▶│  Tests   │──▶│  Health  │──▶│  (if unhealthy)  │    │
         │  │ kubectl  │   │ curl     │   │ Prometheus│   │ kubectl rollout  │    │
         │  │ apply    │   │ /health  │   │ Grafana   │   │ undo             │    │
         │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────────────┘    │
         │                                                                          │
         │  🔑 EVERY step runs in a Docker container → IDENTICAL environment!       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Explain why every CI/CD stage in the pipeline diagram runs
            inside a container.
         [TECH] The pipeline diagram above is push → build → test → scan → push
            → deploy → smoke → monitor → rollback, and EACH stage runs in a
            Docker container. The image built in stage 2 IS the artifact that
            flows through the rest — the exact bytes tested are the exact bytes
            deployed, eliminating environment drift between CI and prod.
         [ANALOGY] A factory conveyor (the diagram): the same part is stamped,
            inspected, and boxed at stations that never touch the part with
            bare hands — every station is a sealed, identical booth (container).
         [DIAGRAM:flow — the artifact is the image]
            build  → easypj:ci-<sha>   (one immutable artifact)
            test   → run tests IN that image
            scan   → Scout/Trivy that same image
            push   → retag ci-<sha> → v1.2.3 (rebuild NOTHING)
            deploy → pull v1.2.3 to prod
         🔬 easypj: `docker build -t easypj:ci-$SHA` once; tests, scan and the
            production tag all reference that one build — what CI green-lit is
            byte-for-byte what ships.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build an image tagged with the git SHA, run the
            test suite inside it, then retag it as a release. Verify: the
            release digest equals the tested digest (`docker inspect` --format
            '{{.Id}}') — no rebuild between test and ship.
         📊 SUMMARY: Docker makes CI/CD deterministic — build the artifact once,
            then test/scan/deploy that identical image through every stage.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: rebuilding at the deploy stage (instead of retagging the
            tested image) reintroduces drift — you would ship an image nobody
            tested. Build once, promote by tag.

    22.2 GitHub Actions + Docker
         - docker/login-action (authenticate)
         - docker/build-push-action (build and push)
         - docker/metadata-action (tags and labels)
         - Complete workflow for easypj
         [CODE: .github/workflows/docker.yml for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Build, scan and push easypj from a GitHub Actions workflow.
         [TECH] The official actions do the heavy lifting: docker/login-action
            authenticates to the registry, docker/metadata-action derives tags
            + labels from git refs, and docker/build-push-action builds
            (with buildx cache) and pushes. Secrets come from GitHub Actions
            secrets, never hardcoded.
         [ANALOGY] Prebuilt LEGO modules for the pipeline: you snap login +
            metadata + build-push together instead of hand-writing docker CLI
            in shell — fewer sharp edges, tested by millions.
         [CODE: .github/workflows/docker.yml — core steps]
            - uses: docker/login-action@v3
              with:
                username: ${{ secrets.REGISTRY_USER }}
                password: ${{ secrets.REGISTRY_TOKEN }}
            - uses: docker/metadata-action@v5
              with: { images: easypj/web }
            - uses: docker/build-push-action@v6
              with:
                push: true
                tags: ${{ steps.meta.outputs.tags }}
                cache-from: type=gha
                cache-to: type=gha,mode=max
         🔬 easypj: a push to main builds easypj/web tagged with both the SHA
            and the branch, scans it, and pushes — with GHA layer caching so
            unchanged pip layers are not rebuilt.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: add the three actions to a workflow and push a
            commit. Verify: the run logs show login → metadata tags → a pushed
            image, and the registry now lists the new tag.
         📊 SUMMARY: login + metadata + build-push actions give easypj a
            cached, tagged, pushed image on every commit with minimal YAML.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: registry creds MUST be GitHub secrets, never inline in
            the YAML — the workflow file is in the repo for everyone to read.

    22.3 GitLab CI + Docker
         - .gitlab-ci.yml with Docker
         - docker-in-docker (dind) vs docker socket binding
         - Complete GitLab CI pipeline for easypj
         [CODE: .gitlab-ci.yml for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Build easypj in GitLab CI and pick between dind and socket
            binding.
         [TECH] GitLab CI runs each job in a container. To build images you
            either run docker:dind as a privileged service (a full Docker
            daemon inside the job) or bind the host's docker.sock. dind is
            isolated but needs --privileged; the socket bind is lighter but
            gives the job root on the runner (see 22.6).
         [ANALOGY] dind is bringing your own portable forge to the job site
            (heavy, but yours); the socket bind is borrowing the landlord's
            master forge — convenient until the job burns the building down.
         [CODE: .gitlab-ci.yml — dind build]
            build:
              image: docker:27
              services:
                - docker:27-dind
              variables:
                DOCKER_TLS_CERTDIR: "/certs"
              script:
                - docker build -t easypj:ci-$CI_COMMIT_SHORT_SHA .
                - docker push easypj:ci-$CI_COMMIT_SHORT_SHA
         🔬 easypj: the GitLab pipeline builds easypj:ci-<sha> in a dind
            service, runs `docker compose run tests`, then pushes — all inside
            ephemeral job containers that vanish after the run.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: write a two-job pipeline (build, test) using dind.
            Verify: the build job produces an image the test job can run, and
            the pipeline goes green only when tests pass.
         📊 SUMMARY: GitLab jobs are containers; build images via dind
            (isolated, privileged) or a socket bind (light, risky) — 22.6 picks.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: dind needs --privileged, which disables the seccomp/cap
            protections from Part 7 for that job — isolate dind runners and
            never reuse them for untrusted code.

    22.4 Jenkins + Docker
         - Jenkinsfile with Docker agent
         - docker.build(), docker.push()
         - Complete Jenkins pipeline for easypj
         [CODE: Jenkinsfile for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Build and push easypj from a Jenkins declarative pipeline.
         [TECH] Jenkins runs stages on agents; the Docker Pipeline plugin adds
            `docker.build()`, `.push()`, and `docker.image().inside {}` to run
            steps inside a container. Agents need Docker access (a dind sidecar
            or a mounted socket) with the same trade-off as every other CI.
         [ANALOGY] Jenkins is the veteran foreman with a huge plugin toolbox;
            the Docker Pipeline plugin is the attachment that lets the foreman
            hand each task to a sealed container crew.
         [CODE: Jenkinsfile — build + push]
            pipeline {
              agent any
              stages {
                stage('Build') { steps { script {
                  img = docker.build("easypj:ci-${env.GIT_COMMIT}")
                } } }
                stage('Push') { steps { script {
                  docker.withRegistry('', 'registry-creds') { img.push() }
                } } }
              }
            }
         🔬 easypj: Jenkins builds easypj:ci-<commit>, runs the Django test
            suite via `img.inside { sh 'python manage.py test' }`, and pushes
            only if the stage is green.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create a Build + Push pipeline with the Docker
            plugin and a credentials ID. Verify: the build stage produces an
            image and the push stage authenticates via the stored credential,
            not a plaintext password.
         📊 SUMMARY: Jenkins + Docker Pipeline plugin gives declarative
            build/test/push stages with credentials pulled from Jenkins' store.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker.withRegistry` credential IDs live in Jenkins'
            credential store — hardcoding a registry password in the Jenkinsfile
            leaks it to everyone with repo read access.

    22.5 Docker Build Cloud
         - Remote builds in the cloud
         - Faster builds (no local resource drain)
         - Shared build cache across team
         - docker buildx build --builder cloud-xxx
         [TERMINAL: Docker Build Cloud workflow]


         🎯 GOAL: Offload easypj image builds to Docker Build Cloud for speed
            and a shared cache.
         [TECH] Docker Build Cloud runs buildx builds on remote, beefy builders
            with a persistent, team-shared cache. `docker buildx build
            --builder cloud-<org>-<name>` sends the build context up; results
            (and cache) are shared, so a teammate's cached layers speed YOUR
            build. Multi-arch (amd64+arm64) builds run in parallel remotely.
         [ANALOGY] A shared industrial kitchen instead of everyone cooking on a
            camping stove: bigger burners, and the prep one cook did (cache) is
            ready for the next — nobody re-chops the same onions.
         [TERMINAL: build easypj in the cloud]
            $ docker buildx create --driver cloud easypj/builder
            $ docker buildx build --builder cloud-easypj-builder \
                --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 \
                -t easypj/web:v1 --push .
         🔬 easypj: the slow pip-install layer, cached in Build Cloud from the
            last run, is reused so a one-line code change rebuilds easypj in
            seconds instead of minutes — on CI runners that stay small.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build easypj once on Build Cloud, change only a
            source file, build again. Verify: the second build reuses the
            cached dependency layers (CACHED in the log) and finishes far faster.
         📊 SUMMARY: Build Cloud = remote builders + shared cache + easy multi-
            arch — faster CI without bigger local/runner machines.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the build CONTEXT is uploaded to the cloud — a fat
            context (node_modules, .git, media) is slow and may leak files;
            tighten .dockerignore before pointing builds at the cloud.

    22.6 dind vs Socket Mount — Choosing a CI Build Strategy
         - Building images in CI needs a Docker engine SOMEWHERE — three ways
         - docker:dind --privileged: isolated daemon, but privileged = risky
         - -v /var/run/docker.sock: light, but = ROOT on the runner host
         - Rootless builders (kaniko, buildah) need no daemon and no privilege
         - Choose by trust level of the code and the runner's blast radius

         🎯 GOAL: Choose a CI build strategy by weighing isolation against the
            root-on-host risk, and know the rootless escape hatch.
         [TECH] Three strategies. (1) docker:dind: a full daemon inside the job,
            requires --privileged — isolated per-job but privileged disables
            Part-7 protections. (2) Socket mount
            (-v /var/run/docker.sock:...): reuses the host daemon, so the job
            is ROOT on the runner (see 20.3) — fast but a breakout owns every
            build. (3) Rootless: kaniko or buildah build images in userspace
            with NO daemon and NO privilege.
         [ANALOGY] dind = your own locked workshop trucked in (heavy, self-
            contained); socket mount = a key to the landlord's workshop
            (convenient, but they can burn the block); kaniko/buildah = a
            portable 3D printer that needs no workshop at all.
         [TABLE: pick a CI build strategy]
            Strategy       | Isolation | Host risk       | Needs
            docker:dind    | high      | privileged job  | --privileged svc
            socket mount   | low       | ROOT on runner  | docker.sock bind
            kaniko/buildah | high      | none (rootless) | no daemon
         🔬 easypj: on a shared runner, easypj images are built with kaniko —
            no daemon, no privilege, no socket — so a poisoned dependency in a
            build cannot escalate to the runner host or other tenants.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build easypj three ways — dind, socket mount,
            kaniko — and for each check `id` / privilege of the build process.
            Verify: only kaniko/buildah run unprivileged with no host daemon
            access; the socket-mount job can control the host's Docker.
         📊 SUMMARY: dind for isolation at the cost of --privileged; socket
            mount only on fully trusted, single-tenant runners; kaniko/buildah
            when you want image builds with neither a daemon nor root.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: mounting docker.sock into a CI job is the most common
            "temporary" CI hack that ships to production runners — it hands
            every pipeline root on the host; prefer rootless builders.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 22 — minimum 10, covering: why every CI stage is a
     container, build-once/promote-by-tag vs rebuild, GitHub Actions login/
     metadata/build-push + secrets, GitLab dind vs socket, Jenkins Docker
     Pipeline credentials, Build Cloud remote cache + multi-arch, .dockerignore
     & context size, dind --privileged trade-off, docker.sock = runner root,
     rootless kaniko/buildah]

Chapter 23: Docker Image Versioning & Promotion
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: A pile of images called :latest is an outage
    waiting to happen — you cannot say what is running or roll back to what
    worked. This chapter imposes discipline: deterministic tags, promotion by
    RETAGGING the tested image across environments, registry topology, and
    signing in the pipeline. Problem solved: "which exact easypj image is in
    production, and how do I promote or roll back without rebuilding?"

    23.1 Image Tagging Strategies for CI/CD
         - Git commit SHA: easypj:abc1234
         - Semantic version: easypj:1.2.3
         - Branch-based: easypj:main, easypj:develop
         - Environment-based: easypj:staging, easypj:production
         - Combined: easypj:1.2.3-abc1234
         [TABLE: Tagging strategy — pros, cons, use case]

         🎯 GOAL: Pick a tagging scheme that makes every easypj image
            identifiable and reproducible.
         [TECH] Tags are pointers, not versions. Use immutable, meaningful
            tags: the git SHA (easypj:abc1234) for exact traceability, semver
            (easypj:1.2.3) for releases, and combined (easypj:1.2.3-abc1234).
            Moving tags (:latest, :main) are convenient but ambiguous — never
            deploy prod from a moving tag.
         [ANALOGY] A SHA tag is a fingerprint (unique, permanent); :latest is a
            nickname that points at whoever answers today — fine for a chat,
            useless for a subpoena.
         [TABLE: tagging strategy trade-offs]
            git SHA  | exact, ugly     | CI builds, traceability
            semver   | human, released | public releases
            branch   | :main, :develop | moving, dev convenience
            combined | 1.2.3-abc1234   | best of both, prod deploys
         🔬 easypj: CI tags every build easypj:ci-<sha>; on release it is
            retagged easypj:1.2.3-<sha>, so prod runs a tag that names both the
            human version AND the exact commit.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build easypj and tag it three ways (SHA, semver,
            combined) pointing at the same image. Verify: `docker images`
            shows three tags, one IMAGE ID — tags are labels on one artifact.
         📊 SUMMARY: prefer immutable SHA/semver tags for anything deployed;
            reserve moving tags for local convenience only.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: :latest is just a default tag, not "newest" — it points
            wherever it was last pushed, which may be an OLD or broken build.

    23.2 Image Promotion Across Environments
         - Dev → Staging → Production promotion pipeline
         - Retagging vs rebuilding (retag!)
         - Promotion gates: tests, scans, approvals
         [FLOWCHART: Image promotion pipeline]

         🎯 GOAL: Promote one tested easypj image dev → staging → prod by
            RETAGGING, never rebuilding.
         [TECH] The image that passed tests is the artifact; promotion just
            adds an environment tag/registry ref to that SAME digest.
            Rebuilding per environment reintroduces drift — a fresh build could
            pull a different base or dependency. Gates (tests, scans, approval)
            guard each hop, but the bytes never change.
         [ANALOGY] A passport getting stamps at each border: same person (same
            digest) admitted to each country (environment) — you do not issue a
            brand-new person at every crossing.
         [DIAGRAM:flow — promote by digest]
            build easypj@sha256:abc  →  test+scan (gate)  →  tag :staging
              →  smoke (gate)  →  tag :production   [same digest throughout]
         🔬 easypj: `docker tag easypj@sha256:abc easypj:production` and push —
            production now points at the exact digest that cleared staging, with
            zero chance of a surprise rebuild.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: build once, promote by retagging staging→production,
            and compare digests with `docker inspect --format '{{.Id}}'`.
            Verify: staging and production tags share one identical digest.
         📊 SUMMARY: promote by moving a tested DIGEST through environments;
            gates decide, but the image bytes are immutable end to end.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker build` at the promotion step is the classic
            anti-pattern — it silently produces a DIFFERENT image than the one
            you tested. Retag the digest; never rebuild to promote.

    23.3 Registry Strategies
         - Single registry, multiple repositories
         - Multiple registries (dev on Docker Hub, prod on ECR)
         - Registry mirroring and caching
         - Retention policies: how long to keep images
         [DIAGRAM: Multi-registry architecture]

         🎯 GOAL: Design easypj's registry layout and retention so images are
            findable, secure, and not infinite.
         [TECH] Options: one registry with many repos (simple), or split
            registries (dev on Docker Hub, prod on a private ECR/GHCR) for a
            security boundary. A pull-through mirror caches upstream images to
            cut bandwidth and rate limits. Retention policies expire old tags
            so storage does not grow forever.
         [ANALOGY] Registries are warehouses: one big shared warehouse vs a
            public receiving dock (dev) feeding a secured bonded warehouse
            (prod), with a local depot (mirror) so you do not truck from the
            factory every time.
         [DIAGRAM:architecture — easypj registry topology]
            CI ──push──▶ dev registry (Docker Hub)
                              │ promote (retag, copy)
                              ▼
            prod pull ◀── private prod registry (ECR/GHCR)
            base images ◀── pull-through mirror (cache)
         🔬 easypj: CI pushes easypj:ci-<sha> to the dev registry; a promotion
            job copies the approved digest into the private prod registry, which
            production hosts are the only ones allowed to pull from.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: configure a pull-through mirror and pull a base
            image twice. Verify: the second pull is served from the mirror
            cache (faster, no upstream hit) — check the mirror's logs.
         📊 SUMMARY: separate dev/prod registries for a trust boundary, mirror
            upstream to save bandwidth, and set retention so storage is bounded.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: no retention policy = a registry that grows without
            limit; but retention that is too aggressive can delete the exact
            old tag you need for an emergency rollback — exempt release tags.

    23.4 Docker Content Trust in CI/CD
         - Signing images in CI/CD
         - DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1 in pipeline
         - Key management for signing
         [CODE: CI/CD sign + verify workflow]


         🎯 GOAL: Sign easypj images inside CI so only pipeline-produced images
            can be deployed.
         [TECH] Set DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1 in the pipeline so the push step
            signs the tag; deployment hosts pull with DCT on and reject
            anything unsigned. Signing keys are injected as CI secrets (a
            delegation key, NOT the root key), and the root key stays offline.
         [ANALOGY] The pipeline is a notary that stamps every release document;
            production refuses to act on any document without the notary's
            seal — and the notary's master stamp lives in a vault, not the office.
         [CODE: sign in CI, verify on deploy]
            # CI (push step)
            export DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1
            export DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST_REPOSITORY_PASSPHRASE=$CI_SIGN_KEY
            docker push easypj/web:1.2.3        # signs on push
            # deploy host
            export DOCKER_CONTENT_TRUST=1
            docker pull easypj/web:1.2.3        # refuses if unsigned/altered
         🔬 easypj: only the CI pipeline holds the delegation signing key, so a
            developer pushing an unsigned easypj image by hand cannot get it
            deployed — prod's DCT-on pull rejects it.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: sign a tag in a pipeline job, then push an unsigned
            tag manually and try to pull both with DCT=1. Verify: the CI-signed
            tag pulls, the hand-pushed one is refused.
         📊 SUMMARY: signing in CI + DCT-enforced pulls makes the pipeline the
            ONLY path to production — provenance you can enforce, not just hope.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: guard signing keys like production creds — a leaked
            delegation key lets an attacker sign malicious images that pass
            verification; keep the ROOT key offline and rotate delegations.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 23 — minimum 10, covering: tags are pointers not
     versions, :latest ambiguity, SHA/semver/combined tagging, promote by
     retagging the tested digest, rebuild-to-promote anti-pattern, digest
     equality checks, single vs split dev/prod registries, pull-through
     mirrors, retention policies + exempting release tags, signing in CI with
     a delegation key + offline root key]

Chapter 24: Container Testing Strategies
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: A green unit-test suite that never touched a real
    database or the actual image proves little about what you ship. This chapter
    layers container testing — unit, integration against real PostgreSQL,
    full-stack e2e, plus image-level structure tests and Dockerfile linting — so
    the thing you deploy is the thing you tested. Problem solved: "tests pass but
    the container still breaks in prod on a missing file, bad env, or real DB."

    24.1 Testing Pyramid for Containers
         - Unit tests: Inside container, test Django views/models
         - Integration tests: Container + PostgreSQL
         - End-to-end tests: Full Compose stack
         - Image tests: Dockerfile linting, vulnerability scan
         [PYRAMID DIAGRAM: Container testing pyramid]

         🎯 GOAL: Place each kind of easypj test on the container testing
            pyramid and know the cost/coverage trade-off.
         [TECH] Bottom-up: many fast UNIT tests (Django view/model logic against
            a lightweight PostgreSQL test DB), fewer INTEGRATION tests (the app
            container + a full real PostgreSQL), fewer still END-TO-END tests
            (the whole compose stack), and cross-cutting IMAGE tests (structure
            tests, scans, lint). Higher = more realistic, slower, fewer.
         [ANALOGY] A food-safety program: taste each ingredient (unit), taste
            the mixed dish (integration), serve a test table the full meal
            (e2e), and inspect the kitchen itself (image tests) — more effort
            as you climb, so you run fewer at the top.
         [PYRAMID DIAGRAM: easypj container tests]
                     ╱────────────╲       e2e: full compose stack
                    ╱  e2e (few)   ╲      (slow, most realistic)
                   ╱────────────────╲
                  ╱ integration: web ╲    + real PostgreSQL
                 ╱────────────────────╲
                ╱  unit: views/models  ╲  (many, fast)
               └────────────────────────┘
            image tests (structure + scan + lint) wrap every layer
         🔬 easypj: hundreds of unit tests on JobPost/Skills logic, a dozen
            integration tests hitting real PostgreSQL, a couple of e2e flows
            (post a job → search → apply), all green before an image ships.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: count your easypj tests by layer. Verify: the shape
            is a pyramid (unit ≫ integration ≫ e2e), not an ice-cream cone
            (mostly slow e2e) — the cone is a CI-time smell.
         📊 SUMMARY: many fast unit tests, fewer real-DB integration tests, a
            handful of e2e, plus image tests — coverage where it is cheapest.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: easypj uses PostgreSQL, so tests must run against a real
            Postgres test database (never sqlite) — sqlite would pass tests that
            break on Postgres-specific SQL and migrations.

    24.2 Testing with docker compose run
         - docker compose run --rm django python manage.py test
         - docker compose run --rm django python manage.py test jobpost
         - Exit code handling in CI/CD
         [TERMINAL: Test execution and results]

         🎯 GOAL: Run the easypj test suite in a throwaway container wired to a
            real PostgreSQL, and fail CI on a bad exit code.
         [TECH] `docker compose run --rm web python manage.py test` starts a
            one-off container on the compose network (so it reaches the db
