            service) and removes it after. The command's exit code is the test
            result — 0 pass, non-zero fail — which CI checks to gate the build.
         [ANALOGY] Renting a car for a single test drive: you get the exact
            same model (image) wired to the real roads (db service), then hand
            it back (--rm) — no residue, repeatable every time.
         [TERMINAL: test easypj against real Postgres]
            $ docker compose run --rm web python manage.py test
              Ran 214 tests in 12.4s — OK
            $ docker compose run --rm web python manage.py test jobpost
            $ echo $?          # 0 = pass, non-zero = CI fails the job
         🔬 easypj: the test container connects to the compose `db` service (a
            real PostgreSQL), so migrations and Postgres-specific queries are
            exercised exactly as they will be in production.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the suite with a deliberately failing test and
            inspect `$?`. Verify: exit code is non-zero and, in CI, that
            non-zero halts the pipeline before build/push.
         📊 SUMMARY: `compose run --rm` gives disposable, network-connected test
            runs whose exit code is the CI gate — real DB, no leftovers.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker compose run` does NOT publish the service's
            ports by default and starts its OWN deps — use --rm and depends_on
            so the db is up, or the first test fails on a refused connection.

    24.3 Testcontainers — Real Database in Tests
         - What is Testcontainers? (throwaway containers for testing)
         - Python: testcontainers-python
         - Django tests with real PostgreSQL container
         - pytest-docker plugin
         [CODE: Django test with Testcontainers for PostgreSQL]

         🎯 GOAL: Spin up a throwaway PostgreSQL per test run with
            Testcontainers so tests own their database.
         [TECH] Testcontainers starts real containers programmatically from the
            test code, hands back the dynamic host/port, and tears them down
            after. testcontainers-python (or pytest-docker) boots a PostgreSQL
            container, points Django at it, runs migrations, and destroys it —
            no shared, stateful test DB to pollute between runs.
         [ANALOGY] Each test crew gets a brand-new rehearsal stage built on
            demand and demolished after — no fighting over one shared stage
            that the last crew left a mess on.
         [CODE: Testcontainers PostgreSQL for easypj]
            from testcontainers.postgres import PostgresContainer
            with PostgresContainer("postgres:16") as pg:
                url = pg.get_connection_url()   # dynamic host:port
                os.environ["DATABASE_URL"] = url
                # run Django migrations + tests against THIS Postgres
         🔬 easypj: CI runs the JobPost/User integration tests against a fresh
            postgres:16 container that exactly matches production's version —
            catching Postgres-specific migration and query bugs early.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: write one test that stands up a PostgresContainer,
            applies migrations, and creates a JobPost. Verify: the row exists
            during the test and the container is gone afterward (`docker ps`).
         📊 SUMMARY: Testcontainers = programmatic, disposable, version-matched
            PostgreSQL per test — real-DB fidelity without a shared test server.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Testcontainers needs a Docker daemon available to the
            test process — in CI that means dind or a socket, with the same
            trust trade-off as 22.6; budget for container startup time too.

    24.4 Container Structure Tests
         - Google's Container Structure Test framework
         - Test: file existence, command output, metadata
         - container-structure-test test --image easypj:v1 --config tests.yaml
         [CODE: container-structure-test config for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Assert facts about the built easypj IMAGE — files, commands,
            metadata — independent of the app's own tests.
         [TECH] Google's container-structure-test runs a YAML spec against an
            image: command tests (run X, assert output/exit), file existence
            tests (path present, right mode/owner), file content tests (regex),
            and metadata tests (USER, EXPOSE, ENTRYPOINT). It validates the
            IMAGE, not runtime behavior.
         [ANALOGY] A pre-delivery vehicle inspection: not a test drive, but a
            checklist that the right engine, the correct VIN, and all the parts
            are actually bolted in before the car leaves the lot.
         [CODE: container-structure-test for easypj]
            schemaVersion: 2.0.0
            commandTests:
              - name: python present
                command: python
                args: ["--version"]
                expectedOutput: ["Python 3.12.*"]
            fileExistenceTests:
              - name: app code copied
                path: /app/manage.py
                shouldExist: true
            metadataTest:
              user: "app"                 # asserts non-root USER
         🔬 easypj: the structure test asserts /app/manage.py exists, python is
            3.12, and USER is "app" — catching a broken Dockerfile (missing
            COPY, wrong base, forgotten USER) before the image is ever deployed.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `container-structure-test test --image easypj:v1
            --config tests.yaml` with a rule that USER is app. Verify: it PASSES;
            then remove USER from the Dockerfile, rebuild, and watch it FAIL.
         📊 SUMMARY: structure tests lock the image's shape — files, versions,
            and metadata like non-root USER — as an independent build gate.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: structure tests check the image at REST, not running
            behavior — they confirm USER app is SET, not that the app works as
            that user; pair them with integration tests, do not substitute.

    24.5 Dockerfile Linting
         - Hadolint: Dockerfile linter
         - hadolint Dockerfile
         - Common warnings and how to fix them
         - Integrating hadolint in CI/CD
         [CODE: ShellCheck + Hadolint in CI pipeline]

         🎯 GOAL: Lint the easypj Dockerfile with Hadolint and fix the warnings
            it flags before they cost you in prod.
         [TECH] Hadolint parses a Dockerfile and applies best-practice rules
            (DLxxxx) plus embedded ShellCheck on RUN lines: pin apt versions,
            avoid `latest`, use `--no-install-recommends`, don't run as root,
            combine RUN layers, prefer COPY over ADD. It exits non-zero on
            findings, so it drops straight into CI.
         [ANALOGY] A spell-checker for your Dockerfile: it will not run the
            build, but it underlines the sloppy phrasing (unpinned apt, stray
            ADD, root user) that turns into bugs and bloat later.
         [CODE: hadolint in CI]
            $ hadolint Dockerfile
              DL3007 warning: Using latest is prone to errors
              DL3008 warning: Pin versions in apt-get install
              DL3002 warning: Last USER should not be root
            # CI gate (fail on warnings)
            $ hadolint --failure-threshold warning Dockerfile
         🔬 easypj: Hadolint flags an unpinned `apt-get install libpq-dev` and
            a missing USER in the easypj Dockerfile — the exact issues that
            cause non-reproducible builds and root containers from Part 7.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `hadolint Dockerfile` on easypj, fix the top
            three warnings, and re-run. Verify: the warning count drops and
            `--failure-threshold warning` now exits 0 — a passing CI lint gate.
         📊 SUMMARY: Hadolint + ShellCheck catch Dockerfile smells (unpinned
            deps, ADD, root, :latest) at lint time — cheapest fix in the pipeline.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: linting is static — a clean Hadolint run does NOT mean
            the image builds or runs; it means the Dockerfile follows best
            practices. Keep build + structure + integration tests alongside it.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 24 — minimum 10, covering: the testing pyramid vs
     ice-cream cone, why tests use real PostgreSQL not sqlite, compose run
     --rm exit-code gating, compose run networking/depends_on, Testcontainers
     disposable per-test Postgres, its Docker-daemon requirement, container-
     structure-test (command/file/metadata incl. USER), image-at-rest vs
     runtime, Hadolint + ShellCheck rules, lint as a CI gate + its static limits]

PART 9: DOCKER TO KUBERNETES — THE BRIDGE                                 🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 9:
      What runs where?    → Pods land on whichever node the scheduler picks.
      What talks to what? → kubectl → API server → etcd; scheduler → kubelet.
      What breaks first?  → "one container, one host" — Pods move, IPs don't.

Chapter 25: Docker → Kubernetes Transition
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Every image, container, network and volume so
    far lived on ONE machine — yours. The moment easypj must survive a
    server dying, or handle more traffic than one box can hold, Docker runs
    out of answers on its own. This chapter draws the exact line: what
    Docker still owns (the image, the Dockerfile, the single-host runtime)
    and what Kubernetes takes over (scheduling, healing, scaling across many
    machines). Problem solved here: "do I throw away Docker to learn K8s?"

    25.1 Why Kubernetes After Docker?
         - Docker runs containers on ONE machine
         - Kubernetes runs containers across MANY machines
         - Docker = package your app | Kubernetes = run it at scale
         - The "where" problem: Docker needs you to pick a server; K8s picks for you

         [DOCKER vs KUBERNETES — SINGLE HOST vs CLUSTER]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │              🐳 DOCKER (Single Host)  vs  ☸️ KUBERNETES (Cluster)           │
         │                                                                          │
         │  DOCKER:                        KUBERNETES:                              │
         │  ┌──────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │   ONE SERVER     │           │         CLUSTER (Many Servers)        │ │
         │  │                  │           │                                      │ │
         │  │  ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ │           │  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐│ │
         │  │  │C1│ │C2│ │C3│ │           │  │      🧠 CONTROL PLANE            ││ │
         │  │  └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ │           │  │  API Server | etcd | Scheduler   ││ │
         │  │                  │           │  └──────────────────────────────────┘│ │
         │  │  IF SERVER DIES: │           │                                      │ │
         │  │  💀 Everything   │           │  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐      │ │
         │  │     goes down!   │           │  │ 💪 Node 1  │  │ 💪 Node 2  │      │ │
         │  │                  │           │  │ ┌──┐┌──┐┌──┐│  │ ┌──┐┌──┐┌──┐│      │ │
         │  │  SCALING:        │           │  │ │C1││C3││C5││  │ │C2││C4││C6││      │ │
         │  │  Manual, buy     │           │  │ └──┘└──┘└──┘│  │ └──┘└──┘└──┘│      │ │
         │  │  more servers    │           │  └────────────┘  └────────────┘      │ │
         │  │                  │           │                                      │ │
         │  │  NO auto-healing │           │  IF NODE DIES: Pods move to Node 2 ✅ │ │
         │  │  NO auto-scaling │           │  SCALING: kubectl scale --replicas=10│ │
         │  └──────────────────┘           └──────────────────────────────────────┘ │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [COMPARISON TABLE: Docker vs Kubernetes — 15 Dimensions]
         | Dimension             | Docker (Standalone)   | Kubernetes             |
         | Single Host           | ✅ Yes                | ✅ Yes (Minikube)      |
         | Multi-Host Cluster    | ❌ No                 | ✅ Yes (core feature)  |
         | Auto-Scaling          | ❌ Manual only        | ✅ HPA + VPA built-in  |
         | Self-Healing          | ❌ restart policies   | ✅ Auto-recreates pods |
         | Service Discovery     | ⚠️ Limited (Compose)  | ✅ DNS + CoreDNS       |
         | Load Balancing        | ⚠️ Compose/Manual     | ✅ Built-in Services   |
         | Rolling Updates       | ⚠️ Swarm only         | ✅ Deployment native   |
         | Rollback              | ❌ None               | ✅ kubectl rollout undo|
         | Secret Management     | ⚠️ Swarm secrets      | ✅ Secrets + etcd      |
         | Config Management     | ⚠️ .env files         | ✅ ConfigMaps          |
         | Storage Orchestration | ❌ Manual volumes     | ✅ PV + PVC + CSI      |
         | Namespace Isolation   | ❌ None               | ✅ Namespaces          |
         | RBAC                  | ❌ None               | ✅ Roles + Bindings    |
         | Cloud-Native          | ❌ Not designed for   | ✅ Built for cloud     |
         | Learning Curve        | 🟢 Low                | 🔴 High                |

         🎯 GOAL: State, from memory, the one sentence that separates what
            Docker does from what Kubernetes adds — and point at where the
            single-host diagram above breaks under real traffic.
         [TECH] Docker Engine schedules containers onto exactly the host you
            ran `docker run` on; there is no concept of "another machine"
            inside the engine itself. Kubernetes adds a control plane (API
            server, etcd, scheduler, controller-manager) that turns MANY
            hosts (nodes) into one schedulable pool — control-plane
            internals: [KUBERNETES GUIDE].
         [ANALOGY] The SINGLE HOST vs CLUSTER diagram above: Docker is one
            kitchen with one chef — if the chef collapses, dinner stops.
            Kubernetes is a restaurant chain with a head office (control
            plane) that reassigns any order to any open kitchen (node).
         🔬 easypj: today `docker run easypj:v1` dies with the laptop. On a
            3-node cluster, the SAME image becomes 3 Pods; killing one node
            just moves its Pods to the survivors (see the table's
            Self-Healing row).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: from the 15-dimension table above, list every
            row Docker marks ❌ or ⚠️ (13 of them), then circle the 2 a
            growing easypj would hit FIRST. Verify: your circled pair is
            Auto-Scaling and Rollback — the two Docker cannot do at all
            without Swarm or K8s.
         📊 SUMMARY: Docker = one host, one daemon; Kubernetes = a cluster
            with a control plane doing the picking for you.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Kubernetes does NOT replace Docker's image format —
            it still runs OCI images your Dockerfiles already build.

    25.2 Concepts Mapping: Docker → Kubernetes
         - Docker Container → Kubernetes Pod
         - Docker Image → Kubernetes Container Image
         - docker-compose.yml → Kubernetes Deployment + Service
         - Docker Network → Kubernetes Network (CNI)
         - Docker Volume → Kubernetes PersistentVolume
         - Docker Compose → Kubernetes + Helm/Kustomize
         [TABLE: Every Docker concept mapped to K8s equivalent]

         🎯 GOAL: Translate any Docker noun to its Kubernetes noun without
            hesitating — this vocabulary swap runs through every later page.
         [TABLE: Docker concept → Kubernetes equivalent — drawn]
            Container          | Pod (1+ containers, one network namespace)
            Image              | Container image (same OCI artifact)
            docker-compose.yml | Deployment + Service (+ Helm chart)
            Docker network     | CNI network (Pod-to-Pod, cluster-wide)
            Docker volume      | PersistentVolume + PersistentVolumeClaim
            docker run         | kubectl apply -f (or run, imperative)
            restart policy     | Pod restartPolicy + Deployment replicas
         [TECH] The mapping is NOT 1:1 everywhere: one Docker container maps
            to a Pod's main container, but a Pod can hold several
            containers sharing localhost and a volume — a shape Docker
            alone cannot express. Sidecar patterns: [KUBERNETES GUIDE].
         [ANALOGY] Relabeling a moving-house inventory: "box" becomes
            "crate", "van" becomes "fleet" — same cargo, new shipping
            company with its own paperwork and bigger trucks.
         🔬 easypj: the postgres_data VOLUME becomes a PersistentVolumeClaim
            bound to a PersistentVolume — same idea (durable disk outside
            the container), different YAML, same Postgres data files.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: take easypj's compose.yaml from Chapter 11 and
            rename each key using this table (django→Deployment,
            postgres_data→PVC, backend network→CNI). Verify: nothing in
            the list has no K8s equivalent — that is the point here.
         📊 SUMMARY: same 5 nouns (image, container, network, volume,
            compose file), new names, bigger scope.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "Docker Compose" itself has NO direct K8s object —
            its job splits across Deployment, Service, ConfigMap and PVC.

    25.3 From Dockerfile to Kubernetes Deployment
         - Step 1: Dockerfile (you already have this!)
         - Step 2: docker build + docker push to registry
         - Step 3: Write Kubernetes Deployment YAML
         - Step 4: kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
         - Step 5: Write Kubernetes Service YAML
         - Step 6: kubectl apply -f service.yaml

         [COMPLETE WORKFLOW: DOCKERFILE → K8s DEPLOYMENT (6 Steps)]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │          🐳→☸️ DOCKERFILE TO KUBERNETES DEPLOYMENT — FULL JOURNEY           │
         │                                                                          │
         │  STEP 1: Dockerfile           STEP 2: Build + Push                       │
         │  ┌──────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────┐               │
         │  │ FROM python:3.11 │        │ $ docker build -t        │               │
         │  │ WORKDIR /app     │        │   ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1 .│               │
         │  │ COPY req.txt .   │        │ $ docker push            │               │
         │  │ RUN pip install  │        │   ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1  │               │
         │  │ COPY . .         │        └───────────┬──────────────┘               │
         │  │ CMD ["gunicorn"] │                    │                               │
         │  └──────────────────┘                    ▼                               │
         │                                   ┌──────────────┐                      │
         │  STEP 3-4: K8s Deployment         │ 🗄️ Registry   │                      │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────┐     │ ghcr.io/you/  │                      │
         │  │ apiVersion: apps/v1      │     │ easypj:v1     │                      │
         │  │ kind: Deployment         │     └──────┬───────┘                      │
         │  │ metadata:                │            │                               │
         │  │   name: easypj           │            │ STEP 5-6: Deploy              │
         │  │ spec:                    │            ▼                               │
         │  │   replicas: 3            │     ┌──────────────────────────┐          │
         │  │   template:              │     │ $ kubectl apply \        │          │
         │  │     containers:          │     │   -f deployment.yaml     │          │
         │  │     - image: ghcr.io/    │     │ $ kubectl apply \        │          │
         │  │       you/easypj:v1      │     │   -f service.yaml        │          │
         │  │       ports:             │     │ $ kubectl get pods       │          │
         │  │       - containerPort:8000│    │ NAME        READY STATUS │          │
         │  └──────────────────────────┘     │ easypj-xxx  1/1   Running│          │
         │                                   │ easypj-yyy  1/1   Running│          │
         │  STEP 5: K8s Service              │ easypj-zzz  1/1   Running│          │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────┐     └──────────────────────────┘          │
         │  │ apiVersion: v1           │                                            │
         │  │ kind: Service            │     RESULT: easypj running on 3 pods,      │
         │  │ spec:                    │     accessible via Service on port 8000!   │
         │  │   selector:              │                                            │
         │  │     app: easypj          │                                            │
         │  │   ports:                 │                                            │
         │  │   - port: 8000           │                                            │
         │  │     targetPort: 8000     │                                            │
         │  │   type: LoadBalancer     │                                            │
         │  └──────────────────────────┘                                            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Walk the 6-step journey above end-to-end and name which
            steps are pure Docker and which are pure Kubernetes.
         [TECH] Steps 1-2 (Dockerfile, build, push) are 100% Docker —
            nothing changes from Part 3. Steps 3-6 hand the SAME image to
            a Deployment (desired replica count + Pod template) and a
            Service (stable network identity for those replicas) — kubectl
            reads these YAMLs and drives the API server toward that state.
         [ANALOGY] The journey diagram above is a factory line: stations
            1-2 (Dockerfile, build+push) are the loading dock you already
            run; stations 3-6 hand the crate to a NEW shipping department
            (K8s) deciding how many trucks (replicas) and which docks
            (Service) receive it.
         🔬 easypj: `docker build -t ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1 .` does not
            change at all; only what happens AFTER `docker push` is new —
            a Deployment named easypj requests replicas: 3 of that tag.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: in the diagram's Deployment YAML, find the ONE
            line that is just the Dockerfile's build output (the image:
            field) and the ONE line that never existed in Docker
            (replicas: 3). Verify: both map to concepts Chapters 4-5
            already taught you cold.
         📊 SUMMARY: build+push stays Docker; "how many, where, reachable
            how" becomes Kubernetes' job via Deployment + Service YAML.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `kubectl apply` never builds or pushes images — if
            the tag in deployment.yaml was never pushed, every Pod sits in
            ImagePullBackOff forever.

    25.4 docker-compose.yml → Kubernetes Manifests
         - Tool: Kompose (kompose convert)
         - Manual translation: compose services → K8s Deployments
         - compose networks → K8s Services
         - compose volumes → K8s PersistentVolumeClaims
         [CODE: Side-by-side: compose.yaml → deployment.yaml + service.yaml]


         🎯 GOAL: Convert a real easypj compose.yaml into K8s manifests two
            ways — automatic and manual — and know when to trust each.
         [CODE: compose.yaml → K8s, side-by-side core — drawn]
            # compose.yaml (Chapter 11)     # kompose convert output
            services:                       # deployment.yaml
              django:                       #   containers:
                image: easypj:v1            #   - image: easypj:v1
                ports: ["8000:8000"]        #     ports: [{containerPort:8000}]
                depends_on: [postgres]      # service.yaml
                                             #   selector: {app: django}
              postgres:                     # deployment.yaml (2nd) + PVC
                volumes: [pgdata:/var/...]  #   volumeMounts + PVC: pgdata
         [TECH] `kompose convert -f compose.yaml` reads the SAME file
            Chapter 11 built and emits one Deployment + Service per
            service, plus a PVC per named volume — a first draft, not a
            final manifest (guesses replicas: 1, no resource limits, no
            probes).
         [ANALOGY] kompose is a rough translator: it gets the nouns right
            (django, postgres, pgdata) fast, but a human still edits the
            grammar (replicas, health checks, secrets) before it ships.
         🔬 easypj: kompose turns `depends_on: [postgres]` into nothing —
            K8s has no depends_on; Chapter 27's readiness probes replace it.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `kompose convert -f compose.yaml` on your
            easypj file. Verify: count the generated files (one Deployment
            + Service per service, one PVC for pgdata) and confirm the
            postgres depends_on silently disappeared.
         📊 SUMMARY: kompose bootstraps manifests from a file you already
            own; manual translation (Chapter 26) gives production control
            kompose's guesses don't.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: never ship kompose's raw output — no resource
            limits and no health checks means an unbounded, unmonitored Pod.

     cluster limits (self-healing, scaling, rollback), the Docker↔K8s

     why `kubectl apply` never builds or pushes images, kompose convert's
     output vs a hand-written manifest, why depends_on has no K8s
     equivalent, ImagePullBackOff root cause, PVC vs Docker volume]

Chapter 26: Deploying easypj to Kubernetes
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapter 25 mapped nouns and traced one
    journey in the abstract; this chapter runs it for real. Every YAML
    here targets the SAME easypj image from Part 3, deployed onto an
    actual cluster you can reach with kubectl. Problem solved here: "I
    understand Deployments and Services in theory — show me easypj
    actually serving traffic from inside a real K8s cluster."

    26.1 Prerequisites for K8s Deployment
         - Kubernetes cluster (Minikube, kind, k3s, or cloud)
         - kubectl configured
         - Container registry access
         - Docker image pushed to registry
         [CHECKLIST: 5 prerequisites verified]

         🎯 GOAL: Confirm all 4 prerequisites before writing a single YAML
            line — most "kubectl apply hangs" reports trace back to here.
         [CHECKLIST: before you write any manifest — drawn]
            [ ] a cluster exists — `kubectl cluster-info` returns a URL
            [ ] kubectl context points at it — `kubectl config current-context`
            [ ] you can push to a registry — `docker login ghcr.io` succeeds
            [ ] the easypj image is already pushed — `docker push` completed
         [TECH] `kubectl` reads ~/.kube/config to pick a context — the SAME
            binary can silently point at Minikube, kind, or a production
            cluster depending on which context is current. Cluster
            bootstrap internals (kubeadm, control-plane HA):
            [KUBERNETES GUIDE].
         [ANALOGY] Checking you have the right house key AND the right
            house before knocking — a valid kubectl config for the WRONG
            cluster fails exactly like no config at all, just more
            confusingly.
         🔬 easypj: `docker push ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1` must succeed BEFORE
            any Deployment references that tag — kubectl never builds
            images, it only schedules ones already in a registry.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the 4 checklist commands above in order.
            Verify: all 4 succeed BEFORE you type `kubectl apply` once —
            if step 2 shows the wrong cluster, stop and fix context first.
         📊 SUMMARY: cluster reachable + correct context + image pushed —
            three gates, checked in order, before any YAML runs.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a local Minikube cluster cannot pull an image that
            only exists in your local `docker images` — push it to a
            reachable registry, or `minikube image load` it directly.

    26.2 Building & Pushing the easypj Image
         - docker build -t ghcr.io/username/easypj:v1.0 .
         - docker login ghcr.io
         - docker push ghcr.io/username/easypj:v1.0
         [TERMINAL: Complete build + push workflow]

         🎯 GOAL: Produce one correctly-tagged, pushed image that 26.3's
            Deployment can reference by exact name.
         [TERMINAL: build + push, drawn]
            $ docker build -t ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.0 .
            $ docker login ghcr.io -u you --password-stdin < token.txt
            $ docker push ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.0
            $ docker manifest inspect ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.0   # confirms it
         [TECH] The tag is the ONLY link between this chapter and the
            Deployment YAML — `image: ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.0` must match
            character-for-character, including the registry host. `:latest`
            is banned here: K8s cannot tell "new latest" from "old latest".
         [ANALOGY] Mailing a package: the tag is the exact street address
            on the label. "v1.0" is a house number; "latest" is "somewhere
            on this street" — the carrier (kubelet) needs the number.
         🔬 easypj: reuse Chapter 8's multi-stage Dockerfile unchanged —
            only the registry host (ghcr.io/you/) is new; the build itself
            is identical to every prior chapter.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: after pushing, run `docker manifest inspect` on
            the exact tag. Verify: it returns JSON, not a 404 — a 404 here
            is the #1 cause of ImagePullBackOff in the next section.
         📊 SUMMARY: build once, tag precisely, push before any YAML
            references it.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: private registries need an imagePullSecret in the
            cluster — pushing successfully from your laptop does NOT mean
            the cluster can pull; 26.5's Secret is half of that fix.

    26.3 Creating the Kubernetes Deployment
         - apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment
         - metadata: name, labels
         - spec: replicas, selector, template
         - container spec: image, ports, env
         - Resource limits: requests and limits
         [COMPLETE deployment.yaml CODE with annotations]

         🎯 GOAL: Write a Deployment that names the exact image, exposes
            the right port, and never lets one runaway Pod starve the node.
         [CODE: deployment.yaml — drawn, core fields only]
            apiVersion: apps/v1
            kind: Deployment
            metadata: {name: easypj, labels: {app: easypj}}
            spec:
              replicas: 3
              selector: {matchLabels: {app: easypj}}
              template:
                metadata: {labels: {app: easypj}}
                spec:
                  containers:
                  - name: django
                    image: ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.0
                    ports: [{containerPort: 8000}]
                    resources:
                      requests: {cpu: "100m", memory: "128Mi"}
                      limits:   {cpu: "500m", memory: "256Mi"}
         [TECH] `selector.matchLabels` must equal `template.metadata.labels`
            — this is how the Deployment finds ITS OWN Pods among all Pods
            in the namespace. requests reserve capacity the scheduler
            counts against; limits are a hard ceiling the kubelet enforces.
         [ANALOGY] selector/labels are a name tag, not a leash: the
            Deployment scans the room for Pods wearing "app: easypj" and
            claims them — mismatched labels means it creates orphan Pods
            forever, never recognizing the ones it already made.
         🔬 easypj: gunicorn's own --workers setting (Chapter 6) now works
            ALONGSIDE replicas: 3 — each Pod runs N gunicorn workers,
            times 3 Pods, for the real concurrency ceiling.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml`, then
            `kubectl get pods -l app=easypj`. Verify: exactly 3 Pods, all
            Running, all showing image ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1.0.
         📊 SUMMARY: replicas + matching selector/labels + a container spec
            with real resource requests/limits — the minimum viable
            Deployment.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: skip resources: and one memory-leaking Pod can
            starve every other Pod on its node — this is not optional
            in production.

    26.4 Creating the Kubernetes Service
         - apiVersion: v1, kind: Service
         - spec: selector, ports, type (ClusterIP/NodePort/LoadBalancer)
         - Service type comparison
         [COMPLETE service.yaml CODE with annotations]

         🎯 GOAL: Give the 3 easypj Pods ONE stable address, and pick the
            right Service type for dev vs production.
         [CODE: service.yaml — drawn]
            apiVersion: v1
            kind: Service
            metadata: {name: easypj}
            spec:
              selector: {app: easypj}
              ports: [{port: 8000, targetPort: 8000}]
              type: ClusterIP
         [TABLE: Service type → who can reach it]
            ClusterIP    | inside the cluster only (default, most services)
            NodePort     | cluster + any node IP:30000-32767 (dev/testing)
            LoadBalancer | the public internet, via a cloud LB (production)
         [TECH] A Service is a stable virtual IP + DNS name (easypj.
            default.svc.cluster.local) in front of a Pod IP set that
            changes every time a Pod restarts — kube-proxy keeps the
            mapping current. CNI/kube-proxy internals: [KUBERNETES GUIDE].
         [ANALOGY] Pods are hotel guests reassigned rooms nightly; the
            Service is the FRONT DESK phone number — always the same, no
            matter which room today's guest is actually in.
         🔬 easypj: nginx's old `proxy_pass http://django:8000` (Chapter
            11) becomes `proxy_pass http://easypj:8000` — same pattern,
            the DNS name now resolves to a Service, not a container.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `kubectl apply -f service.yaml`, then `kubectl
            run tmp --rm -it --image=busybox -- wget -qO- easypj:8000`.
            Verify: it returns HTML even though you never typed a Pod IP.
         📊 SUMMARY: selector finds the Pods, type decides who's allowed to
            call — ClusterIP by default, open up only what needs opening.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a Service's selector uses the SAME label-matching
            rules as a Deployment — a typo silently creates a Service with
            zero endpoints (`kubectl get endpoints easypj` shows empty).

    26.5 ConfigMaps & Secrets for easypj
         - ConfigMap for Django settings (non-sensitive)
         - Secret for SECRET_KEY, DB_PASSWORD
         - Mounting ConfigMap as env vars
         [COMPLETE configmap.yaml + secret.yaml CODE]

         🎯 GOAL: Split easypj's settings into non-secret (ConfigMap) and
            secret (Secret) — never mix SECRET_KEY into a plain YAML again.
         [CODE: configmap.yaml + secret.yaml — drawn]
            apiVersion: v1
            kind: ConfigMap
            metadata: {name: easypj-config}
            data: {DEBUG: "False", ALLOWED_HOSTS: "easypj.example.com"}
            ---
            apiVersion: v1
            kind: Secret
            metadata: {name: easypj-secret}
            type: Opaque
            stringData:
              SECRET_KEY: django-insecure-CHANGE-ME
              DATABASE_URL: postgres://easypj:pg-pass@postgres:5432/easypj_db
         [TECH] A Secret is base64-ENCODED in etcd by default, not
            encrypted — treat it as obscured, not safe from a cluster
            admin. `stringData` lets you write plaintext; K8s encodes it
            on save. Real encryption-at-rest: [KUBERNETES GUIDE].
         [ANALOGY] Directly maps to Chapter 11's .env split: ConfigMap is
            the postcard (DEBUG, ALLOWED_HOSTS), Secret is the sealed
            envelope (SECRET_KEY, DATABASE_URL) — same two piles, now two
            cluster-native objects instead of one file.
         🔬 easypj: settings.py's `os.environ["SECRET_KEY"]` call does not
            change at all — only WHERE the env var comes from changes,
            via envFrom in the Deployment (26.6).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `kubectl apply -f secret.yaml`, then `kubectl
            get secret easypj-secret -o yaml`. Verify: SECRET_KEY appears
            base64-encoded, not plaintext — `base64 -d` proves it round-trips.
         📊 SUMMARY: ConfigMap for what's fine to read, Secret for what
            isn't — same env-var split as .env, now cluster objects.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `kubectl apply -f secret.yaml` from a git-committed
            file defeats the point — real production needs a vault/sealed-
            secrets pipeline, not a checked-in YAML.

    26.6 Deploying & Verifying
         - kubectl apply -f namespace.yaml
         - kubectl apply -f configmap.yaml, secret.yaml
         - kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml, service.yaml
         - kubectl get pods, svc, deploy
         - kubectl port-forward svc/easypj 8000:8000
         - Open browser, verify easypj is running
         [TERMINAL: Full deploy + verify sequence]

         🎯 GOAL: Go from zero to "easypj answering HTTP requests through
            K8s" using only the 4 YAMLs this chapter built.
         [TERMINAL: full deploy + verify, drawn]
            $ kubectl create namespace easypj
            $ kubectl apply -f configmap.yaml -f secret.yaml -n easypj
            $ kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml -f service.yaml -n easypj
            $ kubectl get pods,svc -n easypj
            $ kubectl port-forward svc/easypj 8000:8000 -n easypj
            $ curl -I http://localhost:8000/jobs/       # HTTP/1.1 200 OK
         [TECH] `envFrom: [{configMapRef: {name: easypj-config}},
            {secretRef: {name: easypj-secret}}]` in the container spec is
            what actually wires 26.5's objects into the Pod's environment —
            without it, ConfigMap/Secret exist but nothing reads them.
         [ANALOGY] port-forward is a temporary phone line straight to one
            Service, bypassing the front desk's real routing — perfect for
            verification, never used for production traffic.
         🔬 easypj: the same `/jobs/` view Chapter 11's nginx served now
            answers through kubectl port-forward → Service → Pod →
            gunicorn — one more hop, same Django code, unmodified.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the six lines above in order. Verify:
            `get pods` shows 3/3 Running BEFORE you port-forward —
            forwarding to a CrashLoopBackOff Pod just times out silently.
         📊 SUMMARY: namespace → config/secret → deployment/service →
            verify — the same order every real rollout follows.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: forgetting `-n easypj` on any command runs it
            against the `default` namespace — objects "disappear" only
            because you're looking in the wrong namespace.

    26.7 Pods vs Deployments — Scaling easypj
         - Single Pod (ephemeral, not recommended)
         - Deployment (manages ReplicaSet, ensures replicas)
         - Scaling: kubectl scale deployment easypj --replicas=5
         - kubectl get pods -w (watch pods come up)
         [DIAGRAM: Deployment → ReplicaSet → Pods (×5)]


         🎯 GOAL: Explain why you scale the Deployment, never a Pod, and
            trace the object chain that makes `--replicas=5` create Pods.
         [DIAGRAM:architecture — the ownership chain, drawn]
            Deployment easypj (desired: replicas=5)
                    │ creates & owns
                    ▼
            ReplicaSet easypj-7d9f4 (current: 5)
                    │ creates & owns
                    ▼
            Pod  Pod  Pod  Pod  Pod   (each independently schedulable)
         [TECH] A bare Pod has no controller watching it — kill it and it
            stays dead. A Deployment owns a ReplicaSet, which continuously
            reconciles "Pods running" against "replicas: desired"; killing
            a Pod under a Deployment gets it replaced within seconds.
         [ANALOGY] A bare Pod is a single hire with no manager — vanish
            and the seat stays empty. A Deployment is HR policy: "this
            desk always has 5 people" — someone quits, HR (ReplicaSet)
            posts the job again automatically.
         🔬 easypj: `kubectl scale deployment easypj --replicas=5` takes
            easypj from 3 to 5 Pods — Django code untouched, gunicorn
            config untouched, only the REPLICA COUNT changed.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `kubectl scale deployment easypj --replicas=5`,
            then `kubectl delete pod <any-easypj-pod>` and immediately
            `kubectl get pods -w`. Verify: a NEW Pod appears within
            seconds — the ReplicaSet replaced it without you asking twice.
         📊 SUMMARY: never manage Pods directly — scale/manage the
            Deployment; the ReplicaSet is the enforcement layer underneath.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: deleting a Pod created by a ReplicaSet does not
            "clean up" anything — it triggers an immediate replacement,
            the opposite of what a beginner usually expects.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 26 — minimum 10, covering: the 4 prerequisite
     gates and why context matters, tag precision vs `:latest`, matching
     selector/labels between Deployment and Pod template, requests vs
     limits, ClusterIP vs NodePort vs LoadBalancer, why a Service can have
     zero endpoints, ConfigMap vs Secret and stringData encoding, envFrom
     wiring, namespace scoping mistakes, Deployment→ReplicaSet→Pod
     ownership and why you never scale a bare Pod]

Chapter 27: Kubernetes Deployment Strategies
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: easypj is now running in K8s — but "running"
    and "safely changeable" are different problems. This chapter covers
    what happens the SECOND time you deploy: shipping v2 without downtime,
    undoing a bad release in seconds, proving a Pod is actually healthy
    before it takes traffic, and keeping dev/staging/prod from sharing one
    fragile YAML. Problem solved here: "the first deploy worked — now how
    do I change anything without breaking production?"

    27.1 Rolling Updates — Zero Downtime
         - kubectl set image deployment/easypj django=ghcr.io/username/easypj:v2.0
         - kubectl rollout status deployment/easypj
         - How rolling update works: one pod at a time
         - maxSurge and maxUnavailable

         [ROLLING UPDATE VISUALIZED — 5 Pods, Zero Downtime]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                 🔄 ROLLING UPDATE — Step by Step                           │
         │                                                                          │
         │  BEFORE (v1):  ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐  5 pods running easypj:v1      │
         │                │v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v1│                                  │
         │                └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘                                  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  STEP 1:       ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐  New v2 pod created        │
         │  (maxSurge=1)  │v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v2│  (6 pods total)            │
         │                └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘                             │
         │                                                                          │
         │  STEP 2:       ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐  One v1 terminated             │
         │  (maxUnavail=1)│v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v2│  (5 pods total)                │
         │                └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘                                  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  STEP 3:       ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐  New v2 pod (#2)           │
         │                │v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v1│ │v2│ │v2│                             │
         │                └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘                             │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ...continues one-by-one until...                                         │
         │                                                                          │
         │  AFTER (v2):   ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐  All 5 pods running v2!        │
         │                │v2│ │v2│ │v2│ │v2│ │v2│  ZERO DOWNTIME ✅               │
         │                └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘                                  │
         │                                                                          │
         │  KEY CONFIG:                                                              │
         │  spec:                                                                    │
         │    strategy:                                                              │
         │      type: RollingUpdate                                                 │
         │      rollingUpdate:                                                       │
         │        maxSurge: 1       # Extra pods allowed during update               │
         │        maxUnavailable: 1 # Pods that can be unavailable during update     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Predict, from maxSurge/maxUnavailable alone, exactly how
            many Pods of each version exist at every step of the diagram.
         [TECH] `kubectl set image` changes the Pod template inside the
            Deployment; the ReplicaSet controller then creates a NEW
            ReplicaSet (v2) and shrinks the OLD one (v1) in lockstep,
            bounded by maxSurge (extra Pods allowed above replicas) and
            maxUnavailable (Pods allowed missing below replicas).
         [ANALOGY] The step-by-step diagram above is a relay handoff: with
            maxSurge=1 a 6th runner (v2) joins the track BEFORE the 1st
            (v1) steps off — never fewer than 5 runners moving, per
            maxUnavailable=1.
         🔬 easypj: `kubectl set image deployment/easypj
            django=ghcr.io/you/easypj:v2.0` starts the exact sequence in
            the diagram — 5 Pods stay 5 Pods, never dropping below 4
            Ready with the defaults shown.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run `kubectl rollout status deployment/easypj`
            right after the set image command. Verify: it blocks and
            prints progress until "successfully rolled out" — Ctrl+C
            only stops your terminal's VIEW, not the rollout itself.
         📊 SUMMARY: RollingUpdate replaces Pods gradually, bounded by
            maxSurge (over-capacity) and maxUnavailable (under-capacity).
            ⚠️ Gotcha: rolling update is USELESS against a bad image that
            passes startup but fails at runtime under load — pair it with
            27.3's readiness probes or it ships broken Pods as "healthy".

    27.2 Rollback — Undoing a Bad Deploy
         - kubectl rollout undo deployment/easypj
         - kubectl rollout history deployment/easypj
         - kubectl rollout undo deployment/easypj --to-revision=2
         [TERMINAL: Rollback workflow]

         🎯 GOAL: Undo a bad release in one command, to a specific past
            revision if the immediate-previous one is also broken.
         [TERMINAL: rollback workflow — drawn]
            $ kubectl rollout history deployment/easypj
            #  REVISION  CHANGE-CAUSE
            #  1         initial v1.0
            #  2         v2.0 (bad)
            $ kubectl rollout undo deployment/easypj
            $ kubectl rollout undo deployment/easypj --to-revision=1
         [TECH] Each `kubectl apply`/`set image` that changes the Pod
            template creates a NEW ReplicaSet revision; K8s keeps the
            last N (default 10, via `revisionHistoryLimit`) around so
            undo can re-point the Deployment at an old ReplicaSet.
         [ANALOGY] The same relay from 27.1, run in reverse: `rollout
            undo` reuses the SAME rolling-update machinery, just swapping
            which ReplicaSet scales up and which scales down.
         🔬 easypj: v2.0 shipped a migration that broke `/jobs/` with a
            500 — `kubectl rollout undo deployment/easypj` restores v1.0
            Pods with zero downtime, same mechanism as the original deploy.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: deploy a deliberately broken tag, confirm
            `kubectl rollout status` reports failure, then undo it.
            Verify: `kubectl rollout history` still shows both revisions —
            undo adds a new revision, it does not delete history.
         📊 SUMMARY: rollout history + rollout undo turn a bad release
            into a one-line fix, using the same engine as a forward deploy.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `revisionHistoryLimit` can prune old ReplicaSets —
            you cannot undo to a revision K8s already garbage-collected.

    27.3 Health Checks — Liveness & Readiness Probes
         - Readiness probe: Is the app ready to serve traffic?
         - Liveness probe: Is the app still healthy?
         - Django health check endpoint
         - Probe types: httpGet, tcpSocket, exec
         [CODE: Deployment with liveness and readiness probes]

         🎯 GOAL: Stop K8s from sending traffic to a Pod that is up but
            not READY, and from keeping a Pod alive that is up but stuck.
         [CODE: probes added to 26.3's container spec — drawn]
            containers:
            - name: django
              image: ghcr.io/you/easypj:v2.0
              readinessProbe:
                httpGet: {path: /healthz/, port: 8000}
                initialDelaySeconds: 5
                periodSeconds: 10
              livenessProbe:
                httpGet: {path: /healthz/, port: 8000}
                initialDelaySeconds: 15
                periodSeconds: 20
         [TECH] readinessProbe controls Service membership — a failing
            readiness check removes the Pod's IP from the Service's
            endpoint list (traffic stops) WITHOUT restarting it.
            livenessProbe controls the Pod's own lifecycle — a failing
            liveness check gets the container KILLED and restarted.
         [ANALOGY] Readiness is a "back of house, not seated yet" sign —
            the front desk stops sending guests, staff keeps working.
            Liveness is a "this employee is unresponsive" alarm — pull
            them out and bring in a replacement.
         🔬 easypj: `GET /healthz/` is a tiny Django view checking DB
            connectivity — during a slow migration it fails readiness
            (correctly hides the Pod) without triggering any restart.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: temporarily make `/healthz/` return 500,
            apply, then `kubectl get endpoints easypj`. Verify: the Pod's
            IP disappears from endpoints while `kubectl get pods` still
            shows it Running — readiness failed, liveness didn't.
         📊 SUMMARY: readinessProbe = "can it serve traffic right now?";
            livenessProbe = "is it stuck and needs a restart?" — different
            questions, different consequences.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: identical liveness and readiness thresholds can
            cause restart storms — a slow-but-recovering Pod gets killed
            by liveness before readiness would have simply hidden it.

    27.4 Environment-Specific Deployments
         - Namespace isolation: dev, staging, prod
         - kubectl create namespace staging
         - kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml -n staging
         - Kustomize overlays for env differences
         [CODE: Kustomize structure for dev/staging/prod]

         🎯 GOAL: Run the SAME easypj manifests in dev, staging and prod
            without maintaining three copy-pasted YAML sets.
         [CODE: namespace + Kustomize overlay skeleton — drawn]
            $ kubectl create namespace staging
            $ kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml -f service.yaml -n staging
            # kustomize/base/     deployment.yaml, service.yaml,
            #                     kustomization.yaml
            # kustomize/overlays/staging/  kustomization.yaml, patch.yaml
            # kustomize/overlays/prod/     kustomization.yaml, patch.yaml
            $ kubectl apply -k kustomize/overlays/staging
         [TECH] A Namespace scopes NAMES (two `easypj` Deployments in
            different namespaces never collide); Kustomize scopes FILES —
            a base manifest plus small per-environment patches (replica
            count, resource limits, image tag) merged at apply time.
         [ANALOGY] Namespaces are separate apartments in the same
            building — same address format, no shared furniture.
            Kustomize overlays are one blueprint with sticky notes per
            apartment ("prod: add 2 bedrooms") instead of redrawing the
            whole plan three times.
         🔬 easypj: base/ ships replicas: 1 and DEBUG=True; overlays/prod
            patches replicas: 5 and DEBUG=False — one Deployment source,
            two real behaviors.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create staging and prod namespaces, then
            `kubectl apply -k` each overlay. Verify: `kubectl get deploy
            -A` shows easypj in BOTH namespaces with DIFFERENT replicas.
         📊 SUMMARY: Namespace = isolation boundary; Kustomize = one base
            + per-env patches, no copy-paste drift between environments.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Namespaces do NOT isolate node resources or network
            by default — that needs ResourceQuota (27.5) and
            NetworkPolicy [KUBERNETES GUIDE].

    27.5 Resource Management in K8s
         - Requests: minimum guaranteed (scheduler uses this)
         - Limits: maximum allowed
         - QoS Classes: Guaranteed, Burstable, BestEffort
         - ResourceQuota: namespace-level limits
         [CODE: Deployment with resource requests/limits]

         🎯 GOAL: Predict which QoS class a Pod gets from its
            requests/limits alone, and set a namespace-wide guardrail.
         [TABLE: requests vs limits → QoS class]
            requests == limits (both set)     | Guaranteed (evicted last)
            requests < limits (both set)      | Burstable  (evicted 2nd)
            no requests, no limits set at all | BestEffort (evicted first)
         [CODE: ResourceQuota for a namespace — drawn]
            apiVersion: v1
            kind: ResourceQuota
            metadata: {name: easypj-quota, namespace: easypj}
            spec:
              hard: {requests.cpu: "2", requests.memory: 2Gi,
                     limits.cpu: "4", limits.memory: 4Gi}
         [TECH] The scheduler places Pods using requests (guaranteed
            capacity, never oversold); the kubelet enforces limits (hard
            ceiling — CPU throttled, memory over-limit gets the container
            OOMKilled). ResourceQuota caps the SUM across every Pod in a
            namespace, so one team cannot starve another.
         [ANALOGY] requests are a reserved hotel room (always yours);
            limits are the room's maximum occupancy (fire code); a
            ResourceQuota is the whole floor's total occupancy cap shared
            across every room on it.
         🔬 easypj: setting requests==limits on the django container (as
            in 26.3) makes it Guaranteed — the LAST Pod evicted if the
            node runs low, exactly what you want for the web tier.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: apply the ResourceQuota above, then try to
            scale easypj past what it allows. Verify: `kubectl describe
            resourcequota easypj-quota` shows used vs hard, and the
            over-quota Pods sit Pending with a quota-exceeded event.
         📊 SUMMARY: requests reserve, limits cap, QoS class decides
            eviction order, ResourceQuota caps the whole namespace.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a Pod with NO requests/limits (BestEffort) is the
            FIRST killed under node pressure — silence here is not
            safety, it's the worst QoS class by default.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 27 — minimum 10, covering: maxSurge vs
     maxUnavailable Pod-count math, why rolling update alone doesn't catch
     runtime failures, rollout history + revisionHistoryLimit, undo vs
     redeploy, readiness vs liveness probe consequences, restart-storm
     risk from identical thresholds, Namespace vs Kustomize overlay
     scoping, requests vs limits, the 3 QoS classes and eviction order,
     ResourceQuota at the namespace level]
