PART 10: ADVANCED DOCKER & CERTIFICATION PREP                              🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

    THINK BEFORE PART 10:
      What runs where?    → tasks land on any node; managers also run tasks.
      What talks to what? → docker CLI → leader manager → Raft log → workers.
      What breaks first?  → quorum — lose (N/2)+1 managers, writes halt.

Chapter 28: Docker Swarm — Native Orchestration
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Kubernetes is powerful but heavy — sometimes
    a small team needs multi-host orchestration WITHOUT a new tool, a new
    CLI, or a new YAML dialect. Swarm is that answer: it is Docker itself,
    turned into a cluster with three extra commands. This chapter builds
    Swarm from init to production-grade quorum, update and rollback
    configuration — knowledge the DCA exam weights at 25%, its single
    largest domain (see Chapter 30). Problem solved here: "I know Docker
    commands — how do I run them across a real cluster today?"

    28.1 What is Docker Swarm?
         - Docker's built-in orchestrator (simpler than K8s)
         - Manager nodes (control) + Worker nodes (run containers)
         - Declarative service model: desired state
         - Built-in service discovery + load balancing

         [DOCKER SWARM — CLUSTER ARCHITECTURE]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                     🐝 DOCKER SWARM ARCHITECTURE                            │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
         │  │                     SWARM CLUSTER                                 │    │
         │  │                                                                  │    │
         │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐  ┌───────────────────────────┐ │    │
         │  │  │  🧠 MANAGER NODE 1 (Leader)  │  │  🧠 MANAGER NODE 2        │ │    │
         │  │  │                              │  │  (Follower, standby)      │ │    │
         │  │  │  • Maintains cluster state   │  │                           │ │    │
         │  │  │  • Raft consensus DB         │  │  • Takes over if Leader   │ │    │
         │  │  │  • Scheduler (assigns tasks) │  │    fails!                 │ │    │
         │  │  │  • API endpoint              │  │                           │ │    │
         │  │  │  • docker service create     │  │  ⚠️ Managers also run     │ │    │
         │  │  │                              │  │     tasks (can opt out)   │ │    │
         │  │  └──────────────┬───────────────┘  └───────────────────────────┘ │    │
         │  │                 │                                                 │    │
         │  │                 │  Assign tasks                                   │    │
         │  │                 ▼                                                 │    │
         │  │  ┌───────────────────────────┐  ┌───────────────────────────┐    │    │
         │  │  │  💪 WORKER NODE 1        │  │  💪 WORKER NODE 2         │    │    │
         │  │  │                           │  │                           │    │    │
         │  │  │  easypj:v1 (replica 1)   │  │  easypj:v1 (replica 2)   │    │    │
         │  │  │  postgres:15 (replica 1) │  │  postgres:15 (replica 2) │    │    │
         │  │  │  redis:7                 │  │  nginx:latest             │    │    │
         │  │  │                           │  │                           │    │    │
         │  │  │  Reports health to mgr   │  │  Reports health to mgr    │    │    │
         │  │  └───────────────────────────┘  └───────────────────────────┘    │    │
         │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
         │                                                                          │
         │  ROUTING MESH: curl ANY_NODE:8000 → routes to easypj container ✅        │
         │  OVERLAY NETWORK: Containers on different nodes communicate seamlessly  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         🎯 GOAL: Point to the exact box in the architecture diagram above
            that answers "who decides where MY service's tasks run?"
         [TECH] A manager node runs the Raft-replicated cluster store, the
            orchestrator (reconciles desired vs actual tasks) and the
            scheduler (assigns tasks to nodes); workers only run tasks and
            report health back. `docker service create` is declarative —
            you state desired replicas, Swarm keeps making it true.
         [ANALOGY] The architecture diagram above is a restaurant chain HQ
            (managers, Raft-synced so any one can answer) directing
            several kitchens (workers) — orders (services) go to HQ, HQ
            decides which kitchen cooks which dish (task placement).
         🔬 easypj: `docker service create --name easypj --replicas 3
            easypj:v1` looks exactly like `docker run`, but Swarm spreads
            the 3 tasks across however many worker nodes exist.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: on a running Swarm, `docker node ls` then
            `docker service ps easypj`. Verify: the NODE column shows
            tasks landing on different nodes, not all on the manager.
         📊 SUMMARY: managers hold state + decide placement (via Raft);
            workers just execute — the SAME docker CLI drives both.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: managers, by default, ALSO run tasks — under load
            this steals CPU from the control plane; drain them with
            `docker node update --availability drain` in production.

    28.2 Swarm vs Kubernetes
         - Simplicity: Swarm wins (docker commands you already know)
         - Ecosystem: K8s wins (vast tooling and community)
         - When to use Swarm: small teams, simple deployments
         - When to use K8s: complex apps, multi-cloud, large scale

         [SWARM vs KUBERNETES — 20 DIMENSIONS]
         | Dimension             | 🐝 Docker Swarm        | ☸️ Kubernetes          |
         | Installation          | Built into Docker ✅   | kubeadm/minikube      |
         | CLI                   | docker service create  | kubectl apply -f      |
         | Learning Curve        | 🟢 Low (hours)         | 🔴 High (weeks/months)|
         | Scaling               | docker service scale   | kubectl scale + HPA   |
         | Rolling Updates       | ✅ Built-in            | ✅ Built-in            |
         | Rollback              | ✅ docker service roll.| ✅ kubectl rollout undo|
         | Service Discovery     | ✅ DNS built-in        | ✅ CoreDNS             |
         | Load Balancing         | ✅ Routing Mesh        | ✅ Service + Ingress   |
         | Secrets               | ✅ docker secret       | ✅ K8s Secrets         |
         | Configs               | ✅ docker config       | ✅ K8s ConfigMaps      |
         | Multi-Tenancy         | ❌ None                | ✅ Namespaces          |
         | RBAC                  | ❌ Limited             | ✅ Full RBAC           |
         | Storage Orchestration | ⚠️ Volume plugins      | ✅ PV+PVC+CSI          |
         | Auto-Scaling          | ❌ Manual only         | ✅ HPA built-in        |
         | Auto-Healing          | ✅ Basic (restart)     | ✅ Advanced (reschedule)|
         | Ecosystem             | 🔴 Small               | 🟢 Massive (CNCF)      |
         | Cloud Support         | ⚠️ Limited             | ✅ EKS/GKE/AKS         |
         | GitOps Support        | ❌ Minimal             | ✅ ArgoCD/Flux         |
         | Community             | 🟡 Medium              | 🟢 Huge                |
         | Best For              | 3-10 nodes, simple     | 10-1000+ nodes, complex|

         🎯 GOAL: Given a real team's constraints, choose Swarm or
            Kubernetes in one sentence, using the table above as evidence.
         [TECH] The 20-dimension table above is not close on ecosystem,
            multi-tenancy or auto-scaling — Swarm loses those outright. It
            is genuinely competitive on the FIRST five rows: installation,
            CLI familiarity, learning curve, scaling, rolling updates —
            because Swarm reuses the docker CLI you already know.
         [ANALOGY] Swarm is a reliable pickup truck: no towing rating for
            a highway of microservices, but it starts every morning with
            a key you already own. Kubernetes is the highway fleet — more
            capability, more paperwork, more training to drive it.
         🔬 easypj: a 3-node team demo needs zero-downtime deploys and
            secrets — row-for-row, Swarm's ✅ column covers exactly that;
            the ❌ rows (RBAC, multi-tenancy, HPA) don't matter at 3 nodes.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: from the table, list every row where Swarm is
            ❌ or ⚠️. Verify: your list is a checklist for "when to
            migrate to K8s" — once 2+ of those rows start to matter.
         📊 SUMMARY: same docker vocabulary, smaller blast radius, smaller
            ecosystem — Swarm for small/simple, K8s for large/complex,
            per the table's Best For row.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the DCA exam tests SWARM, not Kubernetes — don't
            let K8s knowledge from Chapters 25-27 substitute for hands-on
            `docker service`/`docker stack` practice.

    28.3 Creating a Swarm Cluster
         - docker swarm init (create first manager)
         - docker swarm join --token ... (add workers)
         - docker node ls (view cluster)
         [TERMINAL: Full swarm setup on 3 nodes]

         🎯 GOAL: Stand up a real 3-node Swarm and prove all 3 nodes are
            visible from the manager, in under 5 commands.
         [TERMINAL: 3-node swarm bootstrap — drawn]
            (node1) $ docker swarm init --advertise-addr 192.168.1.10
            # Swarm initialized. To add a worker, run:
            #   docker swarm join --token SWMTKN-1-xxxx 192.168.1.10:2377
            (node2) $ docker swarm join --token SWMTKN-1-xxxx \
                       192.168.1.10:2377
            (node3) $ docker swarm join --token SWMTKN-1-xxxx \
                       192.168.1.10:2377
            (node1) $ docker node ls
         [TECH] `swarm init` generates TWO join tokens — one for workers,
            one for managers (`docker swarm join-token manager`); each
            token embeds the CA fingerprint, so a node cannot join by
            guessing an address alone. Ports 2377/tcp, 7946/tcp+udp,
            4789/udp must be open between all nodes.
         [ANALOGY] The join token is a wristband with a hidden watermark —
            knowing the door address (IP:2377) alone doesn't get you in;
            you need the exact token from that specific cluster.
         🔬 easypj: once 3 nodes show in `docker node ls`, deploying
            easypj as a Swarm service (28.4) can spread its replicas
            across all 3 — the same laptops that ran `docker compose up`
            in Part 4 now form a real cluster.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run the sequence above (or `docker context
            create` + SSH for remote nodes). Verify: `docker node ls` on
            node1 lists all 3 with STATUS Ready and one MANAGER STATUS
            Leader.
         📊 SUMMARY: init on the first manager, join with its token from
            every other node, confirm with node ls.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: losing the join token is not fatal — regenerate it
            anytime with `docker swarm join-token --rotate worker`.

    28.4 Deploying Services in Swarm
         - docker service create (like docker run, but multi-host)
         - docker service ls, docker service ps
         - docker service scale easypj=5
         - docker service update --image easypj:v2.0 easypj
         - docker stack deploy (like docker compose, for Swarm)
         [TERMINAL: Service lifecycle commands]

         🎯 GOAL: Take easypj from image to running, scaled Swarm service
            using the SAME verbs `docker run` already taught you.
         [TERMINAL: service lifecycle — drawn]
            $ docker service create --name easypj --replicas 3 \
                -p 8000:8000 ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1
            $ docker service ls
            $ docker service ps easypj          # which node runs what
            $ docker service scale easypj=5
            $ docker service update --image ghcr.io/you/easypj:v2 easypj
            $ docker stack deploy -c stack.yaml easypj
         [TECH] `docker service create` is `docker run` plus a desired
            replica COUNT — Swarm's orchestrator keeps re-creating tasks
            until `docker service ps` shows that many Running. `docker
            stack deploy` reads a compose-format file and creates one
            service PER top-level service key — multi-service Compose,
            cluster-wide.
         [ANALOGY] `docker run` promises "start THIS container"; `docker
            service create` promises "always have N of these running,
            somewhere" — a standing order, not a one-time request.
         🔬 easypj: `docker stack deploy -c stack.yaml easypj` turns
            Chapter 11's compose.yaml (with `deploy:` keys added) into
            django, postgres, redis and nginx services in ONE command.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: scale easypj to 5, then back to 3. Verify:
            `docker service ps easypj` always shows exactly N tasks
            Running — extras get shut down, not left orphaned.
         📊 SUMMARY: service create/scale/update = docker run with a
            standing replica count; stack deploy = compose, cluster-wide.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker service update --image` triggers a rolling
            update by DEFAULT — it is not an in-place restart, it follows
            28.8's update-config rules.

    28.5 Swarm Networking & Storage
         - Overlay network (multi-host)
         - Routing mesh: any node can serve any service
         - Swarm volumes: volume drivers for multi-host
         [DIAGRAM: Routing mesh across Swarm nodes]

         🎯 GOAL: Explain why curling ANY node's IP on the published port
            reaches easypj, even nodes running zero easypj tasks.
         [DIAGRAM:flow — routing mesh, drawn]
            client ─▶ curl node3:8000 (node3 runs NO easypj task)
                    ▼
            node3's IPVS load balancer (every node runs one)
                    ▼
            overlay network "ingress" ─▶ routes to a node WITH a task
                    ▼
            easypj task (on node1 or node2) answers
         [TECH] The routing mesh is IPVS-based load balancing running on
            EVERY node, listening on the published port and forwarding
            over the built-in `ingress` overlay network to a node that
            actually holds a task — you never track which node runs what.
            Overlay uses VXLAN (UDP 4789) between nodes.
         [ANALOGY] Same idea as 26.4's Service — but the "front desk"
            here runs on EVERY node's door, not one central switch: knock
            on any door in the building, staff walk you to the right room
            regardless of which door you chose.
         🔬 easypj: expose easypj with `-p 8000:8000` in `service create`;
            `curl http://<any-node-ip>:8000/jobs/` succeeds from all 3
            nodes even though only 2 nodes hold an easypj task.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `docker service ps easypj` to find a node
            holding NO task, then curl exactly that node's IP on :8000.
            Verify: it still returns 200 — proof the mesh, not task
            placement, answered.
         📊 SUMMARY: routing mesh + ingress overlay = every node is a
            valid entry point, regardless of task placement.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: routing mesh needs ports 7946 (tcp/udp) and 4789
            (udp) open between ALL nodes — one blocked node breaks the
            mesh in ways that look like random 502s.

    28.6 Swarm Secrets & Configs
         - docker secret create
         - docker config create
         - Mounting secrets/configs in services
         [CODE: Swarm stack with secrets for easypj]


         🎯 GOAL: Get SECRET_KEY into a running easypj task without ever
            writing it into an image, an env var, or compose.yaml.
         [CODE: stack.yaml with a Swarm secret — drawn]
            services:
              django:
                image: ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1
                secrets: [django_secret_key]
                environment:
                  SECRET_KEY_FILE: /run/secrets/django_secret_key
            secrets:
              django_secret_key:
                external: true
            # created once, out-of-band:
            # $ printf 'django-insecure-CHANGE-ME' | \
            #     docker secret create django_secret_key -
         [TECH] A Swarm secret is encrypted at rest in the Raft log and
            mounted as an in-memory tmpfs FILE at /run/secrets/<name> —
            ONLY inside services that declare it, never as a visible env
            var (`docker inspect` cannot leak it). Configs (`docker
            config create`) use the same mechanism for non-secret files.
         [ANALOGY] A Swarm secret is a note passed hand-to-hand and never
            pinned to the office corkboard (env vars, visible in inspect);
            a Config is the same delivery method for a memo that isn't
            sensitive, just needs to reach exactly the right desk.
         🔬 easypj: settings.py reads `open(os.environ["SECRET_KEY_FILE"]
            ).read().strip()` instead of `os.environ["SECRET_KEY"]` — one
            line changed, and the value never appears in `docker service
            inspect easypj`.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: create the secret, deploy the stack, then
            `docker exec` into the running task and `cat
            /run/secrets/django_secret_key`. Verify: the file exists and
            reads correctly, but `docker service inspect --pretty easypj`
            never prints the value itself.
         📊 SUMMARY: secrets and configs mount as files, encrypted in the
            Raft store, scoped to services that explicitly request them.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: secrets are IMMUTABLE — updating one means creating
            `name_v2`, updating the service to reference it, then removing
            the old one; there is no `docker secret update`.

    28.7 Raft Consensus & Swarm Lock/Unlock
         - Quorum math: managers need (N/2)+1 alive to keep writing state
         - Why odd counts (1,3,5,7) beat even counts for the SAME tolerance
         - --autolock: encrypt the Raft log at rest with a passphrase
         - docker swarm unlock after every manager reboot when autolock is on
         - docker swarm unlock-key --rotate to rotate the unlock key

         🎯 GOAL: Compute how many manager failures a 5-manager Swarm
            tolerates, and know exactly when you must run `swarm unlock`.
         [TABLE: manager count → quorum → tolerated failures]
            1 manager  | quorum 1 | tolerates 0 failures (no HA at all)
            3 managers | quorum 2 | tolerates 1 failure
            4 managers | quorum 3 | tolerates 1 failure (same as 3, wastes 1)
            5 managers | quorum 3 | tolerates 2 failures
            7 managers | quorum 4 | tolerates 3 failures
         [TECH] Raft requires a strict MAJORITY ((N/2)+1, integer division)
            of managers reachable to commit any write to cluster state. An
            even manager count adds a node without adding tolerated
            failures — 4 managers need quorum 3, identical tolerance to 3
            managers, for one more machine to patch and pay for.
            `--autolock` (docker swarm init --autolock) encrypts the
            on-disk Raft log; every manager restart then BLOCKS at "Swarm
            is locked" until `docker swarm unlock` supplies the passphrase.
         [ANALOGY] Quorum is a company board vote: 5 members need 3 to
            agree — losing 2 still passes votes. Adding a 6th member
            (even count) needs 4 to agree, so you gained a salary without
            gaining tolerance. Autolock is a safe that relocks itself on
            every reboot — useful against a stolen disk, useless if you
            lose the combination.
         🔬 easypj: a 3-manager Swarm running easypj survives ONE manager
            dying at 3 AM with zero manual action — the surviving 2 still
            form quorum; a 2-manager Swarm survives ZERO manager failures
            (quorum would need both).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: on a 3-manager test Swarm, stop one manager's
            docker daemon and immediately run `docker service ls` on a
            survivor. Verify: it still responds (quorum intact); then stop
            a second manager and verify writes now hang (quorum lost).
         📊 SUMMARY: managers need a strict majority to write state —
            always deploy ODD counts (3 or 5), and store the autolock
            passphrase somewhere that survives losing every manager at once.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: losing quorum does not stop RUNNING services — tasks
            keep executing, but scaling, updates and new deployments all
            hang until quorum is restored.

    28.8 Advanced Service Update Configuration
         - --update-parallelism: how many tasks update at once
         - --update-delay: pause between each batch
         - --update-failure-action: pause (default) vs continue vs rollback
         - --update-max-failure-ratio: tolerate some failures before acting
         - --update-monitor: how long to watch a new task before judging it
         - --update-order: stop-first (safe) vs start-first (zero-downtime)

         🎯 GOAL: Configure a service update so a bad v2 image pauses
            itself automatically instead of taking down all 5 replicas.
         [TERMINAL: creating a hardened update policy — drawn]
            $ docker service update \
                --update-parallelism 1 \
                --update-delay 20s \
                --update-failure-action rollback \
                --update-max-failure-ratio 0.2 \
                --update-monitor 30s \
                --update-order start-first \
                --image ghcr.io/you/easypj:v2 easypj
         [TECH] Swarm updates ONE batch (parallelism tasks) at a time,
            waits `update-monitor` to see if new tasks stay healthy, then
            either proceeds after `update-delay` or acts on
            `update-failure-action` once failures exceed
            `update-max-failure-ratio`. `stop-first` kills the old task
            before starting new (safe, brief capacity dip); `start-first`
            starts new before killing old (zero-downtime, briefly
            over-capacity) — same maxSurge/maxUnavailable idea as K8s 27.1.
         [ANALOGY] parallelism+delay+monitor is a cautious surgeon:
            operate on one patient, watch their vitals for the monitor
            window, only move to the next batch if they're stable —
            failure-action is the "abort the procedure" protocol if too
            many patients react badly.
         🔬 easypj: `--update-order start-first` matters most for easypj's
            web tier — users never see a moment with fewer gunicorn
            workers than before, unlike the default stop-first.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: deploy a tag that crashes on boot with this
            policy active. Verify: `docker service ps easypj` shows the
            update auto-pause after roughly max-failure-ratio's share of
            tasks fail, rather than cycling through all 5 replicas.
         📊 SUMMARY: parallelism/delay/monitor control the PACE of an
            update; failure-action/max-failure-ratio decide what happens
            when it goes wrong; order picks safe vs zero-downtime.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the default failure-action is `pause`, not
            `rollback` — a bad update just STOPS mid-way unless you set
            `--update-failure-action rollback` explicitly (see 28.9).

    28.9 Service Rollback Configuration
         - --rollback-parallelism, --rollback-delay, --rollback-monitor
         - --rollback-failure-action and --rollback-max-failure-ratio
         - --rollback-order: stop-first vs start-first, same as updates
         - Automatic rollback: triggered by --update-failure-action rollback
         - docker service ps easypj to watch task states during either

         🎯 GOAL: Roll a broken easypj service back to its previous spec
            with the SAME safety knobs an update gets, then verify it.
         [TERMINAL: manual rollback + watching task states — drawn]
            $ docker service update --rollback \
                --rollback-parallelism 1 --rollback-delay 10s \
                --rollback-order start-first easypj
            $ docker service ps easypj
            # NAME       IMAGE       DESIRED STATE  CURRENT STATE
            # easypj.1   easypj:v1   Running        Running 2m ago
            # easypj.1   easypj:v2   Shutdown       Failed 3m ago
         [TECH] `--rollback` re-applies the service's PREVIOUS spec using
            the same batching machinery as a forward update — every
            --update-* flag has a --rollback-* twin. When
            `--update-failure-action rollback` is set on the original
            update, Swarm triggers this automatically once
            max-failure-ratio is exceeded — no human runs the command.
         [ANALOGY] Rollback isn't a separate "undo" engine — it's the
            exact same relay-handoff machinery as 28.8, just running
            backwards: same batching, same pause points, same order flag.
         🔬 easypj: an automatic rollback restores easypj:v1 the moment
            enough v2 tasks fail their startup, often before an on-call
            engineer even opens their laptop.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: deploy a broken image with
            `--update-failure-action rollback --update-max-failure-ratio
            0.2`, then watch `docker service ps easypj` unprompted.
            Verify: it flips back to the previous image WITHOUT you
            typing `--rollback` — task history shows both attempts.
         📊 SUMMARY: rollback reuses update's batching/order flags in
            reverse; set failure-action to rollback to make it automatic.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker service ps` only keeps a short task
            history by default — diagnose a failed update FAST, before
            the Shutdown/Failed tasks needed for `docker service logs`
            age out.

    28.10 Placement Constraints, Preferences & docker_gwbridge
         - node labels: docker node update --label-add
         - constraints (hard, must match) vs preferences (soft, best-effort)
         - --constraint 'node.labels.type==db' vs --placement-pref
         - docker_gwbridge: each node's bridge onto external/host networks
         - ingress network (routing mesh) vs docker_gwbridge (egress) roles

         🎯 GOAL: Pin postgres to labeled storage nodes while spreading
            easypj EVENLY across zones — hard rule vs soft hint, correctly.
         [TERMINAL: labels + constraint + preference — drawn]
            $ docker node update --label-add type=db node1
            $ docker service create --name postgres \
                --constraint 'node.labels.type==db' postgres:15
            $ docker node update --label-add zone=a node1
            $ docker node update --label-add zone=b node2
            $ docker service create --name easypj --replicas 6 \
                --placement-pref 'spread=node.labels.zone' \
                ghcr.io/you/easypj:v1
         [TECH] A constraint is a hard FILTER — Swarm leaves a task
            Pending forever rather than place it where the constraint
            fails. A placement preference is a soft SORT — Swarm spreads
            tasks as evenly as possible across the label's values but
            still places them if perfect spread is impossible.
            `docker_gwbridge` is the bridge network every node creates so
            containers can reach the OUTSIDE world (and the routing mesh
            reach the node); `ingress` is the overlay the mesh uses
            task-to-task — different networks, different jobs.
         [ANALOGY] A constraint is a locked door (db data only enters the
            db room); a preference is a maître d' seating parties evenly
            across sections without ever turning a guest away.
            docker_gwbridge is the building's street-facing exit; ingress
            is the internal hallway connecting every room.
         🔬 easypj: `--constraint 'node.labels.type==db'` keeps postgres's
            volume on the ONE node with the actual disk; easypj's
            `--placement-pref` keeps web tasks balanced across zone a/b
            so a single zone outage doesn't take all replicas at once.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: apply both commands above, then `docker
            service ps postgres` and `docker service ps easypj`. Verify:
            postgres tasks land ONLY on labeled node1; easypj tasks split
            close to evenly between zone a and zone b nodes.
         📊 SUMMARY: constraints are hard filters (can fail to schedule);
            preferences are soft spreaders (always schedule); gwbridge
            handles egress, ingress handles the routing mesh.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: an impossible constraint (label typo, zero nodes
            match) leaves the task Pending FOREVER with no error — confirm
            with `docker node ls --filter` before assuming a scheduler bug.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 28 — minimum 10, covering: manager vs worker
     roles and Raft-held state, quorum math for 3/5/7 managers and why
     even counts waste a node, --autolock and unlock-key rotation,
     service create/scale/update verbs, the routing mesh vs ingress
     overlay, Swarm secrets/configs as mounted files vs env vars,
     update-parallelism/delay/failure-action/order semantics, automatic
     rollback triggers, docker service ps task-state reading, hard
     constraints vs soft placement preferences, docker_gwbridge vs
     ingress network roles]

Chapter 29: Docker Compose Bridge to Production
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Chapters 25-28 gave you two full orchestration
    paths — Kubernetes and Swarm. This chapter answers the question every
    team eventually asks: do we run Compose everywhere, or is there a real
    bridge from a laptop's compose.yaml to whatever orchestrator production
    actually uses? Problem solved here: "my team knows Compose — how much
    of that knowledge survives the move to real production infrastructure?"

    29.1 Docker Compose for Local, K8s for Prod
         - Development: docker compose (fast feedback)
         - Production: Kubernetes (scalability, resilience)
         - Parity: similar concepts, different scale
         [DIAGRAM: Dev → Staging → Production pipeline]

         🎯 GOAL: State which tool owns each stage of easypj's path from a
            laptop to production, and why the SAME image crosses all three.
         [DIAGRAM:flow — dev to prod, drawn]
            laptop: docker compose up  ─▶  CI: docker build + push  ─▶
            staging: kubectl apply -k overlays/staging  ─▶
            prod: kubectl apply -k overlays/prod (or docker stack deploy)
         [TECH] Compose optimizes for FEEDBACK SPEED on one machine — bind
            mounts, hot reload, no registry round-trip. Kubernetes (or
            Swarm) optimizes for RESILIENCE across many machines — the
            same image, no bind mounts, health-checked and self-healing.
            The image built in Chapter 8 is the one constant object.
         [ANALOGY] Compose is a workshop bench (fast, one craftsperson,
            tools within arm's reach); K8s/Swarm is the assembly line
            (slower to set up, built for volume and to keep running when
            one station goes down) — same product design, different stage.
         🔬 easypj: the Dockerfile from Chapter 5 never changes across
            this diagram — only WHO reads it (compose locally, kubectl/
            Swarm after CI pushes it) changes per stage.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: diff your local compose.yaml against
            overlays/prod's patched replica count and env vars. Verify:
            the differences are ONLY scale/config — the image tag and
            container ports match everywhere.
         📊 SUMMARY: one image, one Dockerfile, three consumers — compose
            for feedback speed, K8s/Swarm for resilience at scale.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: bind-mounting source code (Chapter 11.6) in a
            production Deployment defeats the point of an immutable image
            — production always COPIES code in at build time.

    29.2 docker compose up vs kubectl apply
         - compose: docker compose up -d
         - K8s: kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
         - compose: docker compose logs
         - K8s: kubectl logs deployment/easypj
         - compose: docker compose down
         - K8s: kubectl delete -f deployment.yaml
         [COMMAND MAPPING TABLE: 20 docker compose commands → kubectl equivalents]

         🎯 GOAL: Translate any daily `docker compose` command to its
            kubectl equivalent without pausing to think.
         [TABLE: compose → kubectl, the 8 you'll type daily]
            docker compose up -d       | kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
            docker compose down        | kubectl delete -f deployment.yaml
            docker compose ps          | kubectl get pods
            docker compose logs -f app | kubectl logs -f deploy/easypj
            docker compose exec app sh | kubectl exec -it deploy/easypj -- sh
            docker compose restart app | kubectl rollout restart deploy/easypj
            docker compose build       | docker build (K8s never builds)
            compose scale app=3 | kubectl scale deploy/easypj --replicas=3
         [TECH] `compose down` deletes containers, networks (and, with
            -v, volumes) it created; `kubectl delete -f` deletes exactly
            the objects the YAML defines — a PVC survives Deployment
            deletion unless its own manifest is deleted too, mirroring
            compose's volume-survives-down default.
         [ANALOGY] Same muscle memory, new gym: the MOVEMENT (start, stop,
            watch logs, get a shell) is identical, only the equipment
            name changed — you're not learning new exercises, just labels.
         🔬 easypj: `docker compose logs -f django` becomes `kubectl logs
            -f deploy/easypj` — same tail-the-output habit from Chapter
            11.5, same gunicorn "Listening at :8000" line at the end.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: for every command from Chapter 11's labs,
            write its kubectl equivalent from this table. Verify: you can
            operate easypj in K8s without re-deriving a single command.
         📊 SUMMARY: compose and kubectl share the SAME verbs (up/down,
            logs, exec, scale) — only object names and flags differ.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: `docker compose build` has NO kubectl equivalent —
            Kubernetes never builds images, it only schedules ones that
            already exist in a registry (back to 25.3/25.4).

    29.3 kompose — Automatic Conversion
         - kompose convert -f docker-compose.yml
         - Generated files: deployment.yaml, service.yaml, etc.
         - Reviewing and modifying generated manifests
         [TERMINAL: kompose workflow for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Generate a first-draft manifest set from easypj's real
            compose.yaml, then know precisely what still needs hand-editing.
         [TERMINAL: kompose workflow — drawn]
            $ kompose convert -f compose.yaml -o k8s/
            # k8s/django-deployment.yaml, django-service.yaml,
            #     postgres-deployment.yaml,
            #     postgres-claim0-persistentvolumeclaim.yaml
            $ kubectl apply -f k8s/
         [TECH] `kompose convert` maps each compose service to a
            Deployment (+ Service if ports: exist) and each named volume
            to a PersistentVolumeClaim; it does NOT set resource requests/
            limits, probes, or replicas beyond 1 — those are manual
            additions from Chapters 26-27.
         [ANALOGY] kompose is a first-draft translator, exactly as in
            25.4 — fast and literal, never production-ready without a
            human editor adding the parts (health, resources, replicas)
            the source document never had to specify.
         🔬 easypj: kompose correctly builds postgres's PVC from
            `volumes: [postgres_data:/var/lib/postgresql/data]`, but the
            generated Deployment has NO readinessProbe — add 27.3's probe
            by hand before trusting it.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: run kompose convert on your real easypj
            compose.yaml. Verify: open django-deployment.yaml and confirm
            it is MISSING resources: and readinessProbe — that gap is
            your checklist for going to production.
         📊 SUMMARY: kompose bootstraps the boilerplate in seconds; the
            production hardening from Chapters 26-27 is still on you.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: re-running kompose convert after editing generated
            files OVERWRITES your edits — treat its output as a one-time
            starting point, not a regenerable source of truth.

    29.4 Helm — The Package Manager for K8s
         - What is Helm? (apt/yum for Kubernetes)
         - helm create easypj (scaffold chart)
         - values.yaml (parameterization)
         - helm install easypj ./easypj-chart
         [CODE: Helm chart structure for easypj]

         🎯 GOAL: Package easypj as one installable, versioned,
            configurable unit — the way `apt install` packages a binary.
         [CODE: Helm chart layout + values — drawn]
            easypj-chart/
              Chart.yaml            # name, version
              values.yaml           # replicaCount: 3, image.tag: v1.0
              templates/
                deployment.yaml     # {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
                service.yaml
                configmap.yaml
            $ helm install easypj ./easypj-chart --set image.tag=v2.0
            $ helm upgrade easypj ./easypj-chart --set replicaCount=5
            $ helm rollback easypj 1
         [TECH] Helm templates are Deployment/Service YAML with
            `{{ .Values.x }}` placeholders filled from values.yaml at
            install/upgrade time — one chart, N environments, via --set
            or per-env values files (values-staging.yaml). `helm upgrade`
            tracks a release history, so `helm rollback` works like
            27.2's rollout undo but chart-wide.
         [ANALOGY] Kustomize (27.4) is sticky notes on a fixed blueprint;
            Helm is a blueprint with FILL-IN-THE-BLANKS built in from the
            start — replicaCount, image tag and resource sizes are
            parameters, not patches bolted on after the fact.
         🔬 easypj: values-prod.yaml sets replicaCount: 5 and
            resources.limits.memory: 512Mi; values-dev.yaml sets
            replicaCount: 1 — one chart installs BOTH shapes of easypj.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `helm install easypj ./easypj-chart --dry-run
            --debug`. Verify: the rendered output is valid Deployment/
            Service YAML with every {{ .Values.x }} resolved to a real
            value — a placeholder left unresolved means a typo.
         📊 SUMMARY: Helm = templated manifests + a versioned release
            history + one command to install, upgrade or roll back.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Helm's `--set` silently accepts typos in a values
            KEY (unlike a YAML schema error) — a misspelled key just gets
            ignored, and your override never applies.

    29.5 Kustomize — Template-Free Customization
         - What is Kustomize? (built into kubectl)
         - base/ and overlays/ structure
         - kubectl apply -k overlays/staging
         [CODE: Kustomize structure for easypj environments]

         🎯 GOAL: Patch easypj's replica count and resource limits per
            environment using ONLY kubectl and plain YAML — no templating
            engine, no values file, no server-side component to run.
         [CODE: kustomize base + overlay — drawn]
            kustomize/
              base/
                deployment.yaml, service.yaml, kustomization.yaml
              overlays/
                staging/kustomization.yaml   # replicas: 2
                prod/kustomization.yaml      # replicas: 5, resources patch
            $ kubectl apply -k kustomize/overlays/prod
         [TECH] Kustomize is BUILT INTO kubectl (`apply -k`) — it merges a
            base manifest with strategic-merge or JSON patches declared in
            an overlay's kustomization.yaml; no templating language, no
            `{{ }}` syntax, purely YAML-on-YAML patches.
         [ANALOGY] If Helm (29.4) is fill-in-the-blanks, Kustomize is
            sticky notes and highlighter marks directly on a photocopy of
            the base blueprint — same base, a visibly different overlay
            per environment, no separate templating tool required.
         🔬 easypj: base/deployment.yaml ships replicas: 1; overlays/
            prod/kustomization.yaml patches it to replicas: 5 and adds
            the resources block from Chapter 27.5 — base file untouched.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: `kubectl apply -k overlays/staging` then
            `kubectl apply -k overlays/prod` into separate namespaces.
            Verify: `kubectl get deploy -A` shows the SAME base image
            with DIFFERENT replica counts per namespace, no duplicated
            YAML.
         📊 SUMMARY: Kustomize = base + overlays, patch-based, no
            templating engine or extra binary beyond kubectl itself.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: Kustomize has NO release history or rollback
            command like Helm — reverting a bad overlay means editing
            the patch and re-applying, or reaching for git revert.

    [LAB QUESTIONS: Chapter 29 — minimum 10, covering: which stage each
     tool owns (compose vs CI vs kubectl), why compose build has no
     kubectl equivalent, the compose→kubectl command mapping, kompose's
     conversion gaps (resources, probes, replicas), Helm chart anatomy
     and values.yaml templating, helm upgrade/rollback release history,
     Kustomize base/overlay patching vs Helm templating, why Kustomize
     needs no extra binary, choosing kompose vs manual vs Helm vs
     Kustomize for a given team]


Chapter 30: Docker Certification Preparation
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    WHY THIS CHAPTER EXISTS: Every prior chapter built REAL skill; this
    chapter converts that skill into a credential an employer can verify
    in thirty seconds. The DCA exam does not test trivia — its six
    domains map almost exactly onto Parts 1-9 of this guide, weighted
    toward exactly the material you have already practiced hands-on.
    Problem solved here: "I can run Docker in production — now prove it
    on paper, efficiently, without re-studying from zero."

    30.1 Docker Certifications Overview
         - Docker Certified Associate (DCA) — the core certification
         - Exam format: 55 multiple-choice questions, 90 minutes
         - Passing score: ~65-70% (varies per exam version)
         - Delivery: Online proctored or testing center
         - Prerequisites: 6-12 months Docker experience recommended
         - Validity: 2 years from certification date
         [TABLE: DCA Certification Quick Facts]

         🎯 GOAL: State the exam's shape — format, length, passing bar,
            validity — and decide if you are inside the readiness window.
         [TABLE: DCA Certification Quick Facts — drawn]
            Format        | 55 multiple-choice questions
            Duration      | 90 minutes
            Passing score | ~65-70% (Docker does not publish an exact cut)
            Delivery      | Online proctored, or a Pearson VUE test center
            Prerequisite  | none enforced; 6-12 months hands-on recommended
            Validity      | 2 years, then re-certification required
            Retake policy | 14-day wait after a failed attempt
         [TECH] DCA: 55 questions, 90 minutes (~98 seconds each), a mix of
            classic multiple-choice and discrete-option MC (each option
            judged alone — effectively several true/false per question).
            It is administered by Docker/Mirantis via a third-party
            proctor and tests PRACTICAL command recall and
            troubleshooting scenarios, not conceptual essays — exactly
            the 🧪 PRACTICE [LAB] drills this guide has run since Ch1.
            Recommended runway: 6-12 months hands-on — which Parts 1-9 of
            this guide compress substantially if you did every lab.
         [ANALOGY] A driving test, not a physics exam: it checks trained
            reflexes on realistic situations. You cannot cram reflexes —
            you can only have driven (done the labs). Examiners care that
            you can execute the maneuver (`docker service update
            --rollback`) under time pressure, not define orchestration
            in an essay.
         💡 Readiness heuristic: you can rebuild easypj's stack — image,
            compose, swarm service, secured daemon — from an empty shell
            without notes. If yes, schedule the exam.
         🔬 easypj: your study log IS this guide's lab trail; the exam's
            scenarios (broken service, wrong network, missing volume) are
            the same failures you triaged in Chapters 6, 13-15 and 16-18
            — every terminal block in Parts 1-9 that used a real easypj
            command is direct exam rehearsal, just with the names swapped.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: take any 10 practice questions (Appendix H)
            cold, timed at 98s each. Verify: ≥7/10 → begin 30.2's domain
            plan; below → note WHICH domains leaked and reread them.
         📊 SUMMARY: 55 Qs / 90 min / ~65-70% / 2 years — a reflex test
            over Parts 1-10 of exactly this material; know the logistics
            cold so exam day has zero surprises.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: discrete-option questions punish guessing
            patterns — every option is scored, so "pick the longest
            answer" folklore actively hurts here. There is also no
            experience CHECK at registration — Docker will take your
            money for an exam you aren't ready for; 30.2's domain
            weights are the real gatekeeper.

         🎯 GOAL: Convert the domain bars above into a personal study
            budget: hours per domain proportional to weight × weakness.
         [TECH] The bars are your syllabus contract: Orchestration 25% +
            Images 20% = nearly half the exam. Map the domains to this
            guide: D1 → Ch28 (+ 25-27 concepts), D2 → Ch4-9 + Ch23,
            D3 → Ch2-3 (daemon.json, logging/storage drivers), D4 →
            Ch13-15, D5 → Ch19-21, D6 → Ch16-18.
         [ANALOGY] A weighted dartboard: the 25% ring is physically
            bigger — aiming your practice time at the big rings is not
            cheating, it is reading the board.
         💡 Study math: 20 study hours → 5h Swarm, 4h images, 3h each for
            install/networking/security, 2h storage — then REBALANCE
            using your Appendix H per-domain scores.
         🔬 easypj: for each domain, name the easypj artifact that proves
            it: swarm stack (D1), multi-stage Dockerfile (D2), tuned
            daemon.json (D3), custom bridge + overlay (D4), locked-down
            compose (D5), pgdata volume + backup (D6). Six artifacts =
            six domains owned.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: score yourself 1-5 per domain, multiply by
            the weights, write the hour budget. Verify: your two biggest
            products get your next two study sessions.
         📊 SUMMARY: study WHERE the points are — Orchestration + Images
            ≈ 45%; master Ch28 and Ch4-9 before polishing anything else.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: candidates fail Orchestration most — not because
            Swarm is hard, but because they practiced Kubernetes instead.
            The DCA tests SWARM.

    30.3 Essential Docker Commands for the Exam
         - All docker container, image, volume, network commands
         - docker system commands
         - docker service commands (Swarm)
         - docker stack commands
         - docker secret, config commands
         [COMMAND REFERENCE TABLE: 50+ exam-essential commands]

         🎯 GOAL: Recall command families by their NOUN and pick correct
            flags under time pressure.
         [TECH] The CLI is a grammar (Ch3): docker <noun> <verb> —
            container/image/volume/network/system/service/stack/secret/
            config/node/swarm. The exam exploits near-misses: ps vs
            ps -a, rm vs rmi, save vs export, prune scopes, logs vs
            service logs, inspect on EVERY noun.
         [ANALOGY] Conjugation tables for a language you already speak:
            you know the words; the exam checks the irregular verbs —
            asymmetries like stop (SIGTERM + grace) vs kill (SIGKILL now).
         ⚡ Drill set: docker system df | docker system prune -a |
            docker events --since 1h | docker service ps --no-trunc |
            docker inspect --format '{{.State.ExitCode}}' <container>
         🔬 easypj: run every family once against the jobpost stack in
            one sitting — list, inspect, prune, stats — so each command
            has a remembered OUTPUT, not just a remembered name.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: from Appendix A, cover the example column and
            write the command for 20 random rows. Verify: ≥18/20 exact,
            flags included — exam questions quote flags verbatim.
         📊 SUMMARY: noun-first recall + the classic near-miss pairs =
            most of the command points on the exam.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: prune scopes — docker image prune removes DANGLING
            only; add -a and it removes all UNUSED. The exam loves that
            one word.

    30.4 Dockerfile Writing for the Exam
         - Every instruction you must know
         - Multi-stage builds
         - Best practices that exam graders look for
         [DOCKERFILE TEMPLATE with exam annotations]

         🎯 GOAL: Answer any Dockerfile-instruction question by pointing
            at the layer it creates and the moment it executes.
         [TECH] The exam's Dockerfile axis is build-time vs run-time
            (Ch4): RUN executes at BUILD, CMD/ENTRYPOINT at START; COPY
            vs ADD (ADD = COPY + URL + auto-untar — prefer COPY); EXPOSE
            documents, -p publishes; ARG dies when the build ends, ENV
            persists; exec form vs shell form decides who receives
            SIGTERM. Multi-stage: COPY --from=builder carries artifacts,
            dev toolchains never ship.
         [ANALOGY] A recipe's prep column vs serve column: RUN chops
            (baked into layers); CMD plates the dish when a guest
            (container start) arrives. Multi-stage = prep kitchen and
            serving counter in separate rooms.
         💡 The six ENTRYPOINT/CMD combinations (Ch4's table) ARE an exam
            question — rebuild that table from memory the week before.
         🔬 easypj: the Ch9 production Dockerfile contains every examable
            instruction — recite it line by line and give the WHY of each
            (cache order, non-root USER, exec-form CMD).
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: write a two-stage Python Dockerfile on paper
            in 5 minutes: builder installs into a venv, runtime copies
            it, non-root, exec-form gunicorn. Verify: against Appendix
            D.1 — every deviation is a study item.
         📊 SUMMARY: build vs run time, COPY vs ADD, ENTRYPOINT×CMD, and
            multi-stage mechanics cover nearly all Dockerfile questions.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: "EXPOSE opens the port" is the most common WRONG
            answer on the exam — EXPOSE alone publishes nothing.

    30.5 Docker Compose for the Exam
         - compose.yaml structure
         - Service configuration
         - Networks and volumes in Compose
         - docker compose commands
         [COMPOSE TEMPLATE with exam annotations]

         🎯 GOAL: Answer Compose questions from the file's shape: which
            top-level key, which service key, what default.
         [TECH] compose.yaml = four top-level key groups: services,
            networks, volumes, configs/secrets. Exam picks: named volume
            vs bind mount syntax (short vs long form), depends_on ≠
            "wait until ready", the default per-project user-defined
            bridge (DNS by service name), ports "8000:8000" vs expose,
            override/profiles precedence (Ch12).
         [ANALOGY] The file is an org chart, not a script: services are
            departments, networks the hallways, volumes the archive
            room — questions ask who CONNECTS to whom, not what runs
            first.
         🔬 easypj: the Ch11 full-stack compose file is your flashcard:
            django, postgres (PostgreSQL, as always), redis, nginx —
            every examable key appears in it at least once.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: from memory, write the minimal compose.yaml
            for django + postgres with a named volume and a healthcheck-
            gated depends_on. Verify: docker compose config accepts it —
            it validates without starting anything.
         📊 SUMMARY: know the four top-level keys, the two mount syntax
            forms, and what depends_on does NOT promise.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: the exam still shows version: "3.8" headers —
            legacy in modern Compose but valid on the test; don't let it
            rattle you.

    30.6 Docker Swarm for the Exam
         - Swarm initialization and node management
         - Service deployment and scaling
         - Rolling updates and rollbacks
         - Secrets and configs in Swarm
         [SWARM COMMAND REFERENCE TABLE]

         🎯 GOAL: Hit the exam's heaviest domain (Orchestration, 25%)
            with total command of the Swarm lifecycle.
         [TECH] The examable loop: swarm init/join (+ tokens) → node
            ls/update (drain, labels) → service create (replicas,
            publish, secrets, constraints) → scale → update (28.8 flags)
            → rollback (28.9) → stack deploy. Plus the theory spine:
            quorum math (28.7), routing mesh vs host-mode publishing,
            secrets' tmpfs delivery (28.6).
         [ANALOGY] This is the exam's parallel-park: a fixed maneuver
            sequence, practiced until it needs no thought — because a
            quarter of the marks sit on it.
         ⚡ The five most-asked: quorum for N managers, drain a node,
            rotate a join token, publish mode ingress vs host, roll back
            a failed update.
         🔬 easypj: run the ENTIRE loop once on your 3-node lab swarm
            with the jobpost stack — init to rollback in one sitting is
            the single highest-value hour of DCA preparation.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: time that full loop. Verify: under 15 minutes
            without notes, and each command's OUTPUT (not just the
            command) feels familiar when the exam screenshots it.
         📊 SUMMARY: one practiced lifecycle covers a quarter of the
            exam; 28.7-28.10 are the difference between pass and
            near-miss.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: docker service scale easypj=5 and docker service
            update --replicas 5 easypj are BOTH correct — the exam may
            list both; read for "select ALL that apply".

    30.7 Exam Strategy & Tips
         - Time management: 90 minutes, 55 questions
         - Question types: multiple choice, discrete option multiple choice
         - Hands-on vs theory balance
         - Common pitfalls and how to avoid them
         - Recommended practice resources
         [EXAM STRATEGY: 10 tips for success]

         🎯 GOAL: Convert knowledge into points: pacing, flagging, and
            elimination tuned to the DCA's format.
         [TECH] 90 min / 55 Qs = 98s each. Three-pass method: pass 1
            answers everything answerable in <60s and FLAGS the rest
            (~70 min); pass 2 revisits flags (~15 min); pass 3 is a
            sanity sweep (~5 min). Discrete-option MCs: judge each option
            as its own true/false — half-knowledge still harvests points.
            No penalty for wrong answers: never leave blanks.
         [ANALOGY] A buffet with a countdown: sweep the room once taking
            everything easy, THEN queue for the carved roast (the hard,
            flagged questions) — starting with the roast starves you.
         💡 Elimination heuristics that work on the DCA: absolute words
            ("always", "only") are usually wrong; an option naming a
            nonexistent flag is instantly false — your Appendix A drills
            make fakes glow.
         🔬 easypj: translate abstract questions into jobpost concrete:
            "a service's tasks won't schedule" → your 28.10 Pending-
            constraint lab. Recalling the LAB beats recalling the doc.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: one full Appendix H mock, strict 90-minute
            timer, three-pass method. Verify: ≥75% AND fewer than 8 flags
            left after pass 1 — both numbers matter (accuracy AND pace).
         📊 SUMMARY: pace to 98s, three passes, judge options
            independently, answer everything.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: burning 5 minutes on question 3 is how prepared
            candidates fail — the flag button is a skill; practice IT,
            not just content.

    30.8 Docker Exam Day Checklist
         - System requirements for online proctoring
         - Allowed resources (Docker docs!)
         - Pre-exam setup: environment verification
         - During exam: browser-based terminal access
         [CHECKLIST: 15 exam day action items]

         🎯 GOAL: Arrive at exam start with zero environment surprises
            and your reference reflexes ready.
         [TECH] Online proctoring needs: a machine passing the vendor's
            system check (webcam, mic, screen share), ONE display, a
            clean desk, government ID matching your registration, stable
            bandwidth and a quiet room — verified in the ~15-minute
            onboarding before the clock starts. Know the CURRENT exam
            rules on accessible resources; train assuming memory-only.
         [ANALOGY] Race-day rule: nothing new on race day — no new
            browser, no untested webcam, no first-time VPN. Every
            variable you can rehearse, rehearse.
         [CHECKLIST — the night before]
            □ vendor system check passed on THE machine you will use
            □ ID matches the registration name exactly
            □ weakest-domain notes (30.2 budget) reviewed once, closed
            □ Appendix H wrong-answer list re-taken (only the wrongs)
            □ sleep > one more mock — reflexes beat cramming
         🔬 easypj: make your last technical act the 30.6 Swarm loop on
            the jobpost stack — end prep on your strongest, heaviest
            domain.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: T-minus-1-day dress rehearsal: system check,
            desk cleared, one 90-minute mock at the same hour as the real
            slot. Verify: no step surprised you — that is the point.
         📊 SUMMARY: logistics are free points; lose them and your
            knowledge never gets to speak.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: a second monitor left plugged in can void the
            session at check-in — unplug it, don't just switch it off.

    30.9 The 95% Gap-Filling Checklist
         - the ~15 topics candidates most often report missing, mapped to
           the chapter of THIS guide that closes each gap
         - use it as the final pre-exam audit: tick the row, or go reread
         - each row = one Appendix H mini-drill after the reread

         [TABLE: commonly missed DCA topics → where this guide covers it]

         🎯 GOAL: Close the specific gaps that separate a 60% near-miss
            from a comfortable pass.
         [TECH] These topics share a profile: rarely used day-to-day,
            heavily weighted on the exam — infrastructure-owner concerns
            (quorum, daemon config, signing, benchmarks) that app-focused
            developers never touch in normal work. That is WHY they are
            the gap: experience alone does not produce them.
         [ANALOGY] The driving test's hand signals: nobody signals by arm
            in real traffic, but the examiner asks — and candidates fail
            on exactly the parts daily driving never exercises.
         🔬 easypj: shipping jobpost taught you ~85% of the DCA by
            osmosis; this table is the other 15% that only deliberate
            study covers — every row cites the chapter that already did.
         🧪 PRACTICE [LAB]: tick each row you can explain aloud in two
            sentences; reread the cited section for every non-tick, then
            do 3 Appendix H questions from that domain. Verify: a fully
            ticked table AND ≥80% on a fresh Appendix H mixed set.
         📊 SUMMARY: the exam is passed in the corners, not the center —
            audit the corners last.
            ⚠️ Gotcha: don't study this table FIRST — without Parts 1-10
            underneath it, memorized corners collapse under
            scenario-style questions.

    A.9 Docker Trust Commands (sign, verify)
    [COMMAND REFERENCE: Every command with syntax, description, and example]

    A.10 Docker Context Commands (create, ls, use, inspect, rm — manage multiple engines/environments)
         - contexts store endpoint + TLS config under a NAME: docker
           context create prod --docker "host=ssh://deploy@prod-server"
         - beats ad-hoc SSH + DOCKER_HOST exports: named, persistent,
           switchable per command (docker --context prod ps) — no env-var
           leakage between terminals or scripts
         - docker context use switches the DEFAULT target for every
           following command; docker context ls marks the active one (*)

Appendix B: Dockerfile Quick Reference — All Instructions
    B.1 Complete instruction list with syntax and examples
    B.2 Dockerfile templates (Python, Node.js, Go, Java, Static HTML)
    B.3 Multi-stage Dockerfile templates
    B.4 Dockerfile for Django (easypj template)
    B.5 .dockerignore templates

    B.6 Modern Dockerfile Features (BuildKit era)
         - here-doc RUN blocks: RUN <<EOF ... EOF — multi-line scripts
           without && chains; one layer, readable
         - COPY --chmod=755 --chown=app:app — ownership/permissions set
           at copy time (no extra RUN chown layer)
         - RUN --mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/pip — persistent
           build caches across builds; --mount=type=secret for build-time
           credentials that never enter any layer
         - parser directives: # syntax=docker/dockerfile:1 as line 1 opts
           into the latest Dockerfile frontend features

Appendix C: docker-compose.yml Quick Reference
    C.1 Complete compose.yaml reference (all keys)
    C.2 compose.yaml templates (Django+Postgres, Django+Postgres+Redis, etc.)
    C.3 compose override templates (dev, staging, prod)
    C.4 Health check templates
    C.5 watch configuration templates

    C.6 Compose File Power Features
         - extension fields: top-level x-* blocks hold reusable chunks
           the parser ignores (x-common-env, x-logging)
         - YAML anchors & aliases: &defaults / <<: *defaults — define
           once, merge into many services; pairs with x-* fields
         - top-level name: key sets the project name inside the file
           itself (replaces relying on the directory name or -p flag)

    F.8 Exit-Code Decoder
         - 0    clean exit — process completed successfully
         - 1    application error — read the app's own logs first
         - 125  docker run ITSELF failed (bad flag, daemon error) — the
                container never started
         - 126  command found but not executable (permissions, not a
                binary)
         - 127  command not found (typo, missing binary, wrong PATH)
         - 137  SIGKILL — OOM kill (check docker inspect
                .State.OOMKilled) or docker kill / stop-timeout expiry
         - 139  SIGSEGV — segfault inside the process (native code, bad
                library)
         - 143  SIGTERM honored — a graceful docker stop

Appendix G: Docker Glossary — A to Z
    G.1 50+ Docker terms defined clearly
    G.2 Docker acronyms explained

    G.3 Modern & Exam-Critical Terms — Glossary Must-Haves
         - BuildKit — the modern build engine behind docker build
           (parallel stages, cache mounts, build secrets)
         - digest — immutable sha256 identity of an image; tags move,
           digests never do
         - manifest list — one tag serving multiple architectures
           (amd64/arm64); what docker pull resolves on Apple Silicon
         - docker context — named CLI target (local socket, ssh remote);
           see A.10
         - live-restore — daemon.json flag: containers survive dockerd
           restarts
         - routing mesh — Swarm's any-node:port → healthy-task routing
           (ingress network + IPVS)
         - DOCKER-USER chain — the iptables chain reserved for YOUR
           firewall rules, evaluated before Docker's own
